# The facilitation ecology — six surrounding territories, the entries

This file holds the entries behind `ops/facilitation ecology — six surrounding territories.md` for five of its six territories, in five parts:
- Part 1 holds the 42 entries on living systems (chemistry, cells, organisms, and ecosystems): 16 principles, 14 theories, and 12 findings, with the eight regularities the researcher proposes hold across scales placed first;
- Part 2 holds the 34 entries on the body and the nervous system, centered on Jonny Miller's Nervous System Mastery;
- Part 3 holds the 34 entries on what psychedelic guides do, each placed under the earliest phase of the arc it serves;
- Part 4 holds the 33 entries on how a text or a work of art facilitates with no one present;
- Part 5 holds the 33 entries on the pointing-out traditions, grouped by tradition.

The sixth territory, the eleven coaches and groups the corpus gathers, is catalogued move by move in `ops/facilitation ecology — the coaches' moves.md`.

Each entry has a table of its coordinates and, where the researcher recorded them, a second table of its practitioners' own words with plain meanings. The row "What is facilitated" answers the author's question of what is being facilitated for that entry, and the row "Across scales" says where the entry's pattern holds and where it stops. The rows "How it bears on the book" and "Where it differs from the book" are the researcher's readings of the vault, not the author's. The last rows say where the entry came from and whether it was verified there, and the row "In the vault" names the vault file nearest to the entry.

The row "Who decides" uses the survey's five positions. At A the person decides all the content; at B the facilitator shapes the process and the people supply the content; at C the facilitator offers content as an option the person may take or leave; at D the facilitator directs or interprets with authority; and at E no live facilitator is present, and a structure, a text, or a custom does the work. The row "Scale" uses the survey's bands, with three added for this supplement: molecules and cells, one organism, and an ecosystem.

The same entries can be filtered by territory, by phase of the arc, and by the book's concerns on the explorable page at https://claude.ai/artifact/DuVyj9ecaSZuBzXXgBEXHG, under the tab "Surrounding territories."

## Part 1. Living systems: chemistry, cells, organisms, and ecosystems

### Candidate invariants, proposed by the researcher

Each of these states one regularity the researcher proposes holds across scales, with the entries that support it and the cases that break it. They are marked partly verified because they are the researcher's synthesis of verified entries, not findings any single source states.

#### Candidate invariant: facilitation works only within thresholds, capacities, doses and periods

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Facilitation works only within bounds: below a threshold it has no effect, above a certain level it saturates or turns harmful, and in time it is often available only during a limited period. |
| Steps | (1) The entry quorum-sensing records that the collective behavior switches on only when the signal concentration passes a threshold. (2) The entry long-term-potentiation records that a weak input alone does not induce potentiation, and several together can. (3) The entries michaelis-menten-saturation and facilitated-diffusion record that rates level off once every enzyme or carrier is occupied. (4) The entry hormesis records that low doses of a stressor help and high doses harm. (5) The entry sabatier-principle records that the most active catalysts bind with intermediate strength, at the maximum of the volcano plot. (6) The entry critical-period-reopening records that the open state lasts a measured time and then closes. (7) The entry paired-pulse-facilitation records that facilitation occurs only when the second impulse follows within tens of milliseconds. (8) The entry allostasis records that systems forced beyond their operating range break down. |
| What is facilitated | Each supporting entry names its own facilitated process, and this invariant concerns the range over which facilitation works. |
| Across scales | Thresholds, saturation and limited periods are each documented from molecules to organisms in these entries; that the same bounds govern help between people is not tested here. |
| The evidence | Each kind of bound is measured in the entries named, from molecules to organisms; hormesis, one of the supports, is itself contested. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: If help has an upper bound past which it harms, the amount of an intervention is part of whether it helps. Timing and pacing: A limited period means the same input can matter at one time and do nothing at another. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | These bounds are measured quantities, whereas the book's reader has no measure of dose for an experience. |
| Limits and critiques | Hormesis is disputed and has been used to argue for weaker regulation (hormesis). Some critical periods are weak, so learning stays possible after them (critical-period-reopening). Stigmergy depends on positive feedback, in which larger heaps attract more deposits, rather than saturating early (stigmergy). |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the plasticity window reorganizes only what attention is on.md`; The plasticity note concerns one such bound, a period of plasticity with a measured length; this invariant places it among thresholds, capacities and doses. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quorum_sensing; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_potentiation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michaelis%E2%80%93Menten_kinetics; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitated_diffusion; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormesis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_principle; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10284704/fullTextXML; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_facilitation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allostasis |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *threshold* | the level below which an input has no effect |
| *saturation* | the level above which more input produces no more effect |

#### Candidate invariant: whether a facilitator is used up depends on scale

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism, an ecosystem |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | At the molecular scale a facilitator comes out of the process unchanged, while at the scale of organisms and communities a facilitator is often changed, displaced, or outgrown by what it helped, so whether a facilitator is consumed depends on the scale. |
| Steps | (1) The entry catalysis-rate-not-endpoint records that catalysts are not consumed and are recovered unchanged. (2) The entry michaelis-menten-saturation records that each cycle of an enzyme regenerates the free enzyme. (3) The entry chaperones-and-anfinsen records that Hsp70 chaperones release a folded protein and diffuse away. (4) The entry nurse-plant-replacement records that the saguaro increases die-back and death in the paloverde that sheltered it. (5) The entry connell-slatyer-succession records that in the facilitation model, pioneers make the site less suitable for themselves and are replaced. (6) The entry allostasis records that repeated adjustment carries a cumulative cost for the organism that adjusts. |
| What is facilitated | Each supporting entry names its own facilitated process, and this invariant concerns what happens to the facilitator. |
| Across scales | The invariant itself states that one property changes with scale: not being consumed holds for molecules and fails for many living facilitators. |
| The evidence | The molecular half is textbook chemistry and biochemistry; the ecological half rests on a single field study of saguaros and on the codified facilitation model of succession. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | What care costs the one who gives it: The chemistry that gives the word catalyst its meaning describes a helper untouched by helping, which the ecological cases show does not describe living helpers. Timing and pacing: Succession and nurse plants show facilitation that ends with the facilitator's departure, so stepping back can be the completion of facilitation rather than its failure. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's facilitator chooses to step back, whereas pioneer plants and nurse trees are displaced without choice. |
| Limits and critiques | Facilitation cascades are the counter-case at the community scale, since facilitators persist and species accumulate (facilitation-cascade), and engineers such as beavers benefit from the habitats they make (ecosystem-engineers). At the molecular scale, catalysts can be poisoned, and a substance that shifts an equilibrium is consumed as a reactant (catalysis-rate-not-endpoint, sabatier-principle). |
| Quoted from its sources | "certain formulas carry a witness inside the equation — the way some reactions require a catalyst that's never consumed, but without which nothing proceeds at all" (ops/second person ecology.md) |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | moving through stages; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/grief is sever plus love plus persist.md`; The corpus describes the witness as a catalyst 'that's never consumed'; this invariant records that 'never consumed' holds for molecules and fails for many living facilitators. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michaelis%E2%80%93Menten_kinetics; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaperone_(protein); https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A28311446%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connell%E2%80%93Slatyer_model_of_ecological_succession; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allostasis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitation_cascade |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *regeneration* | a catalyst or enzyme returning to its starting form after each cycle |
| *species replacement* | the helped species taking the place of its helper |

#### Candidate invariant: a facilitator must bind strongly enough to act and weakly enough to release

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | A facilitator has to bind or hold what it helps strongly enough to act on it and weakly enough to release it, since too weak a bond does nothing and too strong a bond blocks the result. |
| Steps | (1) The entry sabatier-principle records that surface binding must be neither so weak that reactants do not bind nor so strong that products do not leave and the surface is poisoned. (2) The entry enzyme-induced-fit records that the first enzyme–substrate contact is weak and strengthens through a change of shape, and enzymes bind the transition state more tightly than the substrate. (3) The entry chaperones-and-anfinsen records that Hsp70 binds an unfolded chain and loses its affinity once the chain has folded. (4) The entry catalysis-rate-not-endpoint records that poisons act by binding irreversibly and blocking the catalyst. |
| What is facilitated | What is facilitated is a reaction or a folding that requires the facilitator first to bind and then to release. |
| Across scales | It holds for surfaces, enzymes and chaperones and is untested above the molecular scale in the sources read, so any wider use is a proposal. |
| The evidence | Every supporting case is molecular and well established; no source read measures a counterpart above the molecular scale. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The principle states for molecules that binding which never releases prevents the outcome the binding was for. Timing and pacing: Release is part of the facilitating act, not something that happens after it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Molecular binding strength is fixed by materials, whereas a person can loosen or tighten a hold during a session. |
| Limits and critiques | All supporting cases are molecular. The one ecological case of a lasting close association in this file, the nurse plant, ends in competition rather than release (nurse-plant-replacement). |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/grief is sever plus love plus persist.md`; The corpus's space-around survey proposed catalytic surfaces, holding reactants in the right orientation, as a definition of facilitation; this invariant adds the release the proposal leaves out. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterogeneous_catalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_principle; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme_catalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaperone_(protein); https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *volcano plot* | a curve of rate against binding strength with its peak at intermediate strength |
| *poison* | a substance that binds a catalyst too strongly and blocks it |

#### Candidate invariant: a facilitator changes how easily a change happens, not which change it is

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism, an ecosystem |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | A facilitator makes it easier or faster for a system to reach a state that the system's own conditions already favor, and does not by itself choose which state that is. |
| Steps | (1) The entry catalysis-rate-not-endpoint records that a catalyst lowers the activation energy, leaves the equilibrium unchanged, and leaves the uncatalyzed pathway open. (2) The entry nucleation-sites records that a surface lets a phase change begin only in a system that is already metastable, and it supplies no driving force. (3) The entry facilitated-diffusion records that a channel or carrier opens a route, and the concentration gradient sets the direction of movement. (4) The entry chaperones-and-anfinsen records that most chaperones keep chains from clumping and do not appear to affect the protein's final state, which its sequence specifies. (5) The entry permissive-and-instructive-signals records that in ferret visual cortex, experience is required, but each neuron's tuning is predicted by biases present before experience. |
| What is facilitated | What is facilitated is the system's own approach to a state its conditions favor. |
| Across scales | It holds exactly at the molecular scale and in some developmental cases, and it weakens in open systems such as communities and persons, where facilitators can change what is possible. |
| The evidence | The invariant is exact in chemistry, where thermodynamics forbids a catalyst to shift an equilibrium, and it is supported by several measured biological cases; it has measured exceptions in embryos, in protein folding and in ecology. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: Where the invariant holds, a facilitator affects how easily a person changes but not what the person changes into. Who decides the content: The counter-cases mark where a facilitator does decide content, which is where a method's claim to leave content to the person needs checking. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book does not assume that a person has one favored end state waiting to be reached, which the chemical form of the invariant assumes. |
| Limits and critiques | The invariant breaks where a facilitator changes which outcome is possible: the Spemann–Mangold organizer changes what host cells become (permissive-and-instructive-signals); steric chaperones supply structure and prions impose their own (chaperones-and-anfinsen); nurse plants let seedlings live where they otherwise could not (nurse-plant-replacement, ecological-facilitation); and Branchi holds that context can instruct a person's outcome (permissive-instructive-causality-psychiatry). In chemistry itself, a substance that shifts an equilibrium does so by reacting and is then consumed (catalysis-rate-not-endpoint). |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/grief is sever plus love plus persist.md`; The corpus's grief formula places a witness as a catalyst; this invariant states what the catalyst loan would imply if taken literally, and where it fails. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitated_diffusion; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfinsen's_dogma; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A30001203%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spemann%E2%80%93Mangold_organizer |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *equilibrium* | the state a reaction settles at, which a catalyst does not move |
| *permissive* | an influence needed for a change that does not shape its specific outcome |

#### Candidate invariant: slower recovery signals an approaching shift

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | A system approaching a tipping point recovers more slowly from small disturbances, so rising autocorrelation and variance in its fluctuations can signal that a shift is near. |
| Steps | (1) The entry critical-slowing-down records that Scheffer and colleagues propose generic early-warning signals for a wide class of systems, and van de Leemput and colleagues found such signals before shifts into and out of depression. (2) The entry critical-slowing-down records that the same measure has been used to report declining resilience in most of the Amazon rainforest. (3) The entry alternative-stable-states-hysteresis records that the signals presuppose systems with more than one stable state, documented in lakes and on reefs. (4) The entry ecological-resilience records that the slowing measures a loss of the capacity to absorb disturbance without a regime shift. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated, since the invariant concerns detecting an approaching change. |
| Across scales | It is the most explicit cross-scale claim among these entries, stated by its authors for a wide class of systems, and its evidence is weaker for individual mood than for ecosystems. |
| The evidence | The authors state the claim across ecosystems, climate, finance and mood; its evidence is strongest in ecology and thinnest for individual mood. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: A change can be preceded by a measurable slowing in how a person's moods recover, a sign taken from outside the person's own report. Timing and pacing: If slowing precedes a shift, a period of lingering in each state may mean that a change is near rather than that nothing is happening. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The signal predicts that a shift is near without saying which way it will go or what it will mean to the person. |
| Limits and critiques | In highly random systems the state can flip between basins before slowing appears (critical-slowing-down). For depression the idea was called one that 'still needs empirical proof', and in adolescents the signals did not anticipate increases in depression a year later (critical-slowing-down). The evidence for alternative stable states is itself mixed (alternative-stable-states-hysteresis). |
| Quoted from its sources | "still needs empirical proof" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Critical%20slowing%20down%20in%20depression%20is%20a%20great%20idea%20that%20still%20needs%20empirical%20proof%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no research on tipping points. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Scheffer%20M%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Early-warning%20signals%20for%20critical%20transitions%22%20AND%20PUB_YEAR%3A2009&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Critical%20slowing%20down%20as%20early%20warning%20for%20the%20onset%20and%20termination%20of%20depression%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Critical%20slowing%20down%20in%20depression%20is%20a%20great%20idea%20that%20still%20needs%20empirical%20proof%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A33050891%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_transition; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_stable_state; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_resilience |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *critical slowing down* | slower recovery from small disturbances near a tipping point |
| *tipping point* | a threshold at which a system shifts abruptly to a different state |

#### Candidate invariant: facilitation matters more as conditions grow harsher

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | an ecosystem |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Facilitation between neighbors tends to matter more as conditions grow harsher, while competition dominates where conditions are mild, though the pattern depends on the kind of stress and on the species involved. |
| Steps | (1) The entry stress-gradient-hypothesis records that a synthesis of 727 tests found that plant interactions shift toward facilitation, or toward weaker competition, as stress rises, and never toward stronger competition. (2) The entry ecological-facilitation records that facilitation is more likely in physically stressful environments, as in a salt marsh where black needle rush lowers soil salinity for marsh elder at low elevations and competes with it at higher, less salty ones. (3) The entry nurse-plant-replacement records that saguaros establish mainly under nurse plants in the desert. |
| What is facilitated | What is facilitated is survival under harsh conditions. |
| Across scales | The source applies the hypothesis to microbial and plant communities; above that scale it is untested in the sources read. |
| The evidence | The support comes from plant communities, where it is broad but contested; no source read tests the pattern for persons or human groups. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: If the pattern held for people, help would matter most when conditions are harshest, which bears on when a supportive presence is most needed. Conflict: The pattern predicts that the same neighbors compete when conditions ease, which treats rivalry and help as responses to conditions rather than fixed traits. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Plants' effects on each other are measured in survival and growth, not in intention. |
| Limits and critiques | Maestre and colleagues predict that neighbors compete at both ends of a gradient when the stress is a shared scarce resource such as water and both species have similar life histories, and the hypothesis is called controversial because of contradictory results (stress-gradient-hypothesis). The nurse relation turns into competition as the helped plant grows (nurse-plant-replacement). |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no research on how help depends on harshness. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A23363430%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1111/j.1365-2745.2008.01476.x; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_gradient_hypothesis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_facilitation; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A28311446%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *stress gradient* | a change in harshness across space |
| *resource stress* | stress from the lack of something both neighbors use, such as water |

#### Candidate invariant: the facilitated system's own structure decides the form of its change

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism, one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | What a facilitated system becomes is decided largely by its own structure and history, and a facilitator's input more often permits or triggers that change than specifies it. |
| Steps | (1) The entry chaperones-and-anfinsen records that a small protein's sequence determines its fold, and most chaperones supply no structural information. (2) The entry permissive-and-instructive-signals records that a few hours of unpatterned activation is enough for direction selectivity to appear in ferret cortex, with tuning predicted from prior biases. (3) The entry permissive-instructive-causality-psychiatry records that in Branchi's argument, biological substrates act only permissively on mental states. (4) The entry paired-pulse-facilitation records that a synapse's own starting release probability decides whether repeated input facilitates or depresses. (5) The entry zajonc-drive-theory-and-cockroaches records that on Zajonc's theory an audience strengthens whatever response is already dominant. (6) The entry levin-multiscale-competency records that on Levin's account, rewriting a set point can be enough, with the route left to the tissue's own competence. (7) The vault's Maturana and Varela note reaches the same claim for living systems in general, read at the primary text. |
| What is facilitated | What is facilitated is the system's own change, along lines that its structure sets. |
| Across scales | It is supported from molecules to tissues and, as theory, in psychiatry, and its counter-cases occur at the same scales as the supporting cases. |
| The evidence | The supporting cases are experimental at the molecular, synaptic and tissue scales and theoretical at the scale of persons; the counter-cases are experimental too, so this is a tendency with measured exceptions rather than a law. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The supporting cases describe change whose form comes from the changing system, the biological form of the book's claim that the reader authors her own change. Harm and its conditions: The exceptions, prions and imprinting among them, show that at every scale some inputs do impose form, and some of those are harmful. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The biological cases concern systems without reflection, whereas the book's reader can take up or refuse an influence. |
| Limits and critiques | Counter-cases occur at the same scales: the organizer's signals specify what host cells become (permissive-and-instructive-signals); steric chaperones supply form and prions impose it (chaperones-and-anfinsen); Lorenz's goslings attached to him because he was the first moving object they met (critical-period-reopening); and Branchi holds that factors outside a person can instruct the person's overall outcome, which departs from Maturana and Varela's denial of instructive interaction (permissive-instructive-causality-psychiatry). |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/an interaction can trigger a change in a living system but cannot specify it.md`; The vault's claim note states this invariant for living systems from Maturana and Varela; this entry gathers molecular, synaptic and developmental support and the measured exceptions. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfinsen's_dogma; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A30001203%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A42563280%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_facilitation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_facilitation; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC8988303/fullTextXML; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spemann%E2%80%93Mangold_organizer; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_period |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |

| *permissive* | needed for a change without shaping its specific outcome |
| *instructive* | shaping the specific outcome |

#### Candidate invariant: much facilitation works by changing a shared environment

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | synthesis |
| Originators | Synthesis by the living-systems researcher from the entries named in steps; no single originator |
| Date | 2026-09-26 (this synthesis) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, molecules and cells, a large group |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Much facilitation works by changing a shared environment, such as shade, soil, habitat, a trace, or the concentration of a signal, rather than by acting on the facilitated organism directly. |
| Steps | (1) The entries ecological-facilitation and nurse-plant-replacement record that nurse plants help seedlings through shade, soil moisture and nutrients. (2) The entry ecosystem-engineers records that engineers are defined by modifying resources and habitat, not by their place in the food web. (3) The entry facilitation-cascade records that each species creates habitat for the next. (4) The entry stigmergy records that traces of work left in the environment prompt further work with no direct communication. (5) The entry quorum-sensing records that bacteria coordinate through the concentration of a signal in their medium. (6) The entry niche-construction records that organisms modify environments that then change selection on themselves and their descendants. (7) The entry sabatier-principle records that in heterogeneous catalysis a surface is the place where reactants meet and react. |
| What is facilitated | What is facilitated is the activity of those who use the changed environment, on their own terms. |
| Across scales | The pattern appears in these entries at every scale from surfaces and bacterial signals to insect colonies and whole communities. |
| The evidence | The supporting cases span surfaces, bacteria, insect colonies and plant and marine communities, the widest range of any invariant here, and each is measured at its own scale. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: These cases describe help that works by changing the setting in which others act, the ecological counterpart of tending conditions rather than directing a person. Who decides the content: When help works through a shared environment, those helped decide how to use the changed setting. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | An organism that changes a shared environment usually does so for its own benefit, whereas a facilitator who prepares a room does so for others. |
| Limits and critiques | Direct partnerships such as mycorrhizae exchange nutrients between the partners themselves (ecological-facilitation); enzymes act by binding their substrates directly (enzyme-induced-fit); claims that help passes between trees through fungal networks are not supported (common-mycorrhizal-networks); and environments shaped by one species can exclude others (ecosystem-engineers). |
| Mental model | other. a synthesis across chemistry, biology, ecology and social psychology |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no ecology of habitat change; its container claims come from the practice traditions. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_facilitation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem_engineer; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitation_cascade; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stigmergy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quorum_sensing; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niche_construction; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterogeneous_catalysis |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *trace* | the change an action leaves in a shared environment |
| *habitat* | the place and conditions in which an organism lives |

### The entries

#### Allostasis and allostatic load

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | physiology and social epidemiology |
| Originators | Peter Sterling and Joseph Eyer (allostasis, 1988); Bruce McEwen (allostatic load); McEwen and John C. Wingfield (two types of allostatic overload) |
| Date | 1988 |
| Scale | one organism, a community or public |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Sterling and Eyer proposed allostasis, 'stability through change', as a supplement to homeostasis in which the brain anticipates demands and adjusts the body's set points in advance rather than only correcting deviations. They reached it from epidemiology: cohorts that entered the labor market in times of high competition and insecurity later showed more illness and higher mortality, and hypertension was highest in the most socially disrupted groups, which genetic explanations did not account for. Bruce McEwen named the cumulative cost of these adjustments allostatic load: when demand is chronic, systems forced beyond their operating range break down. |
| What is facilitated | The body's anticipatory adjustment to expected demand is what allostasis describes, and in the short term it helps. The theory's point is that the social conditions setting the demand, and not the body alone, decide whether adjustment turns into disease. |
| Across scales | Sterling and Eyer's argument spans scales by design, from labor-market conditions to blood pressure. McEwen and Wingfield's second type of overload, driven by social conflict, is described as counterable only through learning and changes in social structure, which places part of the remedy outside the individual. |
| The evidence | The concept is widely used in stress research. Critics argue that it adds little: T. A. Day (2005) concluded that it offers nothing not already derivable from an accurate reading of homeostasis, and D. S. Ramsay and S. C. Woods (2014) found the concept diffuse and ambiguous in use. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The theory began from the finding that illness tracked social disruption across cohorts, which places part of a bodily difficulty in the structure around the person. What care costs the one who gives it: Allostatic load names a cumulative cost of repeated adjustment, which applies to helpers who adjust to other people's demands over years. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The theory is about bodily regulation and disease, and it places part of the remedy in social structure, which a book of inward practice cannot change. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics hold that the concept duplicates homeostasis and is vague in use, so a claim built on it should be checked against the specific mechanism it names. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Sterling and Eyer argued that homeostasis did not paint the whole picture of the body's physiological motives." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allostasis) "it is not clear that the concepts of 'allostasis' or 'allostatic load' offer us anything that was not already apparent, or at least readily derivable, from an accurate reading of the original concept of homeostasis" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22no%20help%20from%20allostasis%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Despite growing enthusiasm for allostasis, the concept remains diffuse, due in part to ambiguity as to how the term is understood and used" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Clarifying%20the%20roles%20of%20homeostasis%20and%20allostasis%20in%20physiological%20regulation%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, justice power |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no stress physiology. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allostasis; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22no%20help%20from%20allostasis%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Clarifying%20the%20roles%20of%20homeostasis%20and%20allostasis%20in%20physiological%20regulation%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *allostasis* | regulation by anticipating demand and changing set points, "stability through change" |
| *allostatic load* | the cumulative wear on the body from repeated or chronic adjustment |
| *allostatic overload* | the state in which the cost of adjusting causes disease |

#### Alternative stable states and hysteresis

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | ecology |
| Originators | Richard Lewontin (1969); C. S. Holling (1973); R. M. May (1977); Marten Scheffer and colleagues (2001, 'Catastrophic shifts in ecosystems') |
| Date | 1969; 2001 |
| Scale | an ecosystem, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The theory of alternative stable states holds that an ecosystem can persist in more than one stable state under the same conditions, held in each by its own feedbacks, and can shift between them when disturbed enough. Hysteresis is the case in which the path back differs from the path out, so that reversing the change that caused a shift does not reverse the shift. After Caribbean sea urchin populations crashed in 1983, algae overgrew coral, and when the urchins rebounded the former coral cover did not return; in shallow lakes, cutting nutrient input may not restore clear water once a lake has turned turbid. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated in the theory itself; it describes why some changes, once made, persist and resist reversal. Recovery from an unwanted state may need conditions well beyond those that existed before the shift. |
| Across scales | The same structure, two stable states with a path-dependent shift between them, is proposed for mood by van de Leemput and colleagues (see critical-slowing-down). In ecology its evidence is mixed, so the cross-scale claim begins from contested evidence. |
| The evidence | Most work on the theory has been theoretical, and the source reports a lack of direct manipulative experiments outside laboratory cultures. A 2005 review by Schröder and colleagues found that only some direct experiments were valid tests and that most but not all of those showed alternative stable states; ecologists also disagree on whether hysteresis is required for alternative states to exist. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Hysteresis describes a state held in place by its own feedbacks after its cause is gone, the structure the corpus claims for protective patterns that outlive their occasion. Remaining the author of one's own change: If a person's states behaved this way, removing the original cause of a difficulty would not by itself remove the difficulty, and a new state, once reached, would also persist. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Ecological hysteresis comes from feedbacks among species and nutrients, not from memory or meaning, so applying it to a person's protective patterns is an analogy. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In this ecological context, hysteresis refers to the existence of different stable states under the same variables or parameters." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_stable_state) "When urchins rebounded, the high (pre-crash) coral cover levels did not return, indicating hysteresis (Mumby et al. 2007)." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_stable_state) "There has been a lack of direct, manipulative experimental tests for alternative stable states." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_stable_state) "physics supplies a name for the persistence rather than evidence about it" (ops/queue/candidates.md) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology, memory past |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/wilhelm reich.md`; The Reich note records that the corpus's outward survey reaches for hysteresis from materials physics to explain why a protective pattern persists after its cause, and `ops/queue/candidates.md` records a 2026-08-07 pass judging that the physics supplies a name for the persistence rather than evidence about it. This entry supplies ecological hysteresis with its evidence and the state of that evidence. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_stable_state |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *state shift* | a move from one stable state to another, also called a regime shift |
| *hysteresis* | dependence of a system's state on its history, so that the path back differs from the path out |
| *domain of attraction* | the range of states from which a system returns to the same stable state |

#### Autopoiesis, its extension to social systems, and the sympoiesis objection

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | theoretical biology and its critics |
| Originators | Humberto Maturana and Francisco Varela (autopoiesis, 1972); Niklas Luhmann (application to social systems); Pablo Razeto-Barry (review, 2012); Donna Haraway (sympoiesis, 2016) |
| Date | 1972; 2016 (Haraway, Staying with the Trouble, p. 58) |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism, a society's customs |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Autopoiesis names a system that produces and maintains itself by making its own parts; Maturana and Varela introduced it to define the self-maintaining chemistry of living cells. Niklas Luhmann applied it to social systems, and others have applied it to law, organizations and architecture. Critics object on three fronts: that the concept fails to define or explain the living and serves a radical constructivist or solipsistic epistemology; that its influence on mainstream biology has been limited and it is not commonly used as a criterion for life (Razeto-Barry, 2012); and, in Donna Haraway's words as the source quotes them, that 'nothing makes itself', so sympoiesis, making-with, should take its place. |
| What is facilitated | Autopoiesis describes a living system's self-production, so on this account nothing outside the system produces its change. Haraway's objection denies that any living thing produces itself alone, which places part of every making in the relations between beings. |
| Across scales | Maturana and Varela defined autopoiesis for cells, and its extension to organisms, societies and law is disputed; the first objection above is aimed at those expansions. The sympoiesis objection asserts the opposite cross-scale claim, that at every scale beings are made with others. |
| The evidence | The criticisms are philosophical and historical rather than experimental. Razeto-Barry's 2012 review, 'Autopoiesis 40 Years Later. A Review and A Reformulation', also proposes a reformulation of the concept. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: Autopoiesis supports the claim that a living system makes itself, and Haraway's objection that nothing makes itself marks the limit of that claim. Participation rather than watching: Sympoiesis, making-with, is a biological framing for change that happens between participants rather than inside one of them. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Autopoiesis and its critics make claims about the nature of living systems, whereas the book is concerned with what a reader does, and it holds such claims as company rather than proof. |
| Quoted from its sources | "nothing makes itself; nothing is really autopoietic or self-organizing" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopoiesis) "Razeto-Barry believes that autopoiesis is not commonly used as the criterion for life." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopoiesis) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | identity self, relationship present, nature ecology |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/maturana and varela.md`; The vault holds Maturana and Varela at length, read at the primary text, with the idealism critique kept visible. It does not hold Luhmann's application to social systems, Razeto-Barry's assessment of the concept's limited use in biology, or Haraway's sympoiesis objection, which this entry adds. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopoiesis |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *autopoiesis* | self-production: a network of processes that makes the components that make the network |
| *sympoiesis* | Haraway's "making-with", the proposal that living things are made together rather than each by itself |
| *operational closure* | the condition that a system's own processes are sufficient to maintain the whole |

#### Catalysis: a catalyst lowers the activation energy and leaves the equilibrium unchanged

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | physical chemistry |
| Originators | Elizabeth Fulhame (the concept, 1794); Jöns Jakob Berzelius (the term, 1835); Svante Arrhenius (the term activation energy, 1889) |
| Date | 1794 to 1889 (Fulhame's book of 1794; Berzelius's annual report of 1835; Arrhenius 1889) |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | A catalyst is a substance that increases the rate of a chemical reaction and is recovered unchanged at the end. It works by giving the reaction an alternative pathway whose activation energy, the minimum energy the reactant molecules must have to react, is lower than that of the uncatalyzed pathway. A catalyst does not change the equilibrium of the reaction, that is, the proportion of products to reactants at which the reaction settles, and the uncatalyzed pathway remains open, so a catalyst can only add to the total rate. |
| What is facilitated | The reaction's own conversion of reactants into products is facilitated, and only in its speed. The catalyst does not supply the energy difference between reactants and products, which belongs to the reactants and their surroundings, and it does not choose where the reaction ends. |
| Across scales | The distinction between changing how fast a process goes and changing where it ends is exact for a closed chemical reaction that reaches equilibrium. It does not hold exactly in open living systems, where a facilitator can change which outcome occurs at all, as when a nurse plant lets a seedling survive at a site where it would otherwise die (see nurse-plant-replacement). |
| The evidence | This is textbook physical chemistry grounded in thermodynamics: a catalyst that shifted an equilibrium could be added and removed to release energy each time, which the second law of thermodynamics forbids. The source notes one case at the edge of the definition: a substance that reacts in a later step and so shifts the equilibrium concentrations is consumed, and is then a reactant as well as a catalyst. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: If a facilitator works the way a catalyst does, it can make a change easier and faster but cannot choose the state a person arrives at, which is set by the person's own conditions. The container that holds people while they change: The corpus says a witness is a catalyst "without which nothing proceeds at all", which goes further than the chemistry, since in chemistry the uncatalyzed reaction still proceeds, only more slowly. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Chemistry describes molecules with no aims, and a catalyst's effect is fixed by energies, whereas the book concerns persons whose endpoint is not fixed in advance. A pass through the vault's candidate list on 2026-08-07 recorded that taking more loans from chemistry would deepen the borrowing rather than test it, so this entry is offered as a test of the catalyst loan the corpus already makes. |
| Limits and critiques | Calling a person a catalyst imports the chemical claim that the catalyst is left unchanged; a human helper is changed by the work and can be depleted by it, which the chemistry does not describe. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Catalysts are not consumed by the reaction and remain unchanged after the reaction." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis) "The ratio of the forward and the reverse reaction rates is unaffected" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis) "the total rate (catalyzed plus noncatalyzed) can only increase in the presence of the catalyst and never decrease" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis) "A reaction occurs only if enough molecules have kinetic energy equal to or greater than this barrier, which usually requires sufficiently high temperature." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Activation_energy) "certain formulas carry a witness inside the equation — the way some reactions require a catalyst that's never consumed, but without which nothing proceeds at all" (ops/second person ecology.md) |
| Mental model | other. physical chemistry, outside the list of human-change paradigms |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/grief is sever plus love plus persist.md`; The note records the corpus's grief formula with a witness written in as a catalyst, and lists activation energy, nucleation, and catalytic surfaces as three loans from real chemistry that no one has researched; `ops/queue/candidates.md` records the 2026-08-07 pass that declined further loans. This entry supplies the chemistry of catalysis itself, including the rule that a catalyst never shifts an equilibrium. The corpus's catalyst sentence is quoted through `ops/second person ecology.md`, which cites it to first-telling p. 9 and atlas §IV.5. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Activation_energy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *activation energy* | the minimum energy reactant molecules must have for a reaction to occur |
| *transition state* | the highest-energy arrangement the reactants pass through on the way to products |
| *chemical equilibrium* | the balance of reactants and products at which the forward and reverse reactions run at equal rates |
| *catalyst poison* | a substance that irreversibly lowers a catalyst's activity |

#### Common mycorrhizal networks, and the 2023 critique of claims about them

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | forest ecology |
| Originators | Suzanne Simard (field studies, 1997); J. Karst, M. D. Jones and J. D. Hoeksema (critique, 2023) |
| Date | 1997; 2023 (Karst, Jones and Hoeksema, Nature Ecology & Evolution) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, the more-than-human |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | A common mycorrhizal network forms when the filaments (hyphae) of mycorrhizal fungi connect the roots of several plants underground. Popular accounts, often under the name 'wood wide web', say these networks are widespread in forests, transfer resources that help seedlings, and let mature trees send resources and warning signals preferentially to their offspring. Karst, Jones and Hoeksema reviewed the evidence in 2023: they found the first two claims insufficiently supported, the third without peer-reviewed published evidence, and unsupported claims in the scientific literature doubled over 25 years. |
| What is facilitated | Where the partnership occurs, nutrient uptake by plants is facilitated by fungi in exchange for carbon. Whether resources or signals pass between trees through the networks, to the benefit of seedlings or kin, is not established. |
| Across scales | The networks are often cited as evidence that cooperation and communication hold among trees as among people, and the critique shows that this cross-scale claim was made before the forest data supported it. The case shows the risk of applying a finding at another scale before it is established at its own. |
| The evidence | Mycorrhizal partnerships themselves are well established. The network claims are contested, and the 2023 review concluded that knowledge is too sparse and unsettled to guide forest management. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The case shows a widely repeated claim about help between living things that its own field's evidence does not support, and names citation bias as the mechanism. Being seen and recognized by another: The image of a mature tree recognizing and feeding its offspring is the most appealing version of the claim and the one with no published evidence. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book holds other traditions as company rather than proof, and this case shows the need for that rule: the popular image of trees helping their kin is not a finding. |
| Limits and critiques | Citing the networks as evidence of intelligence or care in nature repeats claims that the 2023 review found unsupported. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The claim that mature trees preferentially send resources and defence signals to offspring through CMNs has no peer-reviewed, published evidence." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Positive%20citation%20bias%20and%20overinterpreted%20results%20lead%20to%20misinformation%20on%20common%20mycorrhizal%20networks%20in%20forests%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "We conclude that knowledge on CMNs is presently too sparse and unsettled to inform forest management." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Positive%20citation%20bias%20and%20overinterpreted%20results%20lead%20to%20misinformation%20on%20common%20mycorrhizal%20networks%20in%20forests%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the ecology itself can become intelligent.md`; The note proposes that intelligence can arise between participants in an ecology of relations, and it does not cite fungal networks. This entry records that the best-known natural-science version of that idea, cooperation through forest fungal networks, is not supported as popularly told. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycorrhizal_network; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Positive%20citation%20bias%20and%20overinterpreted%20results%20lead%20to%20misinformation%20on%20common%20mycorrhizal%20networks%20in%20forests%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mycorrhiza* | a partnership between a fungus and a plant root in which nutrients are exchanged for sugars |
| *common mycorrhizal network* | fungal connections linking the roots of more than one plant |
| *positive citation bias* | a tendency to cite results showing effects and to pass over results that do not |

#### Connell and Slatyer's three models of succession: facilitation, tolerance, and inhibition

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | ecology (succession) |
| Originators | Joseph Connell and Ralph Slatyer |
| Date | 1977 ('Mechanisms of succession in natural communities and their role in community stability and organization', The American Naturalist 111: 1119–1144) |
| Scale | an ecosystem |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Connell and Slatyer proposed three ways that early species affect later ones during succession on newly exposed ground. In the facilitation model, pioneer species change the environment so that it becomes less suitable for themselves and more suitable for later species, which then replace them. In the tolerance model, early species neither help nor hinder later ones and the order follows the species' life histories; in the inhibition model, early species hold the site against newcomers until a disturbance removes them. |
| What is facilitated | In the facilitation model, the arrival and growth of later species is facilitated by the changes pioneers make to soil, shade and shelter. The pioneers do not supply the later species, and their own changes end their tenure. |
| Across scales | The facilitation model is one of the clearest statements that a facilitator can make itself unnecessary, and it holds for plant communities over decades. The same paper's other two models show that early occupants can also be neutral or obstructive. |
| The evidence | The three models codify mechanisms tested in many field studies, such as alder raising soil nitrogen for willow and poplar on an Alaskan floodplain. The older view of succession as an orderly progression to a fixed climax community has been largely abandoned in favor of nonequilibrium accounts. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | What care costs the one who gives it: In the facilitation model the helper's success changes the place so that the helper no longer fits it. Timing and pacing: The three models describe different sequences, so whether an early occupant helps, waits or blocks sets the pace of what follows. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Succession has no participant who aims at the outcome, and the book's facilitator steps back by choice rather than as a consequence of environmental change. |
| Limits and critiques | Real successions mix the three mechanisms and include chance, so assigning a case to one model is often disputed; the saguaro in the shade of other plants has been cited as a case of both facilitation and tolerance. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Thus, the presence of early successional species often changes the environment so that the habitat is less hospitable for the original species’ own ecological demands and facilitates the invasion of later-successional species." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connell%E2%80%93Slatyer_model_of_ecological_succession) "This idea has been largely abandoned by modern ecologists in favor of nonequilibrium ideas of ecosystems dynamics." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_succession) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | nature ecology, death finitude |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no succession ecology. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connell%E2%80%93Slatyer_model_of_ecological_succession; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_succession |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *pioneer species* | the first species to colonize newly exposed ground |
| *facilitation model* | early species make the site better for later species and worse for themselves |
| *tolerance model* | early species neither help nor hinder later species |
| *inhibition model* | early species block later species until a disturbance removes them |

#### Critical periods, and their reopening by psychedelics

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | neuroscience (developmental plasticity and psychedelic pharmacology) |
| Originators | David Hubel and Torsten Wiesel (visual critical period, 1963); Konrad Lorenz (imprinting, 1935 to 1938); R. Nardou, Gül Dölen and colleagues (psychedelic reopening, 2023); W. C. Abraham and M. F. Bear (the term metaplasticity, 1996) |
| Date | 1963; 2023 (Nardou and colleagues, 'Psychedelics reopen the social reward learning critical period', Nature) |
| Scale | one organism, molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | A critical period is a stage of development when the nervous system is especially responsive to particular experience: kittens with one eye sewn shut from birth to three months fully developed vision only in the open eye (Hubel and Wiesel, 1963), and Konrad Lorenz's newly hatched goslings, meeting him as the first moving object, followed him as if he were their parent. The closing of critical periods is linked to maturing inhibitory circuits and to perineuronal nets in the extracellular matrix. Nardou, Dölen and colleagues showed in adult male mice that psilocybin, LSD, ketamine and ibogaine, like MDMA in the group's earlier work, reopen a critical period for learning that social contact is rewarding, and that the open state lasts longer for drugs whose acute effects in humans last longer: present at 48 hours but gone by one week for ketamine, two weeks for psilocybin and MDMA, three weeks for LSD, and at least four weeks for ibogaine. The authors report metaplasticity, a change in how readily synapses can change, rather than a general rise in plasticity, and gene expression changes pointing to reorganization of the extracellular matrix. |
| What is facilitated | The animal's own learning that social contact is rewarding, which becomes difficult after adolescence, is made possible again for a limited time. The drug does not supply what is learned: the mice learned from the pairing of cage mates with a bedding cue that the experiment arranged. |
| Across scales | The finding is at the scale of a mouse's nervous system and one kind of learning, and the authors' statement that it explains the importance of the weeks after a human psychedelic session is an inference. Critical periods also vary in strength: some researchers distinguish strong periods, after which a skill cannot be acquired, from weaker sensitive periods, after which learning is harder but possible. |
| The evidence | The 2023 study is a set of experiments in adult male mice using a social conditioned place preference test, with electrophysiology and gene expression, and the human durations are taken from other studies. The paper states context dependence as a feature of MDMA-assisted psychotherapy and cites in vivo studies showing that spine formation after psychedelics is sparse and context dependent, but it does not itself test whether attention or context decides which circuits are reorganized. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The open state has a measured length that differs by drug, so the period after a peak experience is when change can be made and not only when a person recovers. Knowing that something happened: The paper answers part of the vault's open question: it establishes in mice that the period reopens and how long it stays open, while the claim that what is attended to is what gets reorganized rests on the studies the paper cites and on a statement the vault's synthesis attributes to the researcher, not on this experiment. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The study measures a drug effect in mice, whereas the book walks its reader through the same arc with no substance involved, so the finding bears on the book only by analogy. |
| Limits and critiques | The sentence the vault quotes, that only the circuits relevant to the context are reorganized, does not appear in the paper; the vault's synthesis (`provenance/high-energy states and system decoupling.md`) attributes it to the researcher without saying where it was said. The authors' clinical inference about the integration period goes beyond what the mouse experiments test. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Here we demonstrate in mice that the ability to reopen the social reward learning critical period is a shared property across psychedelic drugs" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10284704/fullTextXML) "the time course of critical period reopening is proportional to the duration of acute subjective effects reported in humans" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10284704/fullTextXML) "psychedelics induce metaplasticity rather than hyperplasticity" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10284704/fullTextXML) "These results provide a mechanistic explanation for the importance of the post-treatment integration period for clinical implementation of psychedelics" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10284704/fullTextXML) "dendritic spine formation following administration of psychedelics is both sparse and context dependent" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10284704/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | moving through stages; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | learning skill, relationship present, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the plasticity window reorganizes only what attention is on.md`; The note is marked incomplete because the primary paper was unread, and `ops/arc ecology.md` (item 17) asks whether the paper supports the directional claim or only the period's existence. This entry reads the paper's full text: the reopening and its durations are the paper's own results; context dependence is cited, not tested; and the sentence the note quotes is not in the paper. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%22Psychedelics%20reopen%20the%20social%20reward%20learning%20critical%20period%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10284704/fullTextXML; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_period; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaplasticity |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *critical period* | a developmental stage when particular experience has an outsized and lasting effect |
| *open state and closed state* | the authors' names for when the social reward learning period is available and when it is not |
| *metaplasticity* | a change in how readily synapses can change, a term coined by Abraham and Bear in 1996 |
| *perineuronal net* | an extracellular matrix structure around inhibitory neurons whose formation coincides with the closing of critical periods |

#### Early-warning signals of critical transitions (critical slowing down), including in mood

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | complex systems (ecology, climate, psychiatry) |
| Originators | M. Scheffer, J. Bascompte, W. A. Brock and colleagues (2009); I. A. van de Leemput, M. Wichers, D. Borsboom, M. Scheffer and colleagues (depression, 2014) |
| Date | 2009 ('Early-warning signals for critical transitions', Nature); 2014 ('Critical slowing down as early warning for the onset and termination of depression', Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Scheffer and colleagues proposed that many complex systems, from ecosystems to financial markets and the climate, show generic signals before a tipping point: as a system nears it, the system recovers more slowly from small disturbances, which shows up as rising autocorrelation and variance in its fluctuations. Van de Leemput and colleagues applied this to mood in healthy people and patients and found that the probability of a coming shift into or out of depression was related to higher autocorrelation, variance, and correlation between emotions in people's repeated self-ratings. They read this as support for the hypothesis that mood has alternative stable states separated by tipping points. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated; the finding concerns detecting that a system is nearing a shift. It offers a way to know that a change is near from the system's own fluctuations, without a model of the change's causes. |
| Across scales | This is one of the few findings among these entries whose authors claim scale invariance directly, for a wide class of systems. The claim holds for transitions that pass a bifurcation point; in highly random systems the state can flip before slowing appears, and in psychopathology the time scale and the kind of symptom decide whether the signal is present. |
| The evidence | The method has been applied to lakes, climate, forests and epileptic seizures, and it has been used to report declining resilience in more than three-quarters of the Amazon rainforest since the early 2000s. For depression, E. H. Bos and P. de Jonge replied in 2014 that the idea 'still needs empirical proof', and M. J. Schreuder and colleagues (2020) found in adolescents that early-warning signals in feeling down did not anticipate increases in depression a year later, concluding that for depression such signals may appear only weeks before a shift. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The finding offers a measurable sign that a person's state is nearing a shift, taken from their day-to-day ratings rather than from their report of insight. Timing and pacing: If a shift is preceded by slower recovery, the period before a change looks like longer lingering in each mood, which is a different sign from a sudden breakthrough. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The method is statistical and predictive, and it describes shifts without saying what they mean to the person, which the book treats as central. |
| Limits and critiques | Predicting a single person's shift from these signals is not established, and the signal was absent for depression in one adolescent sample. |
| Quoted from its sources | "work in different scientific fields is now suggesting the existence of generic early-warning signals that may indicate for a wide class of systems if a critical threshold is approaching" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Scheffer%20M%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Early-warning%20signals%20for%20critical%20transitions%22%20AND%20PUB_YEAR%3A2009&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "Our results support the hypothesis that mood may have alternative stable states separated by tipping points" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Critical%20slowing%20down%20as%20early%20warning%20for%20the%20onset%20and%20termination%20of%20depression%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "still needs empirical proof" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Critical%20slowing%20down%20in%20depression%20is%20a%20great%20idea%20that%20still%20needs%20empirical%20proof%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "for depression, EWS may manifest only several weeks prior to a shift" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A33050891%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "However, in highly stochastic (random) systems, alternative basins of attraction will be reached well before bifurcation points are reached." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_transition) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | emotion, future vision, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no research on tipping points in mood. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Scheffer%20M%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Early-warning%20signals%20for%20critical%20transitions%22%20AND%20PUB_YEAR%3A2009&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Critical%20slowing%20down%20as%20early%20warning%20for%20the%20onset%20and%20termination%20of%20depression%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Critical%20slowing%20down%20in%20depression%20is%20a%20great%20idea%20that%20still%20needs%20empirical%20proof%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A33050891%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_transition |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *critical slowing down* | slower recovery from small disturbances as a system approaches a tipping point |
| *autocorrelation* | how strongly each measurement resembles the one before it, which rises as recovery slows |
| *flickering* | a noisy system switching back and forth between states before a full shift, which can happen before slowing appears |

#### Ecological facilitation (positive interactions between species)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | community ecology |
| Originators | Mark Bertness and Ragan Callaway (1994); Bruno, Stachowicz and Bertness (2003); Stachowicz (2001 review) |
| Date | 1994 ('Positive interactions in communities', Trends in Ecology & Evolution); 2003 ('Inclusion of facilitation into ecological theory', Trends in Ecology & Evolution) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, the more-than-human |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Ecological facilitation is any interaction between species that benefits at least one and harms neither, including mutualisms and commensalisms. The mechanisms the source lists are refuge from physical stress (shade, moisture, shelter from waves), refuge from predators, refuge from competitors, better access to resources, and transport of pollen or seeds. Bertness and Callaway argued in 1994 that the evidence for such positive interactions had grown enough to warrant their formal inclusion in community ecology theory, which had been built mainly on studies of competition. |
| What is facilitated | A species' establishment, growth or survival at a site is facilitated, most often because a neighbor changes local conditions. The facilitator supplies conditions and not the beneficiary's own growth, and facilitation most often lets a species live where stress would otherwise exclude it. |
| Across scales | The listed mechanisms, reducing stress, predation and competition, adding resources, and carrying, correspond to things helpers do for people, but the source's claims are about species. Facilitation can also change a whole community, as when mesquite acts as a nurse plant and drives a shift from grassland to savanna and woodland. |
| The evidence | Field experiments in marine and land systems support facilitation, and the concept is now standard. The source also reports that studies suggest facilitation is rare compared with competition, and that a facilitation can turn into competition as the helped species grows. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: Most ecological facilitation works by making local conditions less harsh, a measured case of help that works through conditions rather than by acting on the one helped. Conflict: The same pair of species can move from facilitation to competition as the helped one grows, so help and rivalry can be stages of one relationship. |
