| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Species facilitate without intent and the benefit is measured in survival and growth, whereas the book's facilitation involves persons who choose to help. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Evidence for the importance of positive interactions - facilitations - in community organization and dynamics has accrued to the point where it warrants formal inclusion into community ecology theory, as it has been in evolutionary biology." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A21236818%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "However, as the seedlings grow into established plants, they are likely to compete with their former benefactors for resources." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_facilitation) "Studies suggest that facilitation events in nature are rare compared to competition events and thus, competition is a greater driver for ecological processes." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_facilitation) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | notes/an ecology is a bounded but permeable domain of multiple irreducible loci of participation, none sovereign.md; The vault uses 'ecology' for a field of standpoints in which none rules the others, from a conversation rather than from ecological science. Ecological facilitation shows that in real communities facilitation and competition co-occur and can turn into each other, which that definition does not address. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_facilitation; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A21236818%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1016/S0169-5347(02)00045-9 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *commensalism* | an interaction in which one species benefits and the other is unaffected |
| *mutualism* | an interaction in which both species benefit |
| *associational refuge* | protection a palatable species gets from growing near an unpalatable one |
| *foundation species* | a species whose structure creates the habitat for a whole community |

#### Ecological resilience and engineering resilience

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | ecology |
| Originators | C. S. Holling; later Brian Walker, Holling and others (four aspects of resilience) |
| Date | 1973 ('Resilience and stability of ecological systems', Annual Review of Ecology and Systematics 4: 1–23) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, a community or public |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Holling distinguished two meanings of resilience. Engineering resilience is the time a system takes to return to its equilibrium after a disturbance; ecological resilience is the amount of disturbance a system can absorb while keeping essentially the same function, structure, identity and feedbacks, a definition that assumes the system has more than one possible stable state, as a shallow lake can be clear or turbid. Walker, Holling and others later named four aspects of resilience: latitude, how far a system can be changed before it cannot recover; resistance, how hard it is to change; precariousness, how close it is to a threshold; and panarchy, how much it is influenced by other levels. |
| What is facilitated | Resilience describes a system's own capacity to absorb change and keep its identity; it is not supplied from outside, although outside pressures can reduce it by reducing diversity, altering the physical environment, or changing the disturbances a system is adapted to. |
| Across scales | Holling's definitions were extended from ecosystems to social-ecological systems, and later authors argue that applying the ecological concept directly to human systems leads to conceptual and normative difficulties, because people anticipate and plan. |
| The evidence | The distinction is foundational in ecology and is supported by documented regime shifts in lakes, rangelands and reefs. The Resilience Alliance reports research suggesting that restoring some systems requires returning to conditions that existed well before the point of collapse. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The distinction separates a return to the same state after a disturbance from a shift into a different state, two events that the single word resilience blurs. Protection and defense: Resistance to change is one of the four aspects of resilience, so resistance is described as part of a system's persistence rather than as a fault. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Ecological resilience values the persistence of a system's identity, whereas the book concerns a change a person wants, which is a shift of regime rather than a return. |
| Limits and critiques | Resilience describes the persistence of whatever state a system is in, including states nobody wants, such as a turbid lake. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Resilience is the capacity of a social-ecological system to absorb or withstand perturbations and other stressors such that the system remains within the same regime, essentially maintaining its structure and functions." (https://www.resalliance.org/resilience) "Research suggests that to restore some systems to their previous state requires a return to conditions that existed well before the point of collapse" (https://www.resalliance.org/resilience) "and it presumes the existence of multiple stable states or regimes" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_resilience) "A direct application of an ecological resilience concept to SES will lead to conceptual and normative difficulties" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6106646/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/a durable perspective is a recognition whose value persists across contexts.md`; The note uses 'cross-contextual resilience', the author's coinage, as the mark of a durable perspective. It does not distinguish Holling's two meanings, and in Holling's sense an unwanted state can be resilient too. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_resilience; https://www.resalliance.org/resilience; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6106646/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *engineering resilience* | the speed of return to equilibrium after a disturbance |
| *ecological resilience* | the size of disturbance a system can absorb without shifting into a different regime |
| *regime* | one of the alternative states a system can persist in, maintained by its own set of processes |
| *latitude* | the maximum change a system can undergo before losing its ability to recover |

#### Ecosystem engineers

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | ecology |
| Originators | Clive Jones, John Lawton and Moshe Shachak |
| Date | 1994 ('Organisms as ecosystem engineers', Oikos 69: 373–386) |
| Scale | an ecosystem |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. An engineer changes the habitat that other species then use on their own terms, and it directs no other organism. |
| What it holds | An ecosystem engineer is a species that creates, significantly modifies, maintains or destroys a habitat, and so changes the resources available to other species. Jones, Lawton and Shachak distinguished allogenic engineers, which change materials from one form to another as beavers do with dams, from autogenic engineers, which create habitat with their own bodies as trees, Sphagnum moss and corals do. Engineers are defined by modifying resources, not by their place in the food web, which separates them from keystone species. |
| What is facilitated | Other species' lives are made possible by habitat the engineer creates or changes. The engineer does not supply those species or their activities, only the altered place. |
| Across scales | One participant shaping the space in which others then act freely has a human counterpart in the design of rooms and structures for groups, but the source's claims are ecological. Engineers can also destroy habitat. |
| The evidence | Cases such as beaver ponds and coral reefs show large effects on the number of species present. The source notes that because every organism affects its environment, some have proposed limiting the term to species whose effects are very strong. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: An engineer changes the place in which others act rather than acting on them, an ecological description of facilitation through setting. Who decides the content: The beaver decides the pond, and the species that live in it decide how they use it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Engineers modify habitat for their own benefit, and the benefit to others is a side effect rather than an aim. |
| Limits and critiques | Because all organisms modify their environments, the concept risks covering everything unless it is limited to strong effects. |
| Quoted from its sources | "they are identified as engineers because of their ability to modify resources, not because of their trophic effect" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem_engineer) "Since all organisms impact the environment they live in one way or another" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem_engineer) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no ecology of habitat modification. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem_engineer |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *allogenic engineer* | a species that changes living or nonliving materials, such as a beaver building a dam |
| *autogenic engineer* | a species whose own body forms habitat, such as a tree or a coral |

#### Facilitated diffusion (passive transport through membrane proteins)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | cell biology (membrane transport) |
| Originators | Membrane physiology; the sources read name no originator for the general concept, and credit the oxygen case to Wittenberg and Scholander |
| Date | not stated in the sources read |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Facilitated diffusion is the movement of molecules or ions across a cell membrane through specific proteins, channels or carriers, where the membrane itself would block them. It is passive: the molecules move down their concentration gradient, from where they are more concentrated to where they are less, and the transport step uses no chemical energy from ATP. It differs from free diffusion in depending on binding to the protein, in being saturable, so that the rate levels off as the concentration difference grows, and in being specific, since each protein passes only certain molecules. |
| What is facilitated | The molecule's own movement down its gradient is facilitated, across a barrier it could not cross alone. The protein supplies neither the energy nor the direction; the gradient supplies both. |
| Across scales | The distinction between opening a route and pushing along it is exact at membranes, where active transport uses energy to move molecules against a gradient. Nothing in the source extends the distinction beyond membranes. |
| The evidence | This is established membrane physiology with well-studied examples such as the glucose transporters. The same article uses the name for a different process, the search of proteins along DNA, which is not the membrane mechanism described here. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: A carrier opens a route but the gradient decides which way things move, a case in which the helper sets neither direction nor destination. The container that holds people while they change: Specificity means the same opening passes some things and not others, so a route is always specific to what it admits. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | A membrane protein has no stake in what passes, whereas a human facilitator has preferences that can bias what they admit. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Being passive, facilitated transport does not directly require chemical energy" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitated_diffusion) "The rate of facilitated diffusion is saturable with respect to the concentration difference between the two phases" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitated_diffusion) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no membrane biology. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitated_diffusion |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *channel protein* | a membrane protein forming a gated pore through which particular ions or small molecules pass |
| *carrier protein* | a membrane protein that binds a molecule and changes shape to move it across |
| *concentration gradient* | a difference in concentration that drives diffusion from high to low |
| *passive transport* | transport that uses no chemical energy in the transport step |

#### Facilitation cascade

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | marine and community ecology |
| Originators | Altieri, Silliman and Bertness |
| Date | 2007 ('Hierarchical organization via a facilitation cascade in intertidal cordgrass bed communities', The American Naturalist) |
| Scale | an ecosystem |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | A facilitation cascade is a chain in which one species benefits a second that in turn benefits a third. Altieri, Silliman and Bertness coined the term for New England cobble beaches, where cordgrass establishes on its own, stabilizes the ground and reduces stress for mussels, and the mussels provide hard surfaces and damp crevices for a community of invertebrates and algae. The source contrasts cascades with the facilitation model of succession: in a cascade species accumulate, whereas in succession the early facilitators are replaced. |
| What is facilitated | The establishment of a whole community is facilitated by a sequence of species, each creating conditions for the next. No single species supplies the community; each supplies the conditions for the next step. |
| Across scales | Cascades show facilitation compounding across levels of a community, and they are the ecological case in which facilitators persist rather than being replaced. The sources read make no claim about human groups. |
| The evidence | Field experiments, including ones that replace the facilitating species with artificial mimics, support cascades in many marine and land systems. The source states that their effects can rival or exceed those of trophic cascades. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Change at the scale of a team or organization: A chain in which each helped member then helps others is a measured mechanism by which help spreads through a group without a central director. What care costs the one who gives it: In a cascade the facilitators persist alongside those they help, in contrast to the nurse plant that is displaced. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The chain is made of species' effects on habitat, not of anyone's intention to help. |
| Quoted from its sources | "whereas in the succession, early species that play a facilitative role are, over time, replaced by later-stage species" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitation_cascade) "The effect size of facilitation cascades can rival or exceed that of trophic cascades" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitation_cascade) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | nested levels; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology, group process |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no ecology of facilitation. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facilitation_cascade |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *primary facilitator* | the first species in the chain, often a foundation species that builds habitat |
| *secondary facilitator* | a species helped by the first that in turn helps others |
| *habitat cascade* | a cascade in which each step is the creation of habitat |

#### Homeostasis

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | physiology |
| Originators | Claude Bernard (the internal environment, 1849); Walter Bradford Cannon (the term, 1926) |
| Date | 1849; 1926 |
| Scale | one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Homeostasis is the process by which organisms keep internal conditions such as temperature, blood glucose, blood pressure and ion concentrations within ranges despite changes inside and outside the body. It commonly works by negative feedback: sensors detect a change, control mechanisms coordinate a response, and effectors act to counter the deviation. The set points themselves are reset, as body temperature follows a daily rhythm, so homeostatic control does not mean a value is fixed. |
| What is facilitated | The organism's own continued functioning is maintained by its internal loops. No outside agent supplies the regulation; outside conditions supply the disturbances it answers. |
| Across scales | Cannon's concept was adopted in cybernetics, and the source notes that in its exact sense homeostasis is an almost exclusively biological term. Regulation of a measured variable is exact in physiology, and its use for families or groups is by analogy. |
| The evidence | Homeostasis is foundational physiology with many measured mechanisms, from insulin and glucagon to the stretch receptors that sense blood pressure. Its adequacy as a complete account of regulation is disputed by the allostasis literature (see allostasis). |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Homeostatic loops counter deviations automatically, a physiological description of a system resisting change in order to keep functioning. Knowing that something happened: Because set points are themselves reset, a changed baseline and a passing deviation are different events, and telling them apart requires following the set point over time. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Homeostasis describes a return to a range, whereas the book concerns a change that does not return to the former state. |
| Quoted from its sources | "If an entity is homeostatically controlled it does not imply that its value is necessarily absolutely steady in health." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeostasis) "our body has just found that homeostasis. It's like, okay, I'm back to balance." (archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/heinz von foerster.md`; Von Foerster's note records his 'Postulate of Cognitive Homeostasis', and the vault's reading record on Joe Hudson shows him describing the end of a fully felt emotion as the body finding homeostasis. The vault holds no physiology of homeostasis; this entry supplies it. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeostasis |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *milieu intérieur* | Bernard's "internal environment" in which the body's cells live |
| *negative feedback* | a response that counters a deviation from a set point |
| *set point* | the value a regulated variable is held near, which can itself change |
| *effector* | the organ or cell that acts to correct a deviation |

#### Hormesis

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | toxicology and physiology |
| Originators | Hugo Schulz (observations on yeast, 1888; with Rudolph Arndt, the Arndt–Schulz rule); Chester M. Southam and J. Ehrlich (the term, 1943); Edward Calabrese (later evaluation) |
| Date | 1888; 1943 |
| Scale | one organism, molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Hormesis is a two-phase dose response in which low doses of a stressor have a beneficial effect and high doses inhibit function or are toxic. Hugo Schulz observed in 1888 that small doses of poisons stimulated the growth of yeast, and Southam and Ehrlich introduced the term in 1943. Calabrese and Baldwin's search of the published literature found U-shaped dose responses meeting their criteria in about one percent of articles (195 of 20,285). |
| What is facilitated | The organism's own adaptive response to a small challenge is facilitated by the challenge itself. The stressor does not supply the adaptation, and above a certain dose it overwhelms it. |
| Across scales | The idea that a small dose of difficulty strengthens and a large dose harms appears in accounts of training and of psychological growth, but this source's evidence is toxicological and is contested even there. |
| The evidence | The source states that the hormesis model is vigorously debated in toxicology and that its mechanisms are not well understood. National and international radiation-protection bodies do not accept radiation hormesis as shown, and there are concerns that the concept has been used by lobbyists to weaken environmental regulation. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The same agent helps or harms depending on dose, so an account of a helpful difficulty that leaves out its dose leaves out what makes it harmful. Timing and pacing: If a challenge helps only within a range, pacing is a matter of setting the dose. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Hormesis concerns chemical and physical stressors measured by dose, whereas the book's difficulties are experiences with meaning, whose dose cannot be measured in the same way. |
| Limits and critiques | Using hormesis to argue that exposure to a harmful agent is safe or good has been criticized as a lobbying strategy, and regulators reject the radiation case. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In the context of toxicology, the hormesis model of dose response is vigorously debated." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormesis) "Hormesis suggests dangerous substances have benefits." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormesis) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds nothing on dose-response relationships. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormesis |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *hormetic zone* | the range of low doses in which a stressor's effect is favorable |
| *linear no-threshold model* | the regulatory assumption that harm rises in proportion to dose with no safe level, which hormesis disputes for some agents |

#### Induced fit (the specificity of enzymes)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | biochemistry |
| Originators | Daniel Koshland (induced fit, 1958), revising Emil Fischer's lock-and-key model (1894) |
| Date | 1958 (Koshland, 'Application of a Theory of Enzyme Specificity to Protein Synthesis', Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences) |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Enzymes are proteins that act as catalysts in living cells, and each acts on a narrow range of substrates, the molecules it converts. Emil Fischer proposed in 1894 that enzyme and substrate have rigid complementary shapes that fit exactly into one another, which is known as the lock and key model. Daniel Koshland proposed in 1958 that the enzyme is flexible: an initially weak binding induces changes in the enzyme's shape that strengthen the binding and move the active site into position to act, so the fit is made during the encounter rather than fixed beforehand. |
| What is facilitated | The substrate's conversion into product is facilitated by an enzyme that adjusts its own shape to the substrate. The enzyme does not supply the substrate's chemistry, and its specificity decides which molecules it will help at all. |
| Across scales | The source's claim is molecular: it describes a helper whose form adjusts during the encounter rather than a fixed template. The biochemistry gives no evidence for the same pattern between people. |
| The evidence | Induced fit is the standard textbook model, and structures show large shape changes on binding in enzymes such as hexokinase. The source adds two limits: induced fit alone does not explain why the energy barrier is lower, and its role in the accuracy of DNA copying has been questioned. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: Induced fit is a case in which the helper changes shape in response to what it meets, rather than requiring the other to fit a fixed form. Who decides the content: Specificity means an enzyme helps only substrates that can bind it, so what can be helped is limited by the structures of both parties. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | An enzyme's adjustment is fixed by physics and has no aim, whereas a facilitator's attunement involves choices that the biochemistry does not describe. |
| Limits and critiques | The model explains specificity and binding better than it explains catalysis: the source states that induced fit does not say why the barrier is lower. |
| Quoted from its sources | "This model proposes that the initial interaction between enzyme and substrate is relatively weak" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme_catalysis) "since enzymes are rather flexible structures, the active site is continuously reshaped by interactions with the substrate as the substrate interacts with the enzyme." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme) "The induced fit only suggests that the barrier is lower in the closed form of the enzyme but does not tell us what the reason for the barrier reduction is." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme_catalysis) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no biochemistry of enzymes. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme_catalysis |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *active site* | the region of the enzyme where the substrate binds and the reaction occurs |
| *lock and key model* | Fischer's 1894 proposal that enzyme and substrate have fixed complementary shapes |
| *induced fit* | Koshland's proposal that binding reshapes the enzyme so that it fits the substrate |
| *differential binding* | an enzyme binding the transition state more tightly than the starting substrate, which lowers the activation energy |

#### Long-term potentiation (LTP)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | neuroscience (synaptic plasticity) |
| Originators | Terje Lømo (first observation, 1966, in Per Andersen's laboratory in Oslo); Timothy Bliss and Terje Lømo (first characterization, 1973); Donald Hebb (the 1949 postulate LTP is often taken to realize) |
| Date | 1966; 1973 |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Long-term potentiation is a lasting strengthening of synapses after a pattern of strong or high-frequency activity, first seen by Terje Lømo in the rabbit hippocampus in 1966. In its common NMDA-receptor-dependent form it has four properties: it stays at the synapses that were stimulated (input specificity); a weakly stimulated pathway is strengthened if it is active at the same time as a strongly stimulated one (associativity); several weak inputs together can induce it when one alone cannot (cooperativity); and it lasts from minutes to many months (persistence). |
| What is facilitated | Later transmission across a synapse is made easier by that synapse's own recent coincident activity. The facilitation is local: only synapses that took part are changed, and a weak input needs a strong one active at the same time. |
| Across scales | Associativity and cooperativity are the synaptic form of weak contributions reaching a threshold together, a pattern that also appears in quorum sensing among bacteria; the source makes no claim beyond neurons. |
| The evidence | LTP is among the most studied phenomena in neuroscience, and experiments such as Jonathan Whitlock and colleagues' 2006 study give strong evidence for its role in some behavioral memory. The source states that its physical and biological mechanism is still not understood. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: Input specificity means only synapses that were active are strengthened, a cellular case of change reaching only what took part. The container that holds people while they change: Associativity means a weak signal is strengthened when it coincides with a strong one, so what accompanies a small change in time affects whether it lasts. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | LTP is a change in the strength of connections between cells and says nothing about meaning or recognition in a person. |
| Limits and critiques | The link from LTP to behavioral learning is still argued, and the source records the mechanism as not understood. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Once induced, LTP at one synapse does not spread to other synapses" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_potentiation) "When one pathway into a synapse is stimulated weakly, it produces insufficient postsynaptic depolarization to induce LTP." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_potentiation) "The physical and biological mechanism of LTP is still not understood, but some successful models have been developed" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_potentiation) "cells that fire together wire together" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_potentiation) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | combining elements; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | learning skill, memory past |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no synaptic plasticity research; its plasticity note concerns reopened critical periods (see critical-period-reopening). |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_potentiation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebbian_theory |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *input specificity* | potentiation stays at the stimulated synapses and does not spread to others |
| *associativity* | a weak input is strengthened when it coincides with a strong input to the same cell |
| *cooperativity* | many weak inputs together reach the threshold that one alone cannot |
| *Hebbian* | requiring activity in the sending and receiving cells at once, summarized as "cells that fire together wire together" |

#### Michaelis–Menten kinetics: the saturation of an enzyme

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | biochemistry (enzyme kinetics) |
| Originators | Leonor Michaelis and Maud Menten (1913), expanding Victor Henri's equation (1902); George Edward Briggs and J. B. S. Haldane's later analysis |
| Date | 1913 |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Michaelis and Menten modeled an enzyme binding its substrate to form a complex that releases the product and regenerates the free enzyme. At low substrate concentration the rate of the reaction rises in proportion to the concentration; at high concentration it levels off at a limiting rate, because nearly every enzyme molecule is occupied. The Michaelis constant is the substrate concentration at which the rate is half the limiting rate, and it differs between enzymes by large factors: four forms of mammalian hexokinase are half-saturated at concentrations spanning a 2500-fold range. |
| What is facilitated | The conversion of substrate into product is facilitated, and the enzyme is regenerated after each cycle. The enzyme supplies a limited number of places where the reaction can happen; it does not supply more capacity when demand rises. |
| Across scales | Saturation holds for any facilitator with a fixed number of sites that are each occupied for a time, including the membrane carriers of facilitated diffusion. Whether a human helper's capacity saturates in the same way is not tested by this source. |
| The evidence | The model is the basic framework of enzyme kinetics and fits many single-substrate reactions. The source records that the equation began with Henri, who did not apply it, and that Michaelis and Menten's use of initial rates is what made it productive. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | What care costs the one who gives it: A facilitator with a finite number of places to act reaches a maximum rate, after which more demand produces no more help. Timing and pacing: Below saturation, help grows with demand; near saturation, the pace is set by the facilitator's own turnover rate. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | An enzyme is not worn down by working at its limiting rate, whereas a human helper working at capacity can be, which the model does not describe. |
| Quoted from its sources | "represents the limiting rate approached by the system at saturating substrate concentration for a given enzyme concentration" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michaelis%E2%80%93Menten_kinetics) "Although Henri derived the equation he made no attempt to apply it." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michaelis%E2%80%93Menten_kinetics) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no enzyme kinetics. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michaelis%E2%80%93Menten_kinetics |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *limiting rate (Vmax)* | the rate approached when the enzyme is saturated with substrate |
| *Michaelis constant (Km)* | the substrate concentration at which the reaction runs at half its limiting rate |
| *saturation* | the condition in which nearly all enzyme molecules are bound, so adding substrate no longer raises the rate |

#### Molecular chaperones and Anfinsen's thermodynamic hypothesis

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | molecular biology |
| Originators | Christian Anfinsen (building on Lisa Steiner's earlier studies of ribonuclease); Ron Laskey (the term 'molecular chaperone', 1978); R. John Ellis (extended the term, 1987) |
| Date | 1957 to 1973 (Anfinsen's ribonuclease papers; 'Principles that govern the folding of protein chains', Science, 1973); 1978 and 1987 (chaperones) |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Anfinsen's thermodynamic hypothesis holds that, at least for a small globular protein in its normal environment, the protein's folded shape is determined by its sequence of amino acids alone. Molecular chaperones are proteins that help other proteins fold, mainly by binding unfolded or partly folded chains and keeping them from clumping with other molecules, and the source states that chaperones do not appear to affect the protein's final state; Hsp70 chaperones, for example, are thought to surround an unfolded chain and to lose their affinity for it once it has folded. The exceptions are measured: a minority of proteins cannot fold without chaperones, a few highly specific 'steric chaperones' supply structural information the protein cannot reach alone, and prions are misfolded stable forms that make normal proteins refold into the same form. |
| What is facilitated | The protein's own folding into the shape its sequence specifies is facilitated. Most chaperones supply protection from clumping and time to fold; they do not supply the shape. |
| Across scales | This is the clearest molecular case of a helper that protects a process without dictating its result, the claim Maturana and Varela make for living systems generally. The same source gives the molecular counter-cases: steric chaperones that do supply form, and prions that impose their own form on others. |
| The evidence | The hypothesis rests on Anfinsen's research on the folding of ribonuclease A and is the standard account for small proteins. The source lists measured challenges: proteins that need chaperones, steric chaperones, prions and other amyloid diseases, and proteins that switch between folds. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: Most chaperones protect a chain while it reaches the shape its own sequence specifies, a measured case of help that does not author the result. Harm and its conditions: Prions show the opposite case at the same scale, a form that converts others into its own shape, and it causes disease. The container that holds people while they change: A holdase keeps a chain from harmful contact with other molecules during a vulnerable period and then lets go, which is a molecular description of protection during a vulnerable period. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | A protein's final shape is fixed by physics, whereas the book does not assume that a person has one predetermined final form to reach. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the chaperones do not appear to affect the final state of the protein" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfinsen's_dogma) "However, at least some chaperones are required for the proper folding of their subject proteins." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfinsen's_dogma) "Some highly specific 'steric chaperones' convey unique structural information onto proteins, which cannot be folded spontaneously." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaperone_(protein)) "at which time the Hsp70s lose affinity for the molecule and diffuse away" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaperone_(protein)) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/an interaction can trigger a change in a living system but cannot specify it.md`; The vault holds Maturana and Varela's claim, read at the primary text, that an interaction triggers but cannot specify a living system's change. It holds no molecular evidence for or against that claim; this entry supplies one supporting case (most chaperones) and two measured exceptions (steric chaperones and prions). |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfinsen's_dogma; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaperone_(protein) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *molecular chaperone* | a protein that helps another protein fold or assemble without becoming part of the final structure |
| *holdase* | a chaperone that binds folding intermediates to keep them from clumping |
| *foldase* | a chaperone system that uses chemical energy to support folding |
| *steric chaperone* | a rare chaperone that supplies structural information the protein cannot fold to on its own |
| *prion* | a misfolded, stable protein form that causes normal proteins to refold into the same form |

#### Multiscale competency and TAME (Technological Approach to Mind Everywhere)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | developmental and synthetic biology |
| Originators | Michael Levin (Tufts University) |
| Date | 2022 (Levin, 'Technological Approach to Mind Everywhere', Frontiers in Systems Neuroscience) |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Levin argues that bodies are built of nested units, cells, tissues, organs and organisms, each with some competence to pursue goals, and that developmental bioelectricity, signaling through ion channels and gap junctions, joins cells into larger agents that pursue anatomical goals. His examples include tadpoles made with their facial organs in scrambled positions that still become normal frogs, because the tissues keep moving until the face matches a stored target shape. He proposes an 'axis of persuadability' on which a system is placed by the kind of intervention that controls it most efficiently, from rearranging a clock's parts, through rewriting a regulating system's set point, to training an animal, to reasoning with a person. |
| What is facilitated | A tissue's own movement toward its target shape is facilitated, on Levin's account, by changing the goal it pursues rather than by directing each cell. The intervention supplies a new set point and leaves the route to the system. |
| Across scales | Levin's framework is itself a claim about what holds across scales: that goal-directed problem solving appears from cells to persons, differing in degree. Critics hold that talk of goals helps at some levels and misleads at others, so the cross-scale claim is contested. |
| The evidence | The regeneration and bioelectric experiments are published laboratory work, but the framework's claims about cognition and agency at every scale are a perspective. P. Bourrat and A. B. Barron (BioScience, 2026), examining the recent move to treat cells, tissues and organisms as agents with agendas, conclude that goal-directed explanations can help pose hypotheses but risk omitting critical details and misdirecting conclusions; their abstract does not name Levin. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The axis of persuadability runs from controlling every part to persuading a whole, and places the most efficient intervention at the level where the system's own competence does the rest. Remaining the author of one's own change: On Levin's account, rewriting a goal while leaving the route to the system works better than micromanagement, but it still sets the goal from outside. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Levin's program aims at prediction and control of living tissue for medicine and engineering, whereas the book declines to set a reader's goal from outside. |
| Limits and critiques | Levin argues that there is no such thing as anthropomorphizing, because humans have no unique property that could be wrongly attributed to other agents; this runs against the traditional caution about goal-directed explanation that Bourrat and Barron describe. |
| Quoted from its sources | "This architecture of multi-scale competency of biological systems has important implications for plasticity of bodies and minds, greatly potentiating evolvability." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Technological%20Approach%20to%20Mind%20Everywhere%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "For class B, one has to know how to read-write the setpoint information, but does not need to know anything about how the system will implement those goals." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC8988303/fullTextXML) "However, they risk omitting critical details and misdirecting conclusions." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A42620954%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | nested levels; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, identity self |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no research on bioelectricity or on cognition in cells. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Technological%20Approach%20to%20Mind%20Everywhere%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC8988303/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A42620954%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Levin_(biologist) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *multi-scale competency* | Levin's claim that each level of a living body can solve problems toward its own goals |
| *target morphology* | the anatomical shape a developing or regenerating tissue works toward and stops at |
| *axis of persuadability* | a scale of how a system is best controlled, from micromanaging its parts to persuading it by argument |
| *setpoint* | the goal state encoded in a regulating system, which on Levin's account can be rewritten without specifying how it is reached |

#### Neural facilitation (paired-pulse facilitation)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | neuroscience (synaptic physiology) |
| Originators | J. del Castillo and B. Katz (1954); B. Katz and R. Miledi (the residual calcium hypothesis, 1968) |
| Date | 1954; 1968 (Katz and Miledi, 'The role of calcium in neuromuscular facilitation', Journal of Physiology) |
| Scale | molecules and cells, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | When a nerve impulse closely follows an earlier one, the response it evokes at the synapse is larger than the first; this is paired-pulse facilitation, a form of short-term synaptic plasticity. The mechanism is presynaptic: calcium left in the nerve terminal from the first impulse adds to the calcium entering with the second, so more neurotransmitter is released. Facilitation is greatest when the impulses are closest together and lasts tens of milliseconds, and synapses whose first release is unlikely tend to facilitate, while synapses whose first release is likely tend instead to depress. |
| What is facilitated | The synapse's own transmission of the next signal is facilitated by the calcium its recent activity left behind. Nothing outside the synapse supplies the effect; the prior impulse does. |
| Across scales | The source's claim is about calcium in nerve terminals. It shows that the same repeated input can strengthen or weaken transmission depending on the synapse's starting state, which limits any general rule that repetition facilitates. |
| The evidence | Experiments since 1954, at the neuromuscular junction and at central synapses, support the residual calcium account, and removing residual calcium with chelators reduces facilitation. The source notes that basic models treat facilitation and depression as additive, but that depression acts earlier and shapes how facilitation is expressed. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The effect exists only if the second event comes soon after the first, so spacing decides whether repetition adds up or fades. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Whether repetition strengthens or weakens a response depends on the synapse's own starting release probability and not on the input alone. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Paired-pulse facilitation lasts milliseconds and is not learning in the ordinary sense, whereas the book concerns lasting change in a person. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The mechanisms underlying neural facilitation are exclusively pre-synaptic" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_facilitation) "In general, however, synapses with low initial probability of vesicle release are more likely to exhibit facilitation, and synapses with high probability of initial vesicle release are more likely to exhibit depression." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_facilitation) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, learning skill |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no synaptic physiology; the vault's mentions of 'Katz' refer to a different person. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_facilitation |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *residual calcium* | calcium remaining in the nerve terminal after an impulse, which raises release for the next one |
| *short-term depression* | the opposite effect, a smaller response to repeated impulses as the pool of ready vesicles runs down |
| *release probability* | how likely a synapse is to release transmitter when an impulse arrives |

#### Niche construction and ecological inheritance

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | evolutionary biology |
| Originators | Richard Lewontin (articles of the 1970s and 1980s); John Odling-Smee (the term, 1988); Kevin Laland and colleagues |
| Date | 1988 (the term) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, one organism, a society's customs |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Niche construction is the process by which organisms change their own or other species' local environments, by building nests and burrows, creating shade, or altering nutrient cycles. Its proponents argue that it is an evolutionary process alongside natural selection, because organisms change the selection pressures acting on themselves and their descendants. Descendants therefore inherit two legacies, genes and a modified environment, which the proponents call ecological inheritance. |
| What is facilitated | The fit between organisms, their descendants, and their surroundings is shaped partly by the organisms' own changes to those surroundings. No outside agent supplies the fit; the organism takes part in making the conditions it is then selected by. |
| Across scales | Proponents apply the concept from earthworms to human agriculture and culture, and its use to explain the origins of agriculture is itself disputed in archaeology. The theory states for evolution a pattern of beings that partly make the setting that then shapes them. |
| The evidence | That organisms modify their environments is not disputed; whether niche construction is a distinct evolutionary process is. A joint paper by critics and an advocate records that most evolutionary biologists define evolutionary processes as those that change gene frequencies and do not see niche construction as one, while advocates use a broader definition. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: Niche construction describes beings that shape the conditions that then shape them, a biological case of partial authorship of one's own formation. The future: Ecological inheritance means a changed environment is passed on, so the effects of present changes reach later generations. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Niche construction is a claim about populations over generations, not about one person's deliberate change. |
| Quoted from its sources | "This theory emphasizes that organisms inherit two legacies from their ancestors: genes and a modified environment." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niche_construction) "Niche construction theory (NCT) remains controversial, particularly amongst orthodox evolutionary biologists." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niche_construction) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology, memory past |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no evolutionary theory of this kind. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niche_construction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *niche construction* | organisms changing the environment in ways that affect selection on themselves or others |
| *ecological inheritance* | the modified environment, with its selection pressures, passed on to descendants |

#### Nucleation, and nucleation at surfaces

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | physical chemistry (phase transitions) |
| Originators | Classical nucleation theory in physical chemistry; the sources read name no single originator |
| Date | not stated in the sources read |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Nucleation is the first step in forming a new phase, such as ice in water or crystals in a solution, and it starts only in a system that is already metastable, for example water cooled below its freezing point or a solution holding more solute than it can keep dissolved. Forming the first small nucleus costs energy for its surface, which creates a barrier, so a metastable liquid can stay liquid for minutes, hours, or weeks. A surface or impurity lowers that barrier, and nucleation at surfaces is much more common than nucleation away from them: purified water droplets freeze only below about −35 °C, while water with impurities may freeze at −5 °C or warmer. |
| What is facilitated | The system's own change into a more stable phase is facilitated by a site where the change can begin. The site does not supply the driving force, which exists only because the system is already metastable; in a system that is not metastable, no site produces the change. |
| Across scales | The pattern of a small site allowing a change that a system is already poised to make is exact for phase changes. The sources read do not test it in cells, persons, or groups. |
| The evidence | Measurements of freezing in droplets show the predicted random timing of nucleation and the dominance of nucleation at impurities. The source notes that classical nucleation theory treats a nucleus of a few molecules as if it were a large droplet, an approximation that is not always justified. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: If recognition behaved like nucleation, a text or a witness could only start a change the reader is already poised to make, and could not produce one in a reader who is not. Timing and pacing: Nucleation is random in time even under fixed conditions, so the moment a change begins cannot be scheduled even when its conditions are present. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Nucleation concerns matter with no history of meaning, and the vault's image of recognition as crystal nucleation is a figure, not a finding. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Heterogeneous nucleation, nucleation with the nucleus at a surface, is much more common than homogeneous nucleation." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleation) "These impurities may be too small to be seen by the naked eye, but they can still control the rate of nucleation." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleation) "Nucleation of the crystal is then prevented by a substantial barrier." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleation) "recognition behaves like crystal nucleation, unmaking the form it replaces" (ops/queue/commonplace.md) |
| Mental model | other. physical chemistry |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/queue/commonplace.md`; The commonplace list holds the image 'recognition behaves like crystal nucleation, unmaking the form it replaces', drawn from the case of a drug crystal form that could no longer be made once a more stable form appeared, and the grief note lists nucleation, with the witness as the nucleation site, as an unresearched loan. This entry supplies the physics, including the condition that nucleation needs a system that is already metastable. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_nucleation_theory |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *metastable* | a state that persists although another state is more stable, because a barrier prevents the change |
| *heterogeneous nucleation* | nucleation that begins at a surface or impurity |
| *homogeneous nucleation* | nucleation that begins away from any surface |
| *supercooling* | cooling a liquid below its freezing point without it freezing |

#### Permissive and instructive causality in mental health

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | biological psychiatry |
| Originators | Igor Branchi |
| Date | 2026 (Psychological Medicine) |
| Scale | one organism, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Branchi proposes two kinds of cause for mental states: permissive causes, which change how likely an outcome is, and instructive causes, which shape its specific features. Treating the person as a complex adaptive system, he argues that biological substrates from genes to brain areas act only permissively on mental states, while contextual factors such as life challenges and the person's own appraisal can also act instructively. Treatments that raise plasticity, including antidepressants and psychedelics, are permissive on this account, so their effect depends on the context that steers the change; he cites evidence that the efficacy of selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors depends partly on their pairing with favorable environmental conditions and that antidepressants combined with psychotherapy work better than the drugs alone. |
| What is facilitated | A transition between mental states is facilitated by raised plasticity, and its direction is set by the person's context and appraisal. The drug or biological change does not supply the direction. |
| Across scales | The framework applies the developmental distinction between permissive and instructive signals to a whole person in context. It departs from Maturana and Varela's denial of instructive interaction, since Branchi holds that factors outside a person can instruct the person's overall outcome. |
| The evidence | The paper is a theoretical framework supported by citations to clinical and animal studies, not a new trial. Branchi limits the claim that internal biological causes are only permissive to complex outcomes such as mental states, and excludes simple direct cases such as disorders caused by a single gene. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: Because plasticity is not beneficial by definition, a period of raised plasticity spent in harmful conditions can steer change toward harm. The container that holds people while they change: On this framework the conditions around a person during a period of raised plasticity decide the direction of change, which makes those conditions the operative part of a method. Remaining the author of one's own change: Branchi counts the person's own appraisal among the instructive causes, so the person's reading of their situation is one of the forces that steers the change. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The framework is written for clinical psychiatry and the design of treatments, whereas the book addresses a reader with no substance and no clinician. |
| Limits and critiques | Branchi warns that treatments that raise plasticity do not by themselves have a predictable therapeutic effect. |
| Quoted from its sources | "biological substrates - from genes to brain areas - operate through permissive causality, shaping a space of possibilities rather than instructing a specific outcome" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A42563280%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "the beneficial outcomes of plasticity-enhancing treatments can be achieved only by integrating contextual factors into the therapeutic approach" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC13468944/fullTextXML) "as plasticity is not inherently beneficial by definition" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC13468944/fullTextXML) "for better and for worse" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC13468944/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences. biological psychiatry drawing on complex-systems theory |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, emotion, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the plasticity window reorganizes only what attention is on.md`; The note holds, from a secondary synthesis, that an opened period of plasticity reorganizes whatever the person is attending to. Branchi gives that claim a published theoretical form, and adds that the same openness raises sensitivity to harmful conditions. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A42563280%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC13468944/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *permissive causality* | a factor that changes the likelihood of an outcome without shaping its specific features |
| *instructive causality* | a factor that shapes the specific features of an outcome |
| *differential susceptibility* | the proposal Branchi cites that higher plasticity raises sensitivity to the environment "for better and for worse" |

#### Permissive versus instructive influences in development

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | developmental biology and developmental neuroscience |
| Originators | Hans Spemann and Hilde Mangold (the organizer, an instructive inducer, 1924); A. Roy, S. D. Van Hooser and colleagues (experience as a permissive influence in visual cortex, 2018); Brett Calcott (philosophical analysis, 2017) |
| Date | 1924; 2018 (Roy and colleagues, Neural Development) |
| Scale | one organism, molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Developmental biologists distinguish an instructive influence, which shapes what a tissue becomes, from a permissive one, which is needed for development to proceed but does not shape its specific outcome. Hilde Mangold's 1921 to 1922 transplant of the upper lip of the blastopore from one newt embryo into another, published with Hans Spemann in 1924, produced a second embryo whose neural plate was made almost entirely of host cells, showing that signals from one cell population can change what other cells become. Roy, Van Hooser and colleagues reviewed experiments in ferrets showing that visual experience is required for direction-selective neurons to develop but does not set their tuning: a neuron's preferred direction can be predicted from small biases present before the eyes open, and a few hours of unpatterned activation of the cortex is enough for direction selectivity to appear. |
| What is facilitated | In the permissive case, a tissue's own development toward a form already implicit in it is facilitated, and the input supplies activity rather than information. In the instructive case, the signal supplies part of the form. |
| Across scales | The distinction applies at the scale of cells and tissues, and Igor Branchi has applied it in psychiatry (see permissive-instructive-causality-psychiatry). The organizer shows that the permissive pattern is not universal even within an embryo, since some signals do specify fate. |
| The evidence | Both results are experimental: the organizer transplant is a founding experiment of developmental biology, for which Spemann received the Nobel Prize in 1935, and the ferret results come from several studies reviewed in 2018. Brett Calcott analyzes the distinction as a matter of causal specificity in Biology and Philosophy (2017); that article's text could not be read here. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The ferret results give a measured case in which experience is necessary for a change whose form was already set by the system's own prior structure. Who decides the content: The organizer is a measured counter-case, in which a signal from outside the responding cells decides what they become. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Embryos and cortices have no view of their own development, whereas the book's reader can reflect on influences and take them up or refuse them. |
| Limits and critiques | Whether an influence is permissive or instructive is settled experimentally case by case, and one system can be subject to both kinds. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the introduction of the organizer provided evidence that the fate of cells can be influenced by factors from other cell populations" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spemann%E2%80%93Mangold_organizer) "It was further shown that the neural plate was almost entirely composed of cells from the host" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spemann%E2%80%93Mangold_organizer) "The direction angle preference that a neuron will acquire can be predicted from small initial biases that are present in the naïve cortex prior to the onset of visual experience." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A30001203%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "These results are consistent with the idea that experience has a permissive influence on the development of direction selectivity." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A30001203%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | learning skill, identity self |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/an interaction can trigger a change in a living system but cannot specify it.md`; The vault holds Maturana and Varela's general claim that an interaction triggers but cannot specify a living system's change. This entry adds the working distinction developmental biology uses to test such claims, with one supporting case (experience in ferret visual cortex) and one counter-case (the organizer). |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spemann%E2%80%93Mangold_organizer; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A30001203%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1007/s10539-017-9568-0 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *instructive* | an influence whose content shapes the specific outcome |
| *permissive* | an influence required for development to proceed that does not shape its specific outcome |
| *organizer* | the group of embryonic cells whose signals induce the body axes and nervous tissue in amphibians |

#### Quorum sensing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | microbiology |
| Originators | Kenneth Nealson, Terry Platt and J. Woodland Hastings (first observations, 1970, called autoinduction); Stephen Winans, with W. Claiborne Fuqua and E. Peter Greenberg (the term quorum sensing, 1994); Bonnie Bassler (its wide occurrence across bacteria, 1998 to 2001) |
| Date | 1970 (autoinduction); 1994 (the term) |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Bacteria that use quorum sensing each release a small signaling molecule and carry a receptor for it. At low cell density the signal diffuses away; as the population grows its concentration rises until it passes a threshold and switches on genes for behaviors that pay off only when many cells act together, such as light production by Aliivibrio fischeri inside the light organ of the Hawaiian bobtail squid, or biofilm formation by Pseudomonas aeruginosa. Nealson, Platt and Hastings first saw the effect in 1970 as luminescence that appeared only after a culture had grown dense. |
| What is facilitated | A population's shift into a collective behavior is facilitated by a signal that measures how many members are present. No single cell or outside agent directs the shift; it happens once enough cells contribute. |
| Across scales | The source notes that some social insects use quorum sensing to choose where to nest and that the principle is used in robotics, so decisions by count recur in groups of animals and machines. It gives no data on human groups. |
| The evidence | The mechanism is well established across many bacteria, and the first three-dimensional structures of quorum-sensing proteins were published in 2001. The Wikipedia article carries a notice that it needs more citations. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Change at the scale of a team or organization: A group behavior that switches on only above a count of participants is a measured mechanism for change that no single member can start alone. Decisions: Quorum sensing is a decision rule with no decider, in which the group acts according to how many of its members are present. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Bacteria do not deliberate and the threshold is chemical, whereas human group decisions involve persuasion and dissent that the model omits. |
| Limits and critiques | The same mechanism lets Pseudomonas aeruginosa grow in a host without harming it until it reaches a threshold concentration and then turn aggressive, so collective switching serves harmful ends as readily as useful ones. |
| Quoted from its sources | "QS signaling molecules are usually secreted at a low level by individual bacteria. At low cell density, the molecules may just diffuse away." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quorum_sensing) "Because Nealson, Platt, and Hastings attributed the conditioning of the growth medium to the growing population of cells itself, they referred to the phenomenon as autoinduction." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quorum_sensing) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | group process, decision choice |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds nothing on bacterial signaling. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quorum_sensing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *autoinducer* | the signaling molecule a bacterium releases and also detects |
| *threshold* | the signal concentration, reflecting population density, at which the collective behavior switches on |
| *autoinduction* | the original name for the effect, a growth medium conditioned by the cells' own growth |

#### Sahuaro–nurse tree associations and the replacement of the nurse

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | desert plant ecology |
| Originators | Joseph R. McAuliffe |
| Date | 1984 ('Sahuaro-nurse tree associations in the Sonoran Desert: competitive effects of sahuaros', Oecologia 64: 319–321) |
| Scale | an ecosystem |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | In the Sonoran Desert, young saguaro cacti (sahuaros) establish mostly under the canopies of trees and shrubs, called nurse plants, whose cover protects them. McAuliffe showed that a saguaro growing close to a foothill paloverde, a common nurse tree, increases the tree's stem die-back and mortality. The interaction speeds the local loss of the nurse tree and produces a predictable pattern in which the cactus replaces the tree that sheltered it. |
| What is facilitated | The saguaro's establishment is facilitated by shelter it cannot provide for itself when young. The nurse tree does not supply the saguaro's growth, and it bears a cost once the saguaro is established. |
| Across scales | A helper that is outgrown and then displaced also appears in Connell and Slatyer's facilitation model of succession, at the scale of whole plant communities. Facilitation cascades are the counter-case, in which facilitators persist alongside what they help. |
| The evidence | The study is a single short field paper comparing paloverdes with and without nearby saguaros. The Wikipedia article on nurse plants, which also describes the case, is flagged as machine-generated and is uncited in places, so the primary abstract is the source used here. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | What care costs the one who gives it: The nurse tree's help is followed by its decline and death, caused by the plant it sheltered, a measured case of help that costs the helper its place. Timing and pacing: The relation changes with the helped plant's growth, from shelter to competition, so what helps at one stage harms at another. Remaining the author of one's own change: A nurse plant helps only while the young plant cannot establish itself, and the grown plant does not remain dependent on it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book does not describe the facilitator's displacement as the aim of facilitation, and plants have no relationship beyond their effects on resources. |
| Quoted from its sources | "This interaction appears to accelerate the local loss of individual nurse trees, resulting in a predictable pattern of species replacement." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A28311446%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | nature ecology, death finitude |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no plant ecology. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A28311446%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurse_plant |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *nurse plant* | a plant under whose canopy seedlings of another species establish and survive |
| *species replacement* | the helped species taking the place of the helper over time |

#### Social facilitation (coaction and audience effects)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | social psychology |
| Originators | Norman Triplett (1898); Floyd Allport (the term, 1924); critique of the textbook account by W. Stroebe (2012) |
| Date | 1898; 1924 |
| Scale | a small group, two people as equals |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Social facilitation is the change in a person's performance when others are present, either doing the same task (coaction) or watching (an audience). Triplett reported that cyclists rode faster when racing others than when riding against the clock, and in 1898 that children wound string faster when competing; Allport coined the term in 1924 for an 'increase in response merely from the sight or sound of others making the same movement'. Later work found that the presence of others improves performance on simple or well-practiced tasks and worsens it on complex or new ones. |
| What is facilitated | The performer's own well-learned responses are made faster or stronger by the presence of others. The others supply no instruction or content; their presence changes the performer's state. |
| Across scales | The effect has been reported in capuchin monkeys and in cockroaches (see zajonc-drive-theory-and-cockroaches), which suggests a mechanism that does not depend on language, although the cockroach result did not replicate as originally reported. |
| The evidence | C. F. Bond and L. J. Titus published a meta-analysis of 241 studies in 1983, and the source reports Bond's finding that the effect on dominant responses occurs even with an audience that cannot be seen or is not evaluating. W. Stroebe showed in 2012 that the textbook account of Triplett is wrong in its details: his study was neither the first social psychology experiment nor the first on social facilitation, the task is usually misdescribed, and his evidence for facilitation was overstated. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: Being watched changes performance before anything is said, helping what is practiced and hindering what is new. Participation rather than watching: The research separates the effect of others doing the same thing beside a person from the effect of others only watching. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research measures speed and accuracy on tasks, not inner change, and the watchers it studies do not respond to what they see. |
| Limits and critiques | The founding study is routinely misreported, which shows how a simple account of the origin of a field can go on being repeated after its evidence has been shown to be weaker than claimed. |
| Quoted from its sources | "increase in response merely from the sight or sound of others making the same movement" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_facilitation) "Triplett's study was not the first social psychology study (it was not even the first study on social facilitation), the experimental task was described wrongly, and the evidence for social facilitation was overstated." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Stroebe%20W%22%20AND%20Triplett&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Mental model | other. experimental social psychology |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | task work, learning skill, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/second person ecology.md`; The survey of second persons sorts the jobs a present other does in the traditions the vault reads, including the receiver, who takes what a person says without overruling it, and the witness. It holds no experimental research on the mere presence of others, which this entry and the next supply. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_facilitation; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Stroebe%20W%22%20AND%20Triplett&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Social%20facilitation%3A%20a%20meta-analysis%20of%20241%20studies%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *coaction* | performing a task alongside others doing the same task, without interacting with them |
| *audience effect* | a change in performance from being watched |
| *social inhibition* | worse performance in the presence of others, usually on complex or new tasks |

#### Stigmergy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | entomology and swarm intelligence |
| Originators | Pierre-Paul Grassé (1959, studying termites); E. O. Wilson (the broader term sematectonic communication) |
| Date | 1959 |
| Scale | an ecosystem, a text or tool, no one present, a large group |
