| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The trace left in the shared environment does the coordinating, and no agent directs the others. |
| What it holds | Stigmergy is coordination through the environment: the trace one action leaves stimulates a later action by the same agent or another. Grassé introduced the term in 1959 for termites and defined it as the stimulation of workers by the work they have already done: termites drop pheromone-marked mud where others have dropped it, so heaps grow into pillars, arches and chambers without a plan. Ants lay pheromone trails that others follow and reinforce, so the trail network works as a shared external memory, and the same mechanism has been used to describe wikis and open-source software projects. |
| What is facilitated | A group's construction of a structure no member plans is facilitated by the traces each member's work leaves. No agent supplies the design or directs the others; each responds to what is already there. |
| Across scales | The source itself applies stigmergy to termites and bacteria and also to robots, online communities and the design of buildings, so it is presented as applying across scales. At the human scale the traces are read by agents who can also plan and talk, which the insect case lacks. |
| The evidence | Stigmergy is well documented in social insects and has been used successfully in computing, as in ant colony optimization. Its application to human collaboration is descriptive and analogical in the sources read. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: In stigmergy the structure exists only because each agent adds to it, and each addition prompts the next contributor. Who decides the content: No one decides the design: the traces decide what the next action is, an extreme case of a structure doing the work with no facilitator present. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Insects respond to traces automatically, whereas a reader interprets a text and can refuse it. |
| Limits and critiques | The mechanism depends on positive feedback, in which larger heaps attract more deposits, so where the structure grows depends on early random placements rather than on any judgment of quality. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The network of trails functions as a shared external memory for the ant colony." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stigmergy) "It produces complex, seemingly intelligent structures, without need for any planning, control, or even direct communication between the agents." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stigmergy) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | group process, task work |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds nothing on coordination through traces. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stigmergy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *stigmergy* | from the Greek words for "mark" and "work": coordination in which traces of work prompt further work |
| *trace* | the change an action leaves in the environment, such as a pheromone or a heap of mud |
| *sematectonic communication* | Wilson's broader term for behavior evoked by evidence of work others have done |

#### The adaptive cycle and panarchy, with the cross-scale links 'revolt' and 'remember'

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | resilience ecology |
| Originators | C. S. Holling (the adaptive cycle, 1986); Lance Gunderson and C. S. Holling (Panarchy, 2002); the Resilience Alliance |
| Date | 1986; 2002 (Gunderson and Holling, eds., Panarchy: Understanding Transformations in Human and Natural Systems) |
| Scale | an ecosystem, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The adaptive cycle describes an ecosystem moving slowly from exploitation, the rapid colonization of disturbed ground, to conservation, the slow accumulation and storage of energy and material, and then quickly through release, or collapse, to reorganization and back, with the possibility of turning into a different system during reorganization. Panarchy nests such cycles across scales: smaller, faster levels invent and experiment, and larger, slower levels stabilize and conserve accumulated memory. Two links between levels are named: 'revolt', in which a small, fast event cascades up to change a larger, slower cycle, and 'remember', in which a level renewing itself after collapse draws on the potential stored in a larger, slower level in its conservation phase. |
| What is facilitated | Renewal after collapse is facilitated by memory and potential stored at slower, larger levels, such as nutrients and biomass accumulated in an ecosystem, or skills, relationships and trust accumulated in a community. The larger level does not supply the new configuration; it supplies material the renewing level draws on. |
| Across scales | The Resilience Alliance proposes the adaptive cycle as a fundamental unit for understanding complex systems 'from cells to ecosystems to societies', an explicit claim of scale invariance. The same proponents acknowledge that the claim for social systems is an abstraction, so it is a heuristic across scales rather than a demonstrated invariant. |
| The evidence | The adaptive cycle was derived from comparative studies of ecosystem dynamics, and the Resilience Alliance describes it as a tool for thought rather than a tested law. Holling and colleagues acknowledged that applying panarchy to social systems is an abstraction whose ecological basis social scientists have challenged, and, as Luthe and Wyss report, Holling named foresight and intentionality, communication of ideas, and technology as features that distinguish human systems. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Change at the scale of a team or organization: Revolt names how a small, fast change can overturn a larger structure that has become rigid, and remember names how a group recovering from collapse draws on what older, larger structures hold. Recognition: Remember describes renewal that draws on accumulated memory rather than starting from nothing, a parallel to the book's claim that change recovers what was already there, held as a parallel and not as evidence. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The adaptive cycle treats collapse as a normal phase of any system, whereas the book's arc of peak and descent concerns one person's experience, which may not follow ecological phases. |
| Limits and critiques | The cycle's own proponents call it a tool for thought, and its application to social systems has been challenged by social scientists. |
| Quoted from its sources | "It is meant to be a tool for thought." (https://www.resalliance.org/adaptive-cycle) "is proposed as a fundamental unit for understanding complex systems from cells to ecosystems to societies" (https://www.resalliance.org/adaptive-cycle) "The smaller, faster, nested levels invent, experiment and test, while the larger, slower levels stabilize and conserve accumulated memory of system dynamics." (https://www.resalliance.org/panarchy) "the ‘revolt’ function stands for a cascading effect where fast and small events trigger a change in larger and slower cycles" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6106646/fullTextXML) "acknowledge that the use of the panarchy concept for social systems is an abstraction and that its original application to ecosystems has been challenged by social scientists" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6106646/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | nested levels; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | nature ecology, group process, death finitude |
| In the survey | Ecocycle Planning; this entry adds the survey's Ecocycle Planning entry is a Liberating Structures method built on Holling's adaptive cycle. This entry adds the ecological source: the phases as Holling described them, the nesting of cycles in a panarchy with the two cross-level links revolt and remember, and the proponents' own statements that the model is a tool for thought and an abstraction when applied to social systems. |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds nothing on the adaptive cycle; the survey's Ecocycle Planning entry is its only trace. |
| Sources | https://www.resalliance.org/adaptive-cycle; https://www.resalliance.org/panarchy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socio-ecological_system; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6106646/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *foreloop and backloop* | the slow phases of growth and accumulation, and the fast phases of release and reorganization |
| *revolt* | a small, fast event cascading up to change a larger, slower level |
| *remember* | renewal at a small, fast level drawing on memory and potential held at a larger, slower level |
| *panarchy* | a nested hierarchy of adaptive cycles across scales of space, time and social organization |

#### The free energy principle and active inference

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | computational neuroscience and theoretical biology |
| Originators | Karl Friston |
| Date | 2010 review ('The free-energy principle: a unified brain theory?', Nature Reviews Neuroscience), after earlier papers |
| Scale | one organism, molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Friston's free energy principle states that a system that keeps itself distinct from its environment behaves as if it minimizes variational free energy, an upper bound on surprise, that is, on how improbable its sensory states are under its own model. Applied to the brain, perception is the updating of internal models to predict sensory input, and action is changing the input to fit the predictions, which is called active inference. The boundary between system and environment is formalized as a Markov blanket of sensory and active states, and the source's figure applies the same partition to a cell, with its membrane as the blanket, and to a brain. |
| What is facilitated | On this account nothing is facilitated from outside: a system keeps itself within expected states by updating its models and by acting. An environment or another agent enters only as a source of sensory states the system has to predict. |
| Across scales | The principle is scale-free by construction, stated in the same terms for a cell and for a brain. Bruineberg and colleagues' critique is aimed at exactly the step from a formal partition to real boundaries at each scale. |
| The evidence | Friston describes the principle as mathematical and, like Hamilton's principle of stationary action, not falsifiable; hypotheses built on it, such as predictive coding, are empirical and can be tested. J. Bruineberg, K. Dołęga, J. Dewhurst and M. Baltieri (Behavioral and Brain Sciences, online 2021, print 2022) identify a confusion between Markov blankets as a tool of statistical inference and their use to mark the physical boundary of an agent, and argue that the second use needs philosophical premises the mathematics does not supply. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: A principle that cannot be falsified cannot by itself tell a real change from an imagined one, so evidence has to come from the specific process theories built on it. Protection and defense: The account describes living systems as acting to keep their sensory states within expected bounds, a general description of resistance to surprise. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The principle is a formal description with no view of what a person should become, whereas the book is about an event the reader recognizes. |
| Limits and critiques | Friston's own statement that the principle cannot be falsified means that support must come from the process theories built on it, which can be tested and can fail. |
| Quoted from its sources | "it cannot be falsified. It cannot be disproven." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_energy_principle) "A free-energy principle has been proposed recently that accounts for action, perception and learning." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20free-energy%20principle%3A%20a%20unified%20brain%20theory%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "We then identify a persistent confusion in the literature between the formal use of Markov blankets as an epistemic tool for Bayesian inference, and their novel metaphysical use in the free energy framework to demarcate the physical boundary between an agent and its environment." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Emperor%27s%20New%20Markov%20Blankets%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | thought belief, attention awareness, body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `provenance/high-energy states and system decoupling.md`; The synthesis records that the REBUS model of psychedelic action was developed with Karl Friston, and `ops/queue/candidates.md` queues Carhart-Harris with Friston. The vault holds nothing on the free energy principle itself or on its critiques. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_energy_principle; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20free-energy%20principle%3A%20a%20unified%20brain%20theory%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Emperor%27s%20New%20Markov%20Blankets%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1017/s0140525x21002351 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *variational free energy* | a quantity that bounds surprise from above and that the system is said to minimize |
| *active inference* | acting on the world so that sensory input matches predictions |
| *Markov blanket* | the set of states that separates internal from external states in the model |

#### The Sabatier principle: binding neither too strong nor too weak

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | physical chemistry (heterogeneous catalysis) |
| Originators | Named after Paul Sabatier; volcano plots introduced by Balandin |
| Date | Named after Paul Sabatier (Nobel Prize in Chemistry, 1912; La Catalyse en Chimie Organique, 1913); the sources read give no date for the principle itself |
| Scale | molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | In heterogeneous catalysis a solid catalyst adsorbs reactant molecules on its surface, the adsorbed molecules react, and the products desorb, leaving the surface free for further reactions. The Sabatier principle states that the interaction between the surface and the adsorbed molecules must be of intermediate strength: if it is too weak the reactants do not bind and nothing happens, and if it is too strong the products do not leave and the surface is blocked. Plotting reaction rate against binding strength gives a curve with a single peak, called a volcano plot. |
| What is facilitated | The reaction between adsorbed molecules is facilitated by a surface that holds them. The surface supplies a place and an orientation for the reaction, and it must also release the product or it stops facilitating. |
| Across scales | The rule that a facilitator must bind strongly enough to act and weakly enough to release is stated for surfaces and molecules. The sources read give no measured counterpart above the molecular scale, so extending it to persons is a proposal, not a finding. |
| The evidence | The source describes the principle as one of the central principles of the modern theory of catalysis and notes that it is qualitative, since the optimum lies in a space of many dimensions. Measured volcano plots, such as that for formic acid decomposition on transition metals, show the predicted peak. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The principle states for surfaces that binding too strongly blocks the outcome just as binding too weakly prevents it. Timing and pacing: Releasing the product is part of the facilitation, so the moment of letting go belongs to the method rather than coming after it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | A catalyst's binding strength is a fixed property of its material, whereas a human helper can change how closely they hold during a session. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Sabatier principle states that the surface-adsorbates interaction has to be an optimal amount: not too weak to be inert toward the reactants and not too strong to poison the surface and avoid desorption of the products." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterogeneous_catalysis) "If the interaction is too weak, the molecule will fail to bind to the catalyst and no reaction will take place." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_principle) "The catalyst itself remains intact and free to mediate further reactions." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterogeneous_catalysis) "catalytic surfaces (holding reactants in the right orientation as a definition of facilitation)" (notes/grief is sever plus love plus persist.md) |
| Mental model | other. physical chemistry |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/grief is sever plus love plus persist.md`; The note records the space-around survey's proposal of catalytic surfaces, which hold reactants in the right orientation, as a definition of facilitation, and marks it as unresearched. The Sabatier principle adds the half that proposal lacks: the surface must also let the product go. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterogeneous_catalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_principle; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Sabatier_(chemist) |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *adsorption and desorption* | a molecule binding to a surface, and a molecule leaving it |
| *volcano plot* | a graph of rate against binding strength whose peak marks the best catalysts |
| *poison* | a substance that binds so strongly to the surface that it blocks the sites where reactions happen |
| *promoter* | a substance that increases a catalyst's activity without being a catalyst itself |

#### The stress-gradient hypothesis

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | community ecology |
| Originators | Mark Bertness and Ragan Callaway (1994); tested and refined by Q. He, M. D. Bertness and A. H. Altieri (2013 synthesis) and by Maestre, Callaway, Valladares and Lortie (2009) |
| Date | 1994 |
| Scale | an ecosystem |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The stress-gradient hypothesis predicts that facilitation becomes more common, and competition less common, as environmental stress rises, so that neighbors help each other in harsh places and compete in mild ones. He, Bertness and Altieri synthesized 727 tests in plant communities worldwide in 2013 and found that interactions shift toward facilitation (for survival) or toward weaker competition (for growth and reproduction) as stress rises, and never toward stronger competition. Maestre and colleagues argued in 2009 that the hypothesis needs refinement by the species' life histories and the kind of stress, and predicted that neighbors can compete at both ends of a gradient when the stress is a lack of a shared resource such as water and both species have similar life histories. |
| What is facilitated | Survival under harsh conditions is facilitated by neighbors that make the conditions less harsh. The hypothesis says the value of that help depends on how harsh the conditions are. |
| Across scales | The source describes the hypothesis as applying in microbial and plant ecology alike. Its application to people, that help matters most under stress and rivalry most in comfort, is not tested by any source read here. |
| The evidence | Support is broad but contested: the source calls the hypothesis controversial among ecologists because of contradictory results, while the 2013 synthesis of 727 tests found consistent shifts toward facilitation. Support is strongest for survival and for drought, fire and nutrient stress. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: If help matters most under stress, the same supportive presence is worth more in a crisis than in ease. Conflict: The hypothesis predicts competition among neighbors where conditions are mild, so rivalry and help are expected to trade places as conditions change. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The hypothesis concerns the net effects of neighbors measured in plant survival and growth, not deliberate help. |
| Limits and critiques | Maestre and colleagues' refinement predicts that the pattern fails when both species compete for the same scarce resource. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In a limited number of cases, plant interactions do not respond to stress, but they never shift towards competition with stress." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A23363430%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "add complexity not considered in the SGH" (https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1111/j.1365-2745.2008.01476.x) "is controversial among ecologists, in part because of contradictory support" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_gradient_hypothesis) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | nature ecology |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no community ecology. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_gradient_hypothesis; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A23363430%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1111/j.1365-2745.2008.01476.x; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A21236818%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *stress gradient* | a change in harshness across space, such as from wet to dry or from low to high salinity |
| *resource and non-resource stress* | stress from lack of something both species use, such as water, as against stress such as cold or wind |

#### Zajonc's drive theory of social facilitation, and the cockroach experiments

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | social and comparative psychology |
| Originators | Robert Zajonc (1965); Zajonc, Alexander Heingartner and Edward M. Herman (cockroaches, 1969); direct replication by Emma Halfmann, Janne Bredehöft and Jan Alexander Häusser (2020) |
| Date | 1965 ('Social facilitation', Science); 1969 (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology); 2020 (Psychological Science) |
| Scale | a small group, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Zajonc proposed in 1965 that the mere presence of others raises arousal, and that arousal makes an organism's dominant, best-learned response more likely, which improves performance on simple or well-learned tasks and worsens it on complex or new ones. In 1969 Zajonc, Heingartner and Herman reported that cockroaches reached the end of a straight runway faster in the presence of other cockroaches, and presented enhanced performance on simple tasks and impaired performance on complex ones as their main conclusion, taking the result to show that the effect needs no evaluation or thought. Rival accounts attribute the effect to fear of being evaluated (Nickolas Cottrell, 1972) or to distraction and conflicting demands on attention. |
| What is facilitated | Well-learned responses are facilitated by an audience on this theory, and new learning is hindered. The audience supplies arousal, not content, and what it strengthens is whatever the performer already does most readily. |
| Across scales | Zajonc's cockroaches were meant to show that the effect holds below the level of thought and across species. The replication's failure to find facilitation weakens that cross-species claim, while the impairment held. |
| The evidence | A preregistered direct replication by Halfmann, Bredehöft and Häusser (2020) found that cockroaches ran more slowly with an audience on both tasks, replicating the impairment but finding no evidence of facilitation, and noted that the 1969 study never tested the predicted interaction statistically. In humans the pattern of help on simple tasks and harm on complex ones is well supported, while the dispute between the mere-presence and evaluation accounts remains unresolved. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: On this theory a watcher strengthens what a person already does well and makes new responses harder, so being watched while learning something new can work against the learning. The container that holds people while they change: Zajonc advised students to study alone and take examinations in front of others, which separates the conditions for learning from the conditions for showing what was learned. Timing and pacing: The theory implies that the presence of others helps after a response is learned and hinders it before, so when a witness is present matters. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research concerns performance, not the inner change the book describes, and its audiences do not respond, whereas the book's witness is one who receives what a person says. |
| Limits and critiques | The cockroach result, often cited as proof of a mechanism below thought, did not replicate for facilitation. |
| Quoted from its sources | "He argued that the presence of others serves as a source of arousal, and heightened arousal increases the likelihood of an organism to do better on well-learned or habitual responses." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_facilitation) "Zajonc suggested that the student study alone, preferably in an isolated cubicle, and arrange to write examinations surrounded by many other students, on stage, and in the presence of a large audience." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_facilitation) "Although we replicated the social-inhibition effect, there was no evidence for a social-facilitation effect." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A32017670%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. experimental social psychology |
| Structure | holding two poles; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | learning skill, task work, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/second person ecology.md`; The survey of second persons records that the traditions place someone beside the practitioner and sorts what that person does; it holds no research on how mere presence changes performance. Zajonc's account adds that presence helps what is well learned and hinders what is new. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_facilitation; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A32017670%20AND%20SRC%3AMED&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *dominant response* | the response highest in an organism's repertoire, the one it produces most readily |
| *mere presence* | the presence of others who neither evaluate nor interact |
| *evaluation apprehension* | concern about being judged, proposed as the real cause of the effect |

## Part 2. The body and the nervous system

### The entries

#### 'If [this] then [breathe]': espresso breath and breath of calm

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | breathwork (somatic coaching) |
| Originators | Jonny Miller (the recipes and their names) |
| Date | essay 'The Operating Manual for Your Nervous System', dated 2024-08-07 on the course site |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person picks the recipe that matches the state they notice and judges the result. |
| What happens | Miller gives two 'automation recipes' in the form of an if-then rule: if lethargic or sleepy, do 'espresso breath'; if anxious or overwhelmed, do 'breath of calm'. Espresso breath is rapid nasal exhalation followed by breath holds and is meant to raise arousal; breath of calm is alternate-nostril breathing with an exhale twice as long as the inhale and is meant to lower it. He frames both with 'state before story': a person changes the breath, and calmer or clearer thoughts are supposed to follow. |
| Steps | (1) Espresso breath: sit with a straight lower spine, tuck the chin, and 'begin rapid exhales through your nose, at the rate of roughly two per second', letting each inhale happen by itself; 'Continue for 30 exhales.' (2) Then exhale completely and hold, 'holding until you feel mild breath-hunger (the desire to inhale)'; inhale deeply and hold for 20 to 30 seconds; release and keep still for 30 seconds; repeat two or three times. (3) Breath of calm: close one nostril with the thumb or ring finger. 'Begin by closing the right nostril and inhaling through the left side for a count of three. Then hold at the top for three. Exhale through the right side for a count of six.' Then reverse the sides; that is one round. (4) Repeat breath of calm for three to five rounds or until calm, starting at counts of two, two, and four if three and six are hard, and end with a full inhale and an audible sigh. |
| What is facilitated | The recipes make it easier for a person to move their own arousal up or down on purpose; the change happens in the person's breathing and body. The essay supplies the rule and the counts, and the person supplies the reading of their own state. |
| The evidence | No trial of these two named recipes was found. Related evidence is that one remote trial found an exhale-focused breathing practice improved mood more than mindfulness meditation, and that meta-analyses find small-to-medium effects of breathwork on self-reported stress in trials mostly at moderate risk of bias (see the breathing entries). The essay's statement that breathing is 'more effective than any prescription drug, with no unwanted side effects' is not supported by any study it cites. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: Espresso breath combines rapid breathing with breath holds, the pattern that the Wikipedia article on Wim Hof links to brief loss of consciousness and to drownings when it is practised in water, and the essay gives no such warning. Timing and pacing: The rule ties the choice of practice to the state noticed in the moment, so the practice follows the reading of the state. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The recipes treat a wanted state as something a person can produce on demand, the position the vault records for Tony Robbins, who calls physiology 'The biggest leverage we have in any situation'; the vault also records Gendlin's view that will cannot be exerted where experiencing does not implicitly function, and the book's felt shift is something a person can invite but not produce by will. |
| Limits and critiques | Rapid breathing followed by breath holds can cause a brief loss of consciousness, and the Wikipedia article on Wim Hof reports drownings among people who practised similar breathing in water. The essay does not mention this risk. |
| Quoted from its sources | "begin rapid exhales through your nose, at the rate of roughly two per second" (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/operating-manual-for-your-nervous-system) "Begin by closing the right nostril and inhaling through the left side for a count of three. Then hold at the top for three. Exhale through the right side for a count of six." (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/operating-manual-for-your-nervous-system) "more effective than any prescription drug, with no unwanted side effects" (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/operating-manual-for-your-nervous-system) "The breathing exercises can induce a form of hyperventilation that may lead to a brief loss of consciousness." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wim_Hof) "The biggest leverage we have in any situation is physiology—because it works so fast, and it works without fail" (archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | body, emotion, task work |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md`; The vault holds Robbins's physiology-first state change in his own words; Miller's recipes are a breath-specific version of the same idea, with the difference that Miller asks people to test each practice on themselves. |
| Sources | https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/operating-manual-for-your-nervous-system; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wim_Hof; archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *espresso breath* | Miller's name for rapid nasal exhalations followed by breath holds, used to raise alertness. |
| *breath of calm* | Miller's name for alternate-nostril breathing with a doubled exhale, used to calm down. |
| *If [this] then [breathe]* | Miller's rule format, which pairs a noticed state with the breathing practice meant to shift it. |
| *breath-hunger* | The urge to inhale during a breath hold. |

#### A Cheat Sheet for Feeling Anger

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic coaching |
| Originators | Jonny Miller |
| Date | essay 'How to Unclench' (mid-2026) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person does the practice alone or with a friend who witnesses; the witness holds space and does not direct. |
| What happens | Miller gives a guide for people who habitually clench against anger. The person prepares a private, safe space for 15 to 25 minutes, grounds themselves, connects to the care the anger protects and to where the anger is felt in the body, and then expresses it physically by pushing against a wall with sound, pounding a mattress, or striking a pillow while shouting. He warns against aiming it at other people and against 'disembodied raging', and he says a shift shows as relief or as another emotion that the anger was guarding. |
| Steps | (1) Set aside 15 to 25 minutes in a private space, or with a friend who is willing to hold space and witness, and remove sharp objects and hard surfaces. (2) Ground oneself, connect to 'the care/presence that the anger is protecting', and find where the anger is felt in the body; optionally play activating music. (3) Express the anger in one of three ways: pushing as hard as possible against a wall with sound or growling, a tantrum on a mattress pounding with the fists, or striking a pillow with a racquet or the forearms while shouting. (4) If shame arises, try the phrase 'I am loved in my anger' or 'I love my anger'; if the anger feels fake, keep acting angry until it feels real. (5) Recognize the shift by relief, or by another emotion surfacing that the anger was guarding, such as heartbreak or helplessness. |
| What is facilitated | The practice aims to let a person feel and express anger they have learned to suppress, in a setting where it harms no one, so that anger becomes available for saying no and setting boundaries; the anger and its release are the person's. A witness, if present, holds space and adds nothing. |
| The evidence | No study of this practice was found. In a 2002 experiment, angered people who hit a punching bag while thinking about the person who had angered them became angrier and more aggressive than people who did nothing, and the author concluded that 'Doing nothing at all was more effective than venting anger.' Miller's practice differs in aim, since it asks the person to stay connected to care and warns that disembodied raging may feel cathartic without creating safety, but that difference has not been tested. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The venting research suggests that physical expression combined with rumination about a person increases anger, which is the condition Miller's rule against aiming anger at others is meant to prevent. Protection and defense: Miller reads suppressed anger as a protective clench and reads anger itself as guarding a deeper feeling, such as heartbreak or helplessness. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The practice takes a side in the dispute the vault records between Joe Hudson, for whom expression through the body is a necessary phase, and Karla McLaren, whose 2010 book held that expression trains the brain to express again; the book's own channeling practice seeks a third way between repression and venting. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Set aside 15-25 minutes, find a private space (unless you have a friend or loved one who is open to holding space and witnessing)." (https://howtounclench.com/) "Disembodied raging/screaming which may feel cathartic but likely won’t create the associations of safety you’re looking for." (https://howtounclench.com/) "You will know when something has shifted, as there will typically be either relief, or another emotion will surface (potentially heartbreak, or helplessness) that the anger is guarding against." (https://howtounclench.com/) "Rumination increased rather than decreased anger and aggression." (https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1177/0146167202289002) "Doing nothing at all was more effective than venting anger." (https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1177/0146167202289002) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md; The vault's reading of Hudson and McLaren finds that they disagree about expression: Hudson says 'The muscles aren't going to learn from just sitting in meditation position', and McLaren's 2010 book says expression costs the emotion's message. Miller's anger practice sides with Hudson and adds a safety frame; the book's own practice is at notes/the affective practice completes a feeling's descent with consent between repression and venting.md. |
| Sources | https://howtounclench.com/; https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1177/0146167202289002; archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *clenched anger* | Anger habitually held back, which Miller links to resentment, an active inner critic, dullness, and difficulty saying no. |
| *kinked* | Miller's word for anger that comes out distorted in early attempts. |

#### A.P.E. check-in (awareness, posture, emotion)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic coaching (interoception training) |
| Originators | Jonny Miller, who writes that he coined the acronym |
| Date | essay 'The Art and Science of Interoception', dated 2024-08-07 on the course site |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person checks their own awareness, posture, and emotion and is told only to notice, not to change anything. |
| What happens | The A.P.E. check-in is a five-minute daily practice in which a person attends in turn to their awareness, their posture, and their emotion, without trying to change anything. Miller presents it inside a wider 'interoceptive palette' of five categories (mental state, awareness, posture, breath, and emotion) and recommends first taking the Multidimensional Assessment of Interoceptive Awareness questionnaire as a benchmark. He reports that students noticed changes after practising five minutes a day for 30 days. |
| Steps | (1) Awareness: notice whether attention is settled or eager to jump elsewhere, sleepy or alert, focused or frantic, including whether one has forgotten the space above and behind oneself. (2) Posture: scan the upper body for tension, including the head on the neck, the jaw, face, and eyes, and feel the breath and perhaps the heartbeat, following the instruction 'Don’t try to change anything for now—just notice.' (3) Emotion: 'Imagine that you’re a scientist objectively reporting on internal sensations' and ask what the emotional tone of the moment is, accepting that nothing may come. (4) Repeat the check-in for five minutes a day; Miller suggests 30 days. |
| What is facilitated | The check-in makes it more likely that a person notices their own bodily and emotional state as it is; the noticing is the person's. The practice supplies three places to look and an instruction not to change what is found. |
| Across scales | The practice is stated for one person; Miller's essay links interoception to empathy and co-regulation with others, citing research, but the check-in itself is done alone. |
| The evidence | No study of the A.P.E. check-in was found. The essay's claim that practice produces 'neuroplastic shifts that serve to permanently heighten your internal self-awareness' has no cited support. Studies on Miller's own literature page report that months of contemplative training raised measured heartbeat-perception accuracy modestly, and that heartbeat-counting tasks may not measure interoception well. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The check-in trains the same bodily attention on which the book's felt-shift standard relies, and interoception research shows that such attention and accurate reading can come apart. Remaining the author of one's own change: The instruction to notice without changing anything leaves what is found, and what to do about it, to the person. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Miller treats interoception as a trainable capacity that improves decisions and performance, under headings such as 'Make more informed decisions'; the book treats bodily sensing as the place where recognition is checked and makes no claim that the capacity can be permanently raised. |
| Quoted from its sources | "A.P.E. is an acronym that I myself coined that stands for “awareness, posture, and emotion”" (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/the-art-and-science-of-interoception) "It’s okay if you don’t connect to anything right now; this is only about listening intently to what is already present." (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/the-art-and-science-of-interoception) "Increased receptivity occurs through neuroplastic shifts that serve to permanently heighten your internal self-awareness." (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/the-art-and-science-of-interoception) "creating an objective benchmark against which to measure progress" (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/the-art-and-science-of-interoception) "We call this the “feather, brick, dump truck” phenomenon." (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/the-art-and-science-of-interoception) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | body, emotion, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/attending to the body is not the same as reading it accurately.md`; The vault holds the finding that interoceptive accuracy, sensibility, and awareness come apart and names the MAIA as the standard sensibility measure. Miller recommends the MAIA as 'an objective benchmark', but it is a self-report of sensibility, the dimension that the vault note says cannot certify accuracy. |
| Sources | https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/the-art-and-science-of-interoception; https://www.nsmastery.com/literature; https://www.nsmastery.com/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *interoception* | In Miller's gloss, awareness of one's internal state; in the research literature, the nervous system's sensing, interpreting, and integrating of signals from inside the body. |
| *interoceptive palette* | Miller's five categories for noticing internal state: mental state, awareness, posture, breath, and emotion. |
| *A.P.E.* | Awareness, posture, and emotion, the three things the check-in attends to. |
| *feather, brick, dump truck* | Miller's name, from a survey of leaders he co-wrote, for escalating signals that high achievers ignore: fatigue, then a minor health crisis, then burnout. |

#### affect labeling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | social and affective neuroscience |
| Originators | M. D. Lieberman, N. I. Eisenberger, M. J. Crockett, S. M. Tom, J. H. Pfeifer, and B. M. Way (2007); Jared B. Torre and Matthew D. Lieberman (2018 review) |
| Date | 2007 (Psychological Science); 2018 (Emotion Review) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | In the 2007 brain-imaging study, choosing a word for the emotion shown in a picture reduced the response of the amygdala to negative images more than other ways of processing them did, and it increased activity in the right ventrolateral prefrontal cortex. Torre and Lieberman's 2018 review argues that putting feelings into words is an implicit form of emotion regulation, since it dampens emotional responses even though it may not feel like regulation while it happens. |
| What is facilitated | Naming the feeling makes it easier for the person's own brain to dampen the emotional response; the naming is the person's, and a helper can at most invite it. |
| Across scales | The finding is measured in individuals naming the feelings in pictures; the vault's related claim applies naming to a room, where a named pattern changes, and nothing in these studies tests the group version. |
| The evidence | The 2007 study used functional MRI, and the 2018 review compares affect labeling with reappraisal across experience, physiology, brain activity, and behaviour. The vault already records a boundary on the finding: labeling a feeling that is present reduces the response, whereas an explanation of why the feeling is there can serve as a defense. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: Because labeling works without feeling like regulation, a person may be changed by naming a feeling without noticing that anything happened. Being seen and recognized by another: When a helper offers the word, the naming becomes shared, which these studies did not test. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Affect labeling reduces reactivity; the book's naming aims at recognition and at changing what can be said next, so a reduced response is not its test. |
| Quoted from its sources | "affect labeling, relative to other forms of encoding, diminished the response of the amygdala and other limbic regions to negative emotional images" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1111/j.1467-9280.2007.01916.x&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "affect labeling may not even feel like a regulatory process as it occurs" (https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1177/1754073917742706) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a single move |
| Subjects | emotion, body |
| In the survey | labelling a feeling; this entry adds the survey's entry is partly verified; this entry confirms the 2007 study's result at its abstract and adds the 2018 review's claim that labeling is implicit emotion regulation. |
| In the vault | `notes/naming aloud is part of what is happening rather than a report about it.md`; The vault holds the claim that naming aloud is part of what happens, with Lieberman's 2007 study and the boundary between labeling and explaining; this entry adds the 2018 review. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1111/j.1467-9280.2007.01916.x&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1177/1754073917742706 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *affect labeling* | Putting a present feeling into words. |
| *implicit emotion regulation* | Regulation of emotion that happens without the person intending it or noticing it. |

#### Aletheia's four depths and the presence anchor

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | coaching (presence-based and somatic) |
| Originators | Steve March, founder of Aletheia and originator of Aletheia Coaching |
| Date | masterclass dated 2024-10-29 in Nervous System Mastery's library; the founding date of Aletheia was not confirmed |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The coach reads which depth the client is in and chooses a method to meet it, and the client's experience supplies the content. |
| What happens | Steve March's Aletheia coaching, presented as a masterclass in Nervous System Mastery's library, holds that methods work when they meet the depth of experience a person is in, and it names four depths: parts, process (the bodily flow of sensation and emotion), presence, and non-dual presence. It replaces the self-improvement question with an 'unfoldment' question, 'What if nothing is missing?', and teaches a 'presence anchor', a felt place of settling to which a person returns while staying with strong feeling. The method's name, Aletheia, is the word the book uses in its title. |
| Steps | (1) Follow curiosity and slow down, exploring very slowly what is here, outside or inside. (2) Outwardly, attend to colours, gradations, textures, and sounds; inwardly, sense bodily sensations. (3) 'Notice when something in you settles or relaxes as you register what's here, and identify where that settling is located.' (4) Use that location as a presence anchor, and pendulate back to it when needed to titrate between intensity and safety. (5) If parts are split between feeling and avoiding an emotion, acknowledge each part and its good intentions before continuing to feel. |
| What is facilitated | The method aims to let a person's experience unfold at the depth they are in, from parts through bodily process to presence; the unfolding is the person's. The coach supplies attunement and a method matched to the depth, and March's question supplies no picture of what the person should become. |
| Across scales | The four depths are stated for one person working with a coach; the claim that methods work when matched to the depth a person inhabits is a claim about helping relationships generally, and the page offers no test of it. |
| The evidence | No study of Aletheia coaching was found. The guide quotes March saying that emotions fully felt 'only tend to last about 90 seconds', a popular claim for which the page gives no source. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: March's question 'What if nothing is missing? What if you're already a whole human being?' states in coaching terms the book's premise that a person comes to know again what never left. Remaining the author of one's own change: March argues that 'Trying to improve ourselves doesn't deliver us from self deficiency, it actually reinforces it', which removes the coach's picture of a better self from the work. Timing and pacing: The presence anchor lets a person move back and forth between intensity and safety, a pacing device that comes from Somatic Experiencing. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | March's depths end in non-dual presence as a realization a coach can help occasion; the book holds the traditions it cites as company and does not sort experience into depths. The shared name is a shared Greek word, and this researcher did not find how March's school chose it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Steve March is the founder of Aletheia and originator of Aletheia Coaching." (https://www.nsmastery.com/masterclasses/self-unfolding-aletheia-steve-march) "The primary question in the unfoldment paradigm is: What if nothing is missing? What if you're already a whole human being?" (https://www.nsmastery.com/masterclasses/self-unfolding-aletheia-steve-march) "Trying to improve ourselves doesn't deliver us from self deficiency, it actually reinforces it." (https://www.nsmastery.com/masterclasses/self-unfolding-aletheia-steve-march) "Use that felt location as a presence anchor, and pendulate back to it if you need to titrate between intensity and safety." (https://www.nsmastery.com/masterclasses/self-unfolding-aletheia-steve-march) "The shallowest depth, which is the depth that most of us tend to occupy, I call the depth of parts." (https://www.nsmastery.com/masterclasses/self-unfolding-aletheia-steve-march) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. The method moves between parts work, somatic process, presence, and non-dual realization, so it is also somatic and parts-based |
| Structure | nested levels; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, identity self, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering is contraction never corruption.md`; The vault's doctrine, in the corpus's words 'Nothing in you is broken. Something in you is folded', is close to March's 'What if nothing is missing?'; the vault holds no note on March or his school. |
| Sources | https://www.nsmastery.com/masterclasses/self-unfolding-aletheia-steve-march; https://howtounclench.com/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *the four depths* | Parts, process, presence, and non-dual presence, the levels of experience that March says a method must meet. |
| *unfoldment* | March's alternative to self-improvement, which begins from the question 'What if nothing is missing?'. |
| *presence anchor* | A felt place of settling, found by slow attention, used to return to safety while staying with intensity. |

#### Alexander technique

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic education |
| Originators | Frederick Matthias Alexander |
| Date | principles developed from the 1890s; back-pain trial (ATEAM) 2008 (BMJ) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. The teacher observes and guides with words and light touch, and the student learns to recognize and avoid their own habits; lessons treat the student as a student rather than a patient. |
| What happens | The Alexander technique is taught in private lessons in which a teacher observes a student and uses words and light hands-on guidance to help them sit, stand, and walk with less strain and a comfortable relationship between the head, neck, and spine. The ATEAM trial describes the lessons as developing lifelong skills for self-care that help people recognize and avoid poor habits of posture and coordination. In that trial, 579 people with chronic or recurrent back pain were randomized to normal care, massage, six or 24 lessons, and, for half of each group, an exercise prescription. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher observes how the student habitually sits, stands, and moves. (2) The teacher gives verbal and gentle manual guidance while the student stands, sits, and walks, often in front of a mirror (chair work), and sometimes while the student lies on a table (table work). (3) The student learns to recognize and avoid the habits that add strain and to keep a comfortable relationship between the head, neck, and spine. (4) The student applies the skill in daily life as self-care. |
| What is facilitated | The lessons help a student notice and stop habitual patterns of excess tension in how they move; the change is in the student's own coordination and continues after the lessons end. The teacher supplies observation and guidance. |
| Across scales | The principle of noticing a habitual response and not carrying it out, so that another becomes possible, is stated for posture and movement in one person, and these sources do not extend it. |
| The evidence | The ATEAM trial found that 24 lessons reduced back-pain disability and days in pain at one year, that six lessons followed by exercise achieved most of that effect, and that massage's early benefit did not last, with no significant harms reported; the authors argue that the lasting effect is unlikely to come from the attention and touch that any treatment gives. Wikipedia reports that NICE and the NHS cite evidence for back pain, neck pain, and Parkinson's disease, while a 2012 Cochrane review found no good evidence for asthma and a 2015 Australian review called the evidence limited. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The trial frames lessons as teaching lifelong skills for self-care, and the student continues the practice without the teacher. Knowing that something happened: The effect that lasted a year, where massage's effect faded, is an outside test that the change was learned rather than received. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The technique trains habits of posture and movement; the book addresses recognition and does not treat pain or posture. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Lessons in the Alexander technique offer an individualised approach designed to develop lifelong skills for self care that help people recognise, understand, and avoid poor habits affecting postural tone and neuromuscular coordination." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC3272681/fullTextXML) "One to one lessons in the Alexander technique from registered teachers have long term benefits for patients with chronic back pain." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1136/bmj.a884&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "Six lessons followed by exercise prescription were nearly as effective as 24 lessons." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1136/bmj.a884&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "is unlikely to result from non-specific placebo effects of attention and touch" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC3272681/fullTextXML) "Individuals who take lessons are regarded as students rather than patients, and the instruction is not considered a form of passive therapy." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_technique) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | body, learning skill, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on the Alexander technique. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1136/bmj.a884&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC3272681/fullTextXML; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_technique |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *chair work* | Lesson work in which the teacher guides the student through sitting, standing, and walking. |
| *table work* | Lesson work with the student lying on a table while the teacher guides by hand. |
| *student rather than patient* | The technique's framing of lessons as education rather than passive therapy. |

#### asking what emotion a clench keeps one from feeling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | somatic coaching |
| Originators | Jonny Miller |
| Date | essay 'How to Unclench' (mid-2026) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. Miller poses the question for self-exploration, so the person supplies the answer; a practitioner who asked it would shape the process without supplying content. |
| What happens | Miller reduces his self-exploration process to one question that a person asks on noticing tension: 'If you couldn’t feel clenched right now, what emotion would you have to feel?' The question rests on his claim that clenching protects against feelings that were once punished or unsafe. In his own example, turning attention from a pending decision to a knot in his belly led to tears, shaking, and a sense of processing fear, after which the decision no longer seemed pressing. |
| Steps | (1) Notice a clench, for example while going round in circles about a problem. (2) Stop trying to solve the problem and turn attention to the body. (3) Ask: 'If you couldn’t feel clenched right now, what emotion would you have to feel?' (4) Stay with whatever sensation or emotion comes, softening and breathing, and let it move; in his example Miller used music and a pillow to make sound into. (5) Rest afterwards and let the experience settle. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes it more likely that a person turns from managing a problem to feeling what their tension was holding back; the feeling and its movement are the person's. The question supplies a direction for attention and no content. |
| Across scales | A practitioner can ask the same question of a client, and Miller recommends a practitioner for people with trauma histories; the question is framed for one person's body and has no stated group form. |
| The evidence | No study of the question was found. Its premise, that a clench guards a specific unfelt emotion, comes from Reich's and Lowen's body psychotherapy, which Miller names, and Miller himself states that 'there is no established bodily mechanism for storing trauma'. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The question treats a protective tension as serving a purpose and asks what it protects, instead of overriding it. The container that holds people while they change: Miller advises that people with a history of trauma, or for whom being in the body does not feel safe, start with another person, ideally a trained somatic practitioner, rather than use the question alone. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Miller locates a specific withheld emotion beneath each clench; the book's doctrine reads suffering as fullness contracted, which does not require a withheld emotion to be found. |
| Limits and critiques | Miller warns that unclenching can provoke intense emotion and that people with trauma histories should begin with support from another person. |
| Quoted from its sources | "If you couldn’t feel clenched right now, what emotion would you have to feel?" (https://howtounclench.com/) "Your body is making a reasonable adaptive choice." (https://howtounclench.com/) "If you have a history of trauma, or being in your body doesn’t feel safe, I strongly recommend starting with support from another person" (https://howtounclench.com/) "as of today, there is no established bodily mechanism for storing trauma" (https://howtounclench.com/) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a single move |
| Subjects | body, emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering is contraction never corruption.md`; The vault's master doctrine holds that suffering is fullness contracted rather than a self damaged, in the corpus's words 'Nothing in you is broken. Something in you is folded'; Miller's question asks what a particular contraction keeps out of feeling. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://howtounclench.com/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *brilliant logic* | Miller's phrase for the adaptive reasoning that keeps a protective clench in place. |
| *Self-Exploration Mode* | Miller's name for running unclenching loops alone, which he advises some people to skip. |

#### co-regulation practice and the Relational Dojo

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic coaching (relational practice) |
| Originators | Jonny Miller; he writes that he co-created the Relational Dojo within Nervous System Mastery |
| Date | Anti-Anxiety Toolkit dated 2024-11-08 on the course site; 'How to Unclench' (mid-2026) |
| Scale | two people as equals, a small group, the more-than-human |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. In the Dojo a facilitator sets up a structured practice and the participants supply what they feel and say; the home exercises have no facilitator. |
| What happens | Miller teaches co-regulation as the third protocol in his Anti-Anxiety Toolkit: adults, like children, settle in the presence of other nervous systems, and he calls the belief that one should face every challenge alone 'Lone Wolf Syndrome'. His home exercises are a 20-second hug and extended eye contact while breathing together, noticing how one feels before and after. In the course, the weekly small-group Relational Dojo is practice in 'unclenching while in connection with another', which the course's guide describes as training in staying in contact while someone else's nervous system is activated. |
| Steps | (1) Consider who one co-regulates with regularly, such as a spouse, children, a pet, or co-workers: 'And acknowledge that your nervous systems are shaping each other.' (2) With a partner, a close friend, or a pet, try a 20-second hug or 'extended eye contact & breathe together'. (3) Pay attention to how one feels before and after. (4) In the Relational Dojo, practise unclenching while in contact with another person, with, in the guide's words, enough safety to stay and enough challenge to reveal one's pattern. |
| What is facilitated | The practices make it easier for a person to settle through contact with someone else; the settling happens in both people's bodies and in their relationship. The course supplies the structure and the partners, and it does not supply what the partners feel. |
| Across scales | Miller describes co-regulation from infant and caregiver to adult pairs, groups, leaders and their teams, and people and their dogs. The studies that test it are almost all of pairs, and the finding that a stranger's hand did not help suggests the effect depends on the relationship rather than on the presence of any body. |
| The evidence | Research supports part of the premise: holding a partner's hand under threat of shock reduced distress and threat-related brain activity, while a stranger's hand had a limited effect in one study and none in a larger one (see the entry on social baseline theory). Miller's stated mechanism, that mirror neurons let a calm person's state be copied and that 'your endocrine systems are literally tuning into each other', claims more than those studies measured, and his statement that loneliness 'can speed up the aging process more than smoking' has no source on the page. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: Miller finds that students who join the Dojo are almost always the ones who report life-changing outcomes, and he writes that solo exploration has limits because 'it is surprisingly easy to bullshit yourself without external feedback'. Being seen and recognized by another: The Dojo trains staying in contact while another person witnesses one's reactions, and an alumnus says that seeing other people reveal themselves made the course workable. Participation rather than watching: The practice happens only in contact with another person, and reading about co-regulation does not supply it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Miller's co-regulation is physiological settling between two bodies; the book's claim about the field between two people is that the field is itself a participant that does work, an ontological claim that the physiology does not test. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I call it Lone Wolf Syndrome." (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/anti-anxiety-toolkit) "extended eye contact & breathe together" (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/anti-anxiety-toolkit) "This practice can be summarized as unclenching while in connection with another." (https://howtounclench.com/) "it is surprisingly easy to bullshit yourself without external feedback" (https://howtounclench.com/) "the relational dojo practice that I co-created as part of the Nervous System Mastery cohort experience" (https://howtounclench.com/) "your endocrine systems are literally tuning into each other and producing similar hormones" (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/anti-anxiety-toolkit) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, body, emotion |
| In the survey | co-regulation; this entry adds the survey's co-regulation entry is a definition with no date and a partly verified source; this entry adds a practitioner's home exercises (a 20-second hug, eye contact while breathing together), a named group practice (the Relational Dojo), and a practitioner's own caution that solo work lacks outside feedback. |
| In the vault | `notes/the field between two people does work.md`; The vault holds the corpus's claim that the field between two people does work, with co-regulation named as its 'physiological floor', two nervous systems settling each other; Miller's practices are concrete exercises in that settling and say nothing about the field as a participant. |
| Sources | https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/anti-anxiety-toolkit; https://howtounclench.com/; https://www.nsmastery.com/podcast/behind-the-scenes-of-nervous-system-mastery; https://www.nsmastery.com/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *co-regulation* | One person's nervous system settling in the presence of another's; Miller writes that children rely on their caregivers' co-regulation before they can regulate themselves. |
| *Lone Wolf Syndrome* | Miller's name for the belief, which he says is common in adult men, that one should face any challenge alone. |
| *Relational Dojo* | The course's weekly small-group practice in staying unclenched while in connection with another person. |

#### cyclic sighing compared with mindfulness meditation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychophysiology |
| Originators | Melis Yilmaz Balban, Eric Neri, Manuela M. Kogon, David Spiegel, Andrew D. Huberman, and colleagues (Stanford) |
| Date | 2023 (Cell Reports Medicine) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | In a remote randomized trial, participants practised one of three five-minute breathing exercises or mindfulness meditation daily for a month. Cyclic sighing, a double inhale followed by a prolonged exhale, improved positive mood and lowered breathing rate more than meditation did. The other two exercises were box breathing and cyclic hyperventilation with breath retention. |
| What is facilitated | The exercise helps a person slow their breathing and lower their arousal; the change is in the person's own breathing and mood. The study supplied instructions remotely, and no facilitator was present. |
| The evidence | The analysed groups were small (22 in meditation and 78 across the breathing groups), mostly Stanford undergraduates, and the study was not preregistered as a clinical trial because it began as exploratory; practice was unsupervised and self-reported. The mood difference was significant at the 0.05 level, a modest result. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The trial measured mood and breathing rate against a meditation control, which shows how small a reliable difference between two practices can be. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The trial tests which five-minute exercise lowers arousal most; the book does not rank practices by effect size, and by the vault's stance its convergences are never ranked by evidential weight. |
| Quoted from its sources | "(1) cyclic sighing, which emphasizes prolonged exhalations" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1016/j.xcrm.2022.100895&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "Daily 5-min cyclic sighing has promise as an effective stress management exercise." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1016/j.xcrm.2022.100895&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "This study was originally intended as an exploratory study in preparation for a larger clinical trial and thus was not pre-registered as a clinical trial." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9873947/fullTextXML) "Most of the participants were recruited from an undergraduate psychology class at Stanford University" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9873947/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | body, emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on breathing trials; Jonny Miller's literature page lists this study first among self-regulation studies. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1016/j.xcrm.2022.100895&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9873947/fullTextXML; https://www.nsmastery.com/literature |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *cyclic sighing* | Repeated double inhales followed by a prolonged exhale. |
| *box breathing* | Equal counts of inhaling, holding, exhaling, and holding. |
| *cyclic hyperventilation with retention* | Fast deep breathing followed by breath holds, the pattern used in Wim Hof-style practice. |

#### Feldenkrais Method (Awareness Through Movement and Functional Integration)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic education |
| Originators | Moshé Feldenkrais |
| Date | developed from the 1940s; taught in Tel Aviv from the 1950s; practitioner trainings in San Francisco, 1975 to 1978 |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The teacher guides movements by voice or by touch, and the student's own perception of ease guides what changes. |
| What happens | The Feldenkrais Method teaches people to move with more ease by attending to how they move. In Awareness Through Movement a teacher guides a group by voice through slow, unfamiliar movements; in Functional Integration a practitioner guides one person through movements by touch. Feldenkrais wrote that the lessons refine a person's ability to distinguish movements that are easy and pleasurable from those that are strained, which leads to new movement possibilities. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher guides slow, often unfamiliar movements, by voice in a group (Awareness Through Movement) or by hand with one person (Functional Integration). (2) The student attends to the quality of each movement and compares easy with strained ways of doing it. (3) The teacher repeats and varies the movements, exploring the parts of an action as well as the whole. (4) The student carries the new, easier options into ordinary movement. |
| What is facilitated | The method helps a person's nervous system find easier ways of moving through the person's own perception of differences; the discovery is the student's. The teacher supplies movement sequences and touch. |
| Across scales | The learning principle, that variation and attention to difference let a system find easier options, is stated for motor learning in one body, and these sources do not extend it further. |
| The evidence | Wikipedia states that there is no conclusive evidence for any medical benefit, an Australian government review in 2015 found no clear evidence of effectiveness, and a 2022 German health-technology report found a 'hint' of benefit for Parkinson's disease and inconsistent evidence for back pain. Researchers do not consider the method to pose serious risks. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The student's own perception of which movement is easier guides what changes. Knowing that something happened: The method's test is a felt difference between an easier and a strained movement, a bodily discrimination rather than an explanation. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Feldenkrais works on movement and posture; the book works on recognition of what a person already is and does not train motor skills. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The purpose of these sensorimotor lessons is to refine one's ability to make perceptual distinctions between movements that are easy and pleasurable and those that are strained and uncomfortable, which results in the discovery of new movement possibilities as well as potential for further improvements." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feldenkrais_method) "There is no conclusive evidence for any medical benefits of the therapy." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feldenkrais_method) "a system of exercise therapy developed in the 1940s by former judo instructor Moshe Feldenkrais" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feldenkrais_method) "reframed as nervous-system medicine" (https://www.globalwellnesssummit.com/trendium/neurowellness-one-of-the-biggest-wellness-trends-of-2026/) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | body, learning skill, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on Feldenkrais; the Global Wellness Summit's 2026 trend report lists it among practices 'reframed as nervous-system medicine'. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feldenkrais_method; https://www.globalwellnesssummit.com/trendium/neurowellness-one-of-the-biggest-wellness-trends-of-2026/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Awareness Through Movement* | Group lessons guided by voice. |
| *Functional Integration* | One-to-one lessons guided by touch. |
| *exploratory versus performative movement* | The principle of trying unfamiliar movements to notice differences in ease rather than performing a correct one. |

#### Felt Sense Dynamics (the Five S's)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic practice (Focusing-oriented) |
| Originators | N. Sanderovsky |
| Date | 2026 (Frontiers in Psychology) |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, one person alone |
