(https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tension_and_Trauma_Releasing_Exercises) "Research into TRE has primarily consisted of pilot studies and small-scale trials." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tension_and_Trauma_Releasing_Exercises) "the specific connection between TRE and polyvagal theory has not been established in clinical research" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tension_and_Trauma_Releasing_Exercises) "On primary outcomes, no significant differences between the TRE intervention group and the control group were detected at follow-up in the unadjusted analyses." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%22trauma%20releasing%20exercises%22%20OR%20%22tension%20releasing%20exercises%22%20OR%20%22tension%20and%20trauma%20releasing%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=25) "While study designs were not consistently rigorous, findings highlight TRE's promise and the need for more research to inform evidence-based practice." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%22trauma%20releasing%20exercises%22%20OR%20%22tension%20releasing%20exercises%22%20OR%20%22tension%20and%20trauma%20releasing%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=25) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | body, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/wilhelm reich.md`; The vault holds Reich's character armor and the body-psychotherapy line that the corpus cites through its descendants; TRE's own account places it in the same line, from Reich's muscular armor through Lowen's bioenergetics. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tension_and_Trauma_Releasing_Exercises; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%22trauma%20releasing%20exercises%22%20OR%20%22tension%20releasing%20exercises%22%20OR%20%22tension%20and%20trauma%20releasing%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=25 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *neurogenic tremors* | Proponents' name for the involuntary shaking that TRE is designed to induce. |
| *psoas* | A deep hip-flexor muscle that TRE's exercises target. |

#### the critique of 'nervous system regulation' as a wellness framing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | critique of wellness culture (psychology and community practice) |
| Originators | Nyle Biondi (the Queer Resilience newsletter); Amy Loughman (University of Melbourne, writing in The Conversation) |
| Date | January 18, 2026 (Biondi); April 27, 2026 (Loughman) |
| Scale | a society's customs, a community or public, one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Two critiques from within the helping professions question the 'nervous system regulation' trend. Nyle Biondi, a practitioner who teaches regulation, argues that dysregulation is often the predictable result of oppression, economic insecurity, and political violence, that telling people to breathe treats it as if it arose in a vacuum, and that individual regulation cannot undo systemic harm, while still defending regulation as support for people who resist. Psychologist Amy Loughman argues that there is no clear evidence that any practice can 'reset' the nervous system, that the language can turn ordinary stress into a medical-sounding condition, and that it may move people toward seeing distress as a biological problem rather than as an experience. |
| What is facilitated | The critique makes it easier for a person or a helper to tell a difficulty caused by conditions from one located in the person's body, so that regulation is not offered in place of changing the conditions. |
| Across scales | The critique locates the cause in society rather than in one body: Biondi reads individual and collective dysregulation as outcomes of systems, and Loughman reads the trend itself as a cultural shift in how distress is explained. The individual practices still act at the scale of one body; the critique is that they cannot reach a cause located at a larger scale. |
| The evidence | These are argued positions, one in a practitioner's newsletter and one in an academic's explainer, not studies. The industry framing they answer is documented: the Global Wellness Summit states as fact that 'our nervous systems are dysregulated, stuck in fight-or-flight mode'. Loughman also reports the 2026 expert statement rejecting polyvagal theory, whose vocabulary the trend uses. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The critique holds that much dysregulation is produced by the structures people live in, so that offering individual regulation instead mistakes the cause. Harm and its conditions: A framing that tells stressed people their nervous system is dysregulated can add a sense of personal failure, which Biondi answers with 'that is not a personal failure'. Knowing that something happened: Loughman's point that there is no evidence a nervous system can be reset separates a felt relief from a claimed physiological repair. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book already holds that the deficit belongs to conditions and the capacity to the person; the critique supports that rule and warns against borrowing a regulation vocabulary that places the deficit in the reader's body. |
| Quoted from its sources | "As if dysregulation emerges in a vacuum." (https://queerresilience.substack.com/p/what-the-wellness-industry-misses) "no amount of individual regulation will undo systemic harm" (https://queerresilience.substack.com/p/what-the-wellness-industry-misses) "Nervous system care is not a substitute for justice—it’s a support for those fighting for it." (https://queerresilience.substack.com/p/what-the-wellness-industry-misses) "If your nervous system feels fried right now, that is not a personal failure." (https://queerresilience.substack.com/p/what-the-wellness-industry-misses) "there’s no clear evidence that they can “reset” the nervous system or that such a thing is even possible" (https://theconversation.com/what-do-people-mean-when-they-say-their-nervous-system-is-overloaded-or-needs-a-reset-277368) "More broadly, our fascination with the nervous system could be a move towards seeing distress as a brain or biological problem, rather than something based on our experience." (https://theconversation.com/what-do-people-mean-when-they-say-their-nervous-system-is-overloaded-or-needs-a-reset-277368) "our nervous systems are dysregulated, stuck in fight-or-flight mode" (https://www.globalwellnesssummit.com/trendium/neurowellness-one-of-the-biggest-wellness-trends-of-2026/) |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | justice power, body, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/pathology belongs to conditions and capacity to persons.md`; The vault holds the book's rule that a diagnosis must land on conditions and never on the reader; this critique applies the same rule to the regulation trend, which often predicates dysregulation of the person. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://queerresilience.substack.com/p/what-the-wellness-industry-misses; https://theconversation.com/what-do-people-mean-when-they-say-their-nervous-system-is-overloaded-or-needs-a-reset-277368; https://www.globalwellnesssummit.com/trendium/neurowellness-one-of-the-biggest-wellness-trends-of-2026/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *concept creep* | The extension of diagnostic labels meant for severe symptoms to mild, everyday experiences. |
| *nervous system reset* | A popular promise, which Loughman says has no clear evidence behind it, that a practice or retreat can restore the nervous system to a normal state. |
| *neurowellness* | The Global Wellness Summit's 2026 name for the trend, which it describes as a set of lo-fi and high-tech interventions. |

#### the three nervous system states (the course page's '3 nervous system modes')

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic coaching (drawing on polyvagal theory) |
| Originators | Jonny Miller, adapting Stephen Porges's polyvagal theory |
| Date | taught in the course's first week; explained on Good Life Project (episode published 2025-09-29) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people as equals |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. Miller offers the model as a map that a person can use to name their own states; he does not assign anyone a state. |
| What happens | Miller teaches three states of the autonomic nervous system, which he says draw on polyvagal theory: a sympathetic state of alertness and energy, a 'ventral' state of connection, safety, play, and social engagement, and a 'dorsal' state that is deep rest when mild and an emergency shutdown when triggered. He compares the three to a car's accelerator, foot brake, and handbrake. He holds that no state is wrong in itself, that the question is whether a response suits the situation, and that the ventral state can combine with activation, which he equates with 'nervous system capacity'. |
| Steps | (1) Learn the three states and the bodily signs of each. (2) Track and map what one's body feels like in each state, asking, in Miller's words, 'What does it feel like in my body when I’m in this state of ventral connection?' and what it feels like when activated or moving toward shutdown. (3) Ask whether the present response suits the situation; Miller's example is that fleeing a shark calls for activation while a conversation with his wife does not. (4) Treat a loss of contact with the ventral state as the sign that reactivity is present, and use that recognition as the first step toward choosing a different response. |
| What is facilitated | The model gives a person words for their current state, which makes the state easier to notice and a practice easier to choose; the naming and noticing are the person's. The model does not supply what a particular state means for that person. |
| Across scales | Miller extends the model from one person to pairs and groups through co-regulation, holding that a grounded nervous system helps other people settle; the physiological claims behind that extension are the part of polyvagal theory that specialists dispute. |
| The evidence | The model rests on polyvagal theory, whose physiological premises a 2023 review and a 2026 statement co-signed by 39 specialists judge untenable (see the polyvagal entry); Miller's own literature page lists Grossman's 2023 critique beside Porges's papers. The course page names '3 nervous system modes' without defining them, so treating those modes as the three states Miller describes in the 2025 interview is this researcher's inference. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The model reads both activation and shutdown as protective responses that suit danger and cost a person when they persist, which is close to the book's reading of protection that has outlived its cause. Knowing that something happened: A person using the model can name a shift between states in plain bodily terms, although the physiological account behind the names is contested. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book does not sort experience into autonomic states, and the corpus's temporal loop, which leans on polyvagal theory, belongs to the system vocabulary that the author has decided is scaffolding rather than the book's center. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So this is drawing from Polyvagal theory." (https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/) "the sympathetic is like the gas pedal like provides the energy. The ventral is like the foot brake." (https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/) "the dorsal is like the handbrake which is like the emergency like the fuse blowing up in the system" (https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/) "is my nervous system response appropriate to the situation?" (https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/) "Understand the 3 nervous system modes" (https://www.nsmastery.com/) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, emotion, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/queue/candidates.md`; The vault's candidate list holds Porges, Levine, Ogden, and van der Kolk as one body of work behind the corpus's temporal loop and notes that an attack on that loop's physiology would land on Porges's polyvagal account, 'which has been contested in the peer-reviewed literature'. Miller's model is a popular teaching version of the same theory. |
| Sources | https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/; https://www.nsmastery.com/; https://www.nsmastery.com/literature |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ventral* | In Miller's use, the state of connection and social engagement in which a person feels grounded and safe; he also calls it nervous system capacity. |
| *dorsal* | In Miller's use, a state that is deep rest and digestion when mild and an emergency shutdown when triggered. |
| *sympathetic* | The branch of the autonomic nervous system associated with alertness, activation, and energy. |

#### the unclenching hypothesis (clenching as protection against feeling)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | somatic coaching |
| Originators | Jonny Miller, drawing on the Buddha's account of craving, Edmund Jacobson, Wilhelm Reich, Alexander Lowen, Peter Levine, and Michael Edward Johnson's vasocomputation model |
| Date | essay 'How to Unclench' (mid-2026); earlier forms in his course essays dated 2024 |
| Scale | one person alone, two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Miller's theory holds that most suffering comes not from feelings but from clenching against them. He defines clenching as unconscious tension that narrows the capacity to feel and respond, reads each clench as the body's once-reasonable protection against a feeling that was punished or unsafe, and holds that feeling good is the default state when clenching falls away. Unclenching cannot be forced; in his account it happens when the nervous system receives enough signals of safety, whether alone, with another person, through laughter, heat and cold, music, or, for stubborn clenches, psychedelics with a guide. |
| What is facilitated | In Miller's account, the work lets a person's nervous system learn that it is safe to stop bracing, so that feelings can be felt through and attention, choice, and aliveness widen; the process is the body's own and cannot be forced. A practitioner, partner, or practice supplies conditions of safety (curiosity, relaxation, intimacy, safety, and play) and does not supply the feeling or its resolution. |
| Across scales | Miller applies the same pattern to a single muscle, a person's emotional life, a couple's argument ('I’m clenched, and it’s your fault'), comedy as 'a cultural technology for collective unclenching', and the Buddhist chain from craving to suffering. A protective contraction that persists after its cause is a plausible pattern at each of these scales, but the evidence he offers at each is anecdote, analogy, or speculation, and the physiological mechanism is unestablished even for one body. |
| The evidence | The theory is a practitioner's synthesis, and Miller marks its limits himself: he writes that unclenching is not yet a hard science, that 'there is no established bodily mechanism for storing trauma', and that Johnson's latch model 'is still theoretical'. He cites psychedelic studies in which letting go or surrender during a session predicted later improvement (Russ and colleagues 2019; Irrmischer and colleagues 2025, as he reports them), which this researcher did not check, and he labels the essay's closing sections on luck, inflammation, and bioelectricity as speculation. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Miller speculates that non-dual pointing-out methods create the conditions for the clenches that hold a separate self to open, while insisting that 'Complete physiological relaxation is not a prerequisite for recognizing freedom/realization'. Protection and defense: The theory reads each clench as an adaptive protection with its own 'brilliant logic', to be met with safety rather than overridden, which is close to the book's reading of a fold as protection that has outlived its cause. The container that holds people while they change: Miller holds that forced openings through psychedelics or holotropic breathwork give a glimpse of the unclenched state but that 'without safety, the clench will return once ordinary consciousness resumes'. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Miller's account is physiological and aims at a higher baseline of wellbeing, citing another practitioner's estimate that happiness becomes '30–50 times higher'; the book makes no claim of efficacy and holds its convergences as company rather than evidence. Miller and Joe Hudson are not independent arrivals at the view that suffering is resistance to feeling: Hudson teaches a masterclass in Miller's course, hosted Miller on his podcast, and is cited in Miller's essay, so their agreement counts once. |
| Limits and critiques | Miller recommends psychedelics, including ketamine and MDMA, 'preferably with a skilled guide in a safe space', notes MDMA's legal status and the risk of adulterated supply, and warns that self-exploration can provoke intense emotion, so that people with trauma histories should begin with a trained somatic practitioner. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Feeling a feeling isn’t what makes an experience unbearable. Our suffering comes from clenching against what we feel." (https://howtounclench.com/) "The working definition I have for clenching is: unconsciously holding tension in a way that narrows your capacity to feel and respond." (https://howtounclench.com/) "Complete physiological relaxation is not a prerequisite for recognizing freedom/realization" (https://howtounclench.com/) "But without safety, the clench will return once ordinary consciousness resumes." (https://howtounclench.com/) "Mike’s vascular-latch model is still theoretical" (https://howtounclench.com/) "the emotion itself isn't what's painful, it's your resistance to it that makes it painful" (archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, emotion, trauma healing, spirit ultimate, identity self |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/contraction-not-corruption.md`; The vault's map of the book's theory of suffering opens with the corpus's 'Nothing in you is broken. Something in you is folded' and holds suffering as fullness contracted. Miller's theory arrives at a physiological version of the same shape, names Reich and Lowen as its sources where the abridged edition's somatic citations omit them (notes/the apparatus descends from the settlement the book positions itself against.md), and uses none of the book's Trika vocabulary. His view that suffering is resistance to feeling matches Hudson's, as the vault records it. |
| Sources | https://howtounclench.com/; https://www.nsmastery.com/; archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *clenching* | 'unconsciously holding tension in a way that narrows your capacity to feel and respond'. |
| *unclenched versus relaxed* | Miller's distinction: a person can be relaxed yet clenched, as in depression or freeze, or highly energized yet unclenched, as in creative flow. |
| *latch* | In Johnson's vasocomputation model, which Miller calls theoretical, a long-held clench in which smooth muscle stays contracted and which may store trauma. |
| *the Stoicism trap* | Miller's name for witnessing emotions at arm's length, which he contrasts with moving toward an emotion with care and compassion. |

#### the unclenching loop

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic coaching |
| Originators | Jonny Miller |
| Date | essay 'How to Unclench' (written in mid-2026, by its own account) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people as equals, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person runs each loop on themselves and decides which moves to keep. |
| What happens | Miller's essay 'How to Unclench' reduces his teaching to one instruction, to unclench, and gives a repeatable 'loop' for it: notice a clench, try a move, track what changes, and keep what worked. The first loop takes 60 seconds and uses the hands, peripheral vision, and the jaw; later loops look for subtler tension, including tension in perception and awareness. He holds that unclenching cannot be forced, only given the conditions to happen, and that pushing to unclench is itself a clench. |
| Steps | (1) Make a fist, open it, and notice how that feels. (2) Hold the hands up at the edges of peripheral vision, relax the gaze, and move the hands slowly back until they fade from view. (3) Drop the lower jaw about one-eighth of an inch so the teeth are not touching. (4) Find another tight place, such as the face or the belly, and relax it the same way. (5) Stay with it, track how one feels, and watch for a spontaneous yawn or sigh, which Miller reads as a sign of unclenching. (6) In general form, notice a clench, try a move (soften it, or tighten and then release it), track what changes, and keep what worked and drop what did not. |
| What is facilitated | The loop makes it more likely that a person notices and lets go of tension they were not aware of holding; the letting go is the body's own and, in Miller's account, cannot be forced. The practice supplies places to look and small moves to try, and it does not supply what the released tension was holding back. |
| Across scales | Miller applies the same notice, try, track, and keep sequence to a couple in an argument, to a group in the Relational Dojo, and to the Buddhist account of craving; the evidence he offers for those larger applications is personal practice and analogy. |
| The evidence | No study of the loop was found, and Miller calls unclenching 'not yet a hard science' and the vascular latch model behind it 'still theoretical'. The loop's tighten-and-release move is the method of progressive muscle relaxation, which Miller lists in his lineage under Edmund Jacobson (1920s and 1930s) and recommends as a starting point. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: Miller warns that the urge to unclench everything at once is itself a clench and recommends 'Patience and titration', moving gradually and leaving time to integrate. Remaining the author of one's own change: The loop's rule to keep what worked and drop what did not leaves the person as the judge of each move. Protection and defense: Miller treats each clench as a protective prediction that was reasonable when it formed and is to be met with safety rather than removed by force. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Miller's loop treats bodily tension as the carrier of suffering and aims at releasing it; the book reads contraction both as the form suffering takes and as a productive bounded vantage, and it does not aim at removing contraction as such. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Every loop has the same four steps: notice a clench, try a move (soften it, or tighten and release it), track what changes, then keep what worked and drop what didn’t." (https://howtounclench.com/) "Drop your lower jaw away from your upper jaw by about one-eighth of an inch so the teeth aren't touching." (https://howtounclench.com/) "Unclenching is like falling asleep—you cannot force yourself to do it, but you can create the conditions for it to arise" (https://howtounclench.com/) "Patience and titration work wonders: move gradually, leaving time to integrate and recover." (https://howtounclench.com/) "unclenching is not yet a hard science" (https://howtounclench.com/) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/contraction is a productive locus and not only a privation.md`; The vault holds contraction as both the form suffering takes and a productive bounded vantage, which it records as the book's own revaluation; Miller's clenching is contraction read mainly as the cause of suffering, although he also calls each clench adaptive. |
| Sources | https://howtounclench.com/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *clenching* | Miller's working definition: 'unconsciously holding tension in a way that narrows your capacity to feel and respond'. |
| *loop* | One self-guided experiment: noticing a clench, trying a move, tracking the change, and keeping what worked. |
| *CRISP* | Curiosity, relaxation, intimacy, safety, and playfulness, the five qualities that Miller says help unclenching. |
| *latch* | In the speculative model Miller borrows from Michael Edward Johnson, a clench held so long that smooth muscle stays locked in a contracted state. |

#### the Wim Hof method's endotoxin study, and voluntary cold-water immersion

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | immunology and physiology |
| Originators | Matthijs Kox, Peter Pickkers, and colleagues (Radboud University), testing the method taught by Wim Hof; cold-water review by D. Esperland, L. de Weerd, and J. B. Mercer (the names as Europe PMC indexes them) |
| Date | 2014 (Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences); review 2022 (International Journal of Circumpolar Health) |
| Scale | one organism, one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Kox and colleagues trained 12 healthy volunteers for ten days in meditation, cyclic hyperventilation with breath holds, and cold exposure, then injected them and 12 untrained controls with bacterial endotoxin. In the trained group the breathing raised adrenaline sharply, anti-inflammatory signalling rose faster, and inflammatory markers and flu-like symptoms were lower, which the authors read as voluntary influence over systems thought to be beyond voluntary control. A 2022 review of 104 studies of voluntary cold-water immersion found physiological effects but called the health question a continuing subject of debate. |
| What is facilitated | The training enabled participants to raise their own adrenaline by breathing and so to dampen their innate immune response; the effect was in their bodies, and the training supplied the method. The cold-water literature describes the body's adaptation to repeated cold. |
| The evidence | The endotoxin study had 24 participants, and its lead researchers warned Hof against the health claims he later made from it, calling them scientifically unjustified (according to Wikipedia). The cold-water review found most studies small, often of one sex, and inconsistent in water temperature, and it links the cold shock response to drowning. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The breathing pattern that produced the measured effect can cause a brief loss of consciousness, and the Wikipedia article reports drownings among practitioners who did it in water. Knowing that something happened: The study measured a real physiological change, and its researchers still refused the larger health claims built on it, which separates a verified effect from the story told about it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The method aims at physiological control and resilience; the book aims at recognition and makes no claims about the body's immune or metabolic function. |
| Limits and critiques | The Wikipedia article on Wim Hof reports four drownings of practitioners in 2015 and 2016, a 2022 lawsuit after a teenager's drowning, and warnings about cold therapy from heart foundations. |
| Quoted from its sources | "However, both the autonomic nervous system and innate immune system are regarded as systems that cannot be voluntarily influenced." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1073/pnas.1322174111&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "Subjects in the intervention group were trained for 10 d in meditation (third eye meditation), breathing techniques (i.a., cyclic hyperventilation followed by breath retention), and exposure to cold (i.a., immersions in ice cold water)." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1073/pnas.1322174111&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "His lead researchers, Pickkers and Kox, had previously warned him against making such health claims, stating they were scientifically unjustified." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wim_Hof) "However, without further conclusive studies, the topic will continue to be a subject of debate." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1080/22423982.2022.2111789&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "the cold shock response in itself can have a major impact on the body and is connected to an increased risk of drowning" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9518606/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on Wim Hof or cold exposure; Jonny Miller's section on temperature change and his espresso breath use related practices. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1073/pnas.1322174111&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wim_Hof; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1080/22423982.2022.2111789&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9518606/fullTextXML; https://www.nsmastery.com/literature; https://howtounclench.com/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *cyclic hyperventilation followed by breath retention* | The Wim Hof breathing pattern: rounds of fast deep breaths, then holding the breath. |
| *experimental endotoxemia* | A controlled laboratory model of inflammation produced by injecting a small dose of bacterial endotoxin. |
| *cold shock response* | Reflex gasping, hyperventilation, fast heart rate, and high blood pressure on sudden immersion in cold water. |

#### the window of tolerance

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | interpersonal neurobiology and trauma therapy |
| Originators | Daniel J. Siegel (The Developing Mind, 1999), as later papers credit it |
| Date | 1999 |
| Scale | one organism, two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The window of tolerance is the range of arousal between hyperarousal and hypoarousal within which a person can feel and still think, so that experience can be processed. Helpers use it to pace work: they keep a person inside the range, slow down when the person leaves it, and try to widen the range over time. A review by Corrigan, Fisher, and Nutt applies the model to complex trauma and reads self-harm and substance use as attempts to regulate a nervous system that is easily pushed to extremes. |
| What is facilitated | The model helps a helper, or the person, keep arousal within the range in which the person's own processing can happen; the processing is the person's. The model supplies a limit on pace and no content. |
| Across scales | A 2021 paper applied the window to political leaders and to a group's collective window, and a 2026 paper built a related model of 'windows of openness to interaction' for a transdisciplinary environmental research project; these extensions are analogies, and the model's clinical use is with one person. |
| The evidence | The window is a clinical model rather than a tested hypothesis, and the reviews found describe and apply it rather than test it. It was used in the phase 3 trials of MDMA-assisted therapy for PTSD, where therapists tracked where each participant was within the window, and the framework paper for those trials notes that widening the window may first require highly aversive experiences. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The model makes the person's arousal, not the helper's plan, the signal for when to go on and when to slow down. The container that holds people while they change: The window names the condition a container has to maintain for change to be processed rather than endured. Harm and its conditions: Work that pushes a person above or below the window is the condition under which a method overwhelms rather than helps. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The window describes arousal to be kept in range; the book's arc includes onset, deepening, and a peak, and the MDMA framework's remark that widening the window may require aversive experience shows a tension between keeping a person in range and taking them through an arc. |
| Quoted from its sources | "between parasympathetic hypoarousal and sympathetic hyperarousal, within which affects and emotions can be experienced and processed effectively" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11555657/fullTextXML) "Dysfunctional behaviours such as deliberate self-harm and substance abuse are seen as efforts to regulate an autonomic nervous system which is readily triggered into extreme states by reminders of the original traumatic events." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:20093318&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "However, therapists were also attuned to where the participant was within their window of tolerance" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11571099/fullTextXML) "may require that the participant first venture into experiences that are highly aversive" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11571099/fullTextXML) "The breath return between sequences ensures that activation does not accumulate beyond the participant’s window of tolerance" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC13490133/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | somatic. The model comes from interpersonal neurobiology and is used across somatic and trauma therapies |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, emotion, trauma healing |
| In the survey | the window of tolerance; this entry adds the survey's entry is marked unverified because the originator and date were not confirmed; this entry confirms the credit to Siegel (1999) in a published paper, adds the Corrigan, Fisher, and Nutt review, and records the model's use in the MDMA-assisted therapy trials. |
| In the vault | `ops/queue/declines.md`; The vault declined the abridged edition's summary of the window of tolerance as a restatement of others' research; this entry concerns the model at its sources, not the corpus's summary of it. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC13490133/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11555657/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:20093318&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11571099/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%22window%20of%20tolerance%22%20AND%20%22Siegel%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=25; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_J._Siegel |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *hyperarousal* | Arousal above the window, such as panic or rage, in which a person cannot think clearly. |
| *hypoarousal* | Arousal below the window, such as numbness or shutdown. |
| *widening the window* | Increasing the range of arousal a person can tolerate while still processing experience. |

#### three buckets of self-regulation: top down, bottom up, and outside in

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic coaching |
| Originators | Jonny Miller |
| Date | course week 3 ('Shift Your State'); explained on Good Life Project (2025); 'state before story' in an essay dated 2024-08-07 on the course site |
| Scale | one person alone, two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person chooses and combines the practices and judges which ones work for their own system. |
| What happens | Miller sorts ways of shifting one's state into three groups. Top-down practices change the story, as cognitive reframes, affirmations, and mindfulness do; bottom-up practices change the body, such as a breathing pattern or the focus of the eyes; outside-in practices change the surroundings, such as light, stimulation, or a walk in nature, and include being near someone whose nervous system is grounded. He recommends bottom-up practices for people who are highly activated, and he frames them with a phrase he uses with clients, 'state before story'. |
| Steps | (1) Top down: change the story, for example by reframing anger as a sign of how much one cares about something. (2) Bottom up: change the body's input, for example with a breathing pattern or by relaxing the gaze. (3) Outside in: change the surroundings or the company, for example by going outside or asking someone for support. (4) Combine and stack practices from the three groups and 'treat yourself as like a scientist of your own experience' to learn which combinations work. |
| What is facilitated | The taxonomy helps a person choose a way to change their own state in a given moment; the change happens in the person's body, thoughts, and surroundings. Miller supplies the categories and example practices, and the person makes the choice. |
| Across scales | The outside-in group extends regulation from the person to their surroundings and relationships, and at that point the practice depends on conditions the person may not control; the taxonomy does not say what to do when the surroundings cannot be changed. |
| The evidence | The three groups are a teaching taxonomy and have not been tested as such. Miller's essay claims that bottom-up practices are four times more effective at changing blood chemistry because most nerve fibres run from body to brain; the essay cites no study for the fourfold figure, which is an inference rather than a measured comparison. The direct comparisons that exist are narrower, such as one trial in which a breathing practice improved mood more than mindfulness meditation did (see the entry on cyclic sighing). |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The outside-in group and the course's final week on redesigning one's environment place part of the cause of a state in a person's surroundings, although the changes proposed are ones an individual makes. The container that holds people while they change: Miller counts the presence of a grounded person as 'one of the fastest ways' to settle, which makes company part of regulation. Protection and defense: The phrase 'state before story' treats the mind's explanations as shaped by the body's protective state, so the story is revisited after the state changes. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Miller uses the body's state as a way to change the story; the book uses the body as the test of whether a word or a recognition is true, through the felt shift, which gives the same bodily signal a different role. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I think about there being three main buckets of self-regulation. So the first is top down. The second is bottom up. And the third is outside in." (https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/) "being in the presence of someone else who has a, a grounded nervous system is actually one of the fastest ways to, to downshift that we have" (https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/) "Working with clients, I will often talk about “state before story”—the idea that the stories we tell ourselves and the perspectives we inhabit are a function of our nervous system state." (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/operating-manual-for-your-nervous-system) "practices that leverage our physiology by consciously shifting our respiratory or visual systems—are 4x more effective at altering our blood chemistry" (https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/operating-manual-for-your-nervous-system) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, thought belief, relationship present, nature ecology |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault's note on the five theories of suffering records 'state before story, the body as switchboard' as the corpus's compression attributed to Tony Robbins (walked IX; the atlas uses the same words), and the vault's reading of Robbins found that his own texts put state and representation in 'a cybernetic loop'. The phrase occurs verbatim in Miller's own essay as something he tells clients, and Deb Dana's site states 'Story follows state'; this does not show where the corpus took the phrase from. |
| Sources | https://www.goodlifeproject.com/podcast/nervous-system-regulation-stress-resilience-autonomic-reset/; https://www.nsmastery.com/blog/post/operating-manual-for-your-nervous-system; https://www.nsmastery.com/; https://www.rhythmofregulation.com/polyvagal-theory; archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *top down* | Practices that change thoughts or stories in order to change one's state. |
| *bottom up* | Practices that change the body, such as breathing or gaze, in order to change one's state. |
| *outside in* | Practices that change the environment or the people nearby in order to change one's state. |
| *state before story* | Miller's coaching phrase for the idea that the stories a person tells are shaped by their nervous system state, so the state is addressed first. |

#### three dimensions of interoception: accuracy, sensibility, and awareness

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychophysiology |
| Originators | Sarah N. Garfinkel, Anil K. Seth, Adam B. Barrett, Keisuke Suzuki, and Hugo D. Critchley |
| Date | 2015 (Biological Psychology) |
| Scale | one organism, one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Garfinkel and colleagues separated three measures of how well people sense their own heartbeat: accuracy on objective heartbeat-detection tests, sensibility as self-rated attention to the body, and awareness as how well a person's confidence tracks their accuracy. In 80 people the three were 'distinct and dissociable', and they corresponded only among the people with the highest accuracy. |
| What is facilitated | The finding itself facilitates nothing; it shows that a person's confidence in reading their body is a separate capacity from the reading, which matters for any practice that asks a person to trust bodily signals. |
| Across scales | The distinction between a signal, a person's belief about the signal, and the fit between the two applies to any system that monitors itself, including a group judging its own mood; the research measures it only in individual bodies and mostly through the heartbeat. |
| The evidence | One normative sample of 80 people established the dissociation, and the three-part model is widely used. The heartbeat-counting tasks behind many accuracy scores are contested, because scores are predicted by prior knowledge of one's resting heart rate and by time estimation (Desmedt, Luminet and Corneille 2018, as Jonny Miller's literature page summarizes it; the vault cites Zamariola and colleagues 2018 for the same problem). |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: A person can feel sure that a bodily shift occurred without reading their body accurately, so felt certainty cannot by itself certify a felt shift. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The study measures heartbeat perception in a laboratory; the book's felt shift is a change in a whole bodily sense of a situation, which no heartbeat task captures. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In a normative sample (N=80), all three dimensions were distinct and dissociable." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1016/j.biopsycho.2014.11.004&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "Significant correspondence between dimensions emerged only within the sub-group of individuals with greatest interoceptive accuracy." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1016/j.biopsycho.2014.11.004&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "The heartbeat counting task largely involves non-interoceptive processes." (https://www.nsmastery.com/literature) |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | body, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/attending to the body is not the same as reading it accurately.md`; The vault already holds this finding, quoted at its abstract, with the contested heartbeat task noted. This entry adds that Jonny Miller's course cites the same paper, and the heartbeat-counting critique beside it, while recommending a sensibility questionnaire as an objective benchmark. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1016/j.biopsycho.2014.11.004&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://www.nsmastery.com/literature |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *interoceptive accuracy* | Performance on objective tests of heartbeat detection. |
| *interoceptive sensibility* | A person's self-rated attention to and perception of internal sensations, measured by questionnaire. |
| *interoceptive awareness* | How closely a person's confidence in their bodily judgments matches their actual accuracy. |

#### three-word state check

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | somatic coaching |
| Originators | Nervous System Mastery, in the course site's written guide to episode 76 of The Inner Frontier (Jonny Miller, Ocean Kiani, and Ben Ward); the guide does not say who devised it |
| Date | episode dated 2025-09-16 |
| Scale | one person alone, a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person names their own state in their own words; in a team check-in each person does the same. |
| What happens | The three-word state check is a ten-second practice for use before a meeting, a hard conversation, or a decision. The person feels several parts of the body, names their state in three plain words, asks what that state makes easy or hard, and makes one small shift. The site's guide presents it as a way to keep 'one more degree of choice' rather than to become perfectly regulated. |
| Steps | (1) 'Pause for ten seconds. Feel your feet, seat, jaw, throat, belly, and breath.' (2) Name three words in plain language, such as 'tired, alert, guarded'. (3) 'Notice the implication. Ask: what does this state make easy? What does it make hard?' (4) Choose one tiny shift, such as a longer exhale, standing up, drinking water, softening the belly, or asking for a pause. (5) Continue with the added information; the guide says a person does not need to be perfectly regulated. |
| What is facilitated | The check makes it more likely that a person notices how their state is shaping what they are about to do, and it keeps a small choice open; the noticing and the choice are the person's. |
| Across scales | The same check is used by one person and, according to the guide, by a team at the start of a meeting; at the team scale each person names their state aloud, which adds being heard to the practice. |
| The evidence | No study of this practice was found. Its naming step resembles affect labeling, which has laboratory support for reducing emotional reactivity (see that entry). |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The guide states the goal as becoming 'more trustworthy to yourself through better experiments, rather than outsourcing belief to a teacher, a paper, or a protocol'. Decisions: The check is placed before charged decisions so that the person can see what their state makes easy and hard before choosing. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The check aims at one more degree of choice in ordinary moments; the book's promised event is recognition, which the vault's record describes as arriving rather than as being chosen. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Pause for ten seconds. Feel your feet, seat, jaw, throat, belly, and breath." (https://www.nsmastery.com/podcast/behind-the-scenes-of-nervous-system-mastery) "Notice the implication. Ask: what does this state make easy? What does it make hard?" (https://www.nsmastery.com/podcast/behind-the-scenes-of-nervous-system-mastery) "You do not need to be perfectly regulated. You just need one more degree of choice than you had a moment ago." (https://www.nsmastery.com/podcast/behind-the-scenes-of-nervous-system-mastery) "The goal is to become more trustworthy to yourself through better experiments, rather than outsourcing belief to a teacher, a paper, or a protocol." (https://www.nsmastery.com/podcast/behind-the-scenes-of-nervous-system-mastery) "Scientism is placing the results of science on a pedestal and outsourcing your inner authority to them." (https://www.nsmastery.com/podcast/behind-the-scenes-of-nervous-system-mastery) |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | body, emotion, attention awareness, decision choice |
| In the survey | the check-in; this entry adds the survey's check-in is a group opening round; this entry adds a solo, body-first version with exact steps, a question about what the state makes easy and hard, and a deliberate small shift afterwards. |
| In the vault | `notes/naming aloud is part of what is happening rather than a report about it.md`; The vault holds the claim that naming a present pattern aloud changes it rather than reporting it, with Lieberman's affect-labeling study behind it; the three-word check is a private version of that naming, followed by a deliberate small change. |
| Sources | https://www.nsmastery.com/podcast/behind-the-scenes-of-nervous-system-mastery |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *state check* | A brief naming of one's current bodily and emotional state before acting. |
| *capacity* | In the guide, having enough resource, safety, choice, and recovery to feel emotion without being flooded. |
| *science and scientism* | Miller's distinction, as the guide reports it, between experimenting on one's own experience and 'placing the results of science on a pedestal and outsourcing your inner authority to them'. |

## Part 3. What psychedelic guides do before, during, and after a session

Each entry sits under the earliest phase of the arc it serves; its full list of phases is in its Arc phase row.

### Preparation

#### Accept-Connect-Embody (ACE) model

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | psychedelic therapy (acceptance and commitment therapy) |
| Originators | Rosalind Watts and Jason Luoma |
| Date | 2020 (Journal of Contextual Behavioral Science 15:92-102); used in the Imperial College psilocybin trial reported by Murphy and colleagues (2022) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The therapist's role changes across integration, from non-directive to more structured to more directive about goals. |
| What happens | The model uses the six processes of acceptance and commitment therapy's psychological flexibility model to support patients in accepting challenges (Accept), moving toward positive aspects (Connect), and taking embodied action that internalizes change (Embody). Bathje and colleagues' review describes its integration work in three stages: first the therapist is non-directive, validating the patient and guiding them through unresolved experiences; then the work becomes more structured, with meaning-making and translating insights; then the therapist becomes more directive, helping the patient set goals and identify changes in behavior. |
| Steps | (1) In the first integration stage, the therapist is non-directive, validating the patient and guiding them through unresolved parts of the experience. (2) In the second stage, the work becomes more structured: meaning-making, building awareness, and translating insights. (3) In the third stage, the therapist is more directive, helping the patient set goals and identify changes in behavior. |
| What is facilitated | The patient's move from accepting what arose to acting differently in daily life is supported, with the therapist taking more initiative as integration proceeds. |
| The evidence | The model was developed in one trial and has not been tested against other integration approaches; in that trial the therapeutic alliance predicted outcomes, but no study isolates the model's contribution. I read the model through Bathje and colleagues' review and the trial abstract, not the 2020 paper itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The model changes who leads during integration, from the patient's material in the first stage to the therapist helping set goals in the third. Timing and pacing: It gives integration an order in which acceptance comes before action. Remaining the author of one's own change: Its last stage asks the patient to turn insight into chosen behavior, the point at which the vault's plasticity note warns that advice about using the period after a peak becomes instrumental. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | ACE is a therapy for depression that directs patients toward goals in its last stage; the book's clause that practice expresses realization rather than serving as a means to it pulls against that direction. |
| Limits and critiques | The directive third stage carries the risk that the EMBARK authors attribute to therapy-inclusive models: pressure for outcomes to conform to a predetermined theory of change. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In the first stage, the therapist is non-directive, validating the patient and guiding them through unresolved experiences." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9386447/fullTextXML) "The third stage is more directive, with the therapist helping the patient come up with goals and identifying behavioral change." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9386447/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | preparation, peak, integration |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral. Acceptance and commitment therapy's psychological flexibility model |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship present, body, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the plasticity window reorganizes only what attention is on.md`; The note holds that what follows a peak decides what changes, and warns that advice to use that period is instrumental by construction; ACE is a worked integration method that becomes more directive over time, which is the case the note warns about. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9386447/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20alliance%20and%20rapport%20modulate%20responses%20to%20psilocybin%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Accept* | supporting the patient in experiencing challenges rather than avoiding them |
| *Connect* | moving toward positive aspects of the experience and of life |
| *Embody* | acting in the body and in daily life so that change is internalized |
| *psychological flexibility* | the capacity, in acceptance and commitment therapy, to stay with difficult experience while acting on values |

#### Ayahuasca ceremony with icaros and the dieta

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Amazonian vegetalismo and ayahuasca shamanism |
| Originators | Indigenous and mestizo healers of the upper Amazon, including the Shipibo-Konibo; contemporary retreat centers such as Takiwasi in Peru (ethnography by D. Dupuis) |
| Date | Centuries old; ceremonies for foreign visitors in Iquitos from about 2004; Dupuis's fieldwork 2008 to 2013 |
| Scale | a small group, a society's customs, the more-than-human |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The shaman leads the ceremony and sings the icaros meant to protect and guide the drinkers; in neo-shamanic centers, facilitators also interpret visions for participants. |
| What happens | Shamans lead the drinking of ayahuasca in a rite that usually lasts all night, singing icaros, medicine songs in languages such as Shipibo, which are said to establish a balance of energy and to protect and guide the drinker; icaros are passed down through lineages or are said to be taught by plant spirits during a dieta, a period of restricted diet. Before a ceremony participants are told to avoid spicy food, red meat, and sex, and the ceremony usually brings purging, which is believed to release built-up emotions and negative energy. At the Takiwasi center in Peru, Dupuis found that facilitators and fellow participants gradually taught visitors to read their visions as communications from supernatural beings consistent with the center's cosmology, often as demonic possession, and he called this a socialization of hallucinations. |
| Steps | (1) Before the ceremony, participants avoid spicy food, red meat, and sex. (2) At night, the shaman leads the drinking of ayahuasca. (3) Through the night, the shaman sings icaros to protect and guide the drinkers. (4) Participants purge, which is understood as releasing built-up emotions and negative energy. (5) In neo-shamanic retreats, discussions after the session interpret participants' visions within the center's cosmology. |
| What is facilitated | The drinker's passage through visions and purging is guided by the shaman's songs; in the neo-shamanic case, the meaning of the visions is supplied by the center. |
| Across scales | The ceremony places a person inside a group, a healer, and relations with plants and spirits; the Takiwasi case shows the group level shaping individual experience so strongly that the content of visions follows the institution's cosmology. |
| The evidence | The descriptions come from Wikipedia pages (the icaro page carries flags for original research and missing citations) and from one ethnography reported by Timmermann and colleagues (2022); Wikipedia notes that the dietary taboos seem specific to the culture around Iquitos, a center of ayahuasca tourism. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The Takiwasi case documents guides and peers teaching participants what their visions mean, which is the socialization of experience that non-directive clinical models try to avoid. The container that holds people while they change: Diet, abstinence, a night-long rite, and the shaman's songs together form a structured setting from preparation through the ceremony. Harm and its conditions: Wikipedia reports people who pose as shamans to lure tourists, and says that shamans and experienced users advise against drinking without well-trained shamans present. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The ceremony assumes a cosmology of spirits and sorcery that interprets suffering; the book offers no such cosmology and leaves interpretation to the reader. |
| Limits and critiques | Ayahuasca has major contraindications because it inhibits the enzyme monoamine oxidase, according to Wikipedia. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The shamans lead the ceremonial consumption of the ayahuasca beverage" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayahuasca) "in a rite that typically takes place over the entire night" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayahuasca) "as a way to establish a "balance of energy" during the ritual to help protect and guide the user during their experience" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayahuasca) "Prior to the ceremony, participants are instructed to abstain from spicy foods, red meat and sex." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayahuasca) "advise against consuming ayahuasca when not in the presence of one or several well-trained shamans" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayahuasca) "who masquerade as real shamans and who entice tourists to drink ayahuasca in their presence" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayahuasca) "icaros may come to a shaman during a ceremony, be passed down from previous lineages of healers, or come to a shaman during a 'dieta' where plant spirits are believed to teach icaros to the shaman directly" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icaro) "it may take many years to learn certain icaros, and experienced shamans may be able to recite hundreds of them" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icaro) "Cultural background and social interactions result here in a “socialisation of hallucinations”" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) "the psychological suffering of participants is frequently understood as resulting from demonic influence or possession" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | body, spirit ultimate, nature ecology, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `provenance/high-energy states and system decoupling.md`; The unread synthesis mentions the ayahuasca dieta as Indigenous preparation and integration; no vault note covers the ceremony, and the synthesis does not mention the icaros or the socialization of visions. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayahuasca; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icaro; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *icaro* | a medicine song sung or whistled by the healer during the ceremony |
| *dieta* | a period of dietary restriction during which plant spirits are believed to teach icaros |
| *purging* | vomiting or diarrhea during the ceremony, understood as a release |
| *brujo* | a person who poses as a shaman, in the Wikipedia account, to take advantage of tourists |
| *socialisation of hallucinations* | Dupuis's term for a group teaching members what their visions mean |

#### Blinding and expectancy confounds in psychedelic randomized controlled trials

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | clinical trial methodology |
| Originators | S. D. Muthukumaraswamy, A. Forsyth, T. Lumley |
| Date | 2021 (Expert Review of Clinical Pharmacology) |
| Scale | an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The authors reviewed psychedelic randomized controlled trials published from 1990 to 2020 and found that they generally reported neither participants' expectations before the trial nor whether the blinding worked. Because a psychedelic's effects are obvious, participants usually know whether they received it, and those who expect benefit may report it. The authors argue that effect sizes are therefore likely overestimated, and they recommend measuring expectancy and unblinding routinely and paying careful attention to trial design and to the instructions given to participants. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated; the critique asks how much of the change attributed to a guided psychedelic session comes from what participants and guides expect. |
| Across scales | Expectancy effects operate in any intervention whose recipients know what they received, from one therapy session to a public program; they are hardest to remove where the intervention's effects are unmistakable. |
| The evidence | A systematic review of Medline, PsycInfo, and EMBASE with an argument about bias; it does not measure how large the bias is, and a published letter (Schenberg 2021, which I did not read) disputed it. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The critique says reported change cannot be credited to the drug when participants can tell they received it and expect benefit. Who decides the content: It names the instructions given to participants as a source of the effect, which makes what the guide says beforehand part of what is being measured. Recognition: A book that promises recognition shapes its reader's expectations in the same way, which the vault already admits as a core risk while holding that change produced by expectancy is not thereby unreal. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The critique concerns proving a drug's effect to regulators; the book claims no efficacy, so the critique bears on how the book describes what happens to readers rather than on any trial. |
| Limits and critiques | The critique was answered by at least one published letter asking who is actually blind in psychedelic research. |
| Quoted from its sources | "currently reported psychedelic RCTs have generally not reported pre-trial expectancy, nor the success of blinding procedures" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Blinding%20and%20expectancy%20confounds%20in%20psychedelic%20randomized%20controlled%20trials%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "we argue that treatment effect sizes in psychedelic RCTs are likely over-estimated due to de-blinding of participants and high levels of response expectancy" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Blinding%20and%20expectancy%20confounds%20in%20psychedelic%20randomized%20controlled%20trials%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "Careful attention should be paid to clinical trial design and the instructions given to participants" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Blinding%20and%20expectancy%20confounds%20in%20psychedelic%20randomized%20controlled%20trials%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Arc phase | preparation, integration |
| Mental model | other. Clinical trial methodology |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, decision choice |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | notes/response expectancy is a two-sided coin, the mechanism of the manufactured artifact and of genuine change alike.md; The note records the author's decision to admit expectancy as a core risk without letting it become self-doubt; this critique is the psychedelic field's own form of the same admission, applied to its trials. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Blinding%20and%20expectancy%20confounds%20in%20psychedelic%20randomized%20controlled%20trials%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *de-blinding* | participants coming to know which treatment they received |
| *response expectancy* | a person's expectation of how they will respond, which can itself produce the response |

#### Controlled use through social sanctions and rituals (drug, set, and setting)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychiatry and sociology of drug use |
| Originators | Norman Zinberg, with R. C. Jacobson and W. M. Harding |
| Date | 1975 (Social sanctions and rituals as a basis for drug abuse prevention, American Journal of Drug and Alcohol Abuse); 1984 (Drug, Set, and Setting: The Basis for Controlled Intoxicant Use, Yale University Press) |
| Scale | a small group, a society's customs, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. No live facilitator is required; the using group's own rules about when, where, and with whom to use do the work. |
| What it holds | Zinberg's group studied people who used marijuana, psychedelics, and opiates over long periods without becoming dependent. They reported that these moderate patterns were supported mainly by social using situations whose shared sanctions and rituals permitted use while condemning abuse, and they compared this with how the wider culture manages alcohol. His 1984 book added the drug itself to set and setting as a third variable, and his Wikipedia biography reports that he studied recreational heroin users over ten years. |
| What is facilitated | The users' own ability to keep a drug in a limited place in their lives is supported by the group's shared rules, not by any helper; no one supplies insight or content. |
| Across scales | The 1975 paper compares the rituals of small using groups with a society's customs around alcohol, so the finding runs from a small group to a culture. It weakens where a person uses alone, since the rituals it describes are social. |
| The evidence | The evidence is qualitative interviews and case histories of users recruited outside treatment, reported in 1975 as preliminary findings and in the 1984 book; Zinberg's Wikipedia page carries several citation-needed flags. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Zinberg places the control of drug use in the group's sanctions and rituals rather than in individual character, which is the structural side of this question. The container that holds people while they change: The finding says a shared set of rules around an intense experience can keep people safe without a professional present. Participation rather than watching: The rituals work only for people who belong to the group that keeps them. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Zinberg studied repeated use of intoxicants, not personal change or recognition, and his controlling variable is a community's norms, which a book read alone cannot supply. |
| Limits and critiques | The studies recruited people who were already succeeding at controlled use, so they describe a selected group. |
| Quoted from its sources | "these patterns are primarily supported by the development of social drug-using situations in which sanctions and rituals permit use while condemning abuse" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%22social%20sanctions%22%20AND%20rituals%20AND%20Zinberg&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=5) "Zinberg studied recreational heroin users over a ten-year period" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Zinberg) "He had a remarkable impact on our understanding that drug effects are not simply a consequence of biochemistry." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Zinberg) |
| Arc phase | preparation, integration |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological. Zinberg was a psychiatrist and psychoanalyst; the model is chosen because his finding places control in a group's rituals |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | group process, decision choice, body |
| In the survey | Set and setting (Leary, Zinberg), a row in the survey table of vault holdings; this entry adds a primary 1975 source for the rituals-and-sanctions finding, which the survey row and the vault note state from secondary knowledge. |
| In the vault | `notes/set and setting.md`; The note states Zinberg's rituals-and-sanctions finding from secondary knowledge and asks whether it is usable or an objection to a book for a solitary reader; reading-queue entry 15 in ops/reading queue.md holds the unread book. This entry supplies the 1975 abstract that states the finding in the authors' words. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%22social%20sanctions%22%20AND%20rituals%20AND%20Zinberg&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=5; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Zinberg; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *controlled use* | moderate, long-term use of an intoxicant without abuse |
| *social sanctions* | a group's shared rules and disapprovals that limit how a drug is used |
| *rituals* | the customary ways a group uses a drug, including when, where, and with whom |
| *drug, set, and setting* | Zinberg's three variables: the drug's pharmacology, the user's mindset, and the social setting |

#### EMBARK model of psychedelic-assisted psychotherapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | psychedelic therapy |
| Originators | W. Brennan and A. B. Belser; training program developed by Cybin, Inc. |
| Date | 2022 (Frontiers in Psychology); training program described in 2025 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Therapists stay undecided about which domain will matter and follow the participant's unfolding process, while holding specified tasks for each domain. |
| What happens | The authors sort existing psychedelic therapy models into basic support models, which leave meaning-making to the participant and may rely on an untested assumption of inner healing intelligence, and models that add evidence-based therapies, which add structure but can narrow what counts as benefit and press outcomes to fit a predetermined theory of change. EMBARK takes a course between them with six parallel domains through which benefit may come (Existential-spiritual, Mindfulness, Body-aware, Affective-cognitive, Relational, and Keeping momentum), four care cornerstones (trauma-informed, culturally competent, ethically rigorous, and collective care), and three phases (preparation, medicine sessions, integration). A medicine session begins with a collaborative ritual, a check-in about intentions, and a brief body-awareness practice, and the collective care cornerstone trains therapists to attend to structural causes of suffering. |
| Steps | (1) Train therapists to competence in all six domains before treatment begins. (2) In preparation, stay undecided about which domain will matter and prepare the ground for any of them. (3) Open each medicine session with a collaborative ritual, a check-in about intentions, and a brief somatic awareness practice. (4) Once the medicine acts, respond to whichever domains the participant's experience makes salient. (5) In integration, use the tasks and goals specific to the domains that arose. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's benefit through whichever domain their experience makes salient; the therapists prepare for several possible sources of benefit instead of directing the participant toward the outcome one theory predicts. |
| Across scales | The collective care cornerstone attributes part of the cause of suffering to social structures rather than to the person, and assigns part of the responsibility to the organization that employs the therapist rather than to the therapist alone. |
| The evidence | A model paper; the 2025 training paper reports its use in two completed and three ongoing Cybin trials, but I found no published study comparing EMBARK with other models. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The model's comparison table names the trade-off directly: basic support models protect participant autonomy and freedom in meaning-making, while structured models risk invalidating participants' experiences to fit a theory. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The collective care cornerstone states that the causes of participants' struggles are not always located in their personal or biological conditions. Harm and its conditions: The ethically rigorous care cornerstone addresses boundary transgressions, touch, power differences, and suggestibility. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | EMBARK serves company-run trials for diagnosed conditions; the book is not a treatment and has no therapist who could attend to six domains as they arise. |
| Limits and critiques | The model was built for one company's trials, and the sources I read report no independent evaluation of it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "They form an acronym that gives the approach its name: Existential-spiritual, Mindfulness, Body-aware, Affective-cognitive, Relational, and Keeping momentum." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "the selection process is guided by the natural unfolding of the participant’s therapeutic process" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "a collaborative ritual (E), a check-in about intentions (K), and a brief somatic awareness practice (B)" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "Protective of participant autonomy" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "Greater participant freedom in meaning-making" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "Pressure to conform outcomes to predetermined theory of change" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "Potential invalidation of participant treatment experiences" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "The underlying causes of the struggles and symptoms that bring PAP participants into treatment are not always entirely locatable in the personal and biological conditions of their lives." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "The EMBARK training program has been used in two successfully completed clinical trials and three ongoing trials."
