(https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22EMBARK%22%20AND%20psychedelic&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | other. An eclectic clinical model that draws on several evidence-based therapies across six domains |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness, body, emotion, relationship present, justice power |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/no single locus or framework may become sovereign over the reality it describes.md`; The note (nascent, meaning stated but not yet substantiated by a reading) holds that a framework which interprets all others has moved from facilitator to sovereign; EMBARK is a clinical model built on the same worry, refusing a single theory of change. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22EMBARK%22%20AND%20psychedelic&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *basic support models* | models that support the drug experience with minimal therapist intervention |
| *EBT-inclusive models* | models that add elements of established evidence-based therapies |
| *care cornerstones* | EMBARK's four ethical commitments: trauma-informed, culturally competent, ethically rigorous, and collective care |
| *collective care* | attention to social and structural causes of suffering, and to the employing organization's responsibility for them |

#### Human hallucinogen research: guidelines for safety

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | psychedelic clinical research |
| Originators | Matthew Johnson, William Richards, Roland Griffiths (Johns Hopkins University) |
| Date | 2008 (Journal of Psychopharmacology) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, three people, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The monitors screen, prepare, and support; the volunteer's experience supplies the content. |
| What happens | The guidelines set out how to give classic psychedelics to research volunteers while limiting psychological risk. They name the most likely risk as overwhelming distress during the drug's action, which can lead to dangerous behavior such as leaving the study site, and a rarer risk of prolonged psychosis. The safeguards are excluding people with a personal or family history of psychotic or other severe psychiatric disorders, building trust and rapport between the session monitors and the volunteer beforehand, careful preparation, a safe physical environment, and support from at least two monitors during the session, followed by questions about lasting perceptual disturbances. |
| Steps | (1) Exclude volunteers with a personal or family history of psychotic disorders or other severe psychiatric disorders. (2) Before the session, build trust and rapport between the session monitors and the volunteer, and prepare the volunteer carefully. (3) Hold the session in a safe physical environment, with at least two monitors present to give interpersonal support. (4) In follow-up contact, ask about hallucinogen persisting perception disorder, a rare lasting disturbance of perception. |
| What is facilitated | The volunteer's passage through an intense drug state without harm is made more likely; the monitors provide safety and company and do not supply the experience's content. |
| Across scales | Screening, preparation, a safe room, and a second person present recur as safety conditions from a single session to a research program; the guidelines turn them from a guide's choices into an institution's requirements. |
| The evidence | The guidelines are an expert consensus paper drawing on the group's research and earlier literature, not a trial comparing safeguards; they report that persisting adverse reactions are rare when research follows them. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The paper lists the conditions (screening, rapport, preparation, a safe room, two monitors) that the research field treats as necessary before asking anyone to go through an intense state. Harm and its conditions: It names acute distress and rare prolonged psychosis as the risks and ties their prevention to exclusion and support rather than to the drug. Being seen and recognized by another: Rapport with the monitors is built before the session because the volunteer will be vulnerable in front of them. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The guidelines protect screened research volunteers under a measured dose; the book has no dose to control, no screening, and no monitor. |
| Limits and critiques | The safety record comes from screened volunteers; people with a family history of psychosis are excluded, and a book cannot exclude anyone. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The most likely risk is overwhelming distress during drug action ('bad trip'), which could lead to potentially dangerous behaviour such as leaving the study site." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Human%20hallucinogen%20research%3A%20guidelines%20for%20safety%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "establishing trust and rapport between session monitors and volunteer before the session, careful volunteer preparation, a safe physical session environment and interpersonal support from at least two study monitors during the session" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Human%20hallucinogen%20research%3A%20guidelines%20for%20safety%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "Persisting adverse reactions are rare when research is conducted along these guidelines." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Human%20hallucinogen%20research%3A%20guidelines%20for%20safety%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | other. Clinical research safety practice |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, body, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/a framework for what is happening is itself a protective factor.md`; The note holds, from an unread synthesis, that lacking a framework for an intense state predicts a frightening experience; the guidelines are a primary source for the preparation and support that synthesis summarizes, though the abstract I read does not use the word framework. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Human%20hallucinogen%20research%3A%20guidelines%20for%20safety%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *session monitor* | a researcher who stays with the volunteer during the drug session to give support |
| *bad trip* | overwhelming distress during the drug's action |
| *hallucinogen persisting perception disorder* | a rare condition in which perceptual changes continue after the drug has worn off |

#### Inner healing intelligence

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | psychedelic therapy |
| Originators | Stanislav and Christina Grof's Holotropic Breathwork; stated as a core concept of the MAPS MDMA-assisted therapy manual (Michael Mithoefer and colleagues); also in the Zendo Project's principles of psychedelic care |
| Date | MAPS manual from 2005 (version 1) to 2015 (version 7); Holotropic Breathwork from the 1970s and 1980s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one organism |
| Who decides | not applicable. The principle states where healing comes from; it tells the guide to follow rather than lead. |
| What it holds | The MAPS manual uses 'inner healing intelligence' for the participant's innate ability to heal and grow, and explains it with a wound: a doctor removes obstacles and brings the edges together, but does not direct or cause the healing. The therapists' job is therefore to support the participant's own process rather than lead it, and to help the participant avoid mistaking the therapists for the source of the healing. Holotropic Breathwork and the Zendo Project use the same term. The EMBARK authors (Brennan and Belser 2022) note that the principle has not been tested and may let therapists introduce their own beliefs about how psychedelics heal. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's own healing process is what is supported; the therapists remove obstacles and arrange conditions but do not claim to cause the healing. |
| Across scales | The manual grounds the principle at the scale of the body (a wound heals itself) and extends it to the mind; the comparison stops holding for injuries that do not heal without intervention, and the principle gives no test for telling which kind of case a person is in. |
| The evidence | The principle is clinical wisdom, not a tested mechanism; Brennan and Belser write that it has yet to be subjected to empirical scrutiny. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The principle places the source of change in the participant and tells the helper to guard against being mistaken for that source. Recognition: If healing comes from a capacity the person already has, the helper's work is to remove obstacles to it, which is close to the book's account of change as uncovering what was already there. Who decides the content: The EMBARK critique shows that the same principle can leave interpretation to the participant or can let the therapist's own beliefs enter the session as if they were the participant's inner guidance. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The MAPS principle serves the treatment of post-traumatic stress with a drug in a clinic; the book offers recognition to readers who are not patients, and recognition concerns what never left rather than a healing process. |
| Limits and critiques | An untestable claim that the participant's process always knows best can excuse a guide's inaction in a real emergency or cover the guide's own interpretations; Brennan and Belser raise the second risk. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Inner healing intelligence is a concept used throughout this manual to help put the participant in touch with their innate ability to heal and grow." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "but the doctor does not direct or cause the healing that ensues" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "help the participant avoid the misconception that the therapists are the source, rather than the facilitators, of her/his therapeutic experience" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "has yet to be subjected to empirical scrutiny and may represent another avenue for the introduction of therapists’ personal beliefs about treatment" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML) "Cultivating presence while letting go of an agenda honors the individual’s inner healing intelligence." (https://zendoproject.org/about/) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | transpersonal |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | trauma healing, identity self, body |
| In the survey | Holotropic Breathwork (breather and sitter pairs); this entry adds the MAPS manual's definition and wound analogy, its warning against the therapist being taken for the source, and the EMBARK critique. |
| In the vault | `notes/the book can only be auxiliary to a sovereign reader.md`; The note holds that the book's honest maximum is to be an instrument in a life that remains the reader's own; the vault mentions inner healing intelligence only in the unread high-energy synthesis in provenance/, not in any note. |
| Sources | https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9201428/fullTextXML; https://zendoproject.org/about/; https://www.holotropic.com/holotropic-breathwork/about-holotropic-breathwork/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *inner healing intelligence* | the participant's own capacity to move toward healing, which the guide supports rather than directs |
| *empathic presence* | the manual's name for non-judgmental listening that acknowledges suffering and validates feeling |

#### MDMA-assisted psychotherapy (the MAPS treatment method)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | psychedelic therapy (MDMA-assisted therapy for post-traumatic stress) |
| Originators | Michael Mithoefer, with Annie Mithoefer, Lisa Jerome, June Ruse, Rick Doblin, Elizabeth Gibson, and Marcela Ot'alora G.; sponsored by the Multidisciplinary Association for Psychedelic Studies (MAPS) |
| Date | 2005 (manual version 1) to 2015 (version 7) |
| Scale | three people, two people, one helping, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The therapists shape the process across the series; the participant's process supplies the content. |
| What happens | The method runs as a series: preparatory sessions without the drug, several MDMA sessions of six to eight hours, and integrative sessions after each. At the start of a drug session the therapists review what may happen and reassure the participant; in one sample exchange, the therapist tells a participant who fears the unknown that 'nothing is going to come up that is not already there.' The participant stays in the treatment area until the therapists judge the effects have subsided, which in many protocols includes an overnight stay, and is advised to spend quiet time alone journaling and to be careful about whom they tell while emotionally open. Integration helps the participant bring the experience into a new relationship with self, others, and their traumatic history, and the manual closes with a section on the therapists' own self-care. |
| Steps | (1) Hold preparatory sessions without the drug to build the relationship, explain the approach, and address concerns. (2) At the start of each drug session, review the approach and the range of possible experiences, and reassure the participant. (3) During the session, provide eyeshades, headphones, and music, stay present, and follow the participant's process with non-directive support. (4) After the session, keep the participant in the treatment area (often overnight) until the therapists judge that the effects have subsided and the participant is stable. (5) Hold integrative sessions to continue emotional processing and to help the participant apply what they gained to daily life. (6) The therapists debrief with each other and practice their own self-care. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's processing of traumatic memory is made more bearable by the drug and by the therapists' presence; the therapists support the participant's own process and do not direct its content. |
| Across scales | The sequence of preparation, an intense session, and integration recurs at the scale of one ceremony night and of a residential retreat; the MAPS version spreads it across weeks and makes it institutional, with adherence checked on video. |
| The evidence | Wikipedia reports phase 3 remission rates of 67 to 71 percent with MDMA-assisted therapy against 32 to 48 percent with placebo-assisted therapy; the FDA nevertheless declined approval in 2024, citing functional unblinding and other concerns, and three papers were retracted over ethics violations (both entered separately). |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The method spaces intense sessions weeks apart with integration between them, so change is paced across a series rather than a single peak. What care costs the one who gives it: The manual names vicarious traumatization as a risk to the therapists and prescribes debriefing, their own therapy, and body-based self-care. The container that holds people while they change: The overnight stay and the rule to remain until stable extend the support past the drug's effects. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The method treats a diagnosed condition with a drug under medical supervision; the book addresses readers who are not patients and offers recognition rather than treatment. |
| Limits and critiques | The method's record was damaged by misconduct at one trial site in 2015 and by the 2024 regulatory rejection. |
| Quoted from its sources | "nothing is going to come up that is not already there" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "The participant must agree that she/he will remain within the treatment area until completion of each session" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "Having a period of quiet time alone after the MDMA-assisted session for journaling or introspection." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "participants should be cautioned to use careful judgment about with whom they want to share this sensitive information" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "The therapists help the participant weave all aspects of the therapeutic experience into a new relationship with self, with others, and with her/his traumatic history." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "Therapists should engage in some form of regular self-care in order to avoid vicarious traumatization resulting from interacting with participants’ trauma." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "rates of 67 to 71% with MDMA-assisted psychotherapy versus 32 to 48% with placebo-assisted therapy" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | transpersonal. The manual draws on Grof's work and on trauma therapies it names, including Internal Family Systems, Sensorimotor Psychotherapy, and Hakomi |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | trauma healing, memory past, emotion, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `provenance/high-energy states and system decoupling.md`; The unread synthesis mentions the MAPS protocol's touch, its therapists' somatic training, and integration over weeks to months; no vault note describes the method. |
| Sources | https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *preparatory session* | a meeting without the drug before the MDMA sessions |
| *experimental session* | an MDMA-assisted session of six to eight hours |
| *integrative session* | a meeting after a drug session to continue processing and apply what was gained |
| *vicarious traumatization* | harm to the therapist from repeated contact with participants' trauma |

#### Native American Church peyote meeting (prayer service)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Native American religion (Native American Church) |
| Originators | The Native American Church (Peyotism), which arose in Oklahoma Territory in the late nineteenth century; its Half-Moon and Cross-fire ways |
| Date | Late nineteenth century (Oklahoma Territory, 1890 to 1907); the Half-Moon way introduced in 1914 or 1924 |
| Scale | a small group, a community or public, a society's customs |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The Roadman conducts a fixed order of service while participants pray and sing in turn; in the Cross-fire way, Bible readings and sermons add directed content. |
| What happens | A family requests a service to cure illness or to mark an occasion such as a birthday, a funeral, or a graduation; it runs from sundown to sunrise, usually in a tipi, and is conducted by an elder called the Roadman. A Fireman keeps the fire burning all night, and the Roadman uses a prayer staff, a gourd rattle, a small drum, cedar, and an eagle feather; singing passes around the circle, each singer singing four songs before passing the staff, gourd, and drum to the next relative. Early in the morning a woman of the Roadman's family brings four sacramental foods (water, sweet meat, corn mush, and berries); a breakfast follows after sunrise, then a community feast, and the sponsoring family gives gifts to the Roadman and his helpers. |
| Steps | (1) A family requests a service for a purpose such as healing, a birthday, or a memorial. (2) At sundown the participants enter the tipi; the Roadman conducts the service and the Fireman tends the fire through the night. (3) Peyote is taken as a sacrament, and prayer, singing, and drumming continue through the night; each singer sings four songs and then passes the staff, gourd, and drum to the next relative. (4) Between 4:30 and 5:00 in the morning, a woman of the Roadman's family brings the four sacramental foods. (5) After sunrise a breakfast is served and the service closes; the community shares a feast, and the sponsoring family gives gifts to the Roadman and his helpers. |
| What is facilitated | The participants' prayer, and the healing or marking of a family's occasion, supported by a fixed night-long order and by the community present; the Roadman leads the order but each person prays and sings. |
| Across scales | The meeting keeps an intense experience inside a small congregation's customs, which is Zinberg's finding about rituals at the scale of a religion; it depends on membership in a community that most readers of a book do not share. |
| The evidence | The account comes from Wikipedia's summary of anthropological sources such as Omer Stewart's Peyote Religion (1987); practice varies by tribe and by way, and the page reports no health outcomes. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: Every participant sings in turn and stays awake through the night, so no one attends as a spectator. The container that holds people while they change: A fixed order from sundown to sunrise, a tended fire, and roles for several people give the experience its structure. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The Wikipedia summary says the plant is meant to heal social, personal, and communal problems, while disease is understood as an imbalance in the individual. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The meeting is a religious service within a community that shares a cosmology; the book is read by individuals who share no rite, and it is not a religion. |
| Limits and critiques | Not every member experiences hallucinogenic effects, practice differs between tribes and between the two ways, and no single description fits every meeting. |
| Quoted from its sources | "services must be conducted by a priest, pastor, or elder, known as the Roadman" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church) "The Roadman is assisted by a Fireman, who cares for the holy fireplace, ensuring it burns consistently all night." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church) "The singer sings four songs, concludes his set, and passes the staff, gourd, and drum to the next relative to sing." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church) "Church services are not regular Sunday occurrences but are conducted upon special request by a family" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church) "The plant is meant to heal or fix social, personal, and communal problems." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church) "However, not every member experiences hallucinogenic effects during peyote rituals." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, body, relationship present, group process |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/set and setting.md`; The note records, from secondary knowledge, Zinberg's finding that rituals and social sanctions control drug use and asks whether it is an objection to a book for a solitary reader; the peyote meeting is a documented instance of such rituals, which the vault has not met. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Roadman* | the elder who conducts the service |
| *Fireman* | the helper who keeps the ceremonial fire burning through the night |
| *Half-Moon way* | the tradition with a half-moon sand altar, no Bible, and a staff passed around during singing |
| *Cross-fire way* | the tradition with a Bible on the altar, baptism, and a minister |
| *sitting up* | staying awake all night as part of the service |

#### Nurturing touch, focused bodywork, and supportive touch

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | psychedelic therapy and its ethics |
| Originators | MAPS treatment manual (Mithoefer and colleagues, drawing on Stanislav Grof); Oregon Health Authority rule OAR 333-333-5120; critics Neşe Devenot, Emma Tumilty, Meaghan Buisson, Sarah McNamee, David Nickles, and Lily Kay Ross; participant interviews by Ham and colleagues |
| Date | 2015 (MAPS manual version 7); 2022 (the precautionary critique; Oregon rules adopted December 2022, since amended); 2026 (participant interview study) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Touch is offered only at the participant's request or with prior agreement; it gives comfort or resistance, not interpretation. |
| What happens | The MAPS manual separates nurturing touch, which comforts, from focused bodywork, in which a therapist gives resistance for the participant to push against in order to release tension. It holds that touch with sexual connotations or touch driven by the therapist's needs has no place in therapy, and that withholding nurturing touch when it is indicated may be felt as abuse by neglect. Oregon allows supportive touch only when the client asks and has given written consent beforehand, limited to hugs or hands on the client's hands, feet, or shoulders. Devenot and colleagues argue for a precautionary approach instead, because psychedelics raise suggestibility and impair memory, and because heavy reliance on touch can reduce work that builds a person's own ability to soothe themselves. |
| Steps | (1) In preparation, discuss whether the participant wants touch during the session and which kinds, and record consent (in Oregon, in writing). (2) During the session, offer touch only when the participant asks for it or has agreed to it, and take cues from the participant's experience. (3) Limit touch to the agreed forms (in Oregon: hugs, or hands on the client's hands, feet, or shoulders). (4) Never use touch with sexual connotations or touch that serves the guide's own needs. (5) Avoid focused bodywork early in the session, and help the participant see the guides as facilitators rather than as the source of healing. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's regulation of distress, or with bodywork the release of held tension, is supported through physical contact; the guide supplies comfort or resistance, and the participant decides whether touch happens. |
| Across scales | Consent-based touch as comfort appears in any helping pair; at the scale of a state it becomes a written rule set, as in Oregon, after documented cases showed individual judgment failing. |
| The evidence | Support for touch rests on practitioner experience and interview studies; a 2026 study of 18 trial participants found most valued having touch available and tied its acceptability to the therapeutic relationship and consent, while some found it distracting. Devenot and colleagues say the field's justifications are mostly opinions of early researchers repeated until they were accepted as fact. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: In the documented MAPS case, the participant's complaint alleged cuddling and spooning during drug sessions, and the critics argue that undefined boundaries make predatory behavior easier. Remaining the author of one's own change: The critics warn that reliance on touch can weaken a person's own capacity to soothe themselves, and the MAPS manual warns that bodywork can suggest that healing comes from outside the person. Who decides the content: Both the manual and Oregon's rules make the participant's request or prior consent the condition for any touch. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book cannot touch its reader, so this area of comfort and risk is absent; what applies is the critics' warning that comfort which replaces a person's own capacity can weaken it. |
| Limits and critiques | Consent given in an altered state is disputed: the critics argue that the idea of an enhanced capacity to consent in altered states has no support in evidence or in legal definitions of consent. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Any touch that has sexual connotations or is driven by the therapist’s needs, rather than the participant’s, has no place in therapy and can be counter-therapeutic or even abusive." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "may even be perceived by the participant as abuse by neglect" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "help the participant avoid the misconception that the therapists are the source, rather than the facilitators, of her/his therapeutic experience" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "Supportive touch is limited to hugs or placing hands on a client's hands, feet or shoulders." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5120) "To ensure clear and safe boundaries, therapists should prioritize non-touch methods of comfort." (https://petrieflom.law.harvard.edu/2022/03/09/precautionary-approach-touch-in-psychedelic-assisted-therapy/) "an overreliance on touch to the detriment of therapeutic interventions that help build clients’ self-soothing capacities" (https://petrieflom.law.harvard.edu/2022/03/09/precautionary-approach-touch-in-psychedelic-assisted-therapy/) "the notion of “enhanced capacity” in altered states is neither supported by evidence nor by legal definitions of consent" (https://petrieflom.law.harvard.edu/2022/03/09/precautionary-approach-touch-in-psychedelic-assisted-therapy/) "Acceptability was consistently linked to the quality of the therapeutic relationship and robust consent processes." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=challenging%20psychedelic%20experiences%20AND%20%28surrender%20OR%20%22let%20go%22%29%20AND%20helpful%20AND%20strategies&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "This included inappropriate physical contact such as cuddling and spooning during psychotherapy sessions in which she was under the influence of MDMA" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meaghan_Buisson) |
| Arc phase | preparation, deepening, peak, descent |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | body, trauma healing, relationship present, justice power |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | notes/the more powerful the facilitator becomes, the more important sovereignty becomes.md; The note (nascent, meaning stated but not yet substantiated by a reading) states that a facilitator's growing skill raises the danger of taking the participant's authorship; touch during altered states is the most physical form of that danger, which the vault has not examined. |
| Sources | https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5120; https://petrieflom.law.harvard.edu/2022/03/09/precautionary-approach-touch-in-psychedelic-assisted-therapy/; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=challenging%20psychedelic%20experiences%20AND%20%28surrender%20OR%20%22let%20go%22%29%20AND%20helpful%20AND%20strategies&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meaghan_Buisson |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *nurturing touch* | comforting physical contact, such as holding a hand |
| *focused bodywork* | giving physical resistance for the participant to push against, meant to release tension held in the body |
| *supportive touch* | Oregon's legally limited form of touch: hugs or hands on hands, feet, or shoulders, with prior written consent |
| *precautionary principle* | the rule that, without evidence of benefit and with known risks, the lower-risk choice should be taken |

#### Oregon Psilocybin Services: licensed, nondirective facilitation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | drug policy and regulated facilitation |
| Originators | Oregon voters (Ballot Measure 109, 2020, whose chief petitioners were the therapists Thomas and Sheri Eckert); the Oregon Health Authority (rules in OAR 333-333) |
| Date | November 2020 (Measure 109 passed); December 2022 (rules adopted); January 2023 (license applications opened); rules amended through 2025 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The facilitator shapes preparation, the session, and integration but is legally required to be nondirective, and the client's experience supplies the content. |
| What happens | Oregon made psilocybin legal for adults 21 and over in licensed service centers, without prescription or referral, for what the law calls personal development. A licensed facilitator must hold a private preparation session 24 hours to 90 days before the first administration session, review the informed consent document and a client bill of rights, make safety and transport plans, and give the client a chance to discuss intentions and expectations; the client must have the chance to approve and meet any facilitator beforehand. During administration the facilitator must be present and continuously watching, must use a nondirective approach, may give supportive touch only on request with prior written consent, and may not diagnose or treat any condition. Integration sessions must be offered and must also be nondirective, and the facilitator may point the client to peer support groups and community resources. |
| Steps | (1) Complete a mental health history with the client using the client information form. (2) Hold a private preparation session at least 24 hours and at most 90 days before the first administration session. (3) Review the informed consent document, client bill of rights, product information, and fees; complete safety, support, and transport plans; obtain written consent for any touch or recording. (4) Give the client an opportunity to discuss intentions, expectations, and other factors that could affect the experience. (5) During the administration session, stay present, keep visual and audio contact, and watch for physical or emotional distress, with a backup facilitator available. (6) Offer one or more nondirective integration sessions, and information on peer support and community resources. |
| What is facilitated | The client's own psilocybin experience, for personal development rather than treatment; the rules forbid the facilitator from diagnosing, treating, or directing, and require the client's written consent for touch. |
| Across scales | The rules turn a guide's ethics into a state's licensing requirements, so the nondirective principle is applied at the scale of public policy; the critique shows what it leaves unresolved at that scale, namely who screens clients and who is responsible when things go wrong. |
| The evidence | Wikipedia reports that more than 20,000 people had used legal psilocybin at more than 20 Oregon centers by 2026; I found no outcome study. Holoyda (2023) argued that the program lacks medical oversight, defines no standard of care, and leaves psychiatric screening to facilitators without mental health training. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Oregon's rules make it a legal duty for the guide to be nondirective and forbid diagnosis and treatment. Harm and its conditions: The rules ban romantic or sexual relationships with clients for a year after services, and the required training covers the history of abuse of power in psychedelic work. Change at the scale of a team or organization: The program moves psychedelic guiding from clinics and ceremonies into licensed businesses regulated by a state health authority. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Oregon's model still depends on a substance, a licensed site, and a facilitator in the room; the book offers the arc with none of these, though it shares the law's refusal to present the experience as medical treatment. |
| Limits and critiques | Holoyda argues that clients can obtain a mind-altering drug from a high school graduate with no mental health training and without medical referral, and that the state defines no standard of care for facilitation. |
| Quoted from its sources | "They also need to complete a mental health history with a licensed facilitator." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Psilocybin_Services) "A facilitator must complete a preparation session with every client who will participate in an administration session at least 24 hours but no more than 90 days prior to the commencement of the client's first administration session with the facilitator." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5000) "A client must have an opportunity to approve and meet any facilitator who will provide psilocybin services prior to receiving services from that facilitator." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5000) "facilitators must provide clients an opportunity to discuss internal and external factors that could impact a psilocybin experience including but not limited to the client's intention and expectations" (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5000) "A facilitator must use a nondirective facilitation approach to providing psilocybin services to clients during preparation, administration and integration sessions." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5120) "A facilitator shall not engage in any conduct that requires additional professional licensure while providing psilocybin services to clients, including but not limited to diagnosing and treating physical or mental health conditions." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5130) "A facilitator shall not engage in any romantic relationships, sexual contact, or sexual intimacy with clients, or clients' partners or immediate family members, for a period of one year following the last date that the facilitator provided psilocybin services to the client." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5120) "A facilitator must always be present during administration sessions and shall continuously monitor any client participating in the administration session." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5200) "A facilitator may provide a client information regarding other services, including but not limited to peer support groups and community resources, in support of a client's ongoing integration needs." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5260) "clients in Oregon can obtain a consciousness-altering drug from a high school graduate with no mental health training or experience and without psychiatric or general medical referral or prescription" (https://jaapl.org/content/51/2/160) "Nowhere does the state define the standards of care for psilocybin facilitation." (https://jaapl.org/content/51/2/160) "As of 2026, more than 20,000 people have used legal psilocybin at more than 20 centers across Oregon." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Psilocybin_Services) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | other. State regulation of non-medical psychedelic services that makes a nondirective approach a legal duty |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, decision choice, justice power |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the helper's restraint is the active ingredient rather than the absence of one and it is trained.md`; The note holds that the helper's restraint is a trained discipline; Oregon made one form of that restraint a legal duty with training requirements, which the vault has not recorded. |
| Sources | https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5000; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5120; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5130; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5200; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5260; https://jaapl.org/content/51/2/160; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Psilocybin_Services; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Oregon_Ballot_Measure_109 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *facilitator* | the state-licensed person who conducts preparation, administration, and integration sessions |
| *nondirective facilitation approach* | the legally required approach in which the facilitator does not direct the client's experience |
| *supportive touch* | hugs or hands on hands, feet, or shoulders, only with prior written consent |
| *service center* | the licensed premises where administration sessions take place |

#### Psychedelic apprenticeship and a 'gentle touch' for insights and revelations

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | psychedelic therapy and anthropology |
| Originators | C. Timmermann, R. Watts, D. Dupuis |
| Date | 2022 (Transcultural Psychiatry; preprint 2020) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a society's customs |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The guide helps the person examine a revelation over time without ruling on its truth, and keeps the context that shaped it in view. |
| What happens | The authors note that psychedelics increase the truth and meaning people attach to what they experience, including apparent memories, which risks the false-memory harms earlier therapies caused. In a psilocybin trial, a participant experienced himself as an infant being smothered by a parent; his depression lifted for two months, but six months later, with the depression returned, he was preoccupied with whether the event had happened, and extended integration therapy was needed. The authors ask guides to hold such revelations with 'gentle agnostic respect,' and they propose that validation take place between people, through empathic resonance with an experienced guide who has gone through something similar. They contrast this with a neo-shamanic ayahuasca center where facilitators and other participants taught visitors to read their visions as communications from spirits and demons, which Dupuis called a socialization of hallucinations. |
| Steps | (1) Before the session, avoid priming the person to believe that everything they see or feel will be 'the Truth'. (2) When a revelation arises, respect its importance to the person without declaring it true or false. (3) In integration, examine the revelation over time with the person, attending to the social and cultural context that shaped it. (4) Let validation happen between the person and an experienced guide through empathic resonance, rather than by the guide's pronouncement or the person's certainty alone. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own judgment about what an experience meant is supported over time; the guide supplies experienced company and questions, not a verdict. |
| Across scales | Validation by an experienced second person also appears in the Zen interview with a teacher; at the scale of an institution, the same influence becomes the socialization of visions into the institution's cosmology, which the authors warn against. |
| The evidence | A conceptual paper built on three illustrative cases (a trial participant, an ethnography at the Takiwasi center in Peru from 2008 to 2013, and a research example); it is not a tested method. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The framework makes judging a revelation a shared process over time rather than a matter of the moment's certainty. Being seen and recognized by another: It places validation in empathic resonance with someone who has been through something similar, so being recognized by another serves as a check rather than as proof. Who decides the content: The ayahuasca case shows guides deciding what visions mean, and the framework is written to prevent that. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book has no second person with whom to share validation; the vault's finding is that a solitary reader can run guards that decline to evaluate but not guards that discriminate, and this is a discriminating guard that needs a guide. |
| Limits and critiques | The paper describes the risk without removing it: the second-person influence it recommends is the same influence that produced the socialization of visions at the ayahuasca center. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the body reports and the relation verifies" (notes/the solitary felt shift rests on one name while the paired felt shift has convergent company.md) "A striking feature of psychedelics is their ability to increase attribution of truth and meaningfulness to specific contents and ideas experienced" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) "Patients primed to believe that everything they see or feel will be “the Truth” may feel preoccupied by any strange or upsetting revelations they might encounter." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) "How to hold this space of gentle agnostic respect for the messages that arise is one of the challenges of psychedelic therapy." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) "the way that these insights are “held” by the session facilitators can have important consequences for the patient" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) "a therapist, a spiritual facilitator or a guide, has undergone a similar process as the experiencer and thus can provide gradual orientation to the process of navigating this experiential domain" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) "Cultural background and social interactions result here in a “socialisation of hallucinations”" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | preparation, peak, integration |
| Mental model | existential and phenomenological. The authors draw on neurophenomenology and on anthropology of ritual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a single move |
| Subjects | thought belief, memory past, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the solitary felt shift rests on one name while the paired felt shift has convergent company.md`; The note concludes, in its own words, that independent traditions agree on one structure: 'the body reports and the relation verifies'. This framework reaches the same structure from psychedelic therapy and adds a documented case of what happens when the relation interprets instead of verifying. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *psychedelic apprenticeship* | learning to understand psychedelic experience in the company of an experienced guide, beyond preparation and integration |
| *gentle agnostic respect* | taking a revelation seriously without deciding whether it is literally true |
| *empathic resonance* | validation that comes from someone who has gone through a similar experience |
| *socialisation of hallucinations* | Dupuis's term for a group teaching its members what their visions mean until the visions follow the group's beliefs |

#### Set and setting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | psychedelic research and drug history |
| Originators | Timothy Leary popularized the terms (1961) and gave them their best-known statement in The Psychedelic Experience (1964, with Ralph Metzner and Richard Alpert); Ludwig von Bertalanffy used them by 1958; Al Hubbard applied setting in early therapy rooms; Ido Hartogsohn later extended the idea historically. |
| Date | 1958 to 1964 (popularized by Leary in 1961); extended by Hartogsohn in 2016 (Journal of Psychopharmacology) and 2020 (American Trip, MIT Press) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, a small group, a society's customs |
| Who decides | not applicable. The principle describes conditions; a guide who applies it arranges the setting and helps prepare the set but does not decide what the person experiences. |
| What it holds | The principle holds that the effects of a psychedelic drug depend on the mindset the person brings (the set: preparation, personality, mood, expectation) and on the physical, social, and cultural surroundings (the setting). Leary wrote that the nature of the experience depends almost entirely on set and setting, which is why he thought manuals were needed. Early therapists applied it by furnishing the session room like a home, which came to be called a Hubbard Room. Hartogsohn later argued that a whole culture supplies a collective set and setting and that LSD behaves as a suggestible technology whose effects follow the expectations around it. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own experience under the drug is made more likely to be bearable and meaningful because the guide arranges what surrounds it. On this principle the drug does not supply the content, and neither does the guide; the person's expectations and surroundings shape it. |
| Across scales | The claim that surroundings shape what an intervention does holds from one person in a room to a whole culture (Hartogsohn's collective set and setting), and Zinberg applied it to heroin and alcohol. It stops being a guide's tool at the cultural scale, where no one arranges the setting on purpose. |
| The evidence | The principle began as clinical observation and is supported mainly by correlational studies of what predicts the acute experience (for example Haijen and colleagues 2018, entered separately). Hartogsohn (2016) argues that set and setting overlaps with placebo research, which means the same factors that make an experience go well also make drug effects hard to separate from expectation in trials. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The principle is the psychedelic field's own statement that the conditions around a change shape its content, and the vault's set-and-setting note records that the corpus identifies it with the book's account of what makes a space enterable. Who decides the content: If the setting shapes the experience, whoever arranges the setting partly decides the content, which Hartogsohn's phrase 'suggestible technology' makes explicit. Harm and its conditions: The Wikipedia summary lists stress, fear, and a hostile social and cultural environment, including racism, as setting factors that make a frightening experience more likely. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Set and setting was built to explain variation in drug effects; the book removes the drug, so the principle's third term has no counterpart, and a reader alone arranges their own setting with no one else present. |
| Limits and critiques | Because the principle makes context decisive, it also makes suggestion decisive, so a guide's own beliefs become part of the setting; Hartogsohn's history describes 1950s and 1960s researchers reaching contradictory results with the same drug. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The nature of the experience depends almost entirely on set and setting." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting) "Set denotes the preparation of the individual, including his personality structure and his mood at the time." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting) "treatment space decorated to feel more like a home than a hospital" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting) "Stress, fear, a disagreeable material, social, and cultural environment, including situations of racism or discrimination" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting) "he develops the idea of LSD as a suggestible technology, the properties of which are shaped by suggestion" (https://api.crossref.org/works?query.bibliographic=American+Trip+Set+Setting+and+the+Psychedelic+Experience+Hartogsohn&rows=3) "He proposes the concept of collective set and setting" (https://api.crossref.org/works?query.bibliographic=American+Trip+Set+Setting+and+the+Psychedelic+Experience+Hartogsohn&rows=3) "set and setting theory is primarily prescriptive, educating therapists and users on how to control and optimize the effects of drugs" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Hartogsohn%20I%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22placebo%20response%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | other. Psychopharmacology and the social history of drug use; clinical, recreational, and harm-reduction practice all claim the principle |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | attention awareness, thought belief, emotion, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | Set and setting (Leary, Zinberg), a row in the survey table of vault holdings; this entry adds the survey row rests on the vault's secondary summary; this entry adds raw-text sources, the earlier 1958 use by Bertalanffy (so Leary popularized rather than coined the terms), the Hubbard Room, and Hartogsohn's collective set and setting and suggestible technology. |
| In the vault | `notes/set and setting.md`; The note seats set and setting as reference lineage, says Leary coined the pair in the early 1960s, and records that neither Leary nor Zinberg has been read in the vault. This entry dates the terms to 1958 and Leary's popularization to 1961 from the Wikipedia history, and adds Hartogsohn's own terms from his book's publisher description. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting; https://api.crossref.org/works?query.bibliographic=American+Trip+Set+Setting+and+the+Psychedelic+Experience+Hartogsohn&rows=3; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Hartogsohn%20I%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22placebo%20response%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *set* | the mental state a person brings to the experience, including preparation, personality, mood, and expectation |
| *setting* | the physical, social, and cultural environment in which the experience happens |
| *Hubbard Room* | a treatment room furnished to feel like a home rather than a hospital, named after Al Hubbard |
| *collective set and setting* | Hartogsohn's term for the expectations and surroundings that a whole society supplies to everyone who takes a drug in it |
| *suggestible technology* | Hartogsohn's description of LSD as a drug whose properties are shaped by suggestion |

#### The co-therapist team (two guides present)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | psychedelic therapy |
| Originators | MAPS treatment manual (Michael Mithoefer and colleagues); the Johns Hopkins safety guidelines (at least two monitors); many psilocybin trial protocols; contrasted with Oregon's single licensed facilitator |
| Date | 2005 to 2015 (MAPS manual versions 1 to 7); 2008 (Johnson, Richards, and Griffiths); 2024 (Vanderijst protocol) |
| Scale | three people, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Two guides shape the process together; the participant supplies the content. |
| What happens | In MAPS MDMA-assisted therapy, sessions without the drug last one to one and a half hours and drug sessions six to eight hours, all with a team of two co-therapists, generally one woman and one man, and at least one therapist in the room at all times. The Johns Hopkins guidelines call for support from at least two monitors during psilocybin sessions, and a 2024 protocol specifies a mixed-gender therapist dyad with a lead therapist and a co-therapist. MAPS listed two providers at every therapy visit, video recording, monitoring, and clinical supervision as its protections, and wrote after a misconduct case that none of them had been sufficient. Oregon's rules for licensed psilocybin services require one facilitator present and continuously watching, with a backup facilitator available. |
| Steps | (1) Assign two guides, often of different genders, to each participant for preparation, the drug session, and integration. (2) Keep at least one guide in the room at all times during the drug session; both commit to stay except for brief breaks. (3) After sessions, the two guides debrief with each other and review how they worked together. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's experience is witnessed by more than one person, which is meant to make it safer and to check each guide's conduct; the pair supplies presence and mutual oversight, not content. |
| Across scales | A second person as a check on the first applies wherever one person has power over a vulnerable other; it fails when the two are not independent of each other, as with the married co-therapists in the documented MAPS case. |
| The evidence | The pair is a design convention; I found no study comparing one guide with two. In the documented 2015 case at a MAPS trial site, the two co-therapists were a married couple, and MAPS stated that its protections, including two providers at every visit, were not sufficient. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: MAPS's own statement says two providers, video, monitoring, and supervision were all in place and none was sufficient when the two providers were a married couple. The container that holds people while they change: The pair makes the session a three-person relationship, so the participant is not left alone with one helper's judgment. What care costs the one who gives it: The MAPS manual has the co-therapists debrief with each other to process their own emotional responses to participants' trauma. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book has no second person at all, and the vault records that the corpus names the missing sitter as its own structural gap ('the codex is a one-chair technology', quoted through ops/the arc as kindness.md from walked p. 25). |
| Limits and critiques | The safeguard assumes the two guides will check each other; in the documented case, one co-therapist knew of the misconduct, tried to stop it, and did not report it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the codex is a one-chair technology" (ops/the arc as kindness.md) "Non-drug sessions range from one to one and a half hours of interaction and MDMA-assisted therapy sessions range from six to eight hours of interaction" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "At least one of the therapists is present in the room at all times throughout the entire MDMA-assisted session." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "The sessions will be conducted by a mixed-gender therapist dyad." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML) "The protective measures in place include having two therapy providers in every therapy visit, video recording of all therapy visits, monitoring of study and therapy activities, and clinical supervision. In this case, none of these measures were sufficient." (https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/) "Facilitators must ensure that a back-up facilitator is available to assist in case of unforeseen circumstances that prevent the primary facilitator from completing the session." (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5200) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | other. Clinical safety design |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/the arc as kindness.md`; The inquiry records that the substance-free arc drops the sitter, which the corpus calls its own structural wound; the vault holds nothing on paired guides. |
| Sources | https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML; https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-5200; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Human%20hallucinogen%20research%3A%20guidelines%20for%20safety%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *co-therapist* | one of the two therapists who share every session with the participant |
| *therapist dyad* | the pair of therapists assigned to one participant |
| *back-up facilitator* | in Oregon, a second facilitator on call to take over if the first cannot complete a session |

#### The instruction to "trust, let go, be open" and the first response to distress

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | psychedelic clinical research |
| Originators | A 2024 psilocybin trial protocol (Vanderijst and colleagues, BMC Psychiatry) that cites Walter Pahnke (1969) for the instruction; the same practices recur across psilocybin trials |
| Date | 1969 (Pahnke, as cited); 2024 (protocol) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The guides tell the participant how to attend and how to meet distress; what arises remains the participant's. |
| What happens | In the dosing session, participants lie on a bed wearing eyeshades and headphones and are told to direct their attention to their internal experience and to 'trust, let go, be open'; the protocol cites Pahnke's 1969 paper for the phrase. The protocol's therapists stay present and are supportive and nondirective. When distress arises, the first response across trials has been support and reassurance and touch such as hand holding within boundaries agreed beforehand, then a return to grounding techniques practiced in preparation (a body scan and breathing from the diaphragm), with medication kept for cases where support is not enough. |
| Steps | (1) In preparation sessions, teach and practice grounding techniques (diaphragmatic breathing, body scanning) and agree the boundaries for therapeutic touch. (2) At the start of the dosing session, invite the participant to lie down wearing eyeshades and headphones and to direct attention toward their internal experience. (3) Give the instruction to 'trust, let go, be open'. (4) If distress arises, respond first with support and reassurance and, within the agreed boundaries, touch such as hand holding. (5) If needed, direct the participant back to the grounding techniques practiced in preparation. (6) Use rescue medication only if psychological support is not enough. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's own ability to stop resisting an intense state is made easier; the guide supplies an instruction for how to attend, not what to find. |
| Across scales | An instruction to accept rather than resist an intense inner state appears in solitary contemplative practice and in paired therapy; it stops holding where the danger is outside the person and real, where resisting is the right response. |
| The evidence | The instruction is standard practice rather than a separately tested intervention; the protocol states that psychological support has generally been enough for passing distress in contemporary trials. Survey data (Wood and colleagues 2024, entered separately) found letting go the response most often rated helpful in difficult experiences. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The instruction asks the person to stop controlling what arises while still choosing where to direct their attention. Protection and defense: It treats the person's resistance to a frightening state as the thing to relax, which the vault holds, from an unread synthesis, as what multiplies suffering in an intense state. Timing and pacing: The guide gives the instruction before distress and returns to rehearsed techniques during it, so the response is prepared in advance. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The instruction assumes a drug state that will pass within hours and a guide in the room; a reader can close the book, so letting go is one choice among several and no one is present to reassure. |
| Limits and critiques | Letting go is advice for a supervised, time-limited state with screened participants; the protocol still keeps rescue medication and emergency treatment available. |
| Quoted from its sources | "instructed to direct their attention toward their internal experience and ‘trust, let go, be open’" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML) "interactions with the participants will be supportive and nondirective" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML) "Across PAT trials, the first response to psychological distress has been psychological support and reassurance, as well as therapeutic touch such as hand holding, according to preestablished individual boundaries." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak |
| Mental model | other. Clinical psychedelic practice; the 2024 protocol places the instruction inside acceptance and commitment therapy |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, emotion, body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering in an intense state is the intensity multiplied by the resistance.md`; The note holds, from an unread synthesis, that resistance multiplies suffering in an intense state; this instruction is the guide's move that acts on that claim, and the vault does not record it. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *trust, let go, be open* | the instruction to accept whatever arises rather than resist it |
| *grounding techniques* | practices such as slow breathing and a body scan that help a person steady themselves |
| *therapeutic touch* | touch such as hand holding, used only within limits agreed before the session |
| *rescue medication* | medication kept ready for anxiety or psychosis that support cannot manage |

#### The Psychedelic Experience: A Manual Based on the Tibetan Book of the Dead

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | psychedelic practice (Harvard Psilocybin Project circle) |
| Originators | Timothy Leary, Ralph Metzner, Richard Alpert |
| Date | Begun in 1962 at Zihuatanejo, Mexico; published August 1964; a spoken reading was issued on a record in 1966 |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, a small group, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The guide reads passages that tell the person taking the drug how to regard each phase, so the text interprets the experience with the authority of its Tibetan source; the person supplies what is experienced. |
| What happens | The book adapts the Tibetan Book of the Dead, a text for guiding a dying person through the states after death, into a manual for psychedelic sessions, and treats the loss of the ordinary sense of self (ego death) as a death and rebirth. It describes the phases a session may pass through and gives instructions on how to regard and act in each. It also includes passages written to be read aloud during group sessions, so that a guide can speak prepared words to people under the drug. Leary held that such manuals were necessary to help people understand what they meet in expanded states. |
| Steps | (1) The manual describes a sequence of phases, modeled on the intermediate states of the Tibetan text, that a session may pass through. (2) For each phase, it gives instructions on how the person should regard what appears and how to act. (3) During a session, selected passages are read aloud to the people who have taken the drug. |
| What is facilitated | The person's passage through the loss and return of an ordinary sense of self is meant to be made less frightening by a prepared interpretation read at the right moment; the manual, not the person, supplies the interpretation. |
| Across scales | A prepared text read aloud to direct a person's inner experience also appears in the Tibetan practice the manual borrows, in which the text is read to the dying; it depends on the listener accepting the text's account. |
| The evidence | The manual is a practitioner text with no outcome study; the purpose section of its Wikipedia page carries a more-citations-needed flag, and the page describes it as a classic of 1960s psychedelic literature rather than as a tested method. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The manual gives the interpreting role to a written text that a guide reads aloud, which makes it the nearest precedent in this territory for a book that tells a reader how to regard what they are going through. Timing and pacing: The passages are tied to phases, so the guide must judge that a phase has arrived before reading it. Harm and its conditions: The vault's set-and-setting note calls Leary the least respectable citation in the territory, and the manual's interpretive authority is the kind of authority later clinical guides deliberately gave up. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The manual assumes a drug state and a guide who watches and reads at the right moment; the book has neither, and its foundation is recognition of what never left rather than a death-and-rebirth sequence taken from a text read to the dying. |
| Limits and critiques | Its phases are an adaptation of a religious text rather than a report of what users go through, and no source I read tested whether reading the passages helps. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the book also includes selections of writing presented with the intent for them to be read aloud during events during which users take psychedelics collectively" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Psychedelic_Experience) "gives specific instructions on how one should regard them and act during each of these different phases" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Psychedelic_Experience) "It was published in August 1964." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Psychedelic_Experience) "It is for this reason that manuals or guide-books are necessary." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting) "to serve as road maps for new interior territories which modern science has made accessible" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent |
| Mental model | transpersonal |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | identity self, death finitude, spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/the arc as kindness.md`; The inquiry finds that the book's drafts perform a pre-dose briefing and give sitter-like instructions, and that the only design precedent the corpus names is Ignatius's Spiritual Exercises; it records no psychedelic precedent for a text that accompanies a person through phases, which this manual is. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Psychedelic_Experience; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Set_and_setting |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ego death* | the loss of the ordinary sense of being a separate self, treated in the manual as a death followed by rebirth |
| *bardo* | in the Tibetan text, an intermediate state after death; the manual uses the bardos as phases of a session |
| *guide-book* | Leary's term for a written account that helps a person understand an expanded state of consciousness |

#### The Psychedelic Preparedness Scale, and prospective predictors of the psychedelic experience

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychedelic research |
| Originators | R. G. McAlpine, G. Blackburne, S. K. Kamboj (2024); E. C. H. M. Haijen and colleagues with R. L. Carhart-Harris (2018) |
| Date | 2018 (Frontiers in Pharmacology); 2024 (Scientific Reports) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. Preparation is done by the person; the scale measures the person's own knowledge, intentions, readiness, and plans for support. |
| What it holds | Haijen's team followed 654 people who planned to take a psychedelic on their own initiative through five surveys before and after. Well-being rose two weeks after the experience and stayed raised at four weeks; a mystical-type experience predicted the increase, clear intentions made a mystical-type experience more likely, and a positive mindset made a challenging experience less likely. McAlpine's team then built and validated a 20-item Psychedelic Preparedness Scale with four factors (knowledge and expectations, intention and preparation, psychophysical readiness, and support planning), and people who scored high before a psilocybin retreat had better mental health outcomes afterward. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own readiness is what these studies measure, and they suggest it can be built; a guide can teach and plan with the person, but the scale scores what the person knows, intends, and has arranged. |
| Across scales | Preparation predicting how an intense experience goes applies to individuals and to ceremonial groups with preparatory diets and rules; at the group scale it becomes a shared rule rather than a personal readiness. |
| The evidence | Both are self-selected samples with attrition (Haijen's sample fell from 654 to 212 by the last survey); the preparedness scale's predictive result comes from a retreat group of 46. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The future: Preparation is the phase in which a person imagines and plans for an experience that has not happened yet, and these studies measure which parts of that planning matter. Remaining the author of one's own change: Setting one's own intention was associated with the kind of experience that predicted benefit. Timing and pacing: The findings give the phase before the experience a measurable part in its outcome. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's reader prepares by reading the book itself; there is no separate event to prepare for, and the corpus's treatment describes its first part as preparing the reader before any content is given. |
| Limits and critiques | Haijen's team found that baseline personality traits had the strongest effect on the change in well-being, so preparation is one factor among stronger ones. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Psychological well-being was increased 2 weeks after a psychedelic experience and remained at this level after 4 weeks." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Haijen%20ECHM%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Predicting%20responses%20to%20psychedelics%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "baseline trait variables had the strongest effect on the change in well-being" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Haijen%20ECHM%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Predicting%20responses%20to%20psychedelics%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) "Exploratory and confirmatory factor analysis identified four factors from the 20-item PPS: Knowledge-Expectations, Intention-Preparation, Psychophysical-Readiness, and Support-Planning." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22McAlpine%20RG%22%20AND%20preparedness&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) |
| Arc phase | preparation |
| Mental model | other. Psychometrics and prospective survey research |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, decision choice, body, learning skill |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/a framework for what is happening is itself a protective factor.md`; The note holds, from an unread synthesis, that lacking a framework predicts difficulty; the scale's knowledge-and-expectations factor is a measured form of having a framework. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Haijen%20ECHM%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Predicting%20responses%20to%20psychedelics%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22McAlpine%20RG%22%20AND%20preparedness&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |
