| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Knowledge-Expectations* | knowing what the experience may involve and holding realistic expectations |
| *Intention-Preparation* | having set intentions and prepared for the experience |
| *Psychophysical-Readiness* | bodily and mental readiness for the experience |
| *Support-Planning* | having arranged support for during and after the experience |

#### Therapeutic alliance and rapport in psilocybin therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychedelic therapy research |
| Originators | R. Murphy and colleagues, with Rosalind Watts and Robin Carhart-Harris (Imperial College London) |
| Date | 2022 (Frontiers in Pharmacology) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The therapists build the relationship and support the session; the patient's experience supplies the content. |
| What it holds | In a trial comparing psilocybin therapy with the antidepressant escitalopram, the 30 patients in the psilocybin arm received two 25 mg doses three weeks apart with preparation, in-session support, and integration under a new model called Accept-Connect-Embody. The strength of the therapeutic alliance, rated by patients, predicted rapport before the session, greater emotional breakthrough and mystical-type experience during it, and lower depression scores at the end, with the effect running through the acute experience. Emotional breakthrough mattered more in the first session and mystical-type experience in the second. |
| What is facilitated | The patient's openness during the drug session is made more likely by trust in the therapists built beforehand; the relationship supports the experience but does not supply its content. |
| Across scales | Across forms of ordinary psychotherapy with individuals, alliance correlates with outcome, and this study extends the finding into a drug session; it does not address groups or ceremonies. |
| The evidence | A secondary path analysis of 30 patients; the relationships are correlational, and a strong alliance may partly reflect patients who were already improving. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The study measures the relationship as a condition under which the acute experience deepens. Being seen and recognized by another: Patients' ratings of their relationship with the therapists predicted how deep their experience went. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book cannot build an alliance through preparation sessions; the corpus instead describes the book's front matter as the point where 'the reader has consented to be worked on' (arc-gloss p. 4, quoted through ops/the arc as kindness.md). |
| Limits and critiques | An alliance strong enough to deepen the experience also increases the patient's trust in the therapists' framing, which the apprenticeship and false-insight papers (entered separately) treat as a risk. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the reader has consented to be worked on" (ops/the arc as kindness.md) "Across psychotherapeutic frameworks, the strength of the therapeutic alliance has been found to correlate with treatment outcomes" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20alliance%20and%20rapport%20modulate%20responses%20to%20psilocybin%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "The strength of therapeutic alliance predicted pre-session rapport, greater emotional-breakthrough and mystical-type experience" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20alliance%20and%20rapport%20modulate%20responses%20to%20psilocybin%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Arc phase | preparation, deepening, peak |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, emotion, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/carl rogers.md`; The note holds Rogers's claim that the helper's congruence, regard, and empathy are necessary and sufficient; this study measures the relationship's contribution inside a psychedelic session. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20alliance%20and%20rapport%20modulate%20responses%20to%20psilocybin%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *therapeutic alliance* | the working relationship and trust between patient and therapist |
| *rapport* | the patient's sense of connection with the therapists just before a dosing session |

#### Training requirements for psychedelic facilitators

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | training of psychedelic facilitators |
| Originators | Oregon Health Authority (OAR 333-333-3060 and 333-333-3070); the MAPS MDMA therapy training described in the MAPS manual; Timmermann, Watts, and Dupuis on apprenticeship |
| Date | 2015 (MAPS manual version 7); 2022 to 2025 (Oregon training rules as adopted and amended); 2022 (Timmermann and colleagues) |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. Training programs set a required curriculum; this describes the training itself, not the stance a trained guide later takes with clients. |
| What happens | Oregon requires approved training programs to teach modules on the history of plant and fungal medicines, cultural equity, safety and ethics, pharmacology, core facilitation skills, and preparation and orientation, among others. The ethics module must cover facilitators' personal bias and motives, the vulnerability of altered states, consent to touch, sexual and emotional boundaries, the history of abuse of power associated with psychedelics, financial conflicts of interest, and reasonable expectations about client outcomes, and the core skills module includes vicarious trauma and compassion fatigue. Students complete at least 40 hours of in-person practicum, which may be replaced where observation is unavailable by alternatives such as taped sessions, peer support, role play, and non-drug altered states such as breathwork or meditation, and must pass an examination. The MAPS manual's training consisted of reading the manual and watching videos of research sessions, sometimes with Holotropic Breathwork training, and Timmermann and colleagues argue that therapists' training should include guided psychedelic experiences of their own. |
| Steps | (1) Complete modules on history, cultural equity, safety and ethics, pharmacology, core facilitation skills, and preparation and integration. (2) Examine one's own biases and motives for facilitating. (3) Complete at least 40 hours of in-person practicum: at least 30 hours of direct practice and at least 10 hours of consultation. (4) Pass the training program's examination (reported by Holoyda as requiring a score of 75 percent). |
| What is facilitated | The trainee's competence and self-examination as a guide; the curriculum also teaches what a guide must not do, such as diagnose, direct, or cross boundaries. |
| Across scales | Requiring guides to study the documented abuses of their own field turns a field's history into each practitioner's preparation; Oregon applies this to every licensed facilitator in a state. |
| The evidence | Oregon's requirements are regulations, not evaluated training outcomes; Holoyda (2023) argues that the entry requirement, a high school diploma at age 21, is too low for the screening facilitators are expected to do. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | What care costs the one who gives it: Oregon's core skills module includes vicarious trauma, empathic stress, and compassion fatigue. Harm and its conditions: The ethics module requires study of historical and contemporary abuse of power associated with psychedelics. Knowing that something happened: The curriculum requires teaching reasonable expectations about client outcomes, which limits what a guide may promise. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | These programs train people to accompany a drug state; the planned Book I trains facilitators without a substance, although the same curriculum topics (bias, boundaries, abuse of power, expectations) apply to it. |
| Limits and critiques | Oregon allows an alternative practicum when observation of real sessions is not available, so a licensed facilitator may never have watched a live psilocybin session. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Awareness of facilitator's personal bias, including examination of facilitator's motives" (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3060) "Historical and contemporary abuse of power associated with psychedelics, including sexual, emotional and physical abuse, and implications for facilitators" (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3060) "Reasonable expectations regarding client outcomes" (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3060) "Trauma informed care, including physiology of trauma, vicarious trauma, empathic stress and compassion fatigue" (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3060) "Students shall complete a minimum of 40 hours of practicum training" (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3070) "experience with altered states of consciousness that are not drug-induced, for example breath work, meditation or spiritual journeys" (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3070) "obtain a score of 75 percent on an examination to pass" (https://jaapl.org/content/51/2/160) "Training on the method consists of reading this manual and participating in a training program that includes watching videos of research sessions." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "training of psychedelic therapists involves opportunities for them to have guided psychedelic experiences" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, justice power |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | notes/the more powerful the facilitator becomes, the more important sovereignty becomes.md; The note states that a facilitator's growing skill must be matched by greater care for the participant's authorship; Oregon's curriculum turns part of that into required modules on bias, boundaries, and power. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3060; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/oregon/Or-Admin-Code-SS-333-333-3070; https://jaapl.org/content/51/2/160; https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9660279/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *practicum* | supervised practical training in which students observe or take part in facilitation |
| *alternative practicum* | substitutes for observing real sessions, such as recorded sessions, role play, or non-drug altered states |
| *core facilitation skills* | the required module on communication, empathy, a nondirective approach, responding to distress, and boundaries |

### Onset

#### Eyeshades, headphones, and inward attention

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | psychedelic therapy |
| Originators | Standard in MAPS MDMA-assisted therapy (Michael Mithoefer and colleagues' manual) and in psilocybin therapy at Imperial College London and elsewhere |
| Date | Described in the MAPS manual (version 7, 2015), in Kaelen and colleagues (2018), and in a 2024 trial protocol |
| Scale | two people, one helping, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The guides arrange conditions for inward attention and adjust how strongly they encourage it; the participant may decline them at any time. |
| What happens | During the drug session the participant lies down wearing eyeshades and headphones that play a prepared music program, so that attention turns to inner experience rather than to the room. The MAPS manual lets the participant give up the eyeshades or headphones at any time or ask for silence, noting that people with post-traumatic stress may not feel safe unless they can keep watch. The guides adjust to the person: they check in sooner with someone who tends to withdraw and isolate, and they more readily encourage someone who avoids painful feelings by talking and intellectualizing to put the eyeshades and headphones back on and focus inward. |
| Steps | (1) Offer the participant eyeshades and headphones with a prepared music program. (2) Tell the participant that they may remove the eyeshades or headphones, or ask for silence, at any time. (3) If the participant tends to withdraw and isolate, check in sooner and ask about their experience. (4) If the participant avoids painful feeling by talking and intellectualizing, encourage them to put the eyeshades and headphones back on and focus attention inward. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's attention to their own inner material is made easier by removing what they would otherwise see; the guides decide how strongly to encourage it, and the participant keeps the right to refuse. |
| Across scales | Reducing outside input so that attention turns inward appears in solitary meditation retreats as well as in paired therapy; it stops helping where watching the room is what keeps a person feeling safe, which the MAPS manual notes for people with post-traumatic stress. |
| The evidence | The practice is a convention of every major protocol; I found no trial that tested eyeshades on their own, and the music that accompanies them has correlational support (Kaelen and colleagues 2018, entered separately). |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The move directs where attention goes without choosing what the participant finds there, and the manual keeps the participant's right to take the eyeshades off. Timing and pacing: The guides judge moment by moment whether a person is avoiding and should turn inward, or withdrawing and should be contacted. Protection and defense: The manual treats both talking as a defense and a need to keep watch as protections to respect rather than override. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book cannot close its reader's eyes, since reading needs open eyes on a page; the book's version of inward attention has to come from its pacing, such as the executed draft's instruction to read the next part in one sitting without interruption. |
| Quoted from its sources | "in a single sitting, with no interruption" (ops/the arc as kindness.md) "The participant may elect to forgo eyeshades and/or headphones at any time." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "This is not uncommon among people with PTSD, who, at least in the early stages of their participation in the study, may not feel safe" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "the therapists would have a lower threshold for checking in with them and asking about their experience" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "the therapists would more readily encourage them to put the eyeshades and headphones back on and focus attention “inward.”" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "patients are encouraged to focus their attention inwards while lying down in a relaxed position and listening to a carefully designed music playlist for the duration of the session" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5893695/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | onset, deepening, peak, descent |
| Mental model | transpersonal |
| Structure | tending conditions; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, emotion, memory past |
| In the survey | The Bonny Method of Guided Imagery and Music (GIM); this entry adds the rule that the participant may refuse the eyeshades, and the guides' two-sided adjustment between checking in and encouraging inward focus. |
| In the vault | `ops/the arc as kindness.md`; The inquiry quotes the executed draft's instruction to read what follows 'in a single sitting, with no interruption' (abridged p. 31, quoted through this vault file) and calls it the pre-dose briefing executed literally; the vault does not record the eyeshade practice itself. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5893695/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *inward focus* | attention directed to one's own inner experience rather than to the room or the guides |
| *music program* | a sequence of recorded music prepared in advance for the session |

#### Music in psychedelic therapy ("the hidden therapist")

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychedelic therapy research |
| Originators | Mendel Kaelen and colleagues (Imperial College London); Justin Strickland, Albert Garcia-Romeu, and Matthew Johnson (Johns Hopkins) |
| Date | 2018 (Kaelen and colleagues, Psychopharmacology); 2021 (Strickland and colleagues, ACS Pharmacology and Translational Science) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The guides choose and adjust the music; the patient's imagery and emotion are the content, and patients reported the music both guiding and misguiding them. |
| What it holds | Kaelen's team interviewed 19 patients who received psilocybin for treatment-resistant depression about how the session music affected them. Patients described welcome effects (meaningful emotion and imagery, a sense of guidance, openness, calm, and safety) and unwelcome ones (unpleasant emotion and imagery, a sense of being misguided, and resistance), and how they experienced the music predicted their reduction in depression one week later, while the general intensity of the drug did not. A later Johns Hopkins study of ten participants found that overtone-based music did at least as well as the Western classical music usually played, and six of the ten chose it for a third session. |
| What is facilitated | The patient's emotional and imaginal process is supported and shaped by music the guides chose; the music supplies mood and sequence, and the patient supplies the imagery and meaning, sometimes against the music. |
| Across scales | Music that organizes an inner or shared process runs from one listener in a clinic to ceremonies built on song, such as the peyote meeting and the icaros of ayahuasca ceremonies (entered separately); the Hopkins result suggests that how the music is received matters more than which genre it is. |
| The evidence | Both studies are small (19 and 10 participants); the first is correlational and rests on interview ratings, so it cannot show that music causes improvement, and the second questions whether the conventional classical playlists have any special benefit. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Music is the part of the setting that changes minute by minute during a session, and patients reported it both guiding and misguiding them, so the playlist is the guides' choice that most directly affects the experience as it happens. Timing and pacing: The 2024 protocol curates its playlist to the drug's onset, peak plateau, and return to ordinary consciousness. The container that holds people while they change: Patients named calm and a sense of safety among the music's welcome effects. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book is silent text; its only music is the rhythm of its prose, which the corpus describes as paced like breathing. |
| Limits and critiques | Music that supports one person can be experienced by another as misguiding, so a fixed playlist imposes content that some people resist. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Crucially, the nature of the music experience was significantly predictive of reductions in depression 1 week after psilocybin, whereas general drug intensity was not." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5893695/fullTextXML) "Welcome influences included the evocation of personally meaningful and therapeutically useful emotion and mental imagery, a sense of guidance, openness, and the promotion of calm and a sense of safety." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5893695/fullTextXML) "a sense of being misguided and resistance" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5893695/fullTextXML) "These data call into question whether Western classical music typically used in psychedelic therapy holds a unique benefit." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI%3A%2210.1021/acsptsci.0c00187%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Music is selected to support emotional experience while minimizing suggestion" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "specifically curated to accompany the three principal intensity phases of the experience: the onset of psychoactive effects, the peak plateau, and the return to an ordinary state of consciousness" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | onset, deepening, peak, descent |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. The music is prepared by the guides, not improvised; this model is chosen because the working medium is music |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, memory past, attention awareness |
| In the survey | The Bonny Method of Guided Imagery and Music (GIM); this entry adds measured findings: the music experience predicting depression outcomes in a psilocybin trial, and a randomized comparison of musical genres. |
| In the vault | `ops/the arc as kindness.md`; The vault holds no note on music in sessions, and the survey found voice and music thin in the vault; the nearest material is the corpus's statement that the prose is paced like breathing, quoted in this inquiry. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5893695/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI%3A%2210.1021/acsptsci.0c00187%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *resonance* | the music being experienced as matching the listener's emotional state |
| *openness* | acceptance of the experience the music evokes |
| *music program* | the prepared playlist, curated in some protocols to the onset, peak, and return of the drug's effects |

#### Non-directive communication: invitation rather than direction, and waiting before intervening

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | psychedelic therapy |
| Originators | MAPS treatment manual (Michael Mithoefer and colleagues), quoting Stanislav Grof |
| Date | 2015 (manual version 7) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The therapists may offer direction late in a session, but only as an invitation the participant can decline, after giving the participant's own process time to lead. |
| What happens | The MAPS manual defines non-directive work by the timing of interventions rather than by a ban on guidance. If a participant feels stuck, the therapists first encourage them to experience and express the stuck feeling fully; if a participant seems to avoid a subject, the therapists note it and wait, because the participant may address it themselves, which the manual expects to have more effect than the therapist's observation. Suggestions are phrased as invitations, and the manual recommends the gerund ('breathing' rather than 'breathe') because it suggests rather than directs. The manual also asks therapists to keep a beginner's mind both about what the participant's process will be and about their own way of interpreting it, and quotes Grof: 'In that situation I prefer to believe something is happening that I do not understand.' |
| Steps | (1) Let the participant's process unfold, and allow ample time before offering any direction. (2) If the participant feels stuck, first encourage them to experience and express the stuck feeling as fully as possible. (3) If the participant seems to avoid an important subject, note it but do not intervene at once, leaving time for the participant to address it themselves. (4) Phrase any suggestion as an invitation (for example, 'This might be a good time to...') and use the gerund ('breathing') rather than the command ('breathe'). (5) Reflect back what the participant says instead of directing the conversation. (6) Later in the session, if stuckness or avoidance remains, offer direction as collaborative inquiry and leave the choice to the participant. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's own capacity to notice and address what they avoid is given time to act before the therapist intervenes; the therapist supplies patience and invitations, not interpretations. |
| Across scales | Waiting before intervening so that a person reaches a conclusion themselves appears in person-centered therapy and in group facilitation; it stops working where delay causes harm, which the manual concedes when it says that failing to offer direction can also be a problem. |
| The evidence | MAPS measured adherence to its method by independent review of session videos, but I found no study that isolates the effect of non-directive timing; the manual draws the approach from clinical experience and from Grof's training. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The manual makes the participant's choice the final word even when the therapists do offer direction. Timing and pacing: It defines the whole stance by when the therapist speaks rather than by whether the therapist speaks. Remaining the author of one's own change: The manual expects a participant's own acknowledgment of an avoided subject to have more effect than the therapist naming it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The manual's therapists watch the participant and time their invitations to what they see; a book's invitations are fixed in advance and reach every reader at the same page. |
| Limits and critiques | The manual concedes that failing to offer direction can be as problematic as being overly directive, so the stance depends on judgment that the manual cannot fully specify. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The essence of what we mean by “non-directive” rests in the timing of interventions." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "if a participant is feeling stuck, the initial approach should be to encourage them to experience and express this stuck feeling as fully as possible, trusting that the inner healing intelligence will guide the response" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "allowing time for the possibility that the participant will acknowledge and address the avoidance themselves" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "Invitation rather than direction" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "This might be a good time to" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "Use the gerund of a word: instead of “breathe” say “breathing” because it is suggestive rather that directive." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "Allowing participants to come to conclusions themselves." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "about their own way of understanding or interpreting the participants process" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "In that situation I prefer to believe something is happening that I do not understand" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "there are occasions when failure to offer direction in a sensitive way would be problematic, just as being overly directive is problematic" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "Adherence to the essential elements of this manual will be measured by independent review of session videos against adherence criteria." (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) |
| Arc phase | onset, deepening, peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, emotion, relationship present |
| In the survey | Section 8 of the survey, the clusters 'The facilitator's restraint from supplying content' and 'The pause before responding'; this entry adds the MAPS definition of non-directive as a matter of timing, the gerund rule, and the two kinds of beginner's mind, inside an altered state. |
| In the vault | `notes/carl rogers.md`; The note holds Rogers's non-directive commitment that the client is the one who knows; the MAPS manual adds a timing rule and a language rule for the same commitment inside a drug session. |
| Sources | https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *non-directive* | in the manual, a matter of when the therapist intervenes, not a ban on intervening |
| *invitation rather than direction* | suggestions phrased so the participant can decline them |
| *beginner's mind* | the therapist's openness both about what will happen and about their own interpretation of it |

#### REBUS (relaxed beliefs under psychedelics) and the anarchic brain

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | neuroscience (predictive processing) |
| Originators | Robin Carhart-Harris and Karl Friston; critics include A. Safron and colleagues (ALBUS) and Tehseen Noorani and B. Alderson-Day |
| Date | 2019 (Pharmacological Reviews); commentary 2020; ALBUS preprint 2020, published 2025 |
| Scale | one organism, molecules and cells |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The model combines the free-energy principle with the entropic brain hypothesis. It proposes that psychedelics relax the weight (precision) the brain gives to high-level beliefs, so that information rising from lower levels, including the limbic system, has more influence, and that in illness this can allow overweighted beliefs to be revised. The authors' own summary says this revision happens with the right intention, care provision, and context, which gives the guide and setting a place in the model. Safron and colleagues' ALBUS model proposes that psychedelics can also strengthen beliefs, and Noorani and Alderson-Day ask for more attention to the contexts that shape extreme experiences. |
| What is facilitated | The brain's revision of entrenched beliefs is what the model says psychedelics make easier; on the authors' own account, intention, care, and context guide which beliefs are revised. |
| Across scales | The model states a principle for one brain; the authors' extension to the revision of political, religious, and philosophical certainties is proposed, not shown. |
| The evidence | The model assembles existing neuroimaging and pharmacological findings into one theory; it has not been tested as a whole and rests on the free-energy principle, which is itself contested. The ALBUS authors link belief strengthening to hallucinations, delusional thinking, and possibly false memories. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The model's summary makes revision depend on intention, care provision, and context, so it assigns the setting and the guide a causal role. Knowing that something happened: If psychedelics loosen beliefs broadly, both true and false new beliefs can form, which the ALBUS and false-insight critics develop. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | REBUS explains a drug's action on the brain; the book removes the drug, so the model can at most be a comparison for what reading is meant to occasion, and the vault already declined the corpus's secondhand summaries of it. |
| Limits and critiques | The ALBUS authors list delusional thinking, paranoia, and possibly false memories among effects of the belief-strengthening side. |
| Quoted from its sources | "psychedelics work to relax the precision of high-level priors or beliefs, thereby liberating bottom-up information flow" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Carhart-Harris%20RL%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Friston%20KJ%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22REBUS%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "which, with the right intention, care provision and context, can help guide and cultivate the revision of entrenched pathological priors" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Carhart-Harris%20RL%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Friston%20KJ%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22REBUS%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "psychedelic experiences can be understood as kinds of waking dream states of varying degrees of lucidity" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22altered%20beliefs%20under%20psychedelics%22%20OR%20TITLE%3A%22ALBUS%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "the delusional thinking that sometimes occurs during psychedelic experiences" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22altered%20beliefs%20under%20psychedelics%22%20OR%20TITLE%3A%22ALBUS%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "we encourage paying greater attention to contextual factors shaping extreme experiences and their sequalae" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22REBUS%20and%20the%20Anarchic%20Brain%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Arc phase | onset, deepening, peak |
| Mental model | the life sciences |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | thought belief, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/queue/candidates.md`; The candidates queue lists Carhart-Harris with Friston for evaluation, and ops/queue/declines.md records declining the corpus's secondhand REBUS summary as a claim; no vault note holds the model. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Carhart-Harris%20RL%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Friston%20KJ%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22REBUS%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22altered%20beliefs%20under%20psychedelics%22%20OR%20TITLE%3A%22ALBUS%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22REBUS%20and%20the%20Anarchic%20Brain%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *priors* | the brain's existing beliefs or expectations, against which incoming information is weighed |
| *precision* | the weight given to a belief or to incoming information |
| *SEBUS* | strengthened beliefs under psychedelics, the opposite effect proposed by the ALBUS authors |

#### Velada (Mazatec healing vigil)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Mazatec healing practice |
| Originators | Mazatec sabios and curanderos of the Sierra Mazateca, Oaxaca, Mexico; María Sabina (1894 to 1985) is the best-known practitioner |
| Date | Pre-Columbian roots; first attended by outsiders in 1953 to 1955 (R. Gordon Wasson); publicized in Life magazine in 1957 |
| Scale | a small group, a society's customs |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The sabia chants, speaks, and divines through the night, interpreting the sick person's condition with the authority of the tradition. |
| What happens | In a velada, a night-long healing vigil, all participants eat psilocybin mushrooms as a sacrament, and the sabia (wise one) chants and speaks through the night to heal the sick and to find missing people or things. Visitors' accounts describe María Sabina's tonal songs, poetic speech, incantations, and chanting, with prayers for the participants beforehand, and Sabina said she was a sabia and not a curandera, a different practice. After R. Gordon Wasson took part in 1955, having gained entry by falsely claiming worry about his son, and published his experience in 1957, foreign seekers came to Huautla in large numbers; the community blamed Sabina, her house was burned, her son was murdered, and she later regretted having introduced Wasson. |
| Steps | (1) A sick person or a family asks the sabia for healing or for help finding what is missing. (2) At night, all participants eat the mushrooms as a sacrament. (3) Through the night, the sabia chants, prays, and speaks, addressing the participants and the illness. |
| What is facilitated | The healing of a sick person, or the finding of what is lost, through the sabia's chanted speech during a shared mushroom vigil; the sabia supplies the words and the reading of the illness. |
| Across scales | The velada worked within one community's cosmology; when outsiders made it known to a global audience, the practice was damaged in its home community, so it did not survive the change of scale intact. |
| The evidence | The ceremony is known to outsiders mainly through Wasson's and later visitors' accounts and recordings; Wikipedia notes that Sabina spoke only Mazatec and that many of her supposed quotations in English are not verified. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The sabia speaks and chants the content of the night, which is the opposite of the non-directive clinical guide. Harm and its conditions: Outsiders' pursuit of the ceremony brought police attention, arson, a murder, and a threat to the custom itself, and the guide and her community suffered the harm. Participation rather than watching: All participants eat the mushrooms; the ceremony had no observers, which is why Wasson entered as a supplicant. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The velada's reported purpose was to cure the sick; a remark attributed to Sabina (unverified in English, according to Wikipedia) contrasts this with foreigners seeking God, and the book's purpose, recognition, is closer to the seeking than to the curing. |
| Limits and critiques | Wasson's later publication of her name and location has been described as violating her consent and abusing her hospitality. |
| Quoted from its sources | "All participants in the ritual ingested psilocybin mushroom as a sacrament to open the gates of the mind." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) "The book reports on María's musical tonal songs, poetic expressions, ventriloquy, incantations and chanting during the" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) "María Sabina herself stated that she was not a curandera, that she was in fact a sabia, and that a sabia and curandera are not the same practice." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) "Wasson lied and told her that he was worried about his son back home" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) "Sabina later regretted having introduced Wasson to the practice" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) "They were always taken to cure the sick." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) "Sabina spoke only Mazatec and many of her supposed quotes in English are not verified." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) "an action which has been described as violating her consent and abusing her hospitality" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina) |
| Arc phase | onset, deepening, peak, descent |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | body, spirit ultimate, relationship absent |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/set and setting.md`; The vault does not mention the Mazatec velada or María Sabina; the nearest material is this note's discussion of rituals as the controlling part of setting. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Sabina; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velada_(Mazatec_ritual) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### Zendo Project's four principles of psychedelic care

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | psychedelic harm reduction |
| Originators | The Zendo Project, a psychedelic harm-reduction and peer-support organization |
| Date | Since 2012 (care space at Burning Man) |
| Scale | two people as equals, a large group, a community or public |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The sitter tends safety and presence and gives up any agenda for the person's experience. |
| What happens | The Zendo Project trains volunteers to support people having difficult experiences on psychedelics at festivals and other events, and its care space has operated at Burning Man since 2012. It teaches four principles: Safe Space (tending to safety is the foundation), Sitting, Not Guiding (presence without an agenda, which honors the person's inner healing intelligence), Through, Not Down (accompanying people through their experience without trying to halt or bypass it), and Difficult Is Not Necessarily Bad (openness to difficult experiences leading to growth). Its training covers supporting someone without trying to fix or guide their experience, the ethics of being a sitter, and deciding when to seek medical or mental health professionals. |
| Steps | (1) Make and keep the space physically and emotionally safe. (2) Sit with the person, present and without an agenda for their experience. (3) Accompany the person through the experience rather than trying to halt it or talk them down. (4) Treat a difficult experience as possibly valuable rather than as a problem to fix. (5) Recognize when the situation needs medical or mental health professionals, and seek them. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own passage through a difficult state at a public event, with safety and company supplied and no direction given. |
| Across scales | The same four principles are taught to lay volunteers and clinicians, and they apply from one sitter with one person to a staffed care space serving a festival crowd; they assume the state will pass on its own within hours. |
| The evidence | The principles are practice knowledge from more than a decade of event work; the organization's pages report volunteer training and testimonials, not outcome studies. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The second principle sets the sitter's agenda to none, which is the plainest statement in this territory of a helper not supplying content. The container that holds people while they change: Safety is the first principle and the condition for the others. Protection and defense: Through, Not Down refuses to shut a difficult experience down, treating the person's distress as something to accompany rather than suppress. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The Zendo sitter responds to a person already in crisis at a public event; the book is read by a person who chose it, and no one is present if a crisis comes. |
| Limits and critiques | Not stopping an experience is safe only within limits; the training itself includes deciding when to call medical or mental health professionals. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Tending to safety is foundational to healing and transformation." (https://zendoproject.org/about/) "Cultivating presence while letting go of an agenda honors the individual’s inner healing intelligence." (https://zendoproject.org/about/) "We accompany people through their experiences, without seeking to halt or bypass the process." (https://zendoproject.org/about/) "We remain open to the possibility that difficult and/or complex experiences can lead to wisdom, growth, and new understandings." (https://zendoproject.org/about/) "Practical guidance for supporting someone without trying to fix or guide their experience" (https://zendoproject.org/) "Since 2012, anyone having a challenging experience on playa could find support in the Zendo." (https://zendoproject.org/) |
| Arc phase | onset, deepening, peak, descent |
| Mental model | other. Harm reduction and peer support, drawing on the idea of inner healing intelligence |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, trauma healing, attention awareness |
| In the survey | Holotropic Breathwork (breather and sitter pairs); this entry adds a harm-reduction setting outside any therapy or workshop, and a stated principle against talking people down. |
| In the vault | `notes/the helper's restraint is the active ingredient rather than the absence of one and it is trained.md`; The note holds that the helper's not-doing is a trained discipline rather than passivity; Sitting, Not Guiding is that discipline taught to festival volunteers, a setting the vault has not met. |
| Sources | https://zendoproject.org/about/; https://zendoproject.org/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sitter* | the person who stays with someone during a psychedelic experience |
| *Sitting, Not Guiding* | presence without an agenda for the other person's experience |
| *Through, Not Down* | accompanying a person through a difficult experience instead of trying to stop it |
| *Difficult Is Not Necessarily Bad* | the view that a hard experience can lead to growth |

### Deepening

#### MAPS public announcement of ethical violation by former sponsored investigators

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | research ethics |
| Originators | The Multidisciplinary Association for Psychedelic Studies (MAPS) and MAPS Public Benefit Corporation; the participant, Meaghan Buisson; the co-therapists Richard Yensen and Donna Dryer |
| Date | Treatment 2014 to 2015; complaints 2018; MAPS statement 24 May 2019; review reopened November 2021; three papers retracted August 2024 |
| Scale | three people, an organization |
| Who decides | not applicable. The entry records a failure of the stance the method prescribes, in which the therapists shaped the process and the participant supplied the content. |
| What it holds | A woman enrolled in a MAPS-sponsored phase 2 trial of MDMA-assisted therapy for post-traumatic stress in Canada in December 2014; her co-therapists were a married couple, Richard Yensen and Donna Dryer. In June 2015, during the treatment period, she moved to where the therapists lived and kept receiving therapy from them; at the end of 2015 or early 2016 Yensen began a sexual relationship with her, and Dryer knew, tried to stop it, and did not report it. She complained to the licensing bodies in January 2018 and to MAPS in October 2018; MAPS barred both therapists, reported to regulators, paid her 15,000 Canadian dollars on what it called a compassionate basis, and announced the violation publicly in May 2019, stating that none of its protective measures had been sufficient. After a reporter viewed session videos in November 2021, MAPS reopened its review and provisionally found that the pair had substantially deviated from the treatment manual during treatment; in August 2024 the journal Psychopharmacology retracted three MAPS papers, and Wikipedia reports that the sponsor had not disclosed the violations to the journal or removed that site's data. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing was facilitated; the entry records how the conditions meant to protect a participant failed when her contact with the therapists continued outside the trial. |
| Across scales | A helper's power over a vulnerable person is the same risk at every scale where one party controls the setting; the institutional safeguards failed here because the two providers were not independent and the relationship continued beyond the recorded sessions. |
| The evidence | The facts come from MAPS's own statement and from Wikipedia's account of the retractions and of the participant's complaint; her allegations of inappropriate contact during the drug sessions are hers, while MAPS's own findings are of manual deviations and a sexual relationship after active treatment. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The case shows harm arising where a participant's dependence continued outside the trial and where the two safeguarding providers were a married couple. The container that holds people while they change: MAPS listed two providers, video, monitoring, and supervision as its protections and wrote that none was sufficient. Remaining the author of one's own change: MAPS's account says the participant grew uncomfortable with the relationship and left in 2017; the case is the clearest record in this territory of guides taking over a participant's life rather than supporting it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book has no live guide who could exploit a reader, so this risk is absent; the vault's note on facilitator power still applies to a book written for facilitators. |
| Limits and critiques | Parts of the record are contested: MAPS's legal counsel concluded that MAPS was not liable, and Wikipedia reports disputed claims about who influenced the 2024 FDA hearing at which the case was raised. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The participant’s co-therapists were Richard Yensen, Ph.D., and Dr. Donna Dryer, a married couple." (https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/) "Yensen and former sub-investigator Donna Dryer verbally confirmed that Yensen engaged in a sexual relationship with the participant." (https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/) "Donna Dryer reportedly became aware of this and tried to stop the sexual relationship but was unsuccessful. She did not report it to MAPS or any regulatory agencies." (https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/) "The protective measures in place include having two therapy providers in every therapy visit, video recording of all therapy visits, monitoring of study and therapy activities, and clinical supervision. In this case, none of these measures were sufficient." (https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/) "MAPS has provisionally determined that Yensen and Dryer substantially deviated from the MDMA-assisted Therapy Treatment Manual on several occasions during the treatment period." (https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/) "Over time, the participant grew uncomfortable with this relationship and eventually moved away in early 2017, breaking ties with both therapists." (https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/) "MAPS and Lykos Therapeutics didn't directly disclose the ethical violations to the journal and didn't remove the data from that site from their analyses." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals) |
| Arc phase | deepening, peak, integration |
| Mental model | other. Research ethics and institutional accountability |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | justice power, trauma healing, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | notes/the more powerful the facilitator becomes, the more important sovereignty becomes.md; The note (nascent, meaning stated but not yet substantiated by a reading, drawn from a recorded conversation with the author on 2026-08-13) states that a facilitator's growing power makes the participant's authorship more important to protect; the vault records no documented case of a guide's misconduct. |
| Sources | https://maps.org/2019/05/24/statement-public-announcement-of-ethical-violation-by-former-maps-sponsored-investigators/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meaghan_Buisson |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

|---|---|

#### Responses to Challenging Psychedelic Experiences Inventory (ReCiPE)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychedelic research |
| Originators | M. J. Wood, R. G. McAlpine, S. K. Kamboj (University College London); related model by M. Wolff and colleagues |
| Date | 2024 (Scientific Reports); 2020 (Wolff and colleagues, Frontiers in Psychiatry) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The inventory records what people themselves did in difficult moments; guides appear as one source of help among others. |
| What it holds | Interviews with 16 psilocybin retreat participants identified how people respond to difficult moments, including what facilitators did: touch, reassurance, and introducing something new (one participant describes a sitter handing her a rose quartz crystal after hours of frustration). A survey of 529 people then found the responses most often tried and rated helpful were trying to let go, accept, or surrender (84.5 percent), trying to observe one's mind and not fight it (77.3 percent), and closing one's eyes and lying or sitting down (73.9 percent). Acceptance and reappraisal, and seeking social support and disclosing the experience, were associated with greater emotional breakthrough, while fear-related challenges went with fewer helpful responses. Wolff and colleagues' model offers a mechanism: in supported therapy, relaxed beliefs make acceptance rewarding, so people practice staying with intensified inner events instead of avoiding them. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own ability to stop fighting a difficult state helped most; facilitators contributed comfort, reassurance, and occasionally a new object, not interpretation. |
| Across scales | Acceptance of an intense inner state as the most helpful response recurs in solitary contemplative practice and in acceptance-based therapy; the social-support factor shows that the same response is also relational. |
| The evidence | Cross-sectional, retrospective self-report from a self-selected online sample plus a small interview study; the associations with emotional breakthrough do not show causation. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Fighting the experience or commanding it to stop was among the responses least often rated helpful, which supports meeting a frightening state without resistance. Remaining the author of one's own change: The responses people rated most helpful were their own acts of attention, not something done to them. Being seen and recognized by another: Disclosing the experience and seeking support were associated with emotional breakthrough. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The data concern drug states that end within hours; a reader's difficulty with a book can be ended by closing it, so letting go is one choice among several rather than the only safe one. |
| Limits and critiques | Among the least helpful responses was taking another drug or drinking alcohol (rated helpful by 12.1 percent), which shows what some people do without support. |
| Quoted from its sources | "“I tried to let go, accept or surrender to the experience” (84.5% of participants), “I tried to observe my mind and not fight it” (77.3%)" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11582610/fullTextXML) "Notably, Acceptance and Reappraisal, and Social Support and Disclosure strategies were positively associated with greater emotional breakthrough." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11582610/fullTextXML) "Fear-related challenges were negatively associated with emotional breakthrough and involved fewer adaptive coping strategies." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11582610/fullTextXML) "one of the sitters came and handed me a rose quartz crystal" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11582610/fullTextXML) "“I took another drug, or consumed alcohol” (12.1%)" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11582610/fullTextXML) "engendering episodes of relatively avoidance-free exposure to greatly intensified private events" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Learning%20to%20let%20go%22%20AND%20psychedelic&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3) |
| Arc phase | deepening, peak, descent |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral. The inventory is empirical; Wolff and colleagues' model of the same process is explicitly cognitive-behavioral |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, attention awareness, body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering in an intense state is the intensity multiplied by the resistance.md`; The note reports the 84.5 and 77.3 percent figures from an unread synthesis and asks for them to be checked at source; they come from this study, which is a self-selected retrospective survey. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11582610/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Learning%20to%20let%20go%22%20AND%20psychedelic&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=3 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Acceptance and Reappraisal* | the factor of responses that accept the experience or reinterpret it |
| *Sensory Regulation and Physical Interaction* | the factor of responses that change posture, breathing, or contact with the surroundings |
| *Social Support and Disclosure* | the factor of responses that seek help from others or tell others about the experience |
| *emotional breakthrough* | a release or resolution of difficult emotion during the experience |

### Peak

#### False insights and beliefs under psychedelics (FIBUS)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | cognitive science of insight |
| Originators | H. T. McGovern, H. J. Grimmer, M. K. Doss, B. T. Hutchinson, C. Timmermann, A. Lyon, P. R. Corlett, R. E. Laukkonen |
| Date | 2024 (Communications Psychology) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The authors review laboratory work showing that the feeling of insight, the sudden sense of seeing a solution, usually accompanies correct solutions but can be induced for false ones, and that an irrelevant insight experience can make an unrelated statement feel true. They propose that psychedelics increase the number and felt intensity of insights, including false ones, and they link the noetic quality of psychedelic experience to this feeling of obviousness. A false insight that feels true without a known reason is hard to revise, because nothing is known that could contradict it. They point to Timmermann and colleagues' proposal that the relationship with a therapist before, during, and after a session can help people hold new insights lightly, and they offer a rubric that sorts insights by adaptiveness, truth, and falsifiability. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated directly; the model warns that the certainty a psychedelic can produce is not evidence of truth and asks guides to help people test insights rather than confirm them. |
| Across scales | The laboratory basis is one person solving problems; the authors also describe psychedelics as amplifiers of environmental influence, which extends the risk to whatever beliefs the surrounding group holds. |
| The evidence | A theory paper built on experimental insight research and modeling; its psychedelic predictions have not been tested directly. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The model separates feeling that something is true from its being true, which is the psychedelic-research form of the vault's distinction between a felt shift and a felt confirmation. Recognition: The book's promised event is a recognition that feels like remembering what one already knew, and the model says that feeling can attach to false content in altered states. Who decides the content: If psychedelics amplify environmental influence, a guide's framing can become the content of an insight that then feels self-evident. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book treats the feeling of knowing as recognition of what never left, while this model treats it as a heuristic that can misfire, so the two disagree about how far that feeling can be trusted. |
| Limits and critiques | Taken alone, the model could teach people to distrust every insight; the authors propose holding insights lightly and checking them instead. |
| Quoted from its sources | "We propose that subjective and brain-based alterations caused by psychedelics increases the quantity and subjective intensity of insights and thence beliefs, including false ones." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11332244/fullTextXML) "participants report a non-specific feeling of truth associated with insights" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11332244/fullTextXML) "an insight feels true, it can be difficult to revise since it is not clear what information could contradict it" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11332244/fullTextXML) "could serve as a scaffold for users’ to hold novel insights lightly" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11332244/fullTextXML) "Psychedelics may serve as amplifiers of environmental influence rather than pushing someone toward one set of views over another" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11332244/fullTextXML) "a rubric for making sense of the adaptiveness, veracity, and falsifiability of psychedelically derived insights" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11332244/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | other. Cognitive science of insight and active-inference modeling |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | thought belief, memory past, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/zen's makyo instruction refuses the self-certifying experience.md`; The note holds that Zen refuses to let a compelling experience certify itself and places verification with the teacher; FIBUS supplies a laboratory mechanism for why a compelling experience cannot certify itself. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11332244/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *false insight* | a sudden feeling of understanding attached to something untrue |
| *noetic quality* | the sense of having gained real knowledge during an experience |
| *Eureka heuristic* | Laukkonen and colleagues' theory that the feeling of insight guides which ideas people trust |

#### Marsh Chapel Experiment (the Good Friday Experiment)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychology of religion and psychedelic research |
| Originators | Walter N. Pahnke, supervised by Timothy Leary, Richard Alpert, and the Harvard Psilocybin Project; long-term follow-up by Rick Doblin |
| Date | April 20, 1962 (experiment); 1986 interviews and 1991 publication (Doblin, Journal of Transpersonal Psychology) |
| Scale | a small group, a society's customs |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The religious service supplied the setting and its content; the sources I read describe no guiding of the participants during the service. |
| What it holds | Before a Good Friday service at Boston University's Marsh Chapel, twenty divinity students were randomly divided; half received psilocybin and half received niacin, a vitamin that flushes the skin, as an active placebo. Almost all of those given psilocybin reported profound religious experiences, and some of those given niacin first believed they had received the drug until the flushing faded while the psilocybin effects grew. Doblin's 25-year follow-up found that all but one psilocybin participant still described the experience as genuinely mystical, and he judged the double-blind procedure incorrectly implemented. Wikipedia's account, citing Michael Pollan, adds that Pahnke did not report that several participants struggled with acute anxiety and that one fled the chapel and was restrained and injected with Thorazine. |
| What is facilitated | The students' own religious experience was made more likely by combining the drug with a service of their own tradition; the service supplied the meaning, and the researchers measured rather than guided. |
| Across scales | The experiment tests set and setting at the scale of a congregation, with the same drug given inside a shared rite; it says nothing about a person alone. |
| The evidence | A single small experiment whose blinding failed as the drug took effect; Doblin found the double-blind procedure incorrectly implemented and some questionnaire items imprecise, and the undisclosed adverse reaction shows the original report was incomplete. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The experiment began the measurement of mystical experience in psychedelic research, and its failed blinding shows how hard it is to separate the drug from what participants expect. Harm and its conditions: The published report left out an acute adverse reaction that came to light decades later, so the record of what happened was itself incomplete. The container that holds people while they change: The setting was a religious service the participants belonged to, which is setting in its most culturally specific form. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The experiment asked whether a drug can occasion religious experience in religious people; the book removes the drug and addresses readers who may have no religious tradition. |
| Limits and critiques | Leary supervised the experiment without institutional approval and was dismissed from Harvard in 1963. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Almost all of the members of the experimental group reported experiencing profound religious experiences" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsh_Chapel_Experiment) "In at least some cases, those who received the niacin initially believed they had received the psychoactive drug." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsh_Chapel_Experiment) "Timothy Leary, who had supervised the experiment without institutional approval, was dismissed from Harvard in 1963." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsh_Chapel_Experiment) |
| Arc phase | peak, integration |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic. The setting was a Christian Good Friday service attended by divinity students |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/what the big experience leaves behind depends on what follows it.md`; The note cites the Johns Hopkins persistence studies that use later versions of the mystical experience measure; the vault does not name Pahnke or the Good Friday experiment. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsh_Chapel_Experiment |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *active placebo* | a control substance with noticeable effects (here niacin) meant to keep participants from knowing which group they are in |
| *mystical experience questionnaire* | Pahnke's questionnaire for rating mystical experience, an ancestor of the instruments used today |

#### Mystical Experience Questionnaire (MEQ30) and the complete mystical experience

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychedelic research measurement |
| Originators | F. S. Barrett, Matthew Johnson, Roland Griffiths (Johns Hopkins); critique by J. W. Sanders and J. Zijlmans |
| Date | 2015 (validation, Journal of Psychopharmacology); 2021 (critique, ACS Pharmacology and Translational Science) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The MEQ30 is a 30-item self-report questionnaire on mystical-type experience, first developed in an online survey and validated in 2015 on pooled data from 184 people given moderate to high doses of psilocybin in five laboratory studies. Scores predicted lasting changes in attitudes, behavior, and well-being that participants attributed to the session, even after accounting for how intense they rated the drug's effects, and the paper validated a way of scoring a complete mystical experience that earlier versions had used. Sanders and Zijlmans argue that the mysticism framework ties the science to supernatural or non-empirical belief systems and call for a demystified model. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated; the questionnaire measures whether a session produced a mystical-type experience, which researchers then use to predict lasting change. |
| Across scales | The questionnaire measures one person's report; nothing in it measures what the experience does to relationships or groups. |
| The evidence | Validation is psychometric and predictive within Johns Hopkins laboratory samples; the outcomes it predicts are the same participants' later self-reports, and critics object to the religious assumptions of the framework. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The MEQ30 is the field's instrument for saying that a peak experience occurred, and its predictive validity rests on the same people's later self-reports. Recognition: The construct includes a noetic quality, the sense of having known something real, which is the nearest measured relative of the book's recognition. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The vault holds that the book's peak is withdrawn rather than staged and that the peak is not the work; a measure that scores the completeness of the peak directs attention to the peak itself. |
| Limits and critiques | Sanders and Zijlmans warn that a framework tied to supernatural belief systems risks bringing those beliefs into science and therapy. |
| Quoted from its sources | "latent variable scores on the MEQ30 positively predict persisting change in attitudes, behavior, and well-being attributed to experiences with psilocybin while controlling for the contribution of the participant-rated intensity of drug effects" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Barrett%20FS%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Validation%20of%20the%20revised%20Mystical%20Experience%20Questionnaire%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "that was used in previous versions of the mystical experience questionnaire is validated in the MEQ30" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Barrett%20FS%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Validation%20of%20the%20revised%20Mystical%20Experience%20Questionnaire%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "unity, sacredness, ineffability, a noetic quality, transcendence of time and space, and a deeply felt positive mood" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML) "We discuss risks and difficulties stemming from the scientific use of a framework associated with supernatural or nonempirical belief systems" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Moving%20Past%20Mysticism%20in%20Psychedelic%20Science%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "encourage researchers to mitigate these risks with a demystified model of the psychedelic state" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Moving%20Past%20Mysticism%20in%20Psychedelic%20Science%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Arc phase | peak, integration |
| Mental model | transpersonal |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/what the big experience leaves behind depends on what follows it.md`; The note cites MacLean, Johnson, and Griffiths (2011), in which lasting openness followed only complete mystical experiences, and Griffiths and colleagues (2018) on practice afterward; this entry adds the instrument's validation and the critique of its framework. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Barrett%20FS%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Validation%20of%20the%20revised%20Mystical%20Experience%20Questionnaire%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Moving%20Past%20Mysticism%20in%20Psychedelic%20Science%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *complete mystical experience* | a score high enough on every scale of the questionnaire to count as a full mystical-type experience |
| *mystical-type experience* | an experience with features such as unity, sacredness, a noetic quality, transcendence of time and space, ineffability, and deeply felt positive mood |

#### Spiritual emergency, and the Grof lineage in psychedelic guiding

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | transpersonal psychiatry |
| Originators | Stanislav Grof and Christina Grof |
| Date | 1989 (Spiritual Emergency: When Personal Transformation Becomes a Crisis, edited volume); Grof's earlier LSD psychotherapy research, from which the MAPS manual takes its bodywork principles |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, a community or public |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | The Grofs' 1989 volume named a crisis that follows a spontaneous spiritual experience a spiritual emergency, and Wikipedia summarizes three recognitions it shares with the term spiritual crisis: such experiences overlap with psychological disturbances such as anxiety and panic, Western medicine may value them differently from the people going through them, and people in crisis need specialized support in their local area. A related diagnostic category, religious or spiritual problem, proposed by Lukoff, Lu, and Turner, entered the DSM-IV in 1994. The Grofs' Holotropic Breathwork and Grof's LSD psychotherapy also supplied principles that psychedelic guiding still uses: the sitter who does not interfere, focused energy release work, and an attitude the MAPS manual quotes from Grof, of preferring to believe that something is happening that one does not understand. |
| What is facilitated | A person's crisis after a spontaneous spiritual experience is treated as a possible emergence to be supported rather than only a disorder to be suppressed; the support is specialized company, not interpretation. |
| Across scales | The concept concerns one person's crisis, but one of its three recognitions is a claim about communities: that people in crisis need specialized support where they live. |
| The evidence | Spiritual emergency is a clinical concept from transpersonal psychology, not a validated diagnosis; Wikipedia's summary notes that the transpersonal emphasis on spirituality's potentials and health benefits has been criticized. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The concept asks helpers to tell a crisis of spiritual emergence from mental illness, which is the question of dissolution versus regression that the vault leaves unresolved. Protection and defense: It argues against automatically suppressing such a crisis, and the vault's Ingram note flags this material as adjacent and never compared. Harm and its conditions: The concept's critics object that transpersonal writing emphasizes spirituality's potentials, which can understate suffering and illness. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The Grofs address people in acute crisis; the book addresses readers who are not in crisis and offers recognition rather than emergence. |
| Limits and critiques | Holotropic Breathwork, the Grofs' method, is contraindicated for several medical and psychiatric conditions and has a limited research base, as the survey's entry records. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Both the terms "spiritual crisis" and "spiritual emergency" (Grof, 1989) share in the common recognition that" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_crisis) "people need specialized support in their local area when in crisis" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_crisis) "However, this emphasis on spirituality's potentials and health benefits has been criticized." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_crisis) "The sitter’s role is simply to be present and available to assist the breather—not to interfere, interrupt, or try to guide the process." (https://www.holotropic.com/holotropic-breathwork/about-holotropic-breathwork/) "Additional elements of the process include focused energy release work and integration practices such as mandala drawing."
