(https://www.holotropic.com/holotropic-breathwork/about-holotropic-breathwork/) "the principles underlying this approach are those developed by Stanislov Grof, M.D. in his research with LSD psychotherapy" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) "In that situation I prefer to believe something is happening that I do not understand" (https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf) |
| Arc phase | peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | transpersonal |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, identity self, trauma healing |
| In the survey | Holotropic Breathwork (breather and sitter pairs); this entry adds spiritual emergency (1989), the related DSM-IV category, and the documented transfer of Grof's non-interfering sitter, his bodywork principles, and his clinical attitude into the MAPS manual. |
| In the vault | `notes/daniel ingram.md`; The note asks how Ingram's account of difficult insight stages relates to the clinical literature on spiritual emergency, noting that Grof's material covers adjacent ground and that the two have never been compared in the vault. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_crisis; https://www.holotropic.com/holotropic-breathwork/about-holotropic-breathwork/; https://maps.org/research-archive/mdma/MDMA-Assisted-Psychotherapy-Treatment-Manual-Version7-19Aug15-FINAL.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *spiritual emergency* | a crisis following a spiritual experience, treated as possible emergence rather than only as disorder |
| *focused energy release work* | bodywork used in Holotropic Breathwork, the source of the MAPS manual's focused bodywork |
| *religious or spiritual problem* | the DSM-IV category for distress involving religious or spiritual experience |

#### The Challenging Experience Questionnaire and the survey of challenging psilocybin experiences

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psychedelic research measurement |
| Originators | F. S. Barrett, M. P. Bradstreet, J. S. Leoutsakos, Matthew Johnson, Roland Griffiths; T. M. Carbonaro and colleagues (Johns Hopkins) |
| Date | 2016 (both papers, Journal of Psychopharmacology) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | Carbonaro's team surveyed 1,993 people about their single most difficult experience with psilocybin mushrooms. Thirty-nine percent rated it among the five most challenging experiences of their lives and 11 percent put themselves or others at risk of physical harm; risk was more likely with higher dose, longer and more difficult experiences, and the absence of physical comfort and social support. Yet 84 percent said they had benefited, and the degree of difficulty was positively associated with lasting increases in well-being. From the same data, Barrett's team built a questionnaire with seven factors of challenging experience: grief, fear, death, insanity, isolation, physical distress, and paranoia. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated; the finding identifies the absence of physical comfort and social support as conditions that raise risk, which are the things guides provide. |
| Across scales | The finding that difficulty and benefit occur together is about one person's experience; the protective factors it names, comfort and social support, are properties of the people around that person. |
| The evidence | Retrospective online self-report from a self-selected sample (78 percent men) about use without guides; the authors state that risky behavior and lasting distress are extremely rare in laboratory studies with screened, prepared, and supported participants. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The survey ties risky behavior to the absence of physical comfort and social support. Protection and defense: It shows that a difficult experience is not the same as a harmful one, since difficulty was associated with lasting benefit. The container that holds people while they change: The contrast between unguided use and supported laboratory use is the field's clearest evidence that the surrounding support changes the risk. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The data come from people who took mushrooms without guides; the book's reader is also unaccompanied but is not in a drug state, so the warning about lacking support applies only by comparison. |
| Limits and critiques | Three respondents' cases appeared associated with the onset of lasting psychotic symptoms and three with attempted suicide. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Thirty-nine percent rated it among the top five most challenging experiences of his/her lifetime." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Survey%20study%20of%20challenging%20experiences%20after%20ingesting%20psilocybin%20mushrooms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Eleven percent put self or others at risk of physical harm" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Survey%20study%20of%20challenging%20experiences%20after%20ingesting%20psilocybin%20mushrooms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "factors increasing the likelihood of risk included estimated dose, duration and difficulty of the experience, and absence of physical comfort and social support" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Survey%20study%20of%20challenging%20experiences%20after%20ingesting%20psilocybin%20mushrooms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Despite difficulties, 84% endorsed benefiting from the experience." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Survey%20study%20of%20challenging%20experiences%20after%20ingesting%20psilocybin%20mushrooms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "The incidence of risky behavior or enduring psychological distress is extremely low when psilocybin is given in laboratory studies to screened, prepared, and supported participants." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Survey%20study%20of%20challenging%20experiences%20after%20ingesting%20psilocybin%20mushrooms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Three cases appeared associated with onset of enduring psychotic symptoms and three cases with attempted suicide." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Survey%20study%20of%20challenging%20experiences%20after%20ingesting%20psilocybin%20mushrooms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Seven Challenging Experience Questionnaire factors (grief, fear, death, insanity, isolation, physical distress, and paranoia) provide a phenomenological profile of challenging aspects of experiences with psilocybin." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Barrett%20FS%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Challenging%20Experience%20Questionnaire%22%20AND%20PUB_YEAR%3A2016&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Arc phase | peak, descent, integration |
| Mental model | other. Psychometrics and survey research |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | emotion, death finitude, body, grief |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering in an intense state is the intensity multiplied by the resistance.md`; The note reports the 39, 11, and 84 percent figures from an unread synthesis and flags them for checking at source; this entry supplies the source (Carbonaro and colleagues 2016), which confirms them. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Survey%20study%20of%20challenging%20experiences%20after%20ingesting%20psilocybin%20mushrooms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Barrett%20FS%22%20AND%20TITLE%3A%22Challenging%20Experience%20Questionnaire%22%20AND%20PUB_YEAR%3A2016&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *challenging experience* | the research field's term for what users call a bad trip |
| *Challenging Experience Questionnaire* | a 26-item measure of seven kinds of difficulty during a psychedelic experience |

### Integration

#### Psychedelic integration (a synthesized definition and model)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | psychedelic integration |
| Originators | Geoff J. Bathje, E. Majeski, M. Kudowor |
| Date | 2022 (Frontiers in Psychology) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, a small group, the more-than-human |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. Integration is defined as the person's own process; therapists, coaches, or the person alone may support it. |
| What it holds | Reviewing definitions, practices, and ten models of integration, all published since 2017, the authors define integration as a process in which a person revisits and actively works to make sense of, work through, translate, and process the content of a psychedelic experience, and through intentional effort and supportive practices gradually brings its lessons into their life, toward balance inside (mind, body, spirit) and outside (lifestyle, social relations, the natural world). Their synthesized model has six domains: mind and emotion, body, spirit and ritual, lifestyle and action, relationships and community, and the natural world. They note that integration is offered by therapists, coaches, and other practitioners or done alone, and that treating integration as a separate phase probably imposes Western dualistic thinking. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own process of making sense of an experience and carrying it into daily life; helpers support it, and the definition places the effort in the person. |
| Across scales | The definition names an inner and an outer side, reaching from mind and body to relationships and the natural world, so integration is described across scales from the person to the living world; it assumes a discrete experience to integrate. |
| The evidence | A concept analysis of published definitions and models, not an outcome study; the authors note that none of the model authors who drew on Indigenous practices identify as Indigenous themselves. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The definition makes integration the person's own effort of making sense and translating, which keeps authorship with the person after the peak. Timing and pacing: It describes integration as gradual, and the authors question whether it should be treated as a phase at all. Recognition: The corpus's arc ends in a phase named integration, and this review supplies the definition the psychedelic field gives that word. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The review assumes a discrete drug experience whose content must be brought back; the book describes recognition as knowing what never left, so there may be no separate content to bring back. |
| Limits and critiques | The authors warn that separating integration into its own phase reflects Western dualistic thinking not shared by cultures where psychedelic use is part of community life. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Integration is a process in which a person revisits and actively engages in making sense of, working through, translating, and processing the content of their psychedelic experience." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9386447/fullTextXML) "treating integration as a separate phase of psychedelic experiences probably imposes Western dualistic thinking" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9386447/fullTextXML) "none of the authors identify as Indigenous themselves" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9386447/fullTextXML) "the process by which a psychedelic experience translates into positive changes in daily life" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML) |
| Arc phase | integration |
| Mental model | other. A concept analysis across transpersonal, Jungian, acceptance-based, somatic, harm-reduction, nature-based, and Indigenous-derived models |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, body, relationship present, nature ecology |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/what the big experience leaves behind depends on what follows it.md`; The note holds that lasting change depends on what follows the peak; this entry supplies the field's working definition of that following process. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9386447/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10821548/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *integration* | making sense of a psychedelic experience and carrying its lessons into daily life |
| *Holistic Model for a Balanced Life* | Françoise Bourzat and Kristin Hunter's integration model with domains of body, mind, spirit, community, and nature |

#### The FDA advisory committee vote and rejection of MDMA-assisted therapy for post-traumatic stress

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | drug regulation |
| Originators | The United States Food and Drug Administration and its Psychopharmacologic Drugs Advisory Committee; applicant Lykos Therapeutics (formerly MAPS Public Benefit Corporation, renamed Resilient Pharmaceuticals in 2025) |
| Date | 4 June 2024 (advisory committee vote); August 2024 (rejection); August 2026 (application resubmitted) |
| Scale | an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| What it holds | In June 2024 an 11-member FDA advisory panel voted 9 to 2 that the data did not show MDMA-assisted therapy to be effective for post-traumatic stress and 10 to 1 that its benefits did not outweigh its risks. The reasons reported included functional unblinding (participants could tell whether they had received MDMA), side effects such as cardiovascular effects, a lack of detailed data on effects related to misuse, and accusations of bias among those running the trials. In August 2024 the FDA declined to approve and asked for another phase 3 trial, although it had agreed to the trial design in 2017; the company resubmitted its application with new data in August 2026. |
| What is facilitated | Nothing is facilitated; the decision records what a regulator requires before crediting a guided drug experience as a treatment, namely evidence that the effect is not produced by participants and therapists knowing who received the drug. |
| Across scales | The panel's question, whether an effect is real or produced by expectation and conduct, applies to any intervention whose recipients know what they received, from a single session to a public health program. |
| The evidence | The two phase 3 trials showed large differences in remission from placebo, which the panel did not credit because of unblinding and conduct concerns; Wikipedia also reports disputed claims that an activist group influenced the hearing. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The panel refused to credit large reported improvements while participants could tell what they had received. Harm and its conditions: The panel weighed conduct concerns, including the documented misconduct at a trial site, alongside the drug's physical risks. Decisions: The case shows an institution deciding about a guided experience on the basis of trial design and conduct rather than on participants' testimony. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book makes no treatment claim and seeks no approval, and the vault's stance forbids postulating efficacy, which places the book outside the question the FDA asked. |
| Limits and critiques | The record is still open: the application was resubmitted in August 2026, and the outcome was not known in the sources I read. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In June 2024, an 11-member FDA advisory panel of independent experts voted overwhelmingly against MDMA-assisted psychotherapy for PTSD." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals) "The reasons cited for the rejection included concerns about" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals) "Historically, the FDA has followed the recommendations of its advisory panels when evaluating and approving NDAs." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals) "In August 2026, Resilient Pharmaceuticals resubmitted its NDA to the FDA with new data." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals) |
| Arc phase | integration |
| Mental model | other. Drug regulation |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, justice power, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | notes/response expectancy is a two-sided coin, the mechanism of the manufactured artifact and of genuine change alike.md; The note admits that expectancy may manufacture an experience while holding that change produced by expectancy is not thereby unreal; the FDA decision is a regulator refusing to credit an effect it could not separate from expectancy. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resilient_Pharmaceuticals |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *functional unblinding* | participants or staff working out who received the active drug from its effects, which defeats the trial's blinding |
| *advisory committee* | an outside expert panel whose vote the FDA usually, but not always, follows |

## Part 4. How a text or a work of art facilitates with no one present

### The entries

#### Ars moriendi (The Art of Dying)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | late-medieval Christian devotional literature |
| Originators | An anonymous Dominican friar, probably at the request of the Council of Constance (the long version); an illustrated short version from the Netherlands |
| Date | 1415 (long version); about 1450 (short version with eleven woodcuts) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping, a small group, a society's customs |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The text and its pictures instruct the dying person and the friends and family at the bedside. |
| What happens | The Ars moriendi are two related Latin texts that advise on the protocols and procedures of a good death according to late-medieval Christian precepts, written some sixty years after the Black Death. The long version has six chapters: dying has a good side and need not be feared; the five temptations of the dying, which are lack of faith, despair, impatience, spiritual pride, and avarice, and how to avoid them; seven questions to ask a dying man, with the consolation available to him; the imitation of Christ's life; rules of behavior at the deathbed for friends and family; and prayers to be said for the dying. The short version turns the second chapter into eleven woodcuts, five pairs each showing a devil presenting a temptation and then the remedy, and a last picture of the dying man received into heaven, and about 50,000 copies were printed before 1501. |
| Steps | (1) The dying person is consoled that dying has a good side and need not be feared. (2) The five temptations of the dying are named, each with its remedy. (3) Seven questions are put to the dying person, with the consolation available to him. (4) The dying person is urged to imitate Christ's life. (5) Friends and family follow rules of behavior at the deathbed. (6) Prayers are said for the dying person. |
| What is facilitated | The dying person's passage through fear and temptation toward a good death, and the conduct of those at the bedside, are what the text guides; it supplies named temptations, remedies, questions, and prayers. In written and pictured form, the guidance can be brought to any deathbed, and the pictures were made to be easily explained and memorized. |
| Across scales | The text addresses the dying person, the friends and family around them, and a whole culture's practice of dying, since it began a tradition of consolatory death literature that lasted in England until the seventeenth century. |
| The evidence | The texts are historical and known from about 300 manuscripts of the long version and many printed editions; their effect on the dying was not measured. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The text sets rules for the people at the bedside, so a book organizes the room in which a dying person faces death. Protection and defense: The short version pairs each temptation with a remedy, meeting each fear of the dying with a named response. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The texts direct the dying toward a Christian good death and name the correct responses to each temptation, while the book does not direct its reader to a doctrine or name correct responses. |
| Limits and critiques | The texts assume the Christian doctrine they teach and a household at the bedside to carry out their rules. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The third chapter lists the seven questions to ask a dying man" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ars_moriendi) "The fifth chapter addresses the friends and family, outlining the general rules of behavior at the deathbed." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ars_moriendi) "as instructive images which could be easily explained and memorized" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ars_moriendi) |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | holding two poles; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | death finitude, spirit ultimate, emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | archive/reading — heidegger, being and time II on being-toward-death (1927).md; The vault holds Heidegger on being-toward-death at the primary text; it holds no practice or text that accompanies the dying. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ars_moriendi |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ars moriendi* | Ars moriendi is Latin for the art of dying, the texts' name for dying well according to Christian precepts. |
| *five temptations* | The five temptations are lack of faith, despair, impatience, spiritual pride, and avarice, which the texts say beset a dying man. |
| *woodcut pairs* | The woodcut pairs are the short version's pictures, each temptation shown beside its remedy, made to be easily explained and memorized. |

#### Art making and cortisol, with an art therapist quietly present

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | art therapy |
| Originators | G. Kaimal, K. Ray, and J. Muniz |
| Date | 2016 (Art Therapy 33:74-80) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. Participants chose what to make; the art therapist followed their lead, talking only if they talked and otherwise staying quietly present. |
| What it holds | Thirty-nine healthy adults made art for 45 minutes in an art therapy studio with collage materials, modeling clay, or markers, told there was no expectation of a finished artwork. The first author, an art therapist, was available to help and followed each participant's lead, conversing if they talked and remaining quietly present if they worked silently. Salivary cortisol fell significantly overall and fell for about 75% of participants, regardless of prior art experience, medium, or gender, and participants described the session as relaxing, enjoyable, freeing, a process of initial struggle leading to resolution, and a losing of themselves in the work. |
| Steps | (1) The participant is offered collage materials, modeling clay, and markers and told there is no expectation of a finished artwork. (2) The participant makes art for about 45 minutes, choosing what and how to make. (3) The art therapist stays available, conversing only if the participant talks and otherwise remaining quietly present. (4) The participant is invited to share anything about the work or the experience. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's absorption in making something, and a physiological lowering of stress, are what the session made more likely, and the participant supplied all the content. The study did not test art making with no one present, because an art therapist was quietly in the room for every participant. |
| Across scales | The effect is measured in one body, in a setting of two people where one makes art and the other stays present without directing. |
| The evidence | A quasi-experimental pretest and posttest study with no control group, which the authors name as its primary limitation; they state that it is hard to tell whether the art making, the interaction with the researcher, or something else lowered cortisol, and cortisol stayed the same or rose for about a quarter of participants. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The session's conditions were materials, time, freedom from producing a finished work, and a person present who followed the participant's lead. Who decides the content: The participants decided everything they made, and the facilitator only responded to what each participant sought. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The study measures stress reduction in healthy adults, while the book is concerned with recognition, and its reader has no one quietly present. |
| Limits and critiques | The finding cannot be claimed for art making alone, because every participant had an art therapist present, and a quarter of participants showed no drop. |
| Quoted from its sources | "art making resulted in statistically significant lowering of cortisol levels" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Reduction%20of%20Cortisol%20Levels%20and%20Participants%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Kaimal%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "if they chose to work silently, the first author remained quietly present" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5004743/fullTextXML) "Although results overall were statistically significant, reductions in cortisol levels were not consistent for all participants. Levels were lowered for about 75% of the sample" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5004743/fullTextXML) "Thus it is hard to determine at present which factors in the session (art making, interactions with the researcher, or something else) contributed to the lowering of cortisol." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5004743/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. Art therapy research |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, creativity play |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the deepest contact with your own inner life is first found alone in the presence of a non-impinging other.md`; The vault holds Winnicott's capacity to be alone in the presence of someone as the state of the book's one confirmed sighting; this study's art therapist, quietly present and following the participant's lead, is a measured instance of that arrangement in art making, and the vault holds no art therapy research. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Reduction%20of%20Cortisol%20Levels%20and%20Participants%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Kaimal%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5004743/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *quietly present* | Quietly present describes the art therapist's stance in this study: staying in the room and available without directing, following the participant's lead. |
| *flow* | Flow is the participants' description of losing themselves in the work. |

#### Art therapy for non-psychotic mental health disorders: effectiveness, benefits, and harms

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | art therapy |
| Originators | L. Uttley and colleagues (UK Health Technology Assessment review); the term art therapy coined by the British artist Adrian Hill |
| Date | 1942 (Hill's term); 1945 (Hill, Art Versus Illness); 2015 (Health Technology Assessment 19(18)) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. An art therapist provides materials and a relationship; the person makes the images. |
| What it holds | The British artist Adrian Hill, recovering from tuberculosis in a sanatorium, found drawing and painting beneficial while convalescing, coined the term art therapy in 1942, and began suggesting art to fellow patients. A 2015 systematic review of 15 randomized trials with 777 participants found that art therapy was associated with significant improvement over controls in 10 trials, though trial quality was generally low and a meta-analysis was not possible. Its qualitative review found that service users named the relationship with the therapist, personal achievement, and distraction as benefits, and named as potential harms emotions activated and then left unresolved, unskilled therapists, and sudden endings of therapy. |
| What is facilitated | The person's expression through images and the achievement of making something are what art therapy supports, and the therapist supplies materials and a relationship, not the images. Hill's discovery was made while convalescing, but service users in the review named the relationship with the therapist as a benefit and named unresolved emotions and sudden endings as harms, which art making alone can neither supply nor prevent. |
| Across scales | Art making is practiced alone, with one therapist, and in groups, and the review's cost scenarios compared group art therapy with group verbal therapy, but these sources do not show whether the benefit differs by scale. |
| The evidence | The review found generally low-quality trials that could not be pooled; the Wikipedia article reports that in November 2023 the National Council of France's National Order of Nurses concluded that about forty meta-analyses covering more than 2,500 studies found no evidence of art therapy's effectiveness on patients' health. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: Service users named emotions activated and then left unresolved as a potential harm, which is the risk when a practice arouses feeling with no one to help complete it. The container that holds people while they change: The relationship with the therapist was one of the benefits service users named, so the relationship was part of what helped, not only the art making. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Art therapy treats mental health disorders within a professional relationship, while the book is not a treatment and offers no therapist. |
| Limits and critiques | The evidence for effectiveness is weak and contested, and the harms service users named come from arousing emotion without resolving it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Hill, recovering from tuberculosis in a sanatorium, discovered the therapeutic benefits of drawing and painting while convalescing." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_therapy) "Themes relating to benefits of art therapy for service users included the relationship with the therapist, personal achievement and distraction." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22clinical%20effectiveness%20and%20cost-effectiveness%20of%20art%20therapy%20among%20people%20with%20non-psychotic%20mental%20health%20disorders%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Areas of potential harms were related to the activation of emotions that were then unresolved, lack of skill of the art therapist and sudden termination of art therapy." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22clinical%20effectiveness%20and%20cost-effectiveness%20of%20art%20therapy%20among%20people%20with%20non-psychotic%20mental%20health%20disorders%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "found no evidence regarding its actual effectiveness on patients' health" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_therapy) |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. Art therapy |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, trauma healing, creativity play |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the affective practice completes a feeling's descent with consent between repression and venting.md`; The vault holds the book's affective practice of letting a feeling complete with consent; the review's named harm, emotions activated and left unresolved, is the case that practice would need to guard against, and the vault holds no art therapy research. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22clinical%20effectiveness%20and%20cost-effectiveness%20of%20art%20therapy%20among%20people%20with%20non-psychotic%20mental%20health%20disorders%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *art therapy* | Art therapy is the use of art making within a therapeutic relationship to address mental health, a term Adrian Hill coined in 1942. |
| *unresolved emotions* | Unresolved emotions are feelings that art making activated and the therapy did not help the person work through, named in the review as a potential harm. |

#### Being moved (a psychological construct)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | empirical aesthetics |
| Originators | W. Menninghaus, V. Wagner, J. Hanich, E. Wassiliwizky, M. Kuehnast, and T. Jacobsen |
| Date | 2015 (PLoS One 10:e0128451) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable. The entry describes an emotional state, not a way of working. |
| What it holds | In three studies, Menninghaus and colleagues found that most episodes of being moved arise from significant relationship events and critical life events, especially death, birth, marriage, separation, and reunion, and that sadness and joy are its two main ingredients. Both the sad and the joyful forms mix positive and negative feeling, and being moved was experienced as intense despite low-to-mid arousal, as much for fictional artworks as for events in one's own life. Its appraisals were distinctive: very low ratings for having caused the event or having the power to change its outcome, and very high ratings for compatibility with social norms and self-ideals. |
| What is facilitated | Being moved is a state an artwork or story can occasion, and the appraisals show it arises when the person neither caused the event nor can change it and finds it consistent with their ideals. With no one present, a fiction can move a reader as strongly as their own life events, but the state does not show that anything in the reader has changed. |
| Across scales | The eliciting events are mostly events between people, such as reunion, separation, birth, and death, so a state felt by one person responds to relationships, but the construct is measured only in individuals. |
| The evidence | Three questionnaire studies build the construct; they describe the state and do not test whether being moved changes a person afterward. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: Being moved goes with appraising oneself as neither the cause of the event nor able to change it, which is the appraisal of a witness rather than a participant. Knowing that something happened: Being moved is intense and is felt as strongly for fiction as for one's own life, so its intensity is not evidence that the reader changed. Recognition: The state goes with judging an event compatible with one's own ideals, so a reader moved by a passage is responding to their ideals being shown, which differs from recognizing what they already are. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research builds a construct of an emotion, while the book's aim is recognition and practice, which the construct does not measure. |
| Limits and critiques | The studies do not test whether being moved leads to lasting change. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Sadness and joy turned out to be the two preeminent emotions involved in episodes of being moved." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Towards%20a%20psychological%20construct%20of%20being%20moved%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "very low ratings for causation of the event by oneself and for having the power to change its outcome" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Towards%20a%20psychological%20construct%20of%20being%20moved%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "this applied to responses to fictional artworks no less than to own-life and other real, but media-represented, events" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Towards%20a%20psychological%20construct%20of%20being%20moved%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Mental model | other. Empirical aesthetics and emotion research |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship present, death finitude |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/beauty recruits the wrong posture.md`; The vault holds the tension that the book's beauty recruits an appreciating posture instead of practice; the being-moved construct gives that appreciating state measured features, including the appraisal that one neither caused the event nor can change it. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Towards%20a%20psychological%20construct%20of%20being%20moved%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *being moved* | Being moved is an emotional state of high intensity, often mixing sadness and joy, usually elicited by relationship and critical life events. |
| *mixed emotion* | A mixed emotion is the co-activation of positive and negative feeling found in both sad and joyful being moved. |
| *appraisal* | An appraisal is a person's evaluation of an event, and being moved went with a low sense of causing or controlling the event and high compatibility with norms and ideals. |

#### Best Possible Self (BPS) exercise

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | positive psychology (writing research) |
| Originators | King (first study, 2001), as cited in the 2019 meta-analysis by A. Carrillo, M. Rubio-Aparicio, G. Molinari, Á. Enrique, J. Sánchez-Meca, and R. M. Baños |
| Date | 2001 (first study); 2019 (PLoS One 14:e0222386) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A written instruction supplies the topic, a best possible future; the person decides its content. |
| What happens | The person writes about themselves in the best possible future, after having worked hard toward it, and in some studies spends a few minutes afterward visualizing what they wrote. A 2019 meta-analysis of 29 controlled studies with 2,909 participants found improvements over controls in well-being (d = .325), optimism (d = .334), and positive affect (d = .511), and small effects on negative affect and depressive symptoms. In the studies that compared it with gratitude exercises, it improved positive and negative affect more. |
| Steps | (1) The person reads an instruction to imagine themselves in the best possible future, after having worked hard toward it. (2) The person writes about that future self. (3) In some versions, the person then visualizes what they wrote for a set time, generally five minutes. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own picture of a future they could work toward is what the exercise helps them form; the instruction supplies only the topic, a best possible future, and the person supplies the future itself. With no one present, a written instruction was enough to produce the measured effects, and six of the studies delivered it over the internet. |
| The evidence | A meta-analysis of 29 studies supports small to moderate effects on well-being, optimism, and positive affect; moderator analyses found only trends toward larger effects for older participants and shorter total practice, and most studies compensated participants with money or university credit. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The future: The exercise is a tested way for a person alone to imagine a future self, a subject the survey found the vault does not hold. Remaining the author of one's own change: The person writes their own future self, and the instruction supplies only the topic. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The exercise builds optimism about a better future self, while the book's recognition doctrine looks to what the reader already is. |
| Limits and critiques | The effects are measured on well-being, optimism, and mood, and these sources do not test lasting change in how people live. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The Best Possible Self (BPS) exercise promotes a positive view of oneself in the best possible future, after working hard towards it." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Effects%20of%20the%20Best%20Possible%20Self%20intervention%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "The BPS is a PPI that requires participants to envision themselves in the future." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6756746/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | other. Positive psychology intervention research |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, identity self, emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; Section 1 of ops/facilitation ecology.md reports that the vault holds nothing on imagining the future; this is a tested writing exercise for it. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Effects%20of%20the%20Best%20Possible%20Self%20intervention%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC6756746/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Best Possible Self* | The Best Possible Self is a positive view of oneself in the best possible future, reached after working hard toward it. |
| *imagery component* | The imagery component is an added period of visualizing the written future self, which fifteen of the included studies used. |

#### Catharsis (katharsis)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | classical aesthetics and its later readings |
| Originators | Aristotle (Politics and Poetics); readings by F. L. Lucas, Gerald F. Else, and Gotthold Ephraim Lessing; critique by Augusto Boal; experiments on catharsis beliefs by B. J. Bushman, R. F. Baumeister, and C. M. Phillips |
| Date | Aristotle's Politics (Book 8) and Poetics; Lucas 1927; Else 1957; Bushman and colleagues 2001 (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 81:17-32) |
| Scale | a large group, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A performance or piece of music acts on spectators, who supply only their response. |
| What it holds | Aristotle first used catharsis in a mental sense in the Politics and the Poetics, comparing the effect of music and tragedy on a spectator's mind to the effect of a purge on the body; in the Politics, listeners to music that violently arouses the soul undergo a purgation and a pleasant feeling of relief. Interpreters disagree: Lucas reads it as the soul purged of excessive passions, Lessing as tragedy bringing pity and fear back to a proper balance, and Else argues that nothing in the Poetics treats the audience as patients to be cured. Experimental psychology tested the related idea that venting anger discharges it and found that people led to believe in catharsis and venting responded more aggressively to insult. |
| What is facilitated | On Aristotle's account, the spectator's relief of emotion is what the performance brings about; the work supplies the pity and fear, and the spectator supplies the feeling. With no one present to guide it, the work does this through its structure, and Augusto Boal, who sought to remove the line between spectator and actor, called this kind of catharsis very harmful, saying that everyone has the power to change and that the bourgeois theater oppresses it. |
| Across scales | Catharsis is described for a single spectator and for a theater audience, and the venting experiments show that a mechanism assumed to discharge emotion can instead rehearse it in the individual. |
| The evidence | The classical theory is interpretive and its meaning is disputed; the psychological test of the venting version found that belief in catharsis increased aggressive responding, and the Wikipedia article reports that blowing off steam may lower stress in the short term while reinforcing future outbursts. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: Boal's critique of catharsis is a critique of change that happens to spectators, made by someone who sought to turn spectators into actors. Harm and its conditions: The belief that expressing anger empties it led to more aggression in experiments, so a theory of emotional release can license the behavior it claims to discharge. Knowing that something happened: A pleasant feeling of relief after a work is Aristotle's sign of catharsis, and the venting research warns that relief in the moment can reinforce the pattern it seemed to release. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Catharsis relieves emotion through watching, while the book asks its reader to take part, and its affective practice lets a feeling complete with consent rather than purging it. |
| Limits and critiques | The theory's meaning is contested, and its popular form, venting, increased aggression in people who believed in it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "But there is not a word to support this in the "Poetics", not a hint that the end of drama is to cure or alleviate pathological states." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catharsis) "tragedy brings them back to a virtuous and happy mean" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catharsis) "In me, too, and in everyone else, there is the power to change." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catharsis) "people who had been induced to believe in the value of catharsis and venting anger responded more aggressively than did control participants to insulting criticism" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Bushman%20BJ%22%20AND%20venting%20AND%20catharsis&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Mental model | other. Classical aesthetics, with later psychological tests of the venting idea |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the affective practice completes a feeling's descent with consent between repression and venting.md`; The vault holds the book's affective practice as a third way between repression and venting; it holds Aristotle only on privation, and notes/reading is remembering.md records that the Poetics, where recognition (anagnorisis) is named, has not been researched. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catharsis; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=AUTH%3A%22Bushman%20BJ%22%20AND%20venting%20AND%20catharsis&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *catharsis* | Catharsis is Aristotle's word, borrowed from medicine, for the relief music and tragedy bring to spectators' emotions. |
| *purgation* | Purgation is Lucas's rendering, in which the soul is purged of its excessive passions. |
| *virtuous and happy mean* | The virtuous and happy mean is Lessing's reading, in which tragedy brings pity and fear back to the right level. |
| *venting* | Venting is expressing anger in order to discharge it, and people led to believe in its value responded more aggressively to insulting criticism. |

#### Change in word use over the days of expressive writing as a predictor of benefit

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | writing research (health psychology) |
| Originators | James W. Pennebaker with T. J. Mayne and M. E. Francis (1997); with J. D. Seagal (1999); R. S. Campbell with Pennebaker (2003) |
| Date | 1997 (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 72:863-871); 1999 (Journal of Clinical Psychology 55:1243-1254); 2003 (Psychological Science 14:60-65) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The writer decides all content under a standard written instruction; researchers count the words afterward, and the writer never sees the counts. |
| What it holds | Pennebaker and colleagues counted the words in people's expressive writing with a computer program, the Linguistic Inquiry and Word Count (LIWC). A reanalysis of 177 participants from six earlier writing studies found that writers who increased their use of words associated with insight and causal thinking showed better physical health afterward, though not better mental health, and that a higher use of positive relative to negative emotion words also went with better health; the same models predicted distress one year after bereavement in interviews with 30 men who had lost partners to AIDS. Pennebaker and Seagal summarized that people who benefit most use many positive-emotion words, a moderate number of negative-emotion words, and more cognitive words as the days go on, which they read as the formation of a narrative. Campbell and Pennebaker later found that flexibility in the use of personal pronouns across the writing sessions was related to fewer physician visits. |
| What is facilitated | The writer's own organizing of an upsetting experience into an account is what the instruction makes more likely, and the change in the writer's words is its measurable sign; the instruction supplies no interpretation, no questions, and no response. With no one present, the page and a fixed instruction are the whole structure, and the writer alone decides whether the account changes. |
| Across scales | Word counting works on one writer's texts and has been extended to the language of speakers in social settings, but these sources do not show that a change in a group's words predicts a change in the group. |
| The evidence | The findings are correlational reanalyses of randomized writing studies, so the words mark who benefited rather than showing what caused the benefit; the 1997 link held for physical health but not for mental health. The Wikipedia article on Pennebaker reports that function words such as pronouns have also been used to signal psychological states, social status, and interpersonal dynamics. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: A measurable shift in the writer's own words across the days of writing is a measurable sign of change that a solitary writer's text records without anyone present to judge it. Remaining the author of one's own change: The benefit went with the writer's own increasing use of causal and insight words, which the instruction invites but does not supply. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research measures physical health and distress, not recognition, and it treats benefit as coming from constructing a new account rather than from coming to know again what was already present. |
| Limits and critiques | A marker is not a method: these sources do not show that telling writers to use more causal words produces the benefit. Alone, the page cannot notice when a writer's words stay the same from day to day, which is the pattern these reanalyses associate with less benefit. |
| Quoted from its sources | "increased use of words associated with insightful and causal thinking was linked to improved physical but not mental health" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Linguistic%20predictors%20of%20adaptive%20bereavement%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "These findings suggest that the formation of a narrative is critical and is an indicator of good mental and physical health." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Forming%20a%20story%3A%20the%20health%20benefits%20of%20narrative%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "when writing about traumatic memories was related to positive health outcomes" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20secret%20life%20of%20pronouns%3A%20flexibility%20in%20writing%20style%20and%20physical%20health%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Experimental health psychology combined with computerized text analysis |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, emotion, trauma healing, grief |
| In the survey | Expressive writing; this entry adds the survey entry gives the procedure and the inhibition account; this entry adds the finding that a change in the writer's own word use over the writing days, counted by LIWC, predicted benefit, and that pronoun flexibility predicted fewer physician visits. |
| In the vault | `notes/a genuine felt shift changes what can be said next.md`; The vault holds, from Gendlin, that a real shift rearranges what can be said next; Pennebaker's word counts are an independent measurement of a change in what writers say across days, and the vault holds no expressive-writing research. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Linguistic%20predictors%20of%20adaptive%20bereavement%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Forming%20a%20story%3A%20the%20health%20benefits%20of%20narrative%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20secret%20life%20of%20pronouns%3A%20flexibility%20in%20writing%20style%20and%20physical%20health%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_W._Pennebaker |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *LIWC (Linguistic Inquiry and Word Count)* | LIWC is a program developed in the early 1990s that reports the percentage of words in a text falling into more than 80 linguistic, psychological, and topical categories. |
| *insightful and causal thinking* | Insightful and causal thinking is the word category whose rising use went with improved physical health in the 1997 reanalysis. |
| *formation of a narrative* | The formation of a narrative is Pennebaker and Seagal's account of what the helpful writing does: it organizes a complex emotional experience into a story. |
| *flexibility in pronoun use* | Flexibility in pronoun use is a change in how a writer uses personal pronouns from one session to the next, which Campbell and Pennebaker linked to health. |

#### Chills and goosebumps from recited poetry

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | neuroaesthetics and psychophysiology |
| Originators | E. Wassiliwizky, S. Koelsch, V. Wagner, T. Jacobsen, and W. Menninghaus (2017); earlier music-chills work by A. J. Blood and R. J. Zatorre (2001) |
| Date | 2017 (Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience 12:1229-1240); 2001 (PNAS 98:11818-11823) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one organism |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A recorded recitation does the work; the listener supplies only the response. |
| What it holds | Participants listened to recordings of professionally recited poems, some chosen by themselves and some by the experimenters, while chills, visible goosebumps, and physiological and brain responses were recorded. Recited poetry produced peak emotional responses, including chills and objectively measurable goosebumps that engaged reward circuitry, and peak pleasure could co-occur with physiological markers of negative affect. Chills clustered toward the end of a poem, the end of a stanza, and the end of single lines, and on a second hearing the skin-conductance response during chills habituated while the response during goosebumps increased. |
| What is facilitated | The listener's peak bodily response is what the poem's composition brings about, concentrated where a line, stanza, or poem closes; the poem supplies the timing, and the listener supplies the body that responds. With no one present, a recording of a recited poem produced these responses. |
| Across scales | The finding joins a text's structure to one listener's physiology, and chills to music and to poems share reward circuitry, with a difference in the nucleus accumbens. |
| The evidence | Two studies, 27 participants in the psychophysiological study plus a neuroimaging study, measured goosebumps objectively, and the closure pattern converged across participants for the experimenter-selected poems; Blood and Zatorre's earlier imaging study linked chills from self-selected music to regions involved in reward and emotion. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: Chills cluster where lines, stanzas, and poems close, so the placement of endings in a text sets when a reader's strongest bodily response comes. Knowing that something happened: Peak pleasure co-occurred with markers of negative affect and the chills response weakened on repetition, so chills mark an aesthetic peak rather than a lasting change. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research measures aesthetic peaks in response to poems, while the book is concerned with whether the reader changes, and chills measure a moment of response. |
| Limits and critiques | The chills response habituated on a second hearing, so part of what a poem produces depends on its being new to the listener; and the main results come from listening to professional recitations rather than silent reading, while the authors' follow-up study of self-reading is reported in supplementary material that these sources do not summarize. |
| Quoted from its sources | "recited poetry can act as a powerful stimulus for eliciting peak emotional responses, including chills and objectively measurable goosebumps" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20emotional%20power%20of%20poetry%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "chills tend to cluster (a) towards the end of a poem, (b) towards the end of a stanza, and (c) towards the end of single lines" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5597896/fullTextXML) "Whereas the pEDA during chills tended to habituate considerably in response to repetition for both subsets of poems" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5597896/fullTextXML) "We used professional recitations of poems rather than self-reading because precise timing is at high risk if participants can read passages a second time." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5597896/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences. Psychophysiology and neuroimaging of aesthetic response |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | emotion, body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the book leaves its last word structurally vacant so the reader's own amen can be the act that finishes it.md`; The vault holds the book's design of a last line left for the reader's answer; this research finds that listeners' strongest bodily responses to poems cluster at closing positions, which the vault does not hold. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20emotional%20power%20of%20poetry%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC5597896/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Intensely%20pleasurable%20responses%20to%20music%20correlate%20with%20activity%20in%20brain%20regions%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *chills* | Chills are shivers of pleasure that participants reported by pressing a button during the poem. |
| *piloerection* | Piloerection is visible goosebumps, which the study recorded on video. |
| *closure positions* | Closure positions are the ends of lines, stanzas, and whole poems, which classical rhetoric held to be emotional peaks and where chills clustered. |

#### Distanced self-talk and generic-you

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | social psychology (self-reflection) |
| Originators | E. Kross and colleagues (2014); A. Orvell, E. Kross, and S. A. Gelman (2017) |
| Date | 2014 (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 106:304-324); 2017 (Science 355:1299-1302) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The person applies the pronoun change to their own reflection; in the experiments an instruction told them which pronoun to use. |
| What it holds | In seven studies with 585 participants, Kross and colleagues found that people who referred to themselves with non-first-person pronouns or their own name, instead of I, while reflecting before a stressful social task showed more self-distancing, performed better in a first-impression task and a public speech as judged by raters, showed less distress, engaged in less maladaptive processing after the event, and appraised future stressors as challenges rather than threats; trait social anxiety did not moderate these effects. In six experiments, Orvell, Kross, and Gelman found that people use generic-you, the you that means people in general, to express norms, and that producing generic-you while reflecting on negative experiences let people normalize the experience by extending it beyond themselves. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own regulation of distress and making of meaning is what the pronoun change makes easier, and no one else supplies content or a view. With no one present, changing the word a person uses for themselves produces the observer's position that the researchers call self-distancing. |
| Across scales | Generic-you lets one person extend an experience to people in general, so a single writer's words state a norm about people in general, but the sources test the effect only in individuals. |
| The evidence | Kross's studies include experiments with rater-judged performance and a meta-analysis across studies; Orvell's six experiments show the pattern in written reflections. Both are laboratory studies of short reflections and do not test change over months. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The person changes their relation to a stressful event by changing only how they refer to themselves. Being seen and recognized by another: Addressing oneself as you or by name produces an observer's position toward oneself without an observer present, which bears on what a solitary reader can supply alone. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research aims at emotion regulation and performance under stress, and it does not test how being addressed as you by a text compares with addressing oneself as you, which is the book's situation. |
| Limits and critiques | The studies measure short-term effects in laboratory tasks and do not show that distanced self-talk changes a person over months. |
| Quoted from its sources | "using non-first-person pronouns and one's own name (rather than first-person pronouns) during introspection enhances self-distancing" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Self-talk%20as%20a%20regulatory%20mechanism%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "generic-you is used to express norms in both ordinary and emotional contexts" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22makes%20meaning%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Orvell%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Social psychology of language and self-reflection |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | emotion, identity self, meaning purpose, thought belief |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/to be addressed as you is to be summoned into being as the one who can answer.md`; The vault holds that the book's second person picks out one reader and calls a respondent into being; these studies add measured effects of a person addressing themselves as you or by name, which the vault does not hold. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Self-talk%20as%20a%20regulatory%20mechanism%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22makes%20meaning%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Orvell%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *self-distancing* | Self-distancing is taking an observer's view of one's own experience, which non-first-person self-reference increased. |
| *generic-you* | Generic-you is the use of you to make timeless statements about people in general. |
| *normalize* | To normalize, in Orvell's sense, is to treat one's own negative experience as something that happens to people generally. |

#### Distancing-Embracing model

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | empirical aesthetics |
| Originators | W. Menninghaus, V. Wagner, J. Hanich, E. Wassiliwizky, T. Jacobsen, and S. Koelsch |
| Date | 2017 (Behavioral and Brain Sciences 40:e347) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | not applicable. The model describes conditions of art reception, not a way of working. |
| What it holds | The model explains why negative emotions are central to art reception: they secure attention, intense involvement, and memorability, which artworks seek. First, distancing mechanisms that come with the schemata of art, representation, and fiction imply personal safety and control over continuing or stopping exposure, so negative emotions stay compatible with enjoyment. Then embracing components, including interplays of positive and negative emotion, the aesthetic qualities of the medium, and meaning-making, let recipients positively embrace negative emotions, and mixed emotions help integrate them into altogether pleasurable trajectories. |
| What is facilitated | The recipient's ability to take in painful emotion without being overwhelmed is what knowing that one is facing art provides, through a sense of safety and of control over whether to continue. With no one present, a work supplies these conditions by being recognizably art and by staying under the reader's control. |
| Across scales | The model describes one recipient's relation to a work; its two conditions, safety and control over exposure, resemble what a facilitator provides in a room, but the model is stated only for art reception. |
| The evidence | This is a theoretical model that reexamines classical aesthetics using recent psychological research; it synthesizes findings rather than reporting a new test. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The model names two conditions a text supplies without a facilitator: the safety of knowing it is art, and the reader's control over continuing or stopping. Harm and its conditions: The model implies that a text which removes the sense of being only a representation, or removes the reader's control over exposure, makes negative emotion harder to bear. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The model explains enjoyment of negative emotion in art, while the book asks a reader to take its content as bearing on their own life, which reduces the distance the model says makes negative emotion bearable. |
| Limits and critiques | The model concerns enjoyment and intensity during reception and does not address whether or how a reader changes afterward. |
| Quoted from its sources | "These schemata imply personal safety and control over continuing or discontinuing exposure to artworks" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20Distancing-Embracing%20model%20of%20the%20enjoyment%20of%20negative%20emotions%20in%20art%20reception%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) |
| Mental model | other. Empirical aesthetics |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/a bounded sheltering intimacy is the condition under which reverie and the inner image come forward.md`; The vault holds, as a nascent claim, one stated but not yet substantiated by a reading, that the book's images come forward in a bounded and sheltered place; the Distancing-Embracing model names conditions under which art makes negative emotion bearable, safety and control, which the vault does not hold. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20Distancing-Embracing%20model%20of%20the%20enjoyment%20of%20negative%20emotions%20in%20art%20reception%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *distancing* | Distancing is the sense of safety and control that comes from knowing one is facing art, representation, or fiction. |
| *embracing* | Embracing is actively accepting negative emotion in art because of how it is composed, presented, and given meaning. |
| *control over exposure* | Control over exposure is the recipient's freedom to continue or stop engaging with the work, part of what makes negative emotion bearable. |

#### Expressive writing after marital separation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | writing research (clinical psychology) |
| Originators | D. A. Sbarra, A. Boals, A. E. Mason, G. M. Larson, and M. R. Mehl |
| Date | 2013 (Clinical Psychological Science 1:120-134) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The writing instructions were the whole intervention; participants wrote alone. |
| What it holds | Ninety adults who had recently separated from a spouse were randomly assigned to traditional expressive writing, a new narrative-based form of expressive writing, or control writing. Up to nine months later, participants who were actively searching for meaning about their separation reported significantly worse emotional outcomes if they had been assigned to either expressive-writing condition than if they had done control writing. Within the control condition, participants actively searching for meaning reported the lowest separation-related disturbance. |
| What is facilitated | The separated person's emotional recovery is what the writing aimed to help, and for people already searching for meaning the writing slowed it. With no one present, the same instruction goes to every writer, whether or not the writer is already absorbed in the reflection it asks for. |
| The evidence | One randomized trial of 90 adults found the harm in a subgroup identified by their current search for meaning, which the authors discuss among the factors that limit and promote recovery. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The instruction that helps some writers worsened emotional outcomes for people already searching for meaning, so a text's effect depends on the state the reader brings to it. Timing and pacing: The finding suggests that asking for more reflection while a person is already searching hard for meaning can slow recovery. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The study treats an active search for meaning as a risk for writing, while the book invites reflection as part of recognition and has no way to identify readers who are already caught in such a search. |
| Limits and critiques | Alone, a text cannot tell a reader who needs to reflect more from one who needs to stop reflecting, which is the difference this trial found mattered. |
| Quoted from its sources | "participants judged to be actively engaged in a search for meaning concerning their separation reported significantly worse emotional outcomes when assigned to either EW condition relative to control writing" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Expressive%20writing%20can%20impede%20emotional%20recovery%20following%20marital%20separation%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Clinical psychology trial of expressive writing |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | grief, relationship absent, meaning purpose, emotion |
| In the survey | Expressive writing; this entry adds it adds a randomized finding that expressive writing worsened emotional outcomes for separated adults who were actively searching for meaning. |
| In the vault | `notes/a solitary reader can run every guard that declines to evaluate and none that discriminates.md`; The vault holds that a reader alone can follow rules of restraint but cannot tell which state they are in; this trial is a measured case where the needed discrimination, between writers helped and harmed, was invisible to the instruction. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Expressive%20writing%20can%20impede%20emotional%20recovery%20following%20marital%20separation%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *search for meaning* | A search for meaning is an active effort to make sense of why the separation happened; participants engaged in it did worse after expressive writing. |
| *narrative-based expressive writing* | Narrative-based expressive writing was a new form of the task tested beside the traditional one, and it did no better for those searching for meaning. |

#### Gratitude letter and gratitude visit

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | positive psychology (writing research) |
| Originators | M. E. Seligman, T. A. Steen, N. Park, and C. Peterson (2005 study that included the gratitude visit); Y. J. Wong and colleagues (2018 trial with psychotherapy clients); A. Kumar and N. Epley (2018) |
| Date | 2005 (American Psychologist 60:410-421); 2018 (Psychotherapy Research 28:192-202; Psychological Science 29:1423-1435) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people as equals, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A written instruction replaces a facilitator; the writer chooses the recipient and the words, and in the gratitude visit the recipient becomes the letter's audience. |
| What happens | The person writes a letter thanking someone in their life. The Wikipedia article on gratitude describes a randomized study with six conditions, matching the design of Seligman and colleagues' 2005 internet study, in which the gratitude visit, writing and delivering such a letter, produced the largest short-term effects, a 10 percent rise in happiness scores and a fall in depression scores lasting up to one month. In a trial with 293 adults starting university psychotherapy, those who also wrote gratitude letters reported better mental health four and twelve weeks later than those who did expressive writing or psychotherapy alone. Kumar and Epley found that letter writers underestimated how surprised and how positive recipients would feel and overestimated how awkward recipients would feel. |
| Steps | (1) The writer chooses a person in their life to thank. (2) The writer writes a letter expressing gratitude to that person. (3) In the gratitude visit, the writer delivers the letter to the person. |
| What is facilitated | The writer's articulation of what a relationship has given them is what the letter supports, and when the letter is delivered, the recipient's response turns the exercise into an exchange whose effect the writer tends to underestimate. With no one present, the instruction supplies only the form of a letter and a named recipient; the writer supplies the content and decides whether to send it. |
| Across scales | The finding that writers undervalue their letters' effect on recipients concerns pairs, and these sources do not test it for groups. |
| The evidence | The gratitude visit's gains are reported as lasting up to one month; Wong's trial is randomized, and in it lower proportions of negative emotion words in the writing mediated the benefit over expressive writing; Kumar and Epley measured recipients' actual reactions against writers' predictions in three experiments. A related exercise, keeping weekly or daily lists of things one is grateful for, heightened well-being on several but not all measures in Emmons and McCullough's three studies, most robustly positive affect. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: The largest short-term gains came when the letter was delivered, so sending turned a private exercise into an exchange with another person. Being seen and recognized by another: Recipients felt more surprised and more positive than writers predicted, so a letter's effect on its reader exceeds what its writer expects.
