The future: Writers' expectations of awkwardness and of the recipient's mood were correlated with their willingness to express gratitude, which bears on why letters go unwritten or unsent. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research concerns thanks addressed to one known person and measures happiness and depression, while the book's letters address readers the writer may not know and aim at recognition rather than gratitude. |
| Limits and critiques | The reported gains of the gratitude visit lasted up to one month, and the sources here do not compare delivered with undelivered letters. |
| Quoted from its sources | "in which participants wrote and delivered a letter of gratitude to someone in their life" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gratitude) "Participants in the gratitude condition wrote letters expressing gratitude to others" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Does%20gratitude%20writing%20improve%20the%20mental%20health%20of%20psychotherapy%20clients%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Expressers significantly underestimated how surprised recipients would be about why expressers were grateful, overestimated how awkward recipients would feel, and underestimated how positive recipients would feel." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Undervaluing%20Gratitude%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "The effect on positive affect appeared to be the most robust finding." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Counting%20blessings%20versus%20burdens%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Positive psychology intervention research |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, relationship absent, emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the book's native genre is the letter from a friend read alone.md`; The vault holds that the book's one confirmed recognition event came reading a friend's letter alone and that a letter's completion is its sending; this entry adds measured findings on letter writers and recipients, including that writers underestimate a letter's effect on the one who reads it. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gratitude; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Positive%20psychology%20progress%3A%20empirical%20validation%20of%20interventions%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Does%20gratitude%20writing%20improve%20the%20mental%20health%20of%20psychotherapy%20clients%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Undervaluing%20Gratitude%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Counting%20blessings%20versus%20burdens%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *gratitude visit* | The gratitude visit is an exercise in which a person writes a letter of gratitude to someone in their life and delivers it. |
| *gratitude writing* | Gratitude writing, in Wong's trial, is writing letters that express gratitude to others as an addition to psychotherapy. |
| *egocentric bias* | The egocentric bias is Kumar and Epley's explanation for why writers undervalue the positive impact of their letters on recipients. |

#### Identification, catharsis, and insight (the psychodynamic account of bibliotherapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | bibliotherapy (library science and psychotherapy) |
| Originators | The three-phase definition is quoted in Wikipedia from the Online Dictionary for Library and Information Science (2011); Redman and colleagues (2024) cite C. Shrodes (1949) and Arleen Hynes and Mary Hynes-Berry (1986) as the field's process models |
| Date | 1949 (Shrodes's dissertation, University of California, Berkeley); 2011 (the dictionary definition); 2024 (review protocol) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The reader works alone with a chosen text; in clinical use a professional selects the text, and the definition advises a trained psychotherapist's help. |
| What it holds | The psychodynamic account holds that fiction and poetry work through three processes: identification with a character, which gives the reader another position from which to view their own issues; catharsis, a release of emotional tension and a gaining of hope through empathy with the character; and insight into how the text's solution applies to the reader's own experience, which can lead to behavioral change. The Online Dictionary for Library and Information Science defines bibliotherapy by these three phases and adds that the assistance of a trained psychotherapist is advised. A 2024 review protocol states that existing theories of creative bibliotherapy rest on minimal empirical evidence and on the paradigm of an individual reader. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's recognizing themselves in a story, releasing feeling, and seeing their own situation anew are the processes the model names; the text supplies a character and a situation, not the reader's conclusions. With no one present, a well-chosen story can supply the other position from which to see one's situation, while the definition's advice of a trained psychotherapist marks what the model assumes a reader may need beyond the text. |
| Across scales | Redman and colleagues note that reading in groups lets readers form connections and community, which the individual-reader model leaves out, so the account written for one reader does not describe what reading together adds. |
| The evidence | The model is a theory of process with little testing: Redman and colleagues write that existing theories rest on minimal empirical evidence and that the evidence base for creative bibliotherapy is far less developed than for self-help bibliotherapy. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The model's first process is the reader recognizing themselves in a character, which is recognition of oneself through another person's story rather than recognition of what one already is. Knowing that something happened: The model's stages are inferred rather than measured, so it offers words for what happened in reading without a way to confirm it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The model locates change in identifying with another person's story, while the book locates its promised event in the reader recognizing what they already are. |
| Limits and critiques | Alone, the text cannot select itself: the model assumes someone chose a work that fits the reader, and the definition advises a trained psychotherapist's assistance. |
| Quoted from its sources | "From a psychodynamic perspective, fictional materials are believed to be effective through the processes of identification, catharsis and insight." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliotherapy) "Assistance of a trained psychotherapist is advised." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliotherapy) "Existing theories are based on minimal empirical evidence and are largely based on the individual reader paradigm" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10936023/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | psychodynamic |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | emotion, identity self, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | Bibliotherapy; this entry adds the survey entry names the forms of bibliotherapy; this entry adds the psychodynamic process model of identification, catharsis, and insight, its named sources, and the 2024 statement that its evidence is minimal. |
| In the vault | notes/recognition is participatory, a move the reader makes rather than a content the reader receives.md; The vault holds that recognition is a move the reader makes; the bibliotherapy model names the reader's identification with a character as its first step, which differs in what is recognized, and the vault holds no bibliotherapy research. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliotherapy; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC10936023/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *identification* | Identification is the reader's seeing themselves in a character, which gives an alternative position from which to view their own issues. |
| *catharsis* | Catharsis, in this account, is a release of emotional tension and a gaining of hope through empathy with the character. |
| *insight* | Insight is the reader's understanding of how the solution in the text applies to their own experience. |
| *creative bibliotherapy* | Creative bibliotherapy is the reading and discussion of fiction, short stories, picture books, and poetry, as distinct from self-help books. |

#### Identity theme (Holland's reader-response model)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | literary theory (psychoanalytic reader-response) |
| Originators | Norman N. Holland, with colleagues at the State University of New York at Buffalo |
| Date | 1968 (The Dynamics of Literary Response); 1975 (5 Readers Reading) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The text is read alone; the model concerns what the reader brings to it. |
| What it holds | Holland's first model, in The Dynamics of Literary Response (1968), held that a reader takes in a process of transformation from unconscious fantasy toward conscious significance that is embodied in the work. After recording actual readers' responses, he found variations too great for a model in which responses are mostly alike, and in 5 Readers Reading (1975), built on five university students' free associations to three short stories, he concluded that readers re-create their own identities when they respond. Each reader has a core identity theme and builds a response from the work, shared codes, variable conventions, and a personal style of reading, so that responses are both like and unlike other readers'. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's re-creation of their own identity in response to a text is what Holland describes; the text supplies material, and the reader's identity shapes what the response becomes. With no one present, the reading still happens, and on this account its shape comes more from the reader than from the text, which does not explain how a text alone could change the reader's identity. |
| The evidence | The model rests on case studies of a few readers' free associations, and the Wikipedia statements summarizing its conclusions carry citation-needed tags; reader-response critics divide between those who treat individual differences among readers as central and those who try to get around them. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Holland found readers' experiences shaped by their identities more than by the texts, so the reader decides much of what a text's content becomes. Recognition: If readers re-create their own identities when they respond, a reader's recognition in a text may be recognition of the self they brought, which the book would need to tell apart from recognizing what they are. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book hopes reading can occasion a recognition that changes the reader, and the account summarized here does not describe how a response could change an identity theme. |
| Limits and critiques | The evidence is five readers' free associations, and the summaries of the model's conclusions carry unresolved citation tags. |
| Quoted from its sources | "readers re-create their own identities when they respond" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_N._Holland) "Holland found variations too great to fit this model in which responses are mostly alike but show minor individual variations" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reader-response_criticism) |
| Mental model | psychodynamic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `provenance/transmission digs/dig 2 — the readers half of the transaction.md`; Dig 2 holds Rosenblatt, Iser, Barthes, Gadamer, and Fish, and sets Fish aside because communities constitute the text; Holland is the psychoanalytic reader-response critic the dig did not treat, and he places the variation among readers in individual identities rather than in communities. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_N._Holland; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reader-response_criticism |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *identity theme* | An identity theme is the core pattern of a person's behavior, understood like a musical theme and its variations, which Holland held shapes how that person reads. |
| *introjection* | Introjection, in Holland's first model, is the reader's taking in of the transformation from fantasy to meaning that the work embodies. |

#### Iso principle

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | music therapy |
| Originators | I. M. Altshuler (music therapy at Eloise Hospital), whose 1944 paper is cited as its early theoretical source |
| Date | 1944 (American Journal of Psychiatry 100:792-794); tested 2014-2025 |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. When recorded music is sequenced in advance, the sequence does the work; in therapy a music therapist chooses it. |
| What it holds | The iso principle holds that a listener should first hear music that matches their current mood and then music that shifts gradually toward a desired mood. It is traced to Altshuler's 1944 paper, has been included in modern music therapy manuals, and has been transferred to other creative therapies such as dance therapy; in the 1950s it was mainly tied to matching the music's tempo to the listener's mental tempo. In a 2021 experiment after a sadness induction, participants who heard sad music followed by happy music ended with the highest positive affect and lowest negative affect of four sequences, though not all differences were significant. |
| Steps | (1) The listener's current mood is identified. (2) Music matching that mood is played first. (3) The following pieces shift gradually in tempo and mood toward the desired state. |
| What is facilitated | The listener's own movement from one mood to another is what the sequence makes easier; the music supplies a sequence of moods, and the listener's own mood is what changes. With no one present, a pre-recorded sequence can apply the principle, and recent studies deliver it by app, web platform, or playlist, but a sequence fixed in advance cannot identify the listener's present mood, which the principle requires. |
| Across scales | The principle of matching a person's present state before changing it is stated and tested for one listener, and a group, whose members are in different moods, is not tested in these sources. |
| The evidence | Support is mixed: the 2021 experiment with healthy participants favored the sad-then-happy sequence without every difference reaching significance; a 2014 trial with transplant patients found unstructured music improved mood more within the group than iso-principle music therapy; and a 2025 feasibility trial found an iso-principle playlist no more effective than a control condition for agitation in dementia. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The principle orders a sequence so that the music first matches a person's current mood before changing toward another mood, a pacing rule that a text could also follow. Who decides the content: When the sequence is fixed in advance, its designer decides the sequence, and the listener's actual mood decides whether the sequence fits. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The iso principle moves a listener toward a mood its designer chooses, while the book does not set a target mood for its reader. |
| Limits and critiques | In two of the three studies here, iso-principle music showed no clear advantage over its comparison, which was unstructured music in one and a book-reading audio script in the other, so the principle's added value is not established. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The iso principle comprises listening to music that matches the current mood of patients at first, and then to gradually shift to music that represents a desired mood." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Emotion%20Modulation%20through%20Music%20after%20Sadness%20Induction%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "An early theoretical approach suggested that patients should listen to pieces of music according to the iso principle" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC8656869/fullTextXML) "the within-group effect was greater for UM participants compared to MT participants" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Music%20therapy%20for%20patients%20who%20have%20undergone%20hematopoietic%20stem%20cell%20transplant%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "The intervention was not more effective than the control condition in de-escalating agitation" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Individualized%20Music%20Playlist%20Based%20on%20Iso-Principle%20for%20De-Escalating%20Agitation%20of%20People%20With%20Dementia%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. Music therapy; the principle has also been transferred to dance therapy |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the book asks only for attention until it has given the reader something to act with.md`; The vault holds that the book paces its requests, asking only for attention before assigning a task; the iso principle is a named pacing rule from music therapy that the vault does not hold. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Emotion%20Modulation%20through%20Music%20after%20Sadness%20Induction%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC8656869/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Music%20therapy%20for%20patients%20who%20have%20undergone%20hematopoietic%20stem%20cell%20transplant%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Individualized%20Music%20Playlist%20Based%20on%20Iso-Principle%20for%20De-Escalating%20Agitation%20of%20People%20With%20Dementia%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=%28ABSTRACT%3A%22isoprinciple%22%20OR%20ABSTRACT%3A%22iso%20principle%22%20OR%20ABSTRACT%3A%22iso-principle%22%29&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=8 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *iso principle* | The iso principle is starting with music that matches the listener's current mood and then shifting gradually toward a desired mood. |
| *mental tempo* | Mental tempo is the listener's inner pace, which 1950s formulations asked the music's tempo to fit. |

#### Journaling about stressful events with an emotion-only focus versus a thoughts-and-emotions focus

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | writing research (behavioral medicine) |
| Originators | P. M. Ullrich and S. K. Lutgendorf |
| Date | 2002 (Annals of Behavioral Medicine 24:244-250) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The journaling instruction sets the focus of attention; the writer supplies all content. |
| What it holds | One hundred twenty-two students were randomly assigned to journal for a month about a trauma or stressor with one of two instructions, either to focus on the emotions alone or to focus on both the thoughts and the emotions, or to write factually about media events. Writers told to attend to thoughts and emotions developed greater awareness of positive benefits of the stressful event, an effect apparently mediated by more cognitive processing during writing. Writers told to focus on emotions alone reported more severe illness symptoms during the study, an effect that appeared to be mediated by a greater focus on negative emotional expression. |
| What is facilitated | The writer's making sense of a stressful event is what the journal supports, and the wording of the prompt decides whether writing moves toward sense-making or stays with negative feeling. With no one present, the prompt's wording directs the writer's attention, as a listener's questions otherwise would. |
| The evidence | One randomized trial with students over one month supports the pattern; the claims about what mediated each effect are the authors' statistical inferences, which they state as apparent rather than proven. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: A prompt that asked only for feelings went with more illness symptoms, so an instruction's focus can make solitary writing worse. Who decides the content: Even with no facilitator present, the instruction decides where attention goes while the writer decides what is written. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's affective practice asks a reader to feel a feeling fully and let it complete with consent, while this study found that writing focused on emotions alone, without thinking them through, went with more illness symptoms; the two are different practices, and the study does not test the book's. |
| Limits and critiques | Alone, a page cannot tell whether a writer is working an emotion through or repeating it, and the emotion-only writers received no signal that their writing was associated with more symptoms. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Writers focusing on emotions alone reported more severe illness symptoms during the study than those in other conditions." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Journaling%20about%20stressful%20events%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Writers focusing on cognitions and emotions developed greater awareness of the positive benefits of the stressful event than the other two groups." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Journaling%20about%20stressful%20events%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Behavioral medicine research on expressive writing |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, thought belief, trauma healing, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | Expressive writing; this entry adds it adds a randomized comparison of prompt wording in month-long journaling, in which an emotion-only focus went with more illness symptoms and a thoughts-and-emotions focus with more benefit-finding. |
| In the vault | `notes/the affective practice completes a feeling's descent with consent between repression and venting.md`; The vault holds the book's affective practice as a third way between walling a feeling off and venting it; this study adds measured evidence that emotion-only writing, without cognitive processing, went with more reported illness symptoms. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Journaling%20about%20stressful%20events%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *cognitive processing* | Cognitive processing is thinking through an event's causes and meaning while writing, which the combined instruction asked for. |
| *emotional expression* | Emotional expression is writing out one's feelings about the event; asked for alone, it went with more reported illness symptoms. |

#### Kama muta

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | emotion research (relational models theory) |
| Originators | Alan Fiske, T. W. Schubert, and B. Seibt; the 19-nation study led by J. H. Zickfeld |
| Date | 2017 (Fiske, Schubert, and Seibt's naming chapter); 2019 (Emotion 19:402-424) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people as equals, a society's customs |
| Who decides | not applicable. The entry describes an emotion, not a way of working. |
| What it holds | Kama muta, Sanskrit for moved by love, is the name Fiske and colleagues gave to the emotion English speakers call being moved, touched, or heart-warmed, which they hold is evoked by experiencing or observing a sudden intensification of communal sharing, the relation in which people perceive their equivalence or oneness with others. In 19 countries and 15 languages with 3,542 participants, it came with a warm feeling in the chest, moist eyes or tears, chills or goosebumps, a lump in the throat, buoyancy, and exhilaration, and it motivated devotion and moral commitment to communal sharing. It can be evoked by watching others, for example in videos of cute targets, such as kittens, interacting affectionately. |
| What is facilitated | The emotion responds to a sudden strengthening of a bond, one's own or one observed between others, so a story, song, or video can evoke it in a person alone. With no one present, a work can show communal sharing intensifying and evoke the emotion, but the devotion it motivates is directed to a relationship the work cannot itself provide. |
| Across scales | The emotion is felt by one person but responds to relationships at many scales, and its vernacular labels include patriotic feeling and being touched by the Spirit, so an individual emotion tracks bonds in pairs, groups, nations, and religious communities. |
| The evidence | Cross-cultural questionnaire studies with a validated scale found the five facets correlated in every sample, with some cultural variation; the theory's assumptions that emotions are universal evolved mechanisms with a measurable footprint are stated as assumptions. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The emotion responds to a sudden sense of oneness with another, which a text can evoke by showing such a moment between others. Knowing that something happened: Tears and chills while reading can mark kama muta, a response to a felt intensification of connection, so these signs alone do not show that a reader recognized anything about themselves. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Kama muta theory explains being moved by relational oneness, while the book's promised event is recognition of what one already is, which the theory does not address. |
| Limits and critiques | The measures are self-reports of sensations and feelings, and the theory's universality is an assumption the studies test only with questionnaires. |
| Quoted from its sources | "kama muta is a distinct positive social relational emotion that is evoked by experiencing or observing a sudden intensification of communal sharing" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22kama%20muta%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Fiske%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "It motivates affective devotion and moral commitment to communal sharing." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22kama%20muta%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Fiske%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "Communal sharing relations are those in which individuals are brought to the perception of their equivalence, or oneness with a set of individuals under a common and equalizing characteristic, or feature." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kama_muta) |
| Mental model | other. Emotion research built on relational models theory |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship present, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/another person can only sound the note already strung in you so influence awakens rather than implants.md`; The vault holds the book's claim that another person's influence can only bring out what is already in the reader rather than install something new; kama muta research gives being moved a measured cause, a sudden intensification of communal sharing, which the vault does not hold. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22kama%20muta%22%20AND%20AUTH%3A%22Fiske%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kama_muta |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *kama muta* | Kama muta is Sanskrit for moved by love, the name for the emotion evoked by a sudden intensification of communal sharing. |
| *communal sharing* | Communal sharing is a relation in which people perceive their equivalence or oneness with others, one of the four basic kinds of social relation in relational models theory. |
| *lexical fallacy* | The lexical fallacy is Fiske's term for mistaking vernacular emotion words for psychological entities, which the Sanskrit name is meant to avoid. |

#### Meta-analyses of written emotional expression (experimental disclosure)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | writing research (health psychology) |
| Originators | J. M. Smyth (1998); J. Frattaroli (2006); the paradigm from James W. Pennebaker and S. K. Beall (1986) |
| Date | 1986 (Journal of Abnormal Psychology 95:274-281); 1998 (Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology 66:174-184); 2006 (Psychological Bulletin 132:823-865) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A written instruction replaces the facilitator; the writer chooses the topic and everything written. |
| What it holds | In the standard paradigm a written instruction asks people to write on three or four days, for as little as fifteen minutes a day, about their deepest thoughts and feelings concerning a traumatic experience or an important emotional issue, and to keep writing until the time is up, while control participants write about neutral topics. Smyth's 1998 synthesis found improved reported physical health, psychological well-being, physiological functioning, and general functioning in healthy participants, no change in health behaviors, and a rise in distress immediately after writing that was unrelated to later health outcomes. Frattaroli's 2006 random-effects meta-analysis of 146 randomized studies found a positive and significant average effect of r = .075 and identified several moderators. |
| What is facilitated | The writer's own putting into words of an experience they had not fully expressed is what the instruction makes easier; the instruction supplies permission, a time limit, several sessions, confidentiality, and freedom from correct spelling and grammar, and it supplies no content and no one to receive what is written. With no one present, these written conditions are all the structure the writer has, and the writer goes through the immediate rise in distress alone. |
| Across scales | The disclosure studied here is one writer's, and these sources do not test whether putting an experience into words works the same way for a pair or a group. |
| The evidence | Two meta-analyses support a small positive average effect, and Frattaroli notes that the earlier syntheses used fixed-effects methods, which limits how far they generalize; moderators include whether participants were students, gender, the length of the task, publication status, and the specific writing instructions. The Wikipedia article on writing therapy reports that the inhibition theory behind the method has mixed empirical support. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: Writing raised distress right after each session even when it helped later, so a writer alone experiences the distress before the benefit. The container that holds people while they change: The instruction sets up all of the writer's conditions in writing: a fixed time, several sessions, confidentiality, and freedom from correctness. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The paradigm aims at health outcomes measured against a neutral-writing control, while the book aims at recognition and does not measure its reader. |
| Limits and critiques | Writers often feel worse immediately after a session, the average effect is small, and nothing in the text can notice or respond when a writer is overwhelmed. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Writing also increased immediate (pre- to postwriting) distress, which was unrelated to health outcomes." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Written%20emotional%20expression%3A%20effect%20sizes%2C%20outcome%20types%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "experimental disclosure is effective, with a positive and significant average r-effect size of .075" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Experimental%20disclosure%20and%20its%20moderators%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "The only rule is that once you begin writing, you continue until the time is up." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing_therapy) "Although many reported being upset by the writing experience, they also found it valuable and meaningful." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing_therapy) |
| Mental model | other. Experimental health psychology |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, memory past, trauma healing, body |
| In the survey | Expressive writing; this entry adds the survey entry says writers often feel worse after a session and that benefits vary; this entry adds the two meta-analytic effect sizes, the finding that the immediate distress was unrelated to later outcomes, the moderators, and the standard instruction's own words. |
| In the vault | `notes/a genuine transformation registers first as loss rather than as gain.md`; The vault holds, from Iser and Barthes, that genuine reading-change registers first as loss; Smyth's measured rise in distress after writing is an empirical counterpart from writing research, which the vault does not otherwise hold. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Written%20emotional%20expression%3A%20effect%20sizes%2C%20outcome%20types%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Experimental%20disclosure%20and%20its%20moderators%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Confronting%20a%20traumatic%20event%3A%20toward%20an%20understanding%20of%20inhibition%20and%20disease%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing_therapy; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Forming%20a%20story%3A%20the%20health%20benefits%20of%20narrative%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *experimental disclosure* | Experimental disclosure is Frattaroli's name for studies that randomly assign people to disclose information, thoughts, and feelings about personal and meaningful topics. |
| *written emotional expression* | Written emotional expression is Smyth's name for the writing task whose health effects he synthesized. |
| *immediate distress* | Immediate distress is the rise in negative mood from before to after a writing session, which Smyth found unrelated to later outcomes. |

#### Narrative transportation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | social psychology (narrative persuasion) |
| Originators | M. C. Green and T. C. Brock |
| Date | 2000 (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 79:701-721) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The story does the work on the reader, who contributes imagery, emotion, and attention. |
| What it holds | Green and Brock defined transportation as absorption into a story, involving mental imagery, emotion, and focused attention, and built a scale to measure it. Across four experiments, more transported readers held more story-consistent beliefs, evaluated the protagonists more favorably, and found fewer false notes in the story; reducing transportation through reading instructions reduced these effects; and labeling the story as fact or as fiction generally made no difference. Later work describes transported readers as less likely to challenge a story's content and reports a sleeper effect, in which the attitude change grows more pronounced over time. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's absorption into a story is what the text brings about, and absorption also changes beliefs, since the transported reader adopts story-consistent beliefs and notices fewer false notes. With no one present, a story does this through imagery, emotion, and attention alone, and it lowers the critical check a reader would otherwise bring. |
| Across scales | The effect is measured in individual readers of stories addressed to the public, and these sources do not measure it for a group reading together. |
| The evidence | Four experiments with 69 to 274 participants, one of which manipulated transportation directly, support the effect; the Wikipedia article reports a later meta-analysis of narrative persuasion by Braddock and Dillard. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: Transported readers found fewer false notes, so part of the conviction an absorbing text produces comes from the absorption itself. Remaining the author of one's own change: Transportation reduces counterarguing, so a reader absorbed in a story has less of the scrutiny that would let them decide what to accept. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research treats transportation as a route to persuasion, while the book wants to be answered rather than believed, which places it against the effect this research measures. |
| Limits and critiques | The same absorption that lets a story change a reader's beliefs lowers the reader's scrutiny of it, and the text itself offers no check on what the reader has been persuaded to accept. |
| Quoted from its sources | "highly transported readers found fewer false notes in a story than less-transported readers" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20role%20of%20transportation%20in%20the%20persuasiveness%20of%20public%20narratives%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "transportation and corresponding beliefs were generally unaffected by labeling a story as fact or as fiction" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20role%20of%20transportation%20in%20the%20persuasiveness%20of%20public%20narratives%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "when exposed to the narrative, the receiver is less likely to challenge the story's content" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_theory_(psychology)) |
| Mental model | other. Social psychology of persuasion |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the book is one of the automatisms it must interrupt.md`; The vault holds that a fluent book induces a reading-trance whose bodily assent it cannot tell from recognition; transportation research measures that absorption's effect on belief and scrutiny, which the vault does not hold. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20role%20of%20transportation%20in%20the%20persuasiveness%20of%20public%20narratives%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_theory_(psychology) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *transportation* | Transportation is absorption into a story, combining imagery, emotion, and focused attention. |
| *false notes* | False notes are parts of a story a reader judges untrue or unconvincing, and transported readers found fewer of them. |
| *reduced counterarguing* | Reduced counterarguing is a transported reader's lower tendency to challenge the story's content. |
| *sleeper effect* | The sleeper effect, in Appel and Richter's use, is attitude change after transportation that becomes more pronounced over time. |

#### Parable (C. H. Dodd's definition)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | biblical scholarship (the parables of Jesus) |
| Originators | C. H. Dodd (The Parables of the Kingdom); the parables of Jesus in the Gospels |
| Date | 1935 (The Parables of the Kingdom, Nisbet, London) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, a small group, a society's customs |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The teller supplies a story and withholds its application; the hearer or reader decides what it means. |
| What it holds | Dodd defined the parable, at its simplest, as a metaphor or simile drawn from nature or common life that arrests the hearers by its vividness or strangeness and leaves the mind in enough doubt about its precise application to tease it into active thought. A teaching page on the parables adds that most parables contain some strange element that should make the hearer stop and think and that most are open-ended, and a journal article reads Dodd's definition as provoking a labour of interpretation rather than indicating a single meaning. In the Gospels, when the disciples ask what a parable meant, Jesus answers that the secrets have been given to them but that he speaks to others in parables so that looking they may not perceive; Mark and Matthew suggest he explained everything privately to his disciples, a claim some modern scholars do not accept. |
| What is facilitated | The hearer's own working-out of what a story means for their life is what the parable provokes; the teller supplies a strange detail and withholds the application. With no one present, a written parable still leaves the reader in doubt, which is its method, but it cannot help a reader who stops at the story, and the Gospels themselves record private explanations to the disciples. |
| Across scales | A parable addresses a crowd and each hearer at once, and the Gospel passage divides hearers into those given the secrets and those who look without perceiving, so one text acts differently on insiders and outsiders. |
| The evidence | This is a scholarly definition of a literary form, not an empirical finding; secondary sources quote it with small differences in wording and page number (p. 5 of a 1961 edition on one page, p. 16 on another), so the exact wording should be checked against the book. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The parable leaves its application to the hearer, so any conclusion drawn is the hearer's own. Recognition: A parable drawn from common life can let a hearer recognize their own situation in it without being told, so the recognition is arrived at by the hearer rather than stated by the teller. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The parables serve a theological teaching about the kingdom of God and, in the Gospel account, divide insiders from outsiders, while the book does not divide its readers or teach a doctrine through withheld meaning. |
| Limits and critiques | A parable's doubt can end in misreading; the teaching page warns that a comparison is not an identity and that parables can be misinterpreted or misapplied. |
| Quoted from its sources | "At its simplest the parable is a metaphor or simile drawn from nature or common life, arresting the hearers by its vividness or strangeness, and leaving the mind in sufficient doubt about its precise application to tease it into active thought." (https://hts.org.za/index.php/hts/article/view/3886/9813) "Most parables are open-ended." (https://catholic-resources.org/Bible/Parables.htm) "looking they may not perceive, and listening they may not understand" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parables_of_Jesus) |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic. Biblical scholarship on the parables of Jesus; the Hebrew word mashal also covered maxims, riddles, and fables |
| Structure | none of the families; a single move |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, spirit ultimate, thought belief |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/double reflection withholds the result so the reader must reproduce it in his own existence.md`; The vault holds Kierkegaard's withholding of the result so the reader must reproduce it, and holds koans with their checking teacher in the transmission digs; Dodd's account of the parable, a text that withholds its application, is not in the vault. |
| Sources | https://hts.org.za/index.php/hts/article/view/3886/9813; https://catholic-resources.org/Bible/Parables.htm; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parables_of_Jesus; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._H._Dodd |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *parable* | A parable is a short comparison or story drawn from ordinary life that leaves its application for the hearer to work out. |
| *tease it into active thought* | Tease it into active thought is Dodd's phrase for the parable's aim, which is leaving enough doubt about its application that the hearer must think it through. |
| *mashal* | Mashal is the Hebrew word for parable, which also covered wisdom genres from maxims to riddles and fables. |

#### Poetry therapy (poemtherapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | poetry therapy |
| Originators | Eli Greifer, a poet, pharmacist, and lawyer; psychiatrists Jack J. Leedy and Sam Spector; the Association for Poetry Therapy (1969), later the National Association for Poetry Therapy (NAPT) |
| Date | 1928 (Greifer's campaign); 1959 (poetry therapy group at Cumberland Hospital); 1969 (Association for Poetry Therapy founded; Leedy, Poetry Therapy, J. B. Lippincott) |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The poetry therapist selects poems and prompts; the participants supply their responses and their own writing. |
| What happens | A poetry therapist selects poems the participants can relate to, reads them with an individual or group, and invites participants to write either a response to the poem or original material drawn from their own experiences and emotions. The selection follows the isoprinciple, which Leedy set out in 1969 and which a 2022 study summarizes as the importance of participants relating to or resonating with the poems shared. NAPT describes its members as working in prisons, support groups, schools, hospitals, and veterans' clinics. |
| Steps | (1) The therapist selects a poem the participants can relate to or resonate with (the isoprinciple). (2) The poem is read with the participant or group. (3) Participants write, either a response to the poem or original material from their own experience. (4) In the interactive form, the group discusses participants' personal reactions to the material. |
| What is facilitated | The participant's own expression of feeling and experience is what poetry therapy facilitates, first through a poem that matches the participant's state and then through the invitation to write. With no one present, a poem can still match a reader's state, and what the method adds is a person who chooses the poem for this participant and asks for their written response. |
| Across scales | The rule of matching a person's present state before asking anything of them is stated for poems in poetry therapy and for music in music therapy, and these sources apply it to individuals and small groups only. |
| The evidence | The sources here are the practitioners' own history and one qualitative study that used the isoprinciple to select poems; no controlled outcome study of poetry therapy is among them. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The isoprinciple asks that a person first be given a poem that matches their state before being asked to write. Recognition: A poem chosen because the reader can relate to it offers the reader their own experience in someone else's words. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Poetry therapy uses other poets' work chosen by a therapist for a participant's current state, while the book's poems and quotations are fixed in advance for every reader, so no one matches a poem to a reader's present mood. |
| Limits and critiques | The method depends on the facilitator's choice of poem, and a fixed text cannot choose a different poem for a reader whose state it does not match. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In 1969, they joined with Dr. Jack Leedy to found the Association for Poetry Therapy." (https://poetrytherapy.org/History) "recognition of the beneficial potential of having clients write either their response to poems written by others or original material, drawing on the clients’ own experiences and emotions" (https://poetrytherapy.org/History) "which expresses the importance of participants relating to or resonating with the poems that are shared" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9634331/fullTextXML) "NAPT represents the language-oriented branch of expressive arts therapies." (https://poetrytherapy.org/mission-history) |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. NAPT describes poetry therapy as the language-oriented branch of the expressive arts therapies |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, grief, identity self |
| In the survey | Bibliotherapy; this entry adds the survey entry mentions poetry among reading materials; this entry adds poetry therapy's history as its own practice, its isoprinciple for selecting poems, and its invitation to write in response. |
| In the vault | `notes/all six doctrine concerns appear in the first fifteen poems.md`; The vault reads the author's own poems as the book's primary layer; it holds no poetry therapy, the field that uses poems chosen for a reader's state as a facilitative tool, and the survey found voice and music nearly absent from the vault. |
| Sources | https://poetrytherapy.org/History; https://poetrytherapy.org/mission-history; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9634331/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *isoprinciple* | The isoprinciple is Leedy's rule for selecting poems: participants should be able to relate to or resonate with the poem that is shared. |
| *poemtherapy* | Poemtherapy was Eli Greifer's name for the group practice he began in the 1950s. |
| *interactive process* | The interactive process is planned discussion of the reader's personal reactions to the material, which the older library practice of bibliotherapy did not include. |

#### Positive self-statements: power for some, peril for others

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | social psychology (self-esteem) |
| Originators | J. V. Wood, W. Q. Perunovic, and J. W. Lee |
| Date | 2009 (Psychological Science 20:860-866) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A sentence supplied by the experimenter, as a self-help text would supply it, is repeated by the person, who decides nothing about its content. |
| What it holds | A survey found that people often use positive self-statements and believe they work. In two experiments, participants with low self-esteem who repeated the statement I'm a lovable person, or who focused on how it was true, felt worse than those who did not repeat it or who focused on how it was both true and not true. Participants with high self-esteem felt better after repeating it, but only to a limited degree. |
| What is facilitated | The person's view of themselves is what the statement tries to change, and a sentence that contradicts that view made the people who most needed it feel worse. With no one present, a text that hands the reader a conclusion about themselves cannot check whether the reader believes it. |
| The evidence | A survey and two experiments; the harm appeared among people with low self-esteem and disappeared when they considered how the statement was both true and not true. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: A reassuring sentence backfired for the people who most needed reassurance, so a text's kind words can harm when they contradict what the reader believes about themselves. Who decides the content: The harm came from a conclusion supplied to the person, and letting the person weigh how it was both true and not true removed the harm. Recognition: A statement of worth that the reader did not already recognize as true worked against them, which bears on the difference between telling a reader what they are and letting them recognize it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book tells its reader that they already know everything in it; for a reader who does not believe this, the sentence may work like the statements tested here, which is this researcher's reading and not something the studies tested. |
| Limits and critiques | The studies measured mood and self-esteem immediately after the task and do not show long-term effects. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Positive self-statements are widely believed to boost mood and self-esteem, yet their effectiveness has not been demonstrated." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Positive%20self-statements%3A%20power%20for%20some%2C%20peril%20for%20others%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "or who focused on how that statement was true felt worse than those who did not repeat the statement or who focused on how it was both true and not true" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Positive%20self-statements%3A%20power%20for%20some%2C%20peril%20for%20others%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Social psychology of self-esteem |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a single move |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, thought belief |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/a book cannot supply the basic trust a reader who never had a reliable other lacks.md`; The vault holds, from Winnicott at second hand, that a book cannot supply basic trust to a reader who lacks it; this entry adds a measured case in which reassuring self-statements made people with low self-esteem feel worse. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Positive%20self-statements%3A%20power%20for%20some%2C%20peril%20for%20others%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *positive self-statement* | A positive self-statement is a sentence affirming one's own worth that a person repeats to themselves. |
| *low self-esteem* | Low self-esteem marked the group for whom repeating the statement backfired. |

#### Reading literary fiction improves theory of mind (the claim and its replications)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | cognitive and social psychology of fiction |
| Originators | D. C. Kidd and E. Castano (2013, 2018); M. E. Panero and colleagues (2016); D. Dodell-Feder and D. I. Tamir (2018); the simulation account of R. A. Mar and K. Oatley (2008) |
| Date | 2013 (Science 342:377-380); 2016 (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 111:e46-e54); 2018 (Social Psychological and Personality Science; Journal of Experimental Psychology: General 147:1713-1727) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. Participants read an assigned passage alone before a test. |
| What it holds | Kidd and Castano reported five experiments in which reading a passage of literary fiction, compared with nonfiction, popular fiction, or nothing, improved performance on tests of theory of mind, the ability to understand others' mental states, and concluded that literary fiction temporarily enhances it. Three independent groups with 792 participants found no advantage for literary fiction on the Reading the Mind in the Eyes Test, while lifetime exposure to fiction predicted scores in every condition, and Kidd and Castano's own three preregistered replications gave two uninformative failures and one success. A 2018 meta-analysis of 14 studies found that reading fiction produces a small improvement in social-cognitive performance (g = .15 to .16) compared with nonfiction or no reading, and Mar and Oatley had argued that fiction offers simulations of the social world through abstraction, simplification, and compression. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's understanding of other minds is the capacity reading is claimed to exercise; the text supplies simulated people and situations, and the reader supplies the inferences about them. With no one present, a single reading produced at most a small and unreliable change on a laboratory test, and the more consistent association is with a lifetime of reading. |
| Across scales | Mar and Oatley argue that fiction helps readers understand people unlike themselves, a claim about relations between people, but the measured effects are within one reader on a test and do not show changes in actual relationships. |
| The evidence | The single-passage effect failed an independent replication with 792 participants and succeeded in one of three of the original authors' preregistered attempts; the meta-analysis finds a small average effect and calls for stronger reading manipulations and tests of transfer to real-world social functioning. Panero and colleagues judge the most plausible link to be that people with strong theory of mind are drawn to fiction or that a lifetime of reading strengthens it, with verbal ability possibly also involved. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: A widely reported single-reading effect shrank or vanished under replication, which cautions against treating one reading's felt effect as a lasting change. Being seen and recognized by another: Fiction exercises the reader's modeling of other minds, which is seeing others rather than being seen, and the measured gains are small. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The research measures a skill of reading other people, while the book aims at the reader's recognition of themselves, which none of these studies measure. |
| Limits and critiques | The single-passage claim did not hold up reliably, and the meta-analysis notes that transfer to real-world social functioning has not been assessed. |
| Quoted from its sources | "reading literary fiction temporarily enhances ToM" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Reading%20literary%20fiction%20improves%20theory%20of%20mind%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "we found no significant advantage in RMET scores for literary fiction compared to any of the other conditions" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Does%20reading%20a%20single%20passage%20of%20literary%20fiction%20really%20improve%20theory%20of%20mind%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "revealed two uninformative failures to replicate and one successful replication" (https://api.crossref.org/works?query.bibliographic=Reading%20Literary%20Fiction%20and%20Theory%20of%20Mind%3A%20Three%20Preregistered%20Replications%20and%20Extensions%20of%20Kidd%20and%20Castano&rows=2) "fiction reading leads to a small, statistically significant improvement in social-cognitive performance (g = .15-.16)" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Fiction%20reading%20has%20a%20small%20positive%20impact%20on%20social%20cognition%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "They offer models or simulations of the social world via abstraction, simplification, and compression." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20function%20of%20fiction%20is%20the%20abstraction%20and%20simulation%20of%20social%20experience%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Cognitive and social psychology of reading |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, thought belief, emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no research on fiction and social cognition; its reader-response holdings are theories of meaning in provenance/transmission digs/dig 2 — the readers half of the transaction.md. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Reading%20literary%20fiction%20improves%20theory%20of%20mind%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Does%20reading%20a%20single%20passage%20of%20literary%20fiction%20really%20improve%20theory%20of%20mind%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://api.crossref.org/works?query.bibliographic=Reading%20Literary%20Fiction%20and%20Theory%20of%20Mind%3A%20Three%20Preregistered%20Replications%20and%20Extensions%20of%20Kidd%20and%20Castano&rows=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Fiction%20reading%20has%20a%20small%20positive%20impact%20on%20social%20cognition%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22The%20function%20of%20fiction%20is%20the%20abstraction%20and%20simulation%20of%20social%20experience%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *theory of mind* | Theory of mind is the ability to understand others' mental states. |
| *Reading the Mind in the Eyes Test (RMET)* | The RMET is an advanced theory-of-mind test used as the main outcome in these studies. |
| *Author Recognition Test* | The Author Recognition Test is a measure of lifetime exposure to fiction, and it predicted theory-of-mind scores across conditions. |
| *simulation of social experience* | The simulation of social experience is Mar and Oatley's account that fiction offers models of the social world that readers experience from inside. |

#### Second-person address in narrative (the pronoun you)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | psycholinguistics and narratology |
| Originators | T. T. Brunyé, T. Ditman, C. R. Mahoney, J. S. Augustyn, and H. A. Taylor (2009 experiments); second-person narration in fiction, for example Italo Calvino, If on a winter's night a traveler (1979) |
| Date | 2009 (Psychological Science 20:27-32); 1979 (Calvino's novel) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The pronoun in the text positions the reader; the reader supplies the mental simulation. |
| What it holds | Brunyé and colleagues found that readers of simple event sentences took the actor's perspective when the pronoun was you or I and an outside onlooker's perspective when it was he, and that a short preceding context made readers take an outside perspective with I, so that readers took the actor's perspective only when directly addressed as the subject of a sentence. In fiction, second-person narration uses you for the viewpoint character, and the Wikipedia article on narration notes that this is not a direct address to any given reader even when it appears to be. Calvino's novel alternates second-person chapters about the reader's attempts to read the book with the opening chapters of ten other novels, and some of its chapters discuss whether the you narrated is the you actually reading. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's taking of the actor's position inside a described event is what the pronoun you makes more likely, and the reader supplies the simulation. With no one present, the word you places the reader in the event, but it cannot confirm that the reader accepts the position, and fiction's you may address a character rather than the reader. |
| Across scales | The effect is on one reader's perspective; the entry on distanced self-talk records that you can also mean people in general, so the same pronoun can address one reader or everyone. |
| The evidence | Two laboratory experiments on single sentences show a perspective effect during comprehension, not change over time; the literary point is a description from narrative theory. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: Readers took the actor's perspective only when addressed as you, so second-person address moves a reader from onlooker toward participant during reading. Being seen and recognized by another: A text that says you addresses the reader without seeing them, which the narration article makes explicit for fiction. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The experiments measure perspective in single sentences, while the book uses the second person across a whole work and hopes for recognition, which is not measured. |
| Limits and critiques | A short context changed the perspective readers took with I, so a pronoun's effect depends on the surrounding text, and no source here shows lasting change from being addressed as you. |
| Quoted from its sources | "they embody an actor's perspective only when directly addressed as the subject of a sentence" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22When%20you%20and%20I%20share%20perspectives%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "This is not a direct address to any given reader even if it purports to be" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narration) |
| Mental model | other. Psycholinguistics of narrative comprehension, and narrative theory |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a single move |
| Subjects | identity self, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/to be addressed as you is to be summoned into being as the one who can answer.md`; The vault holds that the book's second person picks out one reader and calls a respondent into being; Brunyé's experiments add measured evidence that you places readers in the actor's perspective, and the narration article adds that fictional you is not direct address. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22When%20you%20and%20I%20share%20perspectives%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narration; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/If_on_a_winter%27s_night_a_traveler |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *embodied perspective* | An embodied perspective is a mental simulation of an event from the actor's own point of view rather than an onlooker's. |
| *second-person narration* | Second-person narration is narration that refers to the viewpoint character as you. |
| *direct address* | Direct address is speaking to the actual reader, which second-person narration is not, even when it appears to be. |

#### Self-guided treatment by book or program, with no therapist contact

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | clinical psychology (self-help research) |
| Originators | P. Cuijpers (1997 meta-analysis; 2011 meta-analysis with colleagues); M. R. Gualano and colleagues (2017); E. Karyotaki and colleagues (2017) |
| Date | 1997 (Journal of Behavior Therapy and Experimental Psychiatry 28:139-147); 2011 (PLoS One 6:e21274); 2017 (Clinical Psychology Review 58:49-58; JAMA Psychiatry 74:351-359) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The book or program supplies the method and sequence; the reader works through it alone. |
| What it holds | In bibliotherapy for depression, the patient takes a standardized treatment home in book form and works through it more or less independently, and any therapist contact is only supportive. A 2011 meta-analysis of seven randomized trials with 1,362 participants who had no contact at all with a therapist or coach found a small effect on depressive symptoms (d = 0.28 at the end of treatment and 0.23 at four to twelve months). An analysis of individual data from 3,876 participants in 13 trials of self-guided internet cognitive behavioral therapy found an effect of g = 0.27 (number needed to treat 8), with greater adherence associated with lower symptoms. A 2017 review of long-term follow-ups found decreases in depressive symptoms in six studies of adults but no significant results in four studies of young people. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's own learning and practice of cognitive and behavioral skills is what the book or program makes possible at home; it supplies the method, the sequence, and the exercises, and, as Cuijpers notes, no traditional relationship between therapist and patient develops. With no one present, the text alone produces a small measurable effect for adults who complete it. |
| The evidence | Several meta-analyses agree that fully self-guided treatment has a small but significant effect on depressive symptoms in adults; the long-term review found no significant effect in young people, and benefit depended on how much of the material people completed. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: A text with no relationship still produced a small measurable effect, which sets a lower bound on what a book alone can do. Participation rather than watching: Benefit tracked adherence, so the reader who does the exercises is the one who changes. Harm and its conditions: The long-term review found no significant benefit for young people, so the same method does not reach every reader. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | These programs teach a fixed clinical method for depression and measure symptoms, while the book teaches no clinical method and does not measure its reader. |
| Limits and critiques | Alone, the book cannot keep a reader working through it, and the benefit depends on how much of it the reader completes. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In bibliotherapy the patient takes a standardized treatment home, in book form, and works it through more or less independently." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Bibliotherapy%20in%20unipolar%20depression%3A%20a%20meta-analysis%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "No traditional relationship between therapist and patient is developed." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Bibliotherapy%20in%20unipolar%20depression%3A%20a%20meta-analysis%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "self-guided psychological intervention, without any contact between the participants and a therapist or coach" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Self-guided%20psychological%20treatment%20for%20depressive%20symptoms%3A%20a%20meta-analysis%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2) "six studies, including adults, reported a decrease of depressive symptoms, while four studies including young people did not show significant results" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22long-term%20effects%20of%20bibliotherapy%20in%20depression%20treatment%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Adherence to treatment was associated with lower depressive symptoms" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Efficacy%20of%20Self-guided%20Internet-Based%20Cognitive%20Behavioral%20Therapy%20in%20the%20Treatment%20of%20Depressive%20Symptoms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, thought belief, learning skill |
| In the survey | Bibliotherapy; Cognitive bibliotherapy (therapy-based self-help books); Computerized and internet-delivered CBT programs; this entry adds the survey entries describe the methods; this entry adds meta-analytic effect sizes for fully self-guided use with no therapist contact, the association of benefit with adherence, and the long-term finding that young people did not benefit significantly. |
| In the vault | `notes/the alliance holds its size when the exchange is mediated and written.md`; The vault holds that the alliance keeps its size in written, delayed therapy where a person still answers; this entry adds the effect of treatment with no answering person at all, which is small but significant. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Bibliotherapy%20in%20unipolar%20depression%3A%20a%20meta-analysis%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Self-guided%20psychological%20treatment%20for%20depressive%20symptoms%3A%20a%20meta-analysis%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=2; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22long-term%20effects%20of%20bibliotherapy%20in%20depression%20treatment%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Efficacy%20of%20Self-guided%20Internet-Based%20Cognitive%20Behavioral%20Therapy%20in%20the%20Treatment%20of%20Depressive%20Symptoms%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *bibliotherapy* | Bibliotherapy, in Cuijpers's sense, is a standardized treatment in book form that the patient works through at home more or less independently. |
| *self-guided* | A self-guided treatment is used without any contact with a therapist or coach. |
| *adherence* | Adherence is how much of the program a person actually completes, and greater adherence went with lower depressive symptoms. |

#### Social-belonging intervention, and psychological affordances (seed and soil)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | social psychology (wise interventions) |
| Originators | G. M. Walton and G. L. Cohen (2011); G. M. Walton and D. S. Yeager (2020) |
| Date | 2011 (Science 331:1447-1451); 2020 (Current Directions in Psychological Science 29:219-226) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, an organization, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. Prepared materials frame adversity, and the participant generates the message in their own words. |
| What it holds | A brief intervention for first-year college students framed social adversity on campus as common and transient and used subtle attitude-change strategies so that participants generated the message themselves. In a randomized trial with 92 students, it raised African American students' grade-point averages over three years relative to control groups, halving the minority achievement gap, and improved their self-reported health and well-being and reduced doctor visits three years later; the effect worked by preventing students from seeing adversity as a sign they did not belong, and senior-year surveys showed no awareness among participants of the intervention's impact. Walton and Yeager later argued, drawing on large trials designed to study variation across contexts, that such interventions last only where the context affords the way of thinking they offer. |
| What is facilitated | The student's own interpretation of adversity, as common and passing rather than as proof of not belonging, is what the materials make more likely, and the student generates the message themselves. The intervention's content came from prepared materials and the student's own writing rather than from a facilitator's guidance, and it changed an interpretation whose effects lasted for years, but the materials cannot make the student's surroundings support the new interpretation, and Walton and Yeager hold that without that support the effects are unlikely to persist. |
| Across scales | The finding joins a person's interpretation to an institution's context, and the proposed invariant is that a text's effect on a reader depends on the environment the reader returns to, failing where that environment contradicts the message. |
| The evidence | The original was a small randomized trial with 92 students, 49 of them African American; Walton and Yeager present later large-scale trials of belonging and growth-mindset interventions as showing that effects vary with context, which they connect to debates about replicability. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The intervention changed individuals' interpretations, and its authors later held that the effect depends on whether the institution's context supports those interpretations. Remaining the author of one's own change: Participants generated the message in their own words and later showed no awareness of the intervention's impact, so the change was experienced as their own. Knowing that something happened: Participants did not attribute their improvement to the intervention, so a text's real effect may go unrecognized by the person it changed. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The intervention targets one belief about belonging in one institution and measures grades and health, while the book does not target a specific belief and returns its reader to whatever life they have. |
| Limits and critiques | Alone, the text cannot supply a context that supports its message, and Walton and Yeager state that where the context does not, treatment effects are unlikely to persist. |
