| Quoted from its sources | "The intervention aimed to lessen psychological perceptions of threat on campus by framing social adversity as common and transient." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22A%20brief%20social-belonging%20intervention%20improves%20academic%20and%20health%20outcomes%20of%20minority%20students%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Senior-year surveys indicated no awareness among participants of the intervention's impact." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22A%20brief%20social-belonging%20intervention%20improves%20academic%20and%20health%20outcomes%20of%20minority%20students%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Does the context in which the intervention is delivered afford the way of thinking offered by the intervention? If not, treatment effects are unlikely to persist." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Seed%20and%20Soil%3A%20Psychological%20Affordances%20in%20Contexts%20Help%20to%20Explain%20Where%20Wise%20Interventions%20Succeed%20or%20Fail%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Social psychology of brief interventions |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, relationship present, justice power, learning skill |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | archive/reading — nisbett and wilson, telling more than we can know (1977).md; The vault holds Nisbett and Wilson at the primary text on people's limited access to the causes of their own changes; the belonging intervention is a case in which participants showed no awareness of what changed them, and the vault holds no research on brief written interventions. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22A%20brief%20social-belonging%20intervention%20improves%20academic%20and%20health%20outcomes%20of%20minority%20students%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Seed%20and%20Soil%3A%20Psychological%20Affordances%20in%20Contexts%20Help%20to%20Explain%20Where%20Wise%20Interventions%20Succeed%20or%20Fail%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *wise intervention* | A wise intervention is a brief psychological intervention aimed at how people interpret themselves and their situation, which can cause lasting improvement. |
| *psychological affordance* | A psychological affordance is present when the context in which an intervention is delivered supports the way of thinking it offers. |
| *seed and soil* | Seed and soil is Walton and Yeager's image for an adaptive perspective and a context that lets it persist, and they state that change requires both. |
| *self-generate* | To self-generate the message is to produce it in one's own words rather than receive it, which was the intervention's method. |

#### The four-step bibliotherapeutic process (interactive biblio/poetry therapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | biblio/poetry therapy |
| Originators | Arleen McCarty Hynes and Mary Hynes-Berry |
| Date | 1986 (Bibliotherapy: The Interactive Process: A Handbook); Hynes began the work as a patients' librarian at St. Elizabeths Hospital, Washington, DC, in 1970 |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The facilitator selects the literature and asks questions; the reader supplies what the text means to them. |
| What happens | A facilitator selects a poem or other text and guides a reader or group through four steps: recognition, when the reader is caught by something in the reading; examination, looking into the issues and the reader's feeling response to them; juxtaposition, a deeper understanding as the reader considers new feelings or ideas that emerge; and self-application, the integration of the impressions and insights that emerged. The literature is called the catalyst, which a university glossary summarizing the handbook describes as neutral until it makes an impression on the reader. Hynes developed the method with patient groups at St. Elizabeths Hospital, where she said she posed questions that let patients strip a poem to its core and integrate it into their vision of themselves. |
| Steps | (1) The facilitator selects a piece of literature, often a poem. (2) The reader or group reads it, and each reader notices what catches them in it (recognition). (3) The facilitator's questions lead the reader to look into the issues and their own feeling response (examination). (4) The reader considers the new feelings or ideas that emerge (juxtaposition). (5) The reader integrates the impressions and insights into their own life (self-application). |
| What is facilitated | The reader's own response to a text, from the first moment of being caught by it to its integration into their life, is what the process facilitates; the facilitator supplies questions, the text supplies the occasion, and the meaning is the reader's. With no one present, a reader can still be caught by a line, but in this method the facilitator's questions guide the examination and juxtaposition steps. |
| Across scales | Hynes used the steps with patient groups and with individuals, and these sources do not say how the steps change between the two. |
| The evidence | The method is set out in a practitioners' handbook that NAPT called the first comprehensive text on bibliotherapy and that has stayed in print since 1986; no outcome study of the four steps appears in these sources, and the four step names come here through a university glossary's summary of the handbook's third chapter. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The method names its first step recognition, the moment a reader is caught by something in a text, and treats it as the beginning of the work rather than the whole event. Who decides the content: The facilitator chooses the text and the questions, while the method holds the text neutral until it makes an impression on the reader. Participation rather than watching: The steps after recognition are acts the reader performs, so the reading continues past the first moment of being caught by a line into examination and application. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The method's recognition is noticing something in a text that bears on one's own situation, while the book's recognition is coming to know again what one already is, a larger claim than the handbook makes. |
| Limits and critiques | The method assumes a facilitator who selects the text and asks the questions; a solitary reader has the first step and must supply the others without that help. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Recognition – the reader is caught by something in the reading" (https://lacl.missouri.edu/index.php/terms-bibliotherapy/) "Juxtaposition – a deeper level of understanding as the person considers new feelings or ideas that emerge" (https://lacl.missouri.edu/index.php/terms-bibliotherapy/) "Self-application – integration of the impressions and insights that emerged" (https://lacl.missouri.edu/index.php/terms-bibliotherapy/) "It is neutral until it makes an impression on the reader (the respondent)." (https://lacl.missouri.edu/index.php/terms-bibliotherapy/) "questions that would help them to integrate the poem into their vision of themselves" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arleen_McCarty_Hynes) |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. Biblio/poetry therapy, which the National Association for Poetry Therapy calls the language-oriented branch of the expressive arts therapies |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | emotion, identity self, meaning purpose |
| In the survey | Bibliotherapy; this entry adds it adds Hynes and Hynes-Berry's four named steps, beginning with recognition, and the facilitator's questioning role in interactive bibliotherapy. |
| In the vault | `notes/the book is a tuning fork and the reader is the striking surface.md`; The vault holds the book's image of itself as a tuning fork silent until struck; Hynes's catalyst, literature that is neutral until it makes an impression on the reader, is a practitioner's independently named version of the same claim, and the vault holds no biblio/poetry therapy. |
| Sources | https://lacl.missouri.edu/index.php/terms-bibliotherapy/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arleen_McCarty_Hynes; https://poetrytherapy.org/History |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *recognition* | Recognition is the first step, when the reader is caught by something in the reading. |
| *juxtaposition* | Juxtaposition is the third step, a deeper understanding as the reader considers new feelings or ideas that emerge. |
| *catalyst* | The catalyst is the literature used in the session, which stays neutral until it makes an impression on the reader. |
| *interactive process* | The interactive process is bibliotherapy with planned discussion of the reader's personal reactions, which NAPT's history says developed with group therapy in the 1960s. |

#### The open work (opera aperta)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | semiotics and literary theory |
| Originators | Umberto Eco |
| Date | 1962 (Opera aperta); 1979 (The Role of the Reader); 1990 (The Limits of Interpretation) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The author builds a field of possible meanings, and the reader realizes one of them. |
| What it holds | In Opera aperta (1962), Eco argued that literary texts are fields of meaning rather than strings of meaning, open, internally dynamic, and psychologically engaged, and that a closed text, which limits a reader's understanding to a single unequivocal line, is the least rewarding, while open texts, the most active between mind, society, and life, are the liveliest. The Wikipedia article says he reached these positions through semiotics rather than through psychology or historical analysis, as Iser and Jauss did. The article lists the intention of the reader (intentio lectoris) and the limits of interpretation among his notable ideas, and his books include The Limits of Interpretation (1990) and Interpretation and Overinterpretation (1992). |
| What is facilitated | The reader's active realization of one meaning among several is what an open work invites; the author supplies a field of possibilities rather than a conclusion. With no one present, an open text leaves its completion to the reader, and Eco's later books on interpretation and overinterpretation address how far a reader's completion can go. |
| The evidence | This is a theory of texts rather than an empirical finding; Roger Scruton's 1980 review of The Role of the Reader attacked Eco for producing technical rhetoric that leads the reader to blame his own lack of perception rather than the author's lack of illumination. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: An open work gives the reader part of the authorship of its meaning, while the author still decides the field within which the reader chooses. Remaining the author of one's own change: A text built as a field of meanings leaves the reader to realize one of them, which is the reader's own act. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Eco values openness as a property of the art object and its interpretations, while the book's openness is meant to leave room for a reader's recognition, which is an event in the reader rather than a range of meanings in the text. |
| Limits and critiques | Scruton's charge, that difficult theory can make readers blame themselves for not seeing, bears on any text that leaves its reader to complete it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Eco argued that literary texts are fields of meaning, rather than strings of meaning" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umberto_Eco) |
| Mental model | other. Semiotics |
| Structure | tending conditions; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, creativity play |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/iser distinguishes completion from participation as two fates of the textual gap.md`; The vault holds Iser's textual gaps, read at the primary essay; Eco's open work is the semiotic account from the same period, which the vault does not hold, and the Wikipedia article distinguishes Eco's route from Iser's. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umberto_Eco |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *open work (opera aperta)* | An open work is a text whose meaning is a field of possibilities that the reader actively realizes, rather than a single line of meaning. |
| *closed text* | A closed text limits the reader's understanding to a single, unequivocal line, which Eco called the least rewarding. |
| *intentio lectoris* | Intentio lectoris is the intention of the reader, which the Wikipedia article lists among Eco's notable ideas. |

#### The third hand (the art therapist's third hand intervention)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | art therapy |
| Originators | Edith Kramer, who founded the graduate art therapy program at New York University with Laurie Wilson |
| Date | 1971 (Art as Therapy with Children); the third hand was developed at New York University after 1976 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The therapist helps with the means, such as mixing a color the person wants, without supplying the image or its meaning. |
| What happens | Kramer described the third hand as the art therapist's ability to facilitate a person's artistic process, for example by strategically helping mix paints for a color the person wants or by intervening at critical moments during art making. Her approach, art as therapy, treated sublimation as a central goal, forms that successfully contain anger, anxiety, or pain, and she held that the success of the therapy could be measured by the visual product and that art therapy was a supplement to psychotherapy rather than a replacement. Her contemporary Margaret Naumburg instead treated free art expression as a form of symbolic speech that leads to more verbalization in psychoanalytically oriented therapy. |
| Steps | (1) The person works on their own artwork. (2) The therapist helps the process where it serves the person, for example by mixing paints for a color the person wants. (3) The therapist intervenes at critical moments during the art making. |
| What is facilitated | The person's own artistic process is what the third hand serves, and what it supplies is means, not content. With no one present, a person making art has no third hand, since a text cannot mix the color a person wants at the moment they need it. |
| Across scales | The move is made within a pair or a group session and assumes someone watching the process closely enough to know the critical moment. |
| The evidence | The third hand is a practitioner's teaching concept, reported in the Wikipedia article with primary-source tags, not an empirical finding. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The third hand supplies technical help toward what the person wants and leaves the image and its meaning to the person. Timing and pacing: The move depends on intervening at critical moments, which requires someone watching the process as it happens. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Kramer's aim is sublimation judged in the visual product, while the book does not judge its reader's product and does not aim at sublimation. |
| Limits and critiques | The move requires a present observer and cannot be performed by a text, and Kramer held art therapy to be a supplement to psychotherapy. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The third hand can be summed up as the art therapist's ability to facilitate a person's artistic process" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edith_Kramer) "She asserted that art therapy was not a replacement, but a supplement to psychotherapy." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edith_Kramer) "sublimation is attained when forms are created that successfully contain" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_therapy) |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. Kramer's art as therapy is psychoanalytically informed and built on sublimation |
| Structure | tending conditions; a single move |
| Subjects | creativity play, emotion |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the facilitator tends conditions under which recognition might occur rather than transmitting a destination.md`; The vault holds the author's distinction between tending conditions and transmitting a destination; the third hand is an art therapist's named move of that kind, helping the means and leaving the content, and the vault holds no art therapy. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edith_Kramer; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *third hand* | The third hand is the art therapist's help with a person's artistic process in the service of what the person is trying to make. |
| *sublimation* | Sublimation, in the Freudian sense Kramer used, is the transformation of primitive urges into socially productive activity; in art it means forms that contain anger, anxiety, or pain. |
| *art as therapy* | Art as therapy is Kramer's approach, in which making art is itself therapeutic, as distinct from Naumburg's use of art as symbolic speech within psychotherapy. |

#### Therapeutic letters

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | narrative therapy |
| Originators | Michael White and David Epston (Narrative Means to Therapeutic Ends); later taken up in family nursing and other practice |
| Date | 1990 (Narrative Means to Therapeutic Ends); studies of the practice 2009-2022 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The therapist writes the letter from the conversation, and the client reads it alone between sessions and decides what to take from it. |
| What happens | After a session the therapist writes the client a letter that documents and develops the moments that contradicted the problem's story and poses questions and reflections for the client to consider at the start of the next session; letters may also go to family members or loved ones to invite them to sessions or celebrations. A systematic synthesis of narrative therapy for eating disorders found twelve articles that used letter writing between sessions and at the end of treatment. Interview studies in family nursing describe letters as extending the therapeutic relationship across time and place and as a way of relating to clients in dialogue rather than a one-way document. |
| Steps | (1) The therapist and client hold a session in which the client describes the problem's effects and moments that contradict it. (2) After the session, the therapist writes the client a letter that documents those contradicting moments. (3) The letter closes with questions and reflections for the client to consider before the next session. (4) The client reads the letter alone between sessions, and its questions are taken up at the beginning of the next session. |
| What is facilitated | The client's own re-authoring of their story is what the letter supports between sessions, by returning to the client in writing the moments their problem story left out and asking them questions rather than giving conclusions. The letter works with no one present because the client reads and rereads it alone, but its content comes from a conversation, so it cannot exist without one. |
| Across scales | Letters are also sent to family members and loved ones to invite them to sessions or celebrations, so the practice includes families as well as pairs, and the sources do not describe use at the scale of a community. |
| The evidence | The evidence is descriptive: a systematic synthesis of treatment components and small qualitative studies, with nine clinicians and seven clients in Pyle's study and five clients in Rodgers's. No source here measures the letters' separate effect on outcomes. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The letter shows the client in writing what the therapist heard, including moments the client's problem story left out. Remaining the author of one's own change: The letter ends in questions rather than conclusions, so the client supplies the next part of the story. The container that holds people while they change: Rodgers reports that letters extended the relationship beyond the session's time and place, so the relationship continues into the hours the client spends alone with the letter. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | A therapeutic letter is written to one known client from a shared conversation, while the book's letters are written to readers the writer has not heard, so the book cannot return a reader's own words to them. |
| Limits and critiques | Rodgers reports that letters cross boundaries traditionally meant to keep clients safe, which he argues calls for a carefully considered use of boundary; no source here reports harm, and the outcome evidence is qualitative. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Narrative therapy also utilises therapeutic letter writing for the purposes of extending the therapeutic conversation between sessions" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9461296/fullTextXML) "These letters included prompting questions and reflections to be considered at the beginning of the next session" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9461296/fullTextXML) "An invitation is extended to conceptualize letters not as monologic documents but as a means of dialogically relating to clients" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20letters%20as%20relationally%20responsive%20practice%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "Letters challenged and extended the spatial and temporal boundaries of the therapeutic relationship" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20letters%3A%20a%20challenge%20to%20conventional%20notions%20of%20boundary%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, memory past, relationship present |
| In the survey | Narrative therapy; this entry adds the survey entry covers externalizing, unique outcomes, re-authoring, and outsider witnesses; this entry adds the therapist's letter between sessions, with its questions for the next session, and studies of how clients receive such letters. |
| In the vault | `notes/the correspondence vision was chosen and executed as a sent pilot letter.md`; The vault holds the author's choice to shape the book as a correspondence of dated letters and the sent pilot letter; therapeutic letters are a clinical precedent for letters written within an ongoing relationship, and the vault's reading queue lists White and Epston only for their externalizing chapter. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9461296/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20letters%20as%20relationally%20responsive%20practice%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Therapeutic%20letters%3A%20a%20challenge%20to%20conventional%20notions%20of%20boundary%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *therapeutic letter* | A therapeutic letter is a letter a therapist writes to a client between sessions as part of the therapy. |
| *thickening* | Thickening is narrative therapy's word for developing and strengthening a person's hidden or preferred storyline. |
| *unique outcomes* | Unique outcomes are moments that contradict the problem's story, which the letter documents. |
| *relationally responsive practice* | Relationally responsive practice is Pyle's description of letters as a way of relating to clients in dialogue. |

#### Werther effect and Papageno effect

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | suicidology and media research |
| Originators | David Phillips, who coined the term Werther effect after Goethe's The Sorrows of Young Werther; T. Niederkrotenthaler and colleagues (Papageno effect) |
| Date | 1774 (Goethe's novel); 1974 (Phillips's term); 2010 (British Journal of Psychiatry 197:234-243) |
| Scale | a community or public, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A published story does the work on each reader, with no one present to judge who is vulnerable. |
| What it holds | Soon after Goethe's novel The Sorrows of Young Werther appeared in 1774, young men imitated its hero, there were reports of young men killing themselves by his method, and the book was banned in several places; the sociologist David Phillips coined the term Werther effect in 1974 for suicides that imitate a publicized one. Niederkrotenthaler and colleagues analyzed 497 Austrian print reports on suicide from the first half of 2005 and found that repeated reporting of the same suicide and reporting of suicide myths went with higher suicide rates, while reports about people who adopted coping strategies other than suicide in adverse circumstances went with lower rates, which they called the Papageno effect. A 2019 study associated increased suicides among 10- to 19-year-olds in the United States with the three months after the release of the series 13 Reasons Why, while other studies found no association with suicidal ideation. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's sense of what is possible in a crisis is what the story shapes: a reported suicide can make the act seem permitted to a vulnerable reader, and a story of coping can make another course imaginable. With no one present, the reader reads the story alone and its content, repetition, and framing do all the work, and the text cannot know which of its readers is vulnerable. |
| Across scales | The effect appears both in single readers who imitate a story and in population suicide rates after widespread reporting, and it stops being a sufficient explanation at the level of the individual, where psychological and social risk factors matter more than media. |
| The evidence | The evidence is ecological and correlational, relating the content and volume of reporting to later suicide rates; the Wikipedia article states that causal claims about media effects cannot be considered conclusive and that media effects on suicidal behaviour are much less important than individual psychological and social risk factors. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: A text read alone can make a harmful act seem permitted to a vulnerable reader, and repetition and myths raised the risk. Protection and defense: Stories of people who came through a crisis by coping otherwise went with fewer suicides, so a text can protect by showing another course. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The effect shows in population rates while individual risk factors remain the larger cause, so media content is a contributing condition rather than the cause. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | This research concerns mass media and the gravest outcome, while the book is a single work read by choice; they share the condition that a text cannot know which reader is vulnerable. |
| Limits and critiques | Reporting guidelines endorsed by the World Health Organization and others ask for restraint, no romanticizing of the death, and a limited number of stories; a book, like a newspaper, cannot identify which of its readers is vulnerable. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Hence the term "Werther effect" is used in the technical literature to designate copycat suicides." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copycat_suicide) "Hearing about a suicide seems to make those who are vulnerable feel they have permission to do it" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copycat_suicide) "Repetitive reporting of the same suicide and the reporting of suicide myths were positively associated with suicide rates." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Werther%20v.%20Papageno%20effects%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "coverage of positive coping in adverse circumstances, as covered in media items about suicidal ideation, may have protective effects" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Werther%20v.%20Papageno%20effects%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) "the effects that media can have on suicidal behaviour are certainly less important than individual psychological and social risk factors" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copycat_suicide) |
| Mental model | other. Suicidology and media-effects research |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | death finitude, emotion, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no research on how published stories about suicide or crisis affect readers. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copycat_suicide; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22Werther%20v.%20Papageno%20effects%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Werther effect* | The Werther effect is an increase in suicides after a publicized suicide, named after the hero of Goethe's novel. |
| *Papageno effect* | The Papageno effect is a decrease in suicides associated with reports of people who faced suicidal crises and coped in other ways. |
| *mastery of crisis* | Mastery of crisis was the class of reports about people who adopted coping strategies other than suicide in adverse circumstances. |
| *differential identification* | Differential identification is the tendency of people who copy a suicide to share the age and gender of the person whose suicide was reported. |

#### When self-help is no help

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | clinical psychology (depression prevention) |
| Originators | G. J. Haeffel |
| Date | 2010 (Behaviour Research and Therapy 48:152-157) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. Self-directed workbooks were the whole intervention. |
| What it holds | College freshmen at cognitive risk for depression were randomly assigned to one of three self-directed workbooks, teaching traditional cognitive skills, non-traditional cognitive skills, or academic skills. Participants who were high in rumination and experienced stress had significantly more depressive symptoms after the traditional cognitive skills workbook than after the other two, both right after the intervention and four months later. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's own identifying and disputing of negative thoughts is what the workbook teaches, and for readers who ruminate the same exercise went with more depressive symptoms. With no one present, the workbook has no way to notice that, for a particular reader, the exercise is going with more symptoms rather than fewer. |
| The evidence | One randomized trial supports the finding; the author's interpretation, that rumination hinders identifying and disputing negative thoughts at least without a trained professional, was not tested directly. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: A workbook that teaches readers to examine their negative thoughts went with more depressive symptoms in readers who ruminate, and the effect lasted four months. Knowing that something happened: The workbook gave a ruminating reader no signal that the practice was going badly, so the reader alone had to judge whether it was working. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book does not teach disputing thoughts; its practices ask the reader to notice and feel, a different exercise whose interaction with rumination these sources do not test. |
| Limits and critiques | Alone, a workbook cannot tell a reader who ruminates from one who does not; Haeffel raises concerns about the benefits of self-help books, an industry he says generates billions of dollars each year. |
| Quoted from its sources | "rumination may hinder ones ability to identify and dispute negative thoughts (at least without the help of a trained professional)" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22When%20self-help%20is%20no%20help%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion, learning skill |
| In the survey | Cognitive bibliotherapy (therapy-based self-help books); this entry adds the survey entry mentions one study finding a negative effect in people prone to rumination; this entry identifies the study and its design and adds that the harm persisted four months later. |
| In the vault | `notes/a solitary reader can run every guard that declines to evaluate and none that discriminates.md`; The vault holds that a reader alone can follow restraint but cannot discriminate which state they are in; this trial shows the same limitation in a clinical workbook, which could not distinguish ruminating readers, and the vault holds no self-help outcome research. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22When%20self-help%20is%20no%20help%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *rumination* | Rumination is repetitive dwelling on one's negative thoughts and feelings, and high ruminators did worse with the traditional workbook. |
| *traditional cognitive skills training* | Traditional cognitive skills training teaches a person to identify and dispute negative thoughts, the core exercise of cognitive self-help books. |

#### Writing as an early self-help intervention after traumatic injury

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | writing research (clinical psychology) |
| Originators | A. Bugg, G. Turpin, S. Mason, and C. Scholes |
| Date | 2009 (Behaviour Research and Therapy 47:6-12) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. An information booklet and a writing instruction were the only intervention; patients wrote alone. |
| What it holds | Patients attending an accident and emergency department who screened positive for acute stress disorder were randomized to receive an information booklet one month after injury, or the booklet plus three twenty-minute sessions of writing about the emotional aspects of their trauma five to six weeks after injury. Both groups improved over six months on anxiety, depression, and post-traumatic stress, the differences between groups were not significant, and patients rated the writing as useful. The authors concluded that the results do not support writing as a targeted early intervention for injured patients at risk of post-traumatic stress disorder. |
| What is facilitated | The injured person's own processing of the trauma is what the writing aimed to help, and in this trial recovery happened in both groups over time without the writing adding to it. With no one present, the booklet and the instruction supplied information and structure but no judgment of whether a person was ready to write about the trauma. |
| The evidence | One randomized trial with 67 patients found no difference between groups; with groups this size a small effect could go undetected, and the patients' high ratings of the writing were not matched by any measured difference. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: Writing about a trauma five to six weeks after it happened added nothing beyond information, which bears on when a text should ask a reader to turn toward a painful experience. Harm and its conditions: The trial found neither harm nor benefit, so it shows a limit of solitary writing soon after trauma rather than a danger. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The trial targets post-traumatic symptoms in injured patients, while the book does not address acute trauma and its practices are not designed as trauma treatment. |
| Limits and critiques | These sources do not show that early writing harms; they show that it did not help beyond an information booklet for this population. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the results do not currently support the use of writing as a targeted early intervention technique for traumatic injury patients at risk of developing PTSD" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22randomised%20controlled%20trial%20of%20the%20effectiveness%20of%20writing%20as%20a%20self-help%20intervention%20for%20traumatic%20injury%20patients%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1) |
| Mental model | other. Clinical trial of a self-help writing intervention |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | trauma healing, memory past, emotion |
| In the survey | Expressive writing; this entry adds it adds a randomized test of writing soon after a traumatic injury, which found no benefit beyond an information booklet. |
| In the vault | `notes/the book asks only for attention until it has given the reader something to act with.md`; The vault holds that the book paces its requests and assigns a task only once the reader has been given a form to try; this trial is outside evidence on when writing about a trauma helps, and the vault holds no trauma-writing research. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE%3A%22randomised%20controlled%20trial%20of%20the%20effectiveness%20of%20writing%20as%20a%20self-help%20intervention%20for%20traumatic%20injury%20patients%22&format=json&resultType=core&pageSize=1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *acute stress disorder* | Acute stress disorder was the screening condition used to select patients at risk of later post-traumatic stress disorder. |
| *targeted early intervention* | A targeted early intervention is treatment given soon after a trauma to people selected as at risk, which this trial did not find writing to be. |

## Part 5. The pointing-out traditions: how a teacher occasions recognition

### Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen)

#### The three statements that strike the vital point (tshig gsum gnad brdeg)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Originators | Attributed to Garab Dorje, the first human teacher of Dzogchen in the Nyingma account; commented on by Patrul Rinpoche (1808–1887) |
| Date | Attributed to Garab Dorje (c. 665 in the Nyingma account, a date and a figure scholars call historically problematic); transmitted in the Vima Nyingtik; English translation by Adam Pearcey (Lotsawa House, 2025) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The teacher carries out the first stage, the introduction, with authority; the second and third stages, deciding and gaining confidence, are the student's own repeated work. |
| What happens | The tradition condenses the whole Dzogchen path into three statements that Garab Dorje is said to have left as his final testament to his disciple Mañjuśrīmitra. First, the teacher introduces the student directly to the nature of their own awareness (rigpa). Second, the student decides that this recognized awareness is the one thing to rely on in every situation. Third, the student gains confidence that thoughts free themselves as they arise, without a separate remedy. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher introduces the student to their own awareness: "Introducing directly the face of rigpa itself." (2) The student recognizes that same awareness again in movement and stillness, anger and happiness, and decides there is nothing other than it: "Deciding upon one thing and one thing only." (3) The student lets each rising thought free itself in the recognized awareness rather than applying a remedy: "Confidence directly in the liberation of rising thoughts." (4) The student keeps returning to the recognition until it is stable, which the tradition treats as the rest of the path. |
| What is facilitated | The student's recognition of their own awareness is facilitated, together with the student's later habit of returning to it. The teacher supplies the occasion and names what was seen, but the tradition holds that the awareness itself was already present and is not given by the teacher. |
| Across scales | The three stages, one moment of introduction followed by deciding and then confidence, resemble any learning that moves from a first demonstration to reliance to fluency, and the pattern holds wherever a single instance must become a habit. It stops holding at scales below a person, because the thing recognized is a person's own awareness. |
| The evidence | The support is the tradition's own texts and commentaries, which I read in Adam Pearcey's translation and in Patrul Rinpoche's root text; no empirical study of the three statements exists that I found. Wikipedia's article records that scholars describe Garab Dorje as historically problematic and at best a mythical figure. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The tradition's first statement is an introduction to what the student already is, so the book's promised event has an exact named counterpart here with a named second and third stage after it. Timing and pacing: The three statements separate the single moment of recognition from the long work of deciding and gaining confidence, which bears on how the book paces what follows a peak. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition holds that a text alone cannot give the first statement: Lotsawa House marks this very text as restricted to those who have received the empowerments, transmissions, and instructions, so a reader meets the words without the introduction the words describe. The tradition also frames the recognized awareness as the dharmakāya of Buddhist doctrine, whereas the book leaves what is recognized unnamed. |
| Limits and critiques | The tradition itself limits reading and practice of these texts to initiates, and it keeps the teacher's introduction as the only way into the first statement. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Introducing directly the face of rigpa itself." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/indian-masters/garab-dorje/three-statements-that-strike-vital-point) "Deciding upon one thing and one thing only." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/indian-masters/garab-dorje/three-statements-that-strike-vital-point) "Confidence directly in the liberation of rising thoughts." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/indian-masters/garab-dorje/three-statements-that-strike-vital-point) "Vajrayāna Buddhism places restrictions on the reading and practice of certain texts, which are intended only for those who have received the requisite empowerments, transmissions and instructions." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/indian-masters/garab-dorje/three-statements-that-strike-vital-point) "He has been described as "historically problematic" and at best a "mythical figure"." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garab_Dorje) "Meditating means letting this condition be as it is, without seeking." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garab_Dorje) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | Pointing-out instruction (ngo sprod); this entry adds the three-stage sequence that follows the introduction, the restriction the tradition places on reading the text, and Garab Dorje's contested historicity. |
| In the vault | `notes/learning draws out what the learner already holds so the truest teaching midwifes rather than fills.md`; This note cites the Dzogchen pointing-out as one pole of drawing out what the learner already holds, and it records that pole as held from memory with grounding owed; this entry supplies the text of the three statements from a published translation. |
| Sources | https://www.lotsawahouse.org/indian-masters/garab-dorje/three-statements-that-strike-vital-point; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garab_Dorje; https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *rigpa* | Rigpa is the Dzogchen word for the awareness that the introduction points out and that the student recognizes as their own nature. |
| *ngo sprod (introduction)* | Ngo sprod is the teacher's direct introduction of the student to the nature of their own mind, the first of the three statements. |
| *self-liberation of thoughts* | The self-liberation of thoughts is the tradition's claim that a thought recognized within awareness dissolves by itself, so no counter-thought is applied. |

#### The sudden PHAṬ in the introduction to rigpa

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Originators | Patrul Rinpoche (1808–1887), in The Special Teaching of the Wise and Glorious King, his commentary on Garab Dorje's three statements |
| Date | 19th century (Patrul Rinpoche, 1808–1887); English translation by Rigpa Translations, 2008 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The root text is written as an instruction the disciple carries out, but it is sealed for the teacher's heart disciples and it tells the disciple what to take the resulting gap to be. |
| What happens | The practitioner first lets the mind rest, neither scattered nor concentrated, and then suddenly lets out a forceful shout of the syllable PHAṬ. In the gap that the shout opens, the text says there is nothing there, the practitioner is struck with wonder, and everything is transparent and clear. The text then names that moment as the pure awareness to be recognized, and it tells the practitioner to disrupt even stillness, bliss, and clarity with the same syllable again and again. |
| Steps | (1) The practitioner relaxes and releases the mind: "First, relax and release your mind, Neither scattered, nor concentrated, without thoughts." (2) The practitioner abruptly shouts the syllable: "Suddenly let out a mind-shattering 'phaṭ!', Fierce, forceful and abrupt. Amazing!" (3) The practitioner notices the gap the shout leaves: "There is nothing there: transfixed in wonder, Struck by wonder, and yet all is transparent and clear." (4) The text names that moment: "Recognize this as the pure awareness of dharmakāya." (5) The practitioner repeats the interruption whenever meditative states of stillness, bliss, or clarity become something to hold on to. |
| What is facilitated | An interruption of the flow of thought is facilitated, together with the practitioner's noticing of the awareness that remains when thought stops for a moment. The shout supplies only the interruption; the text holds that the awareness noticed in the gap was present before the shout. |
| Across scales | A sudden interruption can halt an ongoing process at any scale where the process continues by its own momentum and a shock can briefly stop it. It stops holding when the interruption becomes expected, because an anticipated shout no longer interrupts anything. |
| The evidence | The support is the tradition's own text, read in the Rigpa Translations version at Lotsawa House; I found no empirical study of the move. The text itself says that until stability is attained the practitioner must still meditate in proper sessions away from busyness, so it does not claim that one shout suffices. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The text describes the moment of recognition as being "struck by wonder," which gives the book a tradition that places wonder at the moment of recognition itself. Knowing that something happened: The text names the gap for the practitioner, so the practitioner's own interpretation of the moment is supplied by the teaching rather than found alone. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition does not hold that this text alone suffices: Patrul writes that the instruction "is entrusted to my heart disciples, sealed to be secret," and it assumes a teacher's introduction behind the words. The move also depends on an abrupt shock timed within practice, which a reader following a book would have to deliver to themselves. |
| Limits and critiques | A shout that interrupts thought can also frighten, and the text's own caution is that practice in formal sessions remains necessary until stability is reached. |
| Quoted from its sources | "First, relax and release your mind, Neither scattered, nor concentrated, without thoughts." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root) "Suddenly let out a mind-shattering 'phaṭ!', Fierce, forceful and abrupt. Amazing!" (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root) "There is nothing there: transfixed in wonder, Struck by wonder, and yet all is transparent and clear." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root) "Recognize this as the pure awareness of dharmakāya." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root) "But, until stability is attained, It is vital to meditate, away from all distractions and busyness," (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root) "It is entrusted to my heart disciples, sealed to be secret." (https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | Pointing-out instruction (ngo sprod); this entry adds one specific pointing-out move with its exact wording, sequence, and the text's own claim that wonder marks the moment. |
| In the vault | `notes/wonder is not the novice's state to be outgrown but the native air of the awake.md`; This note supports, from the Kashmir Śaiva texts, the claim that wonder is the native state of the awake; Patrul's text is an independent Dzogchen instance in which being struck by wonder marks the moment of recognition. |
| Sources | https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/tsik-sum-nedek-root; https://www.lotsawahouse.org/tibetan-masters/patrul-rinpoche/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *PHAṬ* | PHAṬ is a Sanskrit syllable shouted abruptly in Tibetan practice to cut through the flow of thought. |
| *dharmakāya* | Dharmakāya is the Buddhist term for the ultimate nature of a buddha, which the text identifies with the awareness revealed in the gap. |
| *mother and child clear light* | The mother and child clear light are the text's names for the ground awareness and the awareness recognized in practice, which it says reunite once acquainted. |

#### Reporting back after the pointing-out for the teacher's confirmation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen and Mahāmudrā) |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Originators | Practice in the lineage of Tulku Urgyen Rinpoche and Chökyi Nyima Rinpoche, as described by Bruce Newman (A Beginner's Guide to Tibetan Buddhism, 2004) |
| Date | Described in 2004 (Newman); Tulku Urgyen Rinpoche's remarks from 1985 and 1996; Dzogchen Ponlop Rinpoche, 2009 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The student describes the experience, and the teacher decides whether it was the recognition and what the student should do next. |
| What happens | After receiving the pointing-out instruction, the student meditates on it and then returns to the teacher. If nothing has happened, the student says so and asks for advice, and the teacher may assign preliminary practices first. If something has happened, the student describes it, and the teacher asks questions to tell a real experience from something mixed up with what the student has read or heard, then says yes, no, or not quite. |
| Steps | (1) The student receives the pointing-out instruction from the teacher. (2) The student meditates on what was said, for however long it takes. (3) If nothing has happened, the student reports that and asks for one word of advice, and the teacher may assign preliminary practices before trying again. (4) If something has happened, the student describes it: "If you had some experience, report back for confirmation. Don't assume you've understood." (5) The teacher questions the student: "He might ask you several questions, often to discern whether you're talking about a real experience or mostly mixing it up with what you've read or heard." (6) On a definite yes, the student asks how to repeat and stabilize the recognition; on a no or a not quite, the student goes back to the beginning. |
| What is facilitated | The student's ability to tell their own recognition from an imitation of it is facilitated, by exposing the report to someone who has recognized. The teacher supplies the judgment and the next instruction; the recognition, if there is one, is the student's. |
| Across scales | Checking a first-person report against a second person who has done the thing is the pattern of apprenticeship in any craft, and it holds wherever the learner cannot yet tell their own success from a near miss. It stops holding where no one with the relevant experience is available, which is the reader's situation with a book. |
| The evidence | The support is teachers' and students' testimony quoted in Wikipedia's article on the pointing-out instruction; I found no systematic study of how often recognition follows. The testimony itself reports low yield: Tulku Urgyen Rinpoche says there is only a chance of recognizing, and Orgyen Tobgyal Rinpoche reports that of more than a thousand people who received it in Taiwan he later met only a few who truly recognized. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The procedure names the exact confusion the book's reader cannot rule out alone: an experience mixed up with what one has read or heard. Who decides the content: The teacher, not the student, decides whether the recognition occurred, which is the opposite allocation from the book's reader deciding alone. Being seen and recognized by another: The student's recognition is completed by being seen and confirmed by a teacher, so recognition of the self and recognition by another arrive together. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition holds that a text alone cannot supply this step: Dzogchen Ponlop Rinpoche says that without a qualified lineage master "we would have no way to experience such a preview," and the procedure's point is that reading about the experience is one of the things to be ruled out. The book, read alone, has no one to report back to. |
| Limits and critiques | The procedure concentrates in one teacher the authority to say whether a person has recognized their own nature; the entries on Rigpa, on dharma transmission, and on Joshu Sasaki record cases in which teachers holding such authority harmed students. |
| Quoted from its sources | "If you had some experience, report back for confirmation. Don't assume you've understood." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) "He might ask you several questions, often to discern whether you're talking about a real experience or mostly mixing it up with what you've read or heard." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) "Once one has received the pointing-out instruction there is the chance of either recognizing it or not." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) "Yet I later met only a few there who truly recognized their own nature." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) "Without the pure and genuine lineage, without a qualified lineage master, we would have no way to experience such a preview, such a flash experience." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, spirit ultimate, learning skill |
| In the survey | Pointing-out instruction (ngo sprod); this entry adds the report-back and questioning procedure after the introduction, and teachers' own testimony that the introduction often fails. |
| In the vault | `ops/second person ecology.md`; The second person survey (ops/second person ecology.md) holds the certifier, the second person who says afterward whether what happened was real, from Zen (Yasutani in private interview), Ignatius, and Engler; this entry adds the Tibetan version of that role, with its explicit test against what the student has read. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *confirmation* | Confirmation is the teacher's statement that the student's reported experience was the recognition the instruction pointed to. |
| *preview* | Preview is Dzogchen Ponlop Rinpoche's word for the glimpse the pointing-out gives, which he says the student must then extend by practice. |

#### World Wide Transmission: direct transmission of guru yoga by live webcast

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Originators | Chögyal Namkhai Norbu (1938–2018) and the International Dzogchen Community |
| Date | Teachings transmitted over the internet since 2005; World Wide Transmission offered three times a year (community notices of 2013 and 2016–2017) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, a large group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. A living teacher gives the transmission at a scheduled time, and the community requires newcomers to receive an explanation beforehand. |
| What happens | Namkhai Norbu gave what his community calls Direct Transmission of the guru yoga practice over a live internet webcast on three anniversary dates each year, so that people who could not attend in person could receive it. Newcomers had to receive an explanation beforehand, in person at a center or by telephone hookup, and first-time participants were told to obtain the practice book or DVD. Namkhai Norbu also described Dzogchen's three series of teachings as three modes of introduction: direct, symbolic, and oral. |
| Steps | (1) The newcomer attends an explanation of the transmission in person or by telephone: "It is necessary to receive the explanation prior to viewing the webcast of the World Wide Transmission." (2) The newcomer obtains the practice book or DVD of the guru yoga, as the community asks of first-time participants. (3) At the scheduled time the whole worldwide community connects to the live webcast together. (4) The teacher gives the transmission live; afterward the newcomer is encouraged to practice with a local center. |
| What is facilitated | The student's reception of the introduction is facilitated across distance, by a living teacher at a set time with the whole community joined. The webcast supplies the teacher's live voice and presence; it does not replace the explanation beforehand or the practice afterward. |
| Across scales | Synchronous presence at a distance, many people joined at one time to one living teacher, extends a dyadic transmission to a large group. The community's rules mark where it stops: they require a prior explanation from a person and hold the transmission live on set dates. |
| The evidence | The support is the community's own notices and Wikipedia's biography, which confirm the practice and its schedule; no study of what webcast recipients experience exists that I found. The community offers the webcast for people whose circumstances do not permit them to receive transmission in person, and it schedules the transmission as a live event on set dates. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: The community separates watching a webcast from receiving a transmission by requiring preparation and simultaneous participation, which distinguishes a spectator from a recipient at a distance. The container that holds people while they change: The explanation beforehand, the shared time, and the local center afterward are the container that the community keeps around a mediated transmission. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The community holds that a living teacher can transmit at a distance, and it requires that teacher's live transmission at a set time with a prior explanation, which a book read alone at any hour does not provide. The practice also assumes a lifelong relationship to one teacher and one community, which the book does not offer. |
| Limits and critiques | The practice makes recognition depend on one teacher, and the community's rules make the teacher's live transmission, not the practitioner's own looking, the condition of receiving it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Chögyal Namkhai Norbu, Rinpoche gives Direct Transmission via Webcast three times each year, so that Transmission is available to those whose circumstances do not permit them to receive transmission in person." (https://tsegyalgar.blogspot.com/2013/07/world-wide-transmission-webcast-on.html) "It is necessary to receive the explanation prior to viewing the webcast of the World Wide Transmission." (https://tsegyalgar.blogspot.com/2013/07/world-wide-transmission-webcast-on.html) "Transmission of Guru Yoga directly from Chögyal Namkhai Norbu via live internet is available three times a year on special world-wide live transmission dates where the entire world wide community participates at the same time." (https://tsegyalgar.blogspot.com/p/blog-page.html) "Since 2005, some of these teachings were also transmitted worldwide via the internet." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namkhai_Norbu) "Norbu states that Mennagde is a more direct form of introduction, Longde is closely associated with symbolic forms of introducing Dzogchen and Semde is more focused on oral forms of introduction." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzogchen) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, spirit ultimate, relationship absent |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the solitary form stands against seven witnesses who hold a second person constitutive.md`; This note gathers seven traditions that hold a second person necessary against the book's letter read alone; this entry adds a tradition that offered its second person at a distance through a live webcast while still requiring a live teacher and a prior explanation. |
| Sources | https://tsegyalgar.blogspot.com/2013/07/world-wide-transmission-webcast-on.html; https://tsegyalgar.blogspot.com/p/blog-page.html; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namkhai_Norbu; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzogchen; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_yoga |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Direct Transmission* | Direct Transmission is the community's name for the teacher's introduction of the student to the nature of mind, which it holds is the starting point of Dzogchen practice. |
| *guru yoga* | Guru yoga is a practice in which the practitioner unites their mind with the teacher's; in this community it is the practice transmitted on the webcast dates. |
| *three series* | The three series are Semde, Longde, and Mennagde, which Namkhai Norbu presented as oral, symbolic, and direct forms of introduction. |

#### The independent investigation of Sogyal Rinpoche and Rigpa (Lewis Silkin report)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen); organizational investigation |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Dzogchen) |
| Originators | Karen Baxter of the UK law firm Lewis Silkin, commissioned by Rigpa after a 2017 letter from former students |
| Date | Report dated 22 August 2018 and released by Rigpa on 5 September 2018; an earlier civil lawsuit was filed in 1994 |
| Scale | an organization, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | not applicable. The entry records a documented case of harm rather than a way of working. |
| What it holds | Sogyal Rinpoche (1947–2019) founded Rigpa in 1979 and named it after the Dzogchen word for the awareness the pointing-out instruction introduces. After former students alleged abuse in a 2017 letter, Rigpa commissioned an independent investigation, and the report found on the balance of probabilities that some students in his inner circle had been subjected to serious physical, sexual, and emotional abuse by him, and that senior individuals knew of some of it and failed to address it. The setting is one in which, as Matthew Kapstein describes Tibetan tantra, students are instructed to regard the guru as an awakened Buddha, and in which the list of root downfalls under the tantric vows begins with disrespecting the vajra master. |
| What is facilitated | The case shows what a teacher's authority made easier when it was misused: abuse of students in the inner circle, and a failure to act among senior members who knew. It shows that the authority meant to confirm a student's recognition can be turned against the student. |
| Across scales | The arrangement the report describes, a leader whose conduct senior members knew of and did not address, is an organizational pattern rather than a doctrinal one, so it can arise at the scale of any institution. The tantric vows add a religious reason not to question the leader, since their root downfalls begin with disrespect of the teacher. |
| The evidence | The finding rests on a lawyer's independent investigation with witness interviews, summarized in Wikipedia; the report PDF itself was not reachable when I tried, so I rely on the published summary. Sogyal Rinpoche did not respond to the report, and a 1994 lawsuit alleging that he used his position to induce a student into sexual relations was settled out of court. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The case shows harm arising where a teacher was held to be the source of the student's recognition and where senior members did not act on what they knew. Who decides the content: The case is the extreme of the position in which the teacher decides what the student's experience means, and it marks what the book avoids by declining that authority. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition around this case holds that the teacher's introduction cannot be replaced by a text, and the book's refusal to act as an authority over its reader removes the relationship in which this harm occurred while also removing the confirmation that relationship provides. The report addresses an organization's safeguarding failures and says nothing about recognition itself. |
| Limits and critiques | The report was commissioned by the organization under investigation, and I read its findings through Wikipedia's summary rather than in the report itself. |
| Quoted from its sources | "some students of Sogyal Lakar (who were part of the 'inner circle') have been subjected to serious physical, sexual and emotional abuse by him" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigpa_(organization)) "there were senior individuals within Rigpa who were aware of at least some of these issues and failed to address them, leaving others at risk." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigpa_(organization)) "It was alleged that he had used his position as a spiritual leader to induce one of his female students to have sexual relations with him." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sogyal_Rinpoche) ""Devotion is the essence of the path."" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sogyal_Rinpoche) "one is instructed to regard one's guru as an awakened Buddha." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_yoga) "Disrespecting the vajra master." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaya) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. The finding concerns a Dzogchen teaching organization, and the investigation itself used legal fact-finding methods |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | justice power, trauma healing, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the book can only be auxiliary to a sovereign reader.md`; The vault holds no record of teacher misconduct in contemplative communities; this note holds the book's refusal of authority over its reader, the position that this case tests from the side of harm. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigpa_(organization); https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sogyal_Rinpoche; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_yoga; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaya |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *samaya* | Samaya is the set of vows that binds a Vajrayana disciple to the guru after initiation, and its list of root downfalls begins with disrespecting the vajra master. |
| *inner circle* | The inner circle is the report's term for the close students around Sogyal Rinpoche among whom it found the abuse. |
| *guru devotion* | Guru devotion is the practice of regarding one's teacher with reverence; Sogyal Rinpoche often quoted Dilgo Khyentse Rinpoche's saying that devotion is the essence of the path, while Charles Tart reported that he directed students' devotion toward his own teachers rather than himself. |

### Tibetan Buddhism (Mahāmudrā)

#### The sudden method as medicine for some and poison for others

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a principle |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Mahāmudrā and Dzogchen) |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Mahāmudrā) |
| Originators | A Kagyu text cited by the Fourteenth Dalai Lama; Kaydrub Norzang-gyatso's A Lamp for Clarifying Mahamudra |
| Date | Stated in the Dalai Lama and Alexander Berzin, The Gelug/Kagyü Tradition of Mahamudra (1997), citing earlier Kagyu and Gelug sources |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable. The principle describes which practitioners a way of practice suits rather than a way of working. |
| What it holds | The Kagyu and Nyingma traditions distinguish practitioners for whom everything happens at once from practitioners who progress through graded stages. The Dalai Lama reports a Kagyu text that calls resting solely in the nonconceptual state powerful medicine for the first kind and deadly poison for the second. Kaydrub Norzang-gyatso explains the first kind as people who have already trained extensively, in earlier lives or earlier in this one, so that the simple method only triggers what earlier practice prepared. |
| What is facilitated | The principle governs which method a teacher offers to which student, so it facilitates the match between a person's readiness and the directness of the teaching. It holds that the direct method does not supply readiness; it only triggers readiness built earlier. |
| Across scales | The claim that the same intervention helps a prepared system and harms an unprepared one appears at many scales, from a drug dose to a group intervention to an organizational change, and it holds wherever effect depends on prior state. It stops being usable where the prior state cannot be assessed, which the tradition itself leaves unspecified. |
| The evidence | The support is the tradition's own classification, reported in a book by the Dalai Lama and Alexander Berzin and quoted in Wikipedia; it is doctrine, not a measured finding. The tradition does not say how a teacher tells the two kinds of practitioner apart in advance. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The tradition itself says that its most direct method brings "only harm, no benefit" to those not ready, which is a named harm condition for a book that offers directness to every reader. Timing and pacing: The principle makes readiness, built by earlier practice, the condition for the sudden method, which bears on whether a book can offer the peak before the preparation. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition holds that a teacher, not the practitioner and not a text, judges which kind of practitioner someone is, so a text offering the direct method to every reader does exactly what the principle warns against. The principle also explains readiness by practice in earlier lives, which the book does not assume. |
| Limits and critiques | The principle is itself the tradition's warning of harm: the direct method given to someone unprepared is called poison. |
| Quoted from its sources | "According to one Kagyu text, the method of practice of those for whom everything happens at once is powerful medicine. But it is deadly poison for those who progress through graded stages." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) "For those who are not of their level, such practice brings only harm, no benefit." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | attention awareness, spirit ultimate, learning skill |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the kashmir shaiva anupaya turns the negative way on method itself.md`; This note holds that Abhinavagupta's no-means is reserved for the most graced; the Tibetan principle is an independent statement of the same reservation, that the most direct way suits only the most prepared. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *those for whom everything happens at once* | These are practitioners of the sharpest faculties, for whom meditation on the nonconceptual mind alone brings recognition. |
| *those who progress through graded stages* | These are practitioners who need preparatory practices with the body's energy channels and winds before recognition can occur. |

#### The Ninth Karmapa's five lookings and five pointings-out

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Mahāmudrā) |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Mahāmudrā) |
| Originators | Wangchuk Dorje, the Ninth Karmapa (1556–1603), in his Mahāmudrā texts (Ocean of Definitive Meaning; Pointing Out the Dharmakāya; Dispelling the Darkness of Ignorance) |
| Date | Late 16th century; summarized by Reginald Ray (2001) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The teacher assigns each contemplation and its period, and the meditator supplies all of the looking, with no correct answer given in advance. |
| What happens | After calm has been cultivated, the meditator carries out five contemplations of looking at the mind: at the settled mind, at the moving or thinking mind, at the mind reflecting appearances, at the mind in relation to the body, and at the settled and moving minds together, asking questions such as how a thought arises and what its nature is. The five pointings-out repeat the same five objects, but now the meditator repeatedly asks, "What is it?" In retreat each contemplation has an assigned period, and the questions have no specific answers. |
| Steps | (1) The meditator first cultivates some stillness through calm-abiding practice. (2) The meditator looks at the settled mind, asking, for example, "what is its nature? It is perfectly still?" (3) The meditator looks at the moving mind, examining how a thought arises, stays, and ceases. (4) The meditator looks at how visual appearances arise in the mind, then at the relation of mind and body, then at stillness and movement together. (5) The meditator returns to each of the five and asks only "What is it?" to recognize its exact nature. |
| What is facilitated | The meditator's own close examination of their mind is facilitated, until the examination turns into recognition of what was being examined. The teacher supplies the sequence and the questions; the answers are not supplied at all. |
| Across scales | Structured self-observation guided by open questions appears in introspective psychology and in focusing-style inquiry, and it holds wherever a person can attend to their own states. It depends on a prior capacity for stillness, so it does not hold for a mind too agitated to look. |
| The evidence | The support is the tradition's own texts, summarized in Wikipedia from Reginald Ray's 2001 study; I found no empirical study of the series. Ray's summary notes that the practices serve to provoke closer scrutiny over time rather than to reach a set answer. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The questions come without answers, so whatever the meditator finds is found by the meditator, which keeps the content in the person's hands even inside a teacher's structure. Recognition: The series turns examination into recognition by one change of question, from how a state behaves to what it is. |
