| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition places these contemplations inside a teacher's assignment and a retreat schedule and after preliminary practices, so it does not present them as something a reader does from a text alone. The book's reader would meet the questions without the calm-abiding foundation the series presumes. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The practices for "pointing out the nature of mind" build on these. One now looks again at each of the five, but this time repeatedly asks oneself "What is it?"" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahamudra) "The above practices do not have specific "answers"; they serve to provoke one to scrutinize experience more and more closely over time, seeking to understand what is really there." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahamudra) "In retreat, each contemplation would typically be assigned specific time periods." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahamudra) "possibly posing questions to arouse awareness, such as "what is its nature? It is perfectly still?"" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahamudra) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self, body |
| In the survey | Pointing-out instruction (ngo sprod); this entry adds the Ninth Karmapa's named series of ten contemplations, their exact questions, and the rule that the questions have no set answers. |
| In the vault | `notes/what you already carry is a living implicit rather than a stored answer so knowing it stays your own act.md`; This note holds that knowing what one carries is one's own act rather than retrieval of a stored answer, with its Tibetan pole recorded from memory; this entry supplies a text-grounded series in which the questions deliberately carry no answer. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahamudra; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_Karmapa,_Wangchuk_Dorje; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *looking at the mind* | Looking at the mind is the practice of turning attention onto the mind's own states and asking questions about them. |
| *pointing out* | Pointing out, in this series, is the second round of the same contemplations aimed at recognizing the nature of each state rather than examining its behavior. |
| *ordinary mind* | Ordinary mind (tha mal gyi shes pa) is the Mahāmudrā name for the unfabricated mind that is pointed out. |

#### Gampopa's introduction to the nature of mind given outside tantric initiation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Tibetan Buddhism (Mahāmudrā) |
| Tradition | Tibetan Buddhism (Mahāmudrā) |
| Originators | Gampopa Sönam Rinchen (1079–1153), founder of the Dakpo Kagyu; criticized by Sakya Paṇḍita (1182–1251) |
| Date | 12th century; described by David Jackson (1994) and Matthew Kapstein (2000) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The teacher introduces the student to the nature of mind, and the student is led to confront and recognize it. |
| What happens | Gampopa gave the introduction to the nature of mind (sems kyi ngo sprod) to many students, sometimes as a sūtra-based instruction rather than as a secret tantric precept tied to initiation. David Jackson writes that Gampopa conveyed it on a scale never before attempted in his lineage and bent the rules restricting its transmission out of compassion. Sakya Paṇḍita rejected any non-tantric Mahāmudrā and derided Gampopa's teaching as 'Chinese Great Perfection', a label that tied it to Chinese Chan. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher gives the introduction to the nature of mind, leading the disciple to confront and directly recognize the nature of their own mind. (2) The teacher sometimes gives it as a sūtra-based instruction rather than as a secret precept tied to tantric initiation and yogic practices. (3) The teacher gives it to many students rather than only to the few prepared by initiation. (4) The disciple, introduced by the teacher, then takes their own mind as the path, relying on the teacher's blessing. |
| What is facilitated | A wider public's access to the introduction is facilitated, by removing the requirement of prior initiation. The teacher still supplies the introduction; what changed was who could receive it. |
| Across scales | The tension between widening access to a practice and keeping its conditions intact recurs when any craft or therapy is popularized, and it holds wherever a method's effect was thought to depend on its preparation. The dispute itself is unresolved within the tradition. |
| The evidence | The support is historical scholarship by David Jackson and Matthew Kapstein quoted in Wikipedia; the controversy itself is documented in Tibetan sources they cite. Kapstein reports evidence that the practice drew on unacknowledged Chan influence, a contested point in Tibet. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The Sakya critique is the tradition's own objection that removing the preparation changes or endangers the teaching, which is the objection a book offering recognition to any reader faces. Who decides the content: Gampopa kept the teacher's introduction while dropping the initiation, which separates the question of who may receive from the question of who gives. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Even Gampopa's widening kept a teacher giving the introduction in person, so neither side of the dispute holds that a text alone can occasion the recognition. Gampopa's widening still presupposed a disciple meeting a teacher, whereas the book's offer reaches readers who meet no one. |
| Limits and critiques | Sakya Paṇḍita held that all true Mahāmudrā requires full tantric initiation, and the charge that the widened teaching was Chan in disguise remains part of the record. |
| Quoted from its sources | "And convey it he did, on a scale never before attempted within his lineage. To do so, he bent the traditional rules restricting how certain Vajrayana teachings could be transmitted." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) "he castigated its 'sutra tradition of the mahamudra' as what he termed, with apparent derision, 'Chinese Great Perfection'" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction) "relies on blessing and is for those who are intelligent and of sharp faculties." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahamudra) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, spirit ultimate, justice power |
| In the survey | Pointing-out instruction (ngo sprod); this entry adds the historical dispute over giving the introduction outside initiation, with Jackson's and Kapstein's accounts and the Sakya objection. |
| In the vault | `notes/the kashmir shaiva tradition would decline four of the book's deepest moves.md`; This note records the Kashmir Śaiva tradition's refusal of the book alone as vehicle; the Gampopa dispute is a Tibetan case in which one side widened access and the other refused, with the teacher kept on both sides. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahamudra |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sems kyi ngo sprod* | Sems kyi ngo sprod is the Tibetan phrase for the introduction to the nature of mind. |
| *sūtra Mahāmudrā* | Sūtra Mahāmudrā is the contested form of Mahāmudrā given without tantric initiation, which Gampopa's critics rejected. |
| *path of blessing* | The path of blessing is Brunnhölzl's description of Gampopa's third path, which relies on blessing and suits the intelligent and sharp-witted. |

### Zen and Chan

#### A special transmission outside the scriptures, not founded upon words and letters

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Zen (Chan) |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Attributed to Bodhidharma; the four-line stanza is first attested in 1108 (Dumoulin); criticized as a literary construction by Albert Welter (2008) and Stuart Lachs (1999) |
| Date | Stanza dated to 1108; the claim of mind-to-mind transmission developed in Song-dynasty Chan |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a society's customs |
| Who decides | not applicable. The stanza states what Zen claims to transmit and by what means, rather than prescribing a way of working. |
| What it holds | Zen defines itself in a four-line stanza attributed to Bodhidharma: a special transmission outside the scriptures, not founded upon words and letters, that points directly to the mind and lets a person see their own nature. Heinrich Dumoulin dates the stanza to 1108, centuries after Bodhidharma. Albert Welter argues that the scenes of private mind-to-mind transmission in the recorded-sayings literature are themselves a literary artifice, and Stuart Lachs notes that a school defined as beyond words depends heavily on words and titles in its hierarchy. |
| What is facilitated | The stanza names what Zen says its teaching facilitates: a person's direct seeing of their own nature, prompted by pointing rather than by explanation. It holds that the teacher points and the student sees, and that words cannot carry the seeing itself. |
| Across scales | The claim that some knowledge passes only by demonstration between people, and not by text, recurs in crafts and in scientific laboratory skill, and it holds wherever the knowledge is a capacity rather than a proposition. The critique shows where it stops holding as a description: the claim itself was written down and passed on in books for centuries. |
| The evidence | The support for the attribution is weak: the scholarship Wikipedia cites dates the stanza to 1108 and not to Bodhidharma. The historical critique by Welter and Lachs is scholarly argument rather than measurement, and it holds that the claim of transmission outside texts was produced and maintained through texts and institutions. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The stanza is the most famous statement that seeing one's nature is occasioned by pointing and not by reading, which is the claim the book, as a text, has to meet. Who decides the content: Lachs's criticism shows that a tradition claiming to bypass words can still concentrate authority in the titles of those who are said to have received the transmission. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The stanza says plainly that a text alone does not transmit the realization, yet Welter's and Lachs's scholarship shows that the claim itself was recorded and spread in texts, which leaves open what a text can do. The book is a text that describes an arc it hopes will be lived, which is the very situation the stanza declares insufficient. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics such as Lachs hold that the idealized story of mind-to-mind transmission gives Zen teachers an undeserved level of authority, which the entries on dharma transmission and on Joshu Sasaki take up. |
| Quoted from its sources | "A special transmission outside the scriptures Not founded upon words and letters; By pointing directly to [one's] mind It lets one see into [one's own true] nature and [thus] attain Buddhahood." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhidharma) "The stanza, in fact, is not Bodhidharma's, but, rather, dates to the year 1108." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhidharma) "no more than an artifice to bolster Chan pretensions as a secret, mind-to-mind transmission." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan) "despite Zen's self-definition as beyond words and letters, in terms of its hierarchical organization, words and titles matter a great deal." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_transmission) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, identity self, learning skill |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `provenance/transmission digs/dig 3 — margin architectures.md`; The transmission dig records that Zen's commentary-rich koan books ran inside a binding community with living authority and that Zen states a teacher must confirm a claimed awakening; this entry adds the founding stanza, its late dating, and the scholarly argument that the transmission story is itself literary. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhidharma; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_transmission |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *special transmission outside the scriptures* | The special transmission outside the scriptures is Zen's claim that its realization passes from teacher to student directly rather than through texts. |
| *not founded upon words and letters* | This phrase states that the realization does not rest on written or spoken formulations. |
| *pointing directly to the mind* | Pointing directly to the mind is the stanza's name for the teacher's way of occasioning a student's seeing of their own nature. |

#### The Flower Sermon: holding up a flower without words

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Zen (Chan) |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Chan legend attributed to the Buddha and Mahākāśyapa; case 6 of the Gateless Barrier (Wumenguan) |
| Date | Earliest known version 1036; collected in the Wumenguan in the 13th century |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The teacher's gesture carries no content and the disciple supplies the understanding, but the teacher then declares with authority who has received the transmission. |
| What happens | In the story the Buddha gives a sermon without words by holding up a flower before his assembly. No one understands except Mahākāśyapa, who smiles. The Buddha then declares that he possesses the true Dharma eye, a teaching that does not rest on words or letters, and entrusts it to Mahākāśyapa. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher faces the assembly and holds up a flower without speaking. (2) One disciple responds with a smile, and the others do not respond. (3) The teacher confirms aloud that the disciple has received what was transmitted: "This I entrust to Mahākāśyapa." |
| What is facilitated | The story presents a disciple's recognition as occasioned by a single wordless gesture and then confirmed by the teacher's public declaration. The gesture supplies no explanation; the understanding is the disciple's, and the naming of it is the teacher's. |
| Across scales | A gesture that one member of a group understands and the rest do not, followed by a public acknowledgment, occurs in any teaching room, and it holds wherever understanding shows in response rather than in words. It depends on a witness who can recognize the response, so it does not hold in a room with no one to see the smile. |
| The evidence | The story is a legend with no historical claim to the Buddha's time; Wikipedia records that its earliest known version appeared in 1036, in Chinese Chan sources. It serves the school as its origin story for transmission without words. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The disciple's smile is seen and named by the teacher, so the story joins the disciple's recognition to being recognized by another in one scene. Participation rather than watching: The whole assembly watches the same gesture and only one person takes part in it, which is the distinction between a spectator and a participant in its clearest form. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The story is Zen's own statement that the realization passes outside scriptures, so it holds that a text cannot do what the gesture did; yet the story reaches its readers as a text. The book lacks the teacher who sees the smile and names it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the true form of the formless, the subtle dharma gate that does not rest on words or letters but is a special transmission outside of the scriptures. This I entrust to Mahākāśyapa." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flower_Sermon) "The earliest known version of the tale appeared in 1036." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flower_Sermon) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/second person ecology.md`; The second person survey sorts second persons into roles such as the companion, who is present and asks nothing, and the answerer, who speaks the recognition; the Flower Sermon combines a wordless gesture with an answerer's public confirmation. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flower_Sermon; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gateless_Barrier |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *niān huā wéi xiào* | Niān huā wéi xiào means picking up a flower and smiling, the Chinese name of the story. |
| *true Dharma eye* | The true Dharma eye is the Buddha's name in the story for the realization he transmits, which he says rests on no words or letters. |

#### The shout (katsu; Chinese hè)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Zen (Chan, Linji school) |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Linji Yixuan (died 866) and the Mazu Daoyi (709–788) lineage; continued in the Rinzai school |
| Date | 8th–9th century Tang China; recorded in the Record of Linji (translated by Burton Watson, 1999) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The master shouts in answer to a question or at a moment of his choosing, and the student has no say in when or whether it comes. |
| What happens | Instead of answering a question such as "What is the basic meaning of Buddhism?", the master gives a loud shout. Linji distinguished four kinds of shout and ended his explanation of them by shouting when a monk started to answer. Wikipedia's article describes the shout as used to express the master's awakened state or to bring about a student's first awakening, and places it in a lineage of harsh methods that also included striking students. |
| Steps | (1) A student asks the master a question about the basic meaning of the teaching. (2) The master answers with a shout instead of words. (3) The master watches the student's response, as when a monk bowed low and Linji said, "This fine monk is the kind who's worth talking to!" (4) When a student begins to explain, the master may shout again to cut the explanation off. |
| What is facilitated | An interruption of the student's discursive answer is facilitated, at the moment the student reaches for explanation. The shout supplies no content and cannot supply the realization, and the tradition's own death poem records its failure. |
| Across scales | The shout shares its structure with the Tibetan PHAṬ and with any abrupt interruption of an ongoing process, and it holds where an ongoing process can be interrupted by a sudden shock. It stops holding when the shout becomes a routine the student expects. |
| The evidence | The support is the recorded sayings of Linji and later Rinzai practice, as summarized in Wikipedia; the recorded encounters are literary sources whose historical reliability scholars such as Mario Poceski dispute. No study of the shout's effects exists that I found, and a Rinzai priest's death poem, quoted in the same article, says he cried the shout for sixty years to no avail. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The shout depends entirely on its moment: it answers the instant a student reaches for an explanation, which bears on the book's attention to when a move takes effect. Harm and its conditions: The tradition justifies the shout as skill in means that would otherwise count as abusive speech, which names a limit the book's own directness must respect. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The shout exists only between people in the moment, so the tradition gives no reason to think a printed shout occasions anything. It also relies on the master's authority to be harsh, which the book forgoes. |
| Limits and critiques | The tradition itself classes the shout with striking students and justifies both as skillful means that would otherwise count as abusive speech. |
| Quoted from its sources | "What is the basic meaning of Buddhism?" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsu_(Zen)) "The monk bowed low. The Master said, "This fine monk is the kind who's worth talking to!"" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsu_(Zen)) "At times my shout is like a golden-haired lion crouching on the ground. At times my shout is like the search pole and the shadow grass. At times my shout doesn't work like a shout at all. Do you understand?" The monk started to answer, whereupon the Master gave a shout." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsu_(Zen)) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, spirit ultimate, identity self |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no entry on the Zen shout or on an abrupt interruption used as a teaching move. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsu_(Zen); https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linji_Yixuan; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *katsu (hè)* | Katsu is the Japanese reading of a Chinese character meaning to shout, used for the Zen master's shout. |
| *skill in means (upāya-kauśalya)* | Skill in means is the Mahāyāna doctrine, cited to justify the shout, that a harsh act is permissible when it serves to end suffering. |

#### Checking questions (sassho) and their standardized answers

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Zen (Rinzai) |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | The two Japanese Rinzai koan curricula of the Inzan and Takuju lines (Inzan Ien, 1751–1814; Takuju Kosen, 1760–1833, both students of Gasan Jito); described by Victor Sogen Hori; criticized by Ama Samy and in Tominaga Shūho's 1916 critique |
| Date | Late 18th century onward (the Inzan and Takuju curricula); a secret answer record published in 1916; described by Hori (1999–2006) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The teacher asks the questions in private interview and accepts or rejects each response. |
| What happens | After a student passes a first koan such as Mu or the sound of one hand, the teacher tests the insight with further checking questions, between twenty and a hundred of them depending on the lineage. The questions and their accepted answers form a standardized set, recorded in secret instruction books (missanroku) that were published in a critique in 1916. Critics say students learn a ritual performance and submission to the master, and Foulk (2000) writes that the accepted proof of satori in present-day Rinzai is a set of literary and rhetorical skills that takes years to acquire. |
| Steps | (1) The student presents a response to the assigned koan in private interview. (2) The teacher accepts the response and then asks checking questions that probe the same insight from other angles. (3) The student must answer each to the teacher's satisfaction. (4) In Rinzai practice the student also supplies a capping phrase from Chinese poetry that expresses the insight. (5) Only when the checking questions are passed does the student move to the next koan. |
| What is facilitated | The student's insight is tested and deepened from many angles, so that one moment of seeing is made to hold across cases. The teacher supplies the questions and the verdicts; the critics' point is that the teacher can also supply, through the standard answers, what passes for the insight. |
| Across scales | Testing a claimed understanding by varied follow-up questions is the logic of any oral examination, and it holds wherever understanding should transfer to new cases. It stops discriminating once the answers are fixed and learnable, which is what the published answer books show. |
| The evidence | The procedure is documented by Hori's ethnographic and textual work and by the published answer books, as summarized in Wikipedia. The critique that the system trains performance rather than insight comes from Ama Samy and from the 1916 exposé, and it is a judgment rather than a measured finding. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The checking questions are the Zen tradition's instrument for telling insight from imitation, and the answer books show the same instrument can be passed by imitation. Who decides the content: Standardized answers put the content of an accepted insight in the lineage's records rather than in the student's seeing. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition holds that the checking must be done by a teacher in person, and the 1916 publication of the answers shows that a text containing the answers does not reproduce the test. The book asks its reader to recognize without any test of this kind. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics say the system teaches ritualized performance and submission to the master's arbitration. |
| Quoted from its sources | "there are between 20 and 100 checking questions, depending on the teaching lineage." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan) "Those checking questions, and their answers, are part of a standardised set of questions and answers." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan) "In 1916 Tominaga Shūho, using the pseudonym "Hau Hōō", published a critique of the Rinzai" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan) "clever repartees, ritualized language and gestures and be submissive to the master's diktat and arbitration." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan) "is a set of literary and rhetorical skills that takes many years to acquire." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, learning skill, attention awareness |
| In the survey | Dokusan (sanzen), the private interview with a Zen teacher; this entry adds the number of checking questions per koan, the standardized answers and secret answer records, their 1916 publication, and the critique that the proof of satori became literary skill. |
| In the vault | `provenance/transmission digs/dig 3 — margin architectures.md`; The transmission dig holds that sassho distinguish genuine insight from imitation and that capping phrases decay into memorized answers; this entry adds how many checking questions there are, the standardized answer books, and the critics' charge. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_Buddhism |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sassho* | Sassho are checking questions a Rinzai teacher uses to test and deepen a student's insight into a koan. |
| *missanroku* | Missanroku are secret records of a lineage's koan curriculum and its standardized answers. |
| *shokan (first barrier)* | The shokan is the first koan of the curriculum, usually Mu or the sound of one hand. |

#### Observing the key phrase (kanhua, hua tou), taught to lay people by letter

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Zen (Chan, Linji school); Korean Seon |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Dahui Zonggao (1089–1163); popularized in Korea by Chinul |
| Date | 12th century Song China; Dahui's letters later translated as Swampland Flowers (J. C. Cleary, 1977) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The teacher gives the phrase and the instructions, often by letter, and the practitioner carries the phrase through daily life without regular interviews. |
| What happens | Dahui taught students, including lay officials, to hold a single phrase from an old encounter, such as "Mu", "What is it?", or "Who am I?", and to let a great doubt build around it until the doubt breaks. Wikipedia's article records that this practice does not use regular private interviews and that Dahui said it can be carried out by lay people in the midst of their daily activities. He often gave his instructions through letters to his pupils. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher assigns a single key phrase, sometimes one phrase for a whole life. (2) The practitioner holds the phrase in attention during sitting and during daily activities. (3) The practitioner lets a sense of great doubt about the phrase gather rather than answering it. (4) The doubt is expected to shatter in a moment of awakening, after which the practitioner continues with the same phrase. |
| What is facilitated | The practitioner's own gathered doubt is facilitated until it breaks, with the teacher present only through the assigned phrase and, in Dahui's case, through letters. The teacher does not answer the phrase; the practitioner is not meant to answer it either. |
| Across scales | Holding one unanswered question over a long period is a practice found in lay inquiry and in research, and it holds wherever a question can be held in mind alongside ordinary work. Its reliance on a teacher's letters shows the practice can be taught at a distance, though the tradition still assigns the phrase through a teacher. |
| The evidence | The support is historical scholarship (Schlütter 2008; Lachs 2012) summarized in Wikipedia. Ama Samy criticizes concentration on the phrase as a technique that can produce experiences but not wisdom, and says these forced efforts "can even run on auto-pilot". |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Dahui directed lay people toward awakening through letters and a phrase held in mind during daily life, which is the nearest historical precedent in Zen for the book's letter read alone. Participation rather than watching: The practice happens only in the practitioner's own sustained questioning, so no one can receive it by reading about it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition holds that a teacher assigns the phrase and that Dahui's letters were written to particular students, so it supports instruction by letter from a known teacher rather than a text addressed to anyone. Dahui also stressed that initial insight is essential, and the tradition reports that he burned his copy of his teacher's koan collection, the Blue Cliff Record, which marks his distrust of texts as objects of study. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics say concentration on the phrase can become a mechanical technique that produces experiences rather than insight. |
| Quoted from its sources | "What is it?" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Tou) "Who am I?" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Tou) "practice does not use regular interviews and question and answer sessions between student and teacher (dokusan)." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Tou) "can be carried out by laymen in the midst of their daily activities." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Tou) "Dahui stressed that it was possible for laymen to achieve enlightenment through this practice. He often gave instructions through letters to his pupils." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Tou) "These methods and techniques are forced efforts which can even run on auto-pilot." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `provenance/transmission digs/dig 3 — margin architectures.md`; The transmission dig records Dahui's burning of the Blue Cliff Record and his reduction of the koan to one uncommented word; this entry adds that the method ran without regular interviews, could be practiced in daily life, and was often taught by letter. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Tou; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahui_Zonggao |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *hua tou (key phrase)* | A hua tou is a short phrase taken from a koan and held as the object of meditation; the term can be translated as the point beyond which speech exhausts itself. |
| *great doubt* | Great doubt is the gathered, unanswered questioning that the practitioner cultivates around the phrase. |
| *kanhua chan* | Kanhua chan means observing-the-phrase Zen, Dahui's name for the method. |

#### The turning word

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Zen (Chan) |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Chan encounter literature; the wild fox koan of Baizhang Huaihai, case 2 of the Gateless Barrier (Wumenguan) |
| Date | The wild fox story appears as early as 1036 (T'ien-sheng kuang-teng lu); collected in the 13th-century Wumenguan |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The student asks for the word and the teacher chooses and speaks it. |
| What happens | A turning word is a phrase a teacher says to prompt a listener's realization at the right moment. In the wild fox koan, an old man tells Baizhang that he was reborn as a fox for five hundred lifetimes for saying that a person of great practice does not fall into cause and effect, and he asks Baizhang for a turning word. Baizhang answers, "Don't ignore cause and effect," and the story says the man immediately had great realization and was freed from the fox body. |
| Steps | (1) A person stuck in a wrong understanding asks the teacher directly: "Reverend master, please say a turning word for me and free me from this wild fox body." (2) The person restates the question that trapped them. (3) The teacher answers with a short phrase that shifts the question rather than explaining it: "Don't ignore cause and effect." (4) The listener's understanding turns on hearing it, and the story records the realization at once. |
| What is facilitated | A listener's shift out of a fixed misunderstanding is facilitated by a few words said at the moment they ask. The word supplies a turn in the question; the story locates the realization in the listener, who had been asking for it. |
| Across scales | A short reframing sentence that changes how a person holds a problem is a move found in therapy and in coaching, and it holds when the listener is already asking and ready. It does not hold when the same words are delivered to someone who has not asked, because the story's word works as an answer. |
| The evidence | The support is a literary koan whose events are legendary; Wikipedia's article records its appearance in 1036 and Dōgen's later rejection of a common reading of it. No study of turning words exists that I found. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The turning word works as an answer to a request, so its force depends on the moment of asking rather than on the words alone. Recognition: The story shows recognition arriving as a turn in a question the listener already had, which fits the book's claim that the reader already holds what is recognized. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition gives the turning word from teacher to asker in the moment, so it does not claim that the same words on a page turn a reader; the koan itself survives as a written case that students work on under a teacher. The book's last line is written to be answered, whereas a turning word answers a question the listener asked first. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Reverend master, please say a turning word for me and free me from this wild fox body." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_fox_koan) "Baizhang said, "Don't ignore cause and effect." Immediately the man had great realization." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_fox_koan) "a "turning word," a phrase intended to prompt one to realization" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_fox_koan) "dating back as early as 1036, when it appeared in the Chinese biographical history" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_fox_koan) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a single move |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, identity self, memory past |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/a word at madhyama can be perfected forever a word at vaikhari can be answered.md`; This note holds that a spoken word, unlike a word kept in the mind, can be answered, which bears on the book's last line; the turning word is a spoken word that answers a request and turns the person who asked. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_fox_koan; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baizhang_Huaihai; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gateless_Barrier |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *turning word* | A turning word is a phrase intended to prompt a listener to realization, spoken by a teacher at the moment of need. |
| *not ignoring cause and effect* | Not ignoring cause and effect is Baizhang's turning word; Dōgen later wrote that those who say one cannot ignore cause and effect identify with it, while those who say one does not fall into it deny causation. |

#### Juzhi's one finger, and the attendant who copied it

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Zen (Chan) |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Juzhi Yizhi (Japanese Gutei), 9th-century Chan master, student of Hangzhou Tianlong; case 3 of the Gateless Barrier |
| Date | 9th century; collected in the 13th-century Wumenguan; English translation by Nyogen Senzaki and Paul Reps |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The master alone raises the finger, and the story shows that the same gesture made by an imitator carries nothing. |
| What happens | Juzhi was enlightened when his teacher Tianlong held up a finger, and afterward Juzhi answered every question about Zen by raising one finger. A boy attendant began to imitate him, raising a finger when asked what his master taught. In the story Juzhi cut off the boy's finger, called to him as he ran, and raised his own finger, and in that instant the boy was enlightened; Wumen's comment says the enlightenment has nothing to do with a finger. |
| Steps | (1) The master answers a question about the teaching by raising one finger instead of speaking. (2) An imitator copies the gesture without the understanding behind it. (3) The master removes the imitation, in the story violently, and then raises his own finger at the moment the boy turns back. (4) The commentary warns against clinging to the finger: "Enlightenment, which Gutei and the boy attained, has nothing to do with a finger." |
| What is facilitated | The story presents a recognition occasioned by a gesture only at the moment the boy's copied gesture has been taken from him. The gesture supplies nothing by itself; the story says so through the boy, whose copied gesture conveyed nothing. |
| Across scales | A form copied without the understanding that produced it has no effect in any practice, from a ritual repeated by rote to a management technique adopted by name, and this holds wherever the form's effect depended on the person using it. The story's second half, the same form becoming effective at the right moment, depends on a teacher and cannot be reproduced by copies. |
| The evidence | The support is a koan story from the Wumenguan in Senzaki and Reps's translation; it is a teaching legend, and the violence in it is not historical testimony. Its point, stated in the commentary, is that the gesture is not the realization. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The copied finger is the tradition's clearest picture of a counterfeit: the outward sign of realization reproduced without the realization. Harm and its conditions: The story's violence toward the attendant is presented as a teaching act, which shows how the tradition could frame harm by a teacher as instruction. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition's point is that a gesture reproduced without the understanding behind it transmits nothing, which argues against any text's reproduction of a teacher's pointing as the vehicle; a printed pointing is a copy of this kind. The story also relies on a teacher's violent act, which the book would never frame as teaching. |
| Limits and critiques | The story presents a teacher mutilating an attendant as a teaching act; it is legend, but it records a tradition that could describe harm by a teacher as instruction. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Gutei raised his finger whenever he was asked a question about Zen. A boy attendant began to imitate him in this way." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juzhi_Yizhi) "When the boy turned his head to Gutei, Gutei raised up his own finger. In that instant the boy was enlightened." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juzhi_Yizhi) "Enlightenment, which Gutei and the boy attained, has nothing to do with a finger." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juzhi_Yizhi) "Tenryū held up his finger, and at that moment Gutei was" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juzhi_Yizhi) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a single move |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, learning skill, identity self |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the felt sense of truth has a documented counterfeit catalogue.md`; This note catalogues measured ways a felt sense of truth can be produced without truth; the copied finger is the Zen tradition's own emblem of the outward sign of realization produced without the realization. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juzhi_Yizhi; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gateless_Barrier |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *finger-Zen* | Finger-Zen is Juzhi's name for the teaching he received from Tianlong and said he could not exhaust in his whole life. |
| *Gutei Isshi* | Gutei Isshi, meaning Gutei One-finger, is the name the story gave Juzhi. |

#### Wisdom without a teacher (mushi dokugo), including awakening from hearing or reading scripture

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Zen (Chan) and its Indian sources |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Indian sources (the Buddha's claim to have no teacher; the Lotus Sūtra's anācāryaka jñāna); Huineng and the Platform Sūtra; Zongmi; Yongjia Xuanjue; Takuan Sōhō; Tokugawa self-enlightened masters; Shin'ichi Hisamatsu (1889–1980) |
| Date | From early Chan sources such as the Platform Sūtra through early Tokugawa Japan (17th century) to Hisamatsu (1889–1980); the suspicion of it is described by William Bodiford (1991) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. No live teacher occasions the awakening; a scripture heard or read sometimes does, and sometimes nothing external is named. |
| What it holds | Zen keeps a minority record of awakening without a teacher. Huineng is said to have awakened on hearing someone chant the Diamond Sūtra; Yongjia said he awoke from reading the Vimalakīrti Sūtra, and a disciple of Huineng called self-enlightenment without a teacher the heretical path of naturalism; Peter Gregory writes that Zongmi's first awakening came from reading several lines of scripture. The idea is often considered suspect because the risk of self-delusion is high, yet masters such as Takuan held that what can be passed on is not the Dharma, and Hisamatsu taught that one confirms one's own awakening. |
| What is facilitated | In these accounts a text heard or read, or no outside occasion at all, occasions the recognition, and the person certifies it themselves. What no one supplies is the confirmation, which is exactly what the mainstream tradition says is missing. |
| Across scales | A person reaching a finding alone and later meeting others who reached it is the pattern of independent discovery, and it holds wherever the finding does not depend on instruction. It is weakest at certification, since self-certification cannot tell a real finding from a self-deception. |
| The evidence | The support is the tradition's own records and later scholarship (McRae, Gregory, Haskel, Bodiford) summarized in Wikipedia; the awakening stories are hagiographic, and Morten Schlütter notes that the Yongjia episode was added to the Platform Sūtra from a later source. Bodiford reports that because the risk of self-delusion is high, Zen disciples commonly rely on a teacher to authenticate their experience. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Zen's own record includes recognitions occasioned by hearing or reading a text, which is the book's hoped-for case inside a tradition that officially denies it. Knowing that something happened: The tradition's suspicion rests on self-delusion, and Hisamatsu's contrary position makes the recognizer the only judge, so the two positions frame the question the book's reader faces. Remaining the author of one's own change: Hisamatsu's view that the awakened self is one's true master is the strongest statement in Zen of a person remaining the sole authority over their own recognition. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | This minority strand holds that a text heard or read can occasion awakening, and Huineng is quoted as saying that insisting a teacher is necessary is "not right"; the mainstream tradition, by contrast, treats teacherless awakening as suspect. Even in these stories the text was a Buddhist scripture heard or read within a Buddhist culture, not a book designed to produce the event. |
| Limits and critiques | The tradition's own warning is self-delusion: an awakening no one else has checked may be a counterfeit. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The idea of wisdom without a teacher is often considered suspect among various Zen schools" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_without_a_teacher) "since the risk of self-delusion is high, it is common for Zen disciples to rely on their teacher to "authenticate and formally acknowledge" their enlightenment experience." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_without_a_teacher) "Rather, it came about as a result of reading several lines of scripture." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_without_a_teacher) "Those who realize on their own do not need to seek outside. If you insist absolutely that a teacher is necessary in order to attain liberation, that is not right." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_without_a_teacher) "enlightenment by oneself without a teacher was the "heretical path of naturalism."" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_without_a_teacher) "That which can be passed on is not the Dharma." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_without_a_teacher) "Instead of having another verify or confirm one's awakening, one does so for oneself." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kensh%C5%8D) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, identity self, learning skill |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/zen's makyo instruction refuses the self-certifying experience.md`; This note holds that Zen refuses the self-certifying experience and installs relational verification; this entry records the minority strand inside Zen that accepts self-certified awakening and awakening from scripture. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_without_a_teacher; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kensh%C5%8D |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mushi dokugo* | Mushi dokugo is Japanese for independent realization without a master. |
| *jigo-jishō* | Jigo-jishō means self-enlightened and self-certified. |
| *anācāryaka jñāna* | Anācāryaka jñāna is Sanskrit for wisdom without a teacher, a phrase in the Lotus Sūtra. |

#### Dharma transmission as a credential, and the critique that it enables abuse

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Zen (Chan); religious studies |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Stuart Lachs (1999, 2006); Bodhin Kjolhede (Rochester Zen Center); Robert Sharf (1995) on the Sanbō Kyōdan |
| Date | 1995–2018 (Sharf 1995; Lachs 1999 and 2006; Kjolhede's remark published by the Rochester Zen Center, 2018) |
| Scale | an organization, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | not applicable. The entry describes a condition of institutions rather than a way of working. |
| What it holds | In Zen the teacher's confirmation of a student's awakening and the formal authorization of teachers are joined in dharma transmission. Stuart Lachs argues that idealized lineage stories give Western roshis an imputed quasi-divine status and that scandals have been possible because of that status and students' desire for the master's recognition and approval. Bodhin Kjolhede reports a teacher urging disciples to get dharma transmission because afterward no one could touch them, and Robert Sharf, writing about the Sanbō Kyōdan, argues that such an institution survives only by balancing the autonomy of seeing one's nature with a training that inspires obedience and loyalty. |
| What is facilitated | The critique shows what the authority to confirm another person's awakening facilitates when it becomes a credential: the student's dependence on the master's approval, and the teacher's freedom from correction. It does not concern the student's recognition itself, which the critics say the credential cannot certify. |
| Across scales | A credential that confers the power to judge others and cannot itself be reviewed carries the same risk in any institution where the people judged want the judge's approval. Sharf's point about autonomous confirmation shows the opposite pressure too: an institution cannot survive if every person certified by their own seeing is equally authoritative. |
| The evidence | The support is scholarly argument and teachers' testimony, summarized in Wikipedia; Lachs's claim that transmission status made the scandals possible is an interpretation of documented cases rather than a measured finding. The same article records that authorization through dharma transmission does not mean a teacher is infallible and is described as no guarantee of attainment. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The critique names the conditions of harm: an idealized lineage, a title that protects the teacher from challenge, and students who want the master's recognition. Being seen and recognized by another: Lachs names the desire for the master's recognition and approval as part of what made abuse possible, which is the harmful side of the wish to be seen and approved by another. Who decides the content: When the teacher alone can confirm awakening, the teacher decides what counts as the student's realization, which is the arrangement the book declines. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The critics do not claim that a text could replace the teacher; their point is that the confirming teacher is not infallible, which removes some of the advantage the traditions claim over a text but not the need for a second person. The book avoids the credential problem by claiming no authority to confirm, and so also gives no confirmation. |
| Limits and critiques | The critique is itself about harm: it holds that the credential structure has enabled sexual and power abuse in Western Zen communities. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Just get Dharma transmission; once you get that, no one will be able to touch you." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_transmission) "This worship of formalized legitimacy invites abuses" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_transmission) "a desire for the master's aura, recognition, and approval." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_transmission) "Seeing one's nature gives an autonomous confirmation of Zen's ultimate truth, which may conflict with the need to maintain institutions and traditions." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanbo_Kyodan) "with a course of training that inspires obedience and loyalty to the tradition" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanbo_Kyodan) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. The critique comes from scholars and teachers inside and near the Zen tradition and draws on religious studies |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | justice power, spirit ultimate, identity self |
| In the survey | Dokusan (sanzen), the private interview with a Zen teacher; this entry adds the institutional side of the teacher's confirming authority and the documented critique that it enables abuse; the survey entry says only that the practice depends on the teacher's integrity. |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the discrimination family (ignatius §§313–336, engler 1984, kapleau's three pillars).md; This reading record holds Yasutani's statement that awakening is not self-authenticating and must be checked by a teacher; this entry records the scholarly critique of the institution that holds that checking power. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_transmission; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanbo_Kyodan; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koan |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *dharma transmission* | Dharma transmission is the formal recognition by which a Zen teacher authorizes a student as a successor in the lineage. |
| *inka* | Inka is a teacher's formal seal confirming a student's enlightenment and authority. |
| *roshi* | Roshi is an honorific title for a Zen teacher, which Lachs says Western students invest with quasi-divine qualities. |

#### The Joshu Sasaki case: a teacher's authority over enlightenment used to silence students

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | Zen (Rinzai); documented misconduct |
| Tradition | Zen and Chan |
| Originators | Students and senior teachers of Kyozan Joshu Sasaki (1907–2014) at Mount Baldy Zen Center and Rinzai-ji; reported by the New York Times and the Albuquerque Journal (2013) |
| Date | Allegations in a 1982 resignation letter and a 1997 letter from members; public accusation in 2012; the senior teachers' admission in January 2013 |
| Scale | an organization, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | not applicable. The entry records a documented case of harm rather than a way of working. |
| What it holds | Joshu Sasaki, a Rinzai teacher in Los Angeles from 1962, was accused over decades of sexual misconduct with female students, and in 2013 his community's senior teachers admitted in a statement that it had struggled with his sexual misconduct for a significant portion of his career in the United States. A student who raised concerns reported being told by Sasaki that he would never get enlightened if he thought about these things, and the New York Times reported students' accounts that Sasaki told them sexual contact with a Zen master would help them attain new levels of non-attachment. A 1997 letter from members said open discussion had been discouraged. |
| What is facilitated | The case shows the authority to rule on a student's enlightenment being used to silence a student's questions and to recast abuse as practice. It documents the opposite of facilitation: the teacher's judgment of what counts as progress was used against the students. |
| Across scales | Using a group's highest value to demand silence about its leader's conduct occurs in any community where the leader interprets the value, and this holds wherever the leader cannot be overruled. In this case the admission came from the community's senior teachers in 2013, after a former monk's public accusation in 2012, and not from the teacher. |
| The evidence | The evidence is students' letters, a senior teachers' public statement, and newspaper reporting, summarized in Wikipedia; a report on the allegations was published with the New York Times coverage. Wikipedia's article places Sasaki among several Zen teachers who came to the United States in the 1960s and were involved in publicized sexual scandals. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The case shows harm made possible when the person who judges a student's enlightenment also defines what the student's doubts mean. Knowing that something happened: Sasaki's reported claim that sexual contact advanced non-attachment is a case in which the teacher's authority over what counts as progress was used to relabel harm as realization. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition around the case keeps the teacher as the one who confirms awakening, and a book cannot abuse its reader in this way because it holds no such power; for the same reason it cannot confirm anything either. The case is about misconduct by a person, not about what a text can occasion. |
| Limits and critiques | The case is a documented instance of harm: students reported sexual contact justified as practice and pressure not to question it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Joshu Roshi told me I would never get enlightened if I thought about these things." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyozan_Joshu_Sasaki) "sexual contact with a Zen master (roshi) like him would help them attain new levels of" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyozan_Joshu_Sasaki) "In the past open discussion was discouraged, and people were left feeling afraid to raise their concerns about this matter." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyozan_Joshu_Sasaki) "has struggled with our teacher Joshu Sasaki Roshi's sexual misconduct for a significant portion of his career in the United States." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyozan_Joshu_Sasaki) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. The finding concerns a Rinzai teaching community and is documented by letters and journalism rather than by research |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | justice power, trauma healing, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/second person ecology.md`; The second person survey holds the certifier as the role that says whether what happened was the thing; this case documents a certifier using that role against the people he certified. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyozan_Joshu_Sasaki; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma_transmission |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *non-attachment* | Non-attachment is a central Zen aim, which students reported Sasaki invoked to justify sexual contact. |
| *roshi* | Roshi is the honorific title of a Zen teacher, the position from which Sasaki taught. |

### Advaita Vedānta (Ramana Maharshi)

#### Teaching in silence

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Advaita Vedānta |
| Tradition | Advaita Vedānta (Ramana Maharshi) |
| Originators | Ramana Maharshi (1879–1950); compared by devotees to Dakṣiṇāmūrti, the form of Śiva who teaches in silence |
| Date | From his arrival at Arunachala in 1896 until his death in 1950; his statement on silence recorded in Talks with Sri Ramana Maharshi (Venkataramiah, 2000 edition, p. 433) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The teacher supplies presence and silence, and the visitor supplies whatever question and inquiry arise in it. |
| What happens | Ramana Maharshi's main means of instruction was to sit silently with visitors, using words sparingly. When devotees asked him to explain a hymn to Dakṣiṇāmūrti, the teacher who instructs by silence, he sat motionless in total silence. He said that silence is the true instruction, suited only to the most advanced seekers, and that others need words, which can only indicate the truth; Ankur Barua notes that in dialogue he turned attention from the question to the nature of the questioner, often leaving the devotee in silence. |
| Steps | (1) The visitor comes into the teacher's presence and sits. (2) The teacher remains silent, or answers a question sparingly. (3) When the visitor asks, the teacher turns the inquiry back from the question to the one asking it. (4) The visitor is left in the silence, which the teacher calls the true instruction. |
| What is facilitated | The visitor's attention to the one who is asking is facilitated, in a silence the teacher keeps. The teacher supplies no content; Ramana says truth can only be indicated, and the indicating is left to silence. |
| Across scales | Sitting silently with another person in a way that turns their attention back on themselves appears in therapy and in contemplative companionship, and it holds where a person can bear the silence. It depends on the seeker's readiness, which Ramana himself names as its limit. |
| The evidence | The support is devotees' accounts and Ramana's recorded statements, summarized in Wikipedia from Ebert (2006) and Talks; there is no study of what silent sitting with him did. Ramana's own statement limits the method to the most advanced seekers. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The teacher offers nothing to be learned and turns attention to the questioner, which leaves the recognition entirely to the person who came. Who decides the content: The silent teacher decides no content at all, which is the most complete form of a facilitator's restraint. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Ramana held that silence is suited only to the most advanced and that others need words, so his tradition allows words and texts a secondary, indicating role, while it gives the living presence the primary one. The book offers words to every reader and cannot offer presence. |
| Limits and critiques | Ramana himself said the method suits only the most advanced seekers, so silence offered to others may teach nothing. |
| Quoted from its sources | "They waited for his answer, but in vain. The Maharishi sat motionless on his seat, in total silence." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramana_Maharshi) "It is suited only for the most advanced seeker. The others are unable to draw full inspiration from it. Therefore, they require words to explain the truth. But truth is beyond words; it does not warrant explanation. All that is possible is to indicate It." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramana_Maharshi) "In his dialogues with devotees, Ramana emphasised the importance of exploring the nature of the questioner, and not the question." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramana_Maharshi) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | Self-enquiry (ātma-vichāra), 'Who am I?'; this entry adds ramana's teaching by silence as a move distinct from self-enquiry, with his own statement of whom it suits. |
| In the vault | `ops/second person ecology.md`; The second person survey lists Laozi's sage who teaches without words among companions who open nothing and judge nothing; Ramana's silence is a teacher of this kind who nevertheless calls the silence the true instruction. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramana_Maharshi |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *upadeśa* | Upadeśa is spiritual instruction given by a guru to a disciple. |
| *darśana* | Darśana is the sight of a holy person, which many devotees came to Ramana to receive. |
| *Dakṣiṇāmūrti* | Dakṣiṇāmūrti is the form of Śiva as the teacher who instructs his disciples in silence. |

#### The guru only shows the way, and the Self is not found in books

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Advaita Vedānta |
| Tradition | Advaita Vedānta (Ramana Maharshi) |
| Originators | Ramana Maharshi (1879–1950), in Who Am I? (Nan Yar?), answers first given around 1902 to M. Sivaprakasam Pillai; translation by T. M. P. Mahadevan |
| Date | c. 1902 (first published by Pillai in 1923); Mahadevan's translation published by Sri Ramanasramam |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The guru shows the way and the seeker's own effort and own eye of knowledge do the rest. |
| What it holds | In Who Am I? Ramana says that God and the guru only show the way to release and do not take anyone there; each person must pursue the path by their own effort, and one can know oneself only with one's own eye of knowledge. Asked whether reading books helps, he answers that all texts teach that the mind should be quieted, that once this is understood there is no need for endless reading, and that it is futile to search for the Self in books. The answers were first given by gesture and writing, because Ramana was not talking in 1902; and Ramana later said, as Heinrich Zimmer (1948) cites him, that sacred books named what he had already felt in his own awakening. |
| What is facilitated | The seeker's own inquiry is facilitated, with the guru or a text pointing the way and the seeker doing the looking. Ramana holds that neither the guru nor a book supplies the knowing of the Self; they can only show where to look. |
| Across scales | The division in which a teacher indicates what to do and the learner must do it holds for any learning that the learner must perform, and Ramana's image of Rama needing no mirror applies wherever the thing known is the knower. It stops at the scale of shared or institutional knowledge, which does require books and others. |
| The evidence | The support is Ramana's own text in a published translation and biographical accounts summarized in Wikipedia; it is doctrine and testimony, not a measured finding. The translator's introduction states that the answers were first given by gesture and writing and recorded from memory by Pillai. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Ramana's own account, that books later named what he had already felt, runs in the same direction as the book's origin story: noticing first and finding company afterward. Remaining the author of one's own change: Ramana places release in the seeker's own effort and own eye of knowledge even within the guru's grace, which keeps the seeker the author of the change. Being seen and recognized by another: His question whether Rama needs a mirror to know he is Rama denies that self-knowledge requires another's seeing, which answers the question from the opposite side from the Zen and Tibetan certifiers. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Ramana allows a text a real but limited role, since texts all point to quieting the mind and his own teaching was first given in writing, but he holds that the Self cannot be found in books and that reading beyond the instruction is futile. The book is a text that hopes to occasion recognition, whereas Ramana would allow it only to point toward an inquiry the reader must carry out without it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "God and the Guru will only show the way to release; they will not by themselves take the soul to the state of release." (https://www.gururamana.org/Teachings/who_am_I.pdf) "One can know oneself only with one's own eye of knowledge, and not with somebody else's. Does he who is Rama require the help of a mirror to know that he is Rama?" (https://www.gururamana.org/Teachings/who_am_I.pdf) "once this has been understood there is no need for endless reading." (https://www.gururamana.org/Teachings/who_am_I.pdf) "Since the Self has to be inquired into by discarding the five sheaths, it is futile to search for it in books." (https://www.gururamana.org/Teachings/who_am_I.pdf) "he answered the questions put to him by gestures, and when these were not understood, by writing." (https://www.gururamana.org/Teachings/who_am_I.pdf) "these books named and analysed, what I before involuntarily felt, without being able to appoint or analyse." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramana_Maharshi) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | identity self, spirit ultimate, learning skill |
| In the survey | Self-enquiry (ātma-vichāra), 'Who am I?'; this entry adds ramana's own answers on the guru's role and on reading books, the origin of the text in gestures and writing, and his account that books named his experience afterward. |
| In the vault | `notes/the book's honest origin story is noticing first and finding company after.md`; This note holds that the book's origin runs from the author's own noticing to the traditions found afterward; Ramana reports the same order in his own life, awakening first and learning the names from books later. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.gururamana.org/Teachings/who_am_I.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramana_Maharshi |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Nan Yar? (Who am I?)* | Nan Yar? is the title of the questions and answers in which Ramana set out self-enquiry. |
| *the five sheaths* | The five sheaths are the layers of body and mind that Advaita says must be set aside in inquiring into the Self, which Ramana says books lie outside of. |
| *the guru's gracious look* | The guru's gracious look is Ramana's phrase for the guru's grace, which he says saves those within its range and keeps them from getting lost, while each must still pursue the path by their own effort. |

### The direct path (Atmananda Krishna Menon and successors)

#### Books are dead; only the living Karana guru can give the truth

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Advaita Vedānta (the direct path) |
| Tradition | The direct path (Atmananda Krishna Menon and successors) |
| Originators | Atmananda Krishna Menon (1883–1959), in talks recorded by Nitya Tripta (S. Balakrishna Pillai) |
| Date | Talks of 1950–1959, published as Notes on Spiritual Discourses of Shri Atmananda (1963); edition arranged by subject |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. Atmananda holds that the truth is gained only through personal contact, in complete surrender, with a living sage whose words the disciple listens to. |
| What it holds | Atmananda held that only one who has heard the truth from a Karana guru, a living sage, can realize it, citing the Chāndogya Upaniṣad's "He who has a Guru alone knows." He told students that books will not help much and may do harm, because a reader reads their own sense into the words, and that a book is dead and inert and cannot answer a new question that arises from it. He also held that a sage who has died cannot become the guru of a new aspirant, and that the realization comes when the disciple, brought to the brink of the mind, listens to the guru explaining the Self and is suddenly thrown into the ultimate. |
| What is facilitated | The disciple's sudden realization of the Self is facilitated, in Atmananda's account, by listening to a living sage's words at the brink of the mind. Books and other objective instruments can at most show a person that they are not the body, senses, or mind. |
| Across scales | The claim that a living teacher can answer the next question and a text cannot holds for any interactive learning, and it is Atmananda's precise reason for preferring the living teacher. A text that anticipates questions, such as a dialogue or a letter written to be answered, meets his objection only in part, because it still cannot answer a question it did not anticipate. |
| The evidence | The support is Atmananda's recorded talks in his disciple's notes; they are a teacher's positions, not findings. He also argued that no one can test a guru in advance, since one would have to be higher than the guru to apply the test, and he advised putting one's doubts to a proposed guru and relying more on the response of one's heart than on intellectual satisfaction. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Atmananda locates the moment of realization in listening to a living sage's words, which is a direct challenge to a book's hope of occasioning it. Being seen and recognized by another: Atmananda holds that the truth is heard from a person who has realized it, so recognition, in his account, comes through relationship with a realized other. The future: His ruling that a dead sage cannot be a new aspirant's guru bears on what a book can be for readers who come after its author. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Atmananda holds that a text alone cannot occasion realization: "nobody can get at the essence of Truth through books or through language alone," because a book cannot answer the next question. The book's form as a letter written to be answered meets his objection only in part, because it remains a text that cannot hear the reader's reply. |
| Limits and critiques | Atmananda asks for complete surrender to a living guru and says a guru cannot be tested in advance; his only safeguard is to put one's doubts to the proposed guru and accept him if both heart and intellect are satisfied, which rests on the seeker's own judgment. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Books will not help you much to understand the Truth. Sometimes they may even do you much harm." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "A book, as such, is dead and inert, and cannot answer a new question arising from the one dealt with in the book." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "Therefore nobody can get at the essence of Truth through books or through language alone." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "It means: He who has a Guru alone knows." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "No. Never. When a Sage leaves his" (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "he is suddenly thrown into that supreme experience of the ultimate." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "If a child asks where its body is, no book can teach it where it is." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | identity self, spirit ultimate, learning skill |
| In the survey | Guru–shishya relationship (paramparā); this entry adds a named twentieth-century teacher's reasoned case that books cannot give realization, including the specific argument that a book cannot answer a new question. |
| In the vault | `notes/the solitary form stands against seven witnesses who hold a second person constitutive.md`; This note gathers seven traditions that hold a second person necessary against the book's letter read alone; Atmananda is a further witness who gives a reason, that a book cannot answer a new question. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmananda_Krishna_Menon |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Karana guru* | A Karana guru is Atmananda's term for the living sage through whom a disciple realizes the truth, as distinct from teachers of objective knowledge. |
| *tattvopadeśa* | Tattvopadeśa is formal instruction into the truth, which Atmananda said cannot by itself be the criterion of the guru relationship. |
| *the 'I'-principle* | The 'I'-principle is Atmananda's name for the self-luminous 'I' that needs no proof and to which all objects relate. |

#### Drawing attention to the interval between two thoughts

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| Field | Advaita Vedānta (the direct path) |
| Tradition | The direct path (Atmananda Krishna Menon and successors) |
| Originators | Atmananda Krishna Menon (1883–1959); reported by Joseph Campbell, who visited him in the 1950s |
| Date | 1950s (talks recorded 1950–1959; Campbell's visit in the 1950s) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. Atmananda says that only a sage can draw a person's attention to the interval, though the person then looks for themselves. |
| What happens | Atmananda asked students to notice the interval between two thoughts, where, he taught, the pure 'I' shines with no thought of any state. Joseph Campbell recalled Atmananda giving him the meditation "Where are you between two thoughts?" Atmananda illustrated the move with a picture painted on paper: finding a patch of bare paper proves the paper is behind the whole picture, and finding oneself between two thoughts shows one is in the thoughts as well; he rejected any attempt to prolong the interval as absurd, since it is timeless. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher asks, in Campbell's report, "Where are you between two thoughts?" (2) The student notices the gap between one thought and the next, where no thought of any state is present. (3) The student recognizes what is present in the gap as the 'I' itself rather than as a blank. (4) The student concludes that the same 'I' is present within the thoughts too, as the paper is present under the whole picture. (5) The student does not try to prolong the gap, which Atmananda says is timeless rather than short. |
| What is facilitated | The student's noticing of the awareness present between thoughts, and then within them, is facilitated by a question that turns attention to the gap. The question supplies a place to look; the teacher says that what is found there is the student's own self. |
