| Across scales | Attending to the pause between events in order to notice what is constant through them is a move used in meditation, music, and conversation, and it holds wherever there is a sequence with gaps. It depends on attention fine enough to notice a gap, and Atmananda's own caution is that treating the gap as a thing to prolong defeats it. |
| The evidence | The support is Atmananda's recorded talks and Campbell's recollection quoted in Wikipedia; there is no study of the move. Atmananda himself says the attention must be drawn by a sage, so the move as he states it is not self-administered. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The move points to something present between and within thoughts rather than something to be attained, which matches the book's claim that what is recognized never left. Timing and pacing: Atmananda forbids prolonging the gap, which bears on the book's handling of its peak as something not to be held on to. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Atmananda says that only a sage can draw a person's attention to the interval (the Notes, section 1013), so his tradition does not hold that a text alone can occasion the recognition, although the question itself can be printed and asked by anyone. His aim is the realization that one is Consciousness itself, whereas the book leaves what is recognized unnamed. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So also, if you succeed in discovering yourself between two mentations, you easily come to the conclusion that you are in the mentations as well." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "The idea of prolonging the timeless is absurd." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "The limitation is mere time, and the content of the interval is beyond both time and mind." (http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf) "Then [Menon] gave me a little meditation: 'Where are you between two thoughts?'" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmananda_Krishna_Menon) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the self you are seeking has been nearer than the seeking itself.md`; This note holds that what is sought has been nearer than the seeking; Atmananda's interval move is a concrete way of turning to what was nearest, found between and within thoughts. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | http://www.advaita.org.uk/discourses/downloads/AnandaWoodNotesBySubjectWebDownload.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmananda_Krishna_Menon |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *interval between mentations* | The interval between mentations is Atmananda's phrase for the gap between two thoughts, which he says holds the pure 'I'. |
| *deep sleep as touchstone* | Deep sleep as touchstone is Atmananda's claim that the experience of deep sleep, understood rightly, is the clue to one's real nature. |

#### The direct path experiments (Greg Goode's forty experiments)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Advaita Vedānta (the direct path) |
| Tradition | The direct path (Atmananda Krishna Menon and successors) |
| Originators | Greg Goode, drawing on the direct path teachings of Atmananda Krishna Menon as elaborated by Jean Klein, John Levy, Alexander Smit, Philip Renard, Francis Lucille, and Rupert Spira |
| Date | 2012 (The Direct Path: A User Guide; an excerpt from the book published on the Awakening Clarity blog, 8 March 2012) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The book of experiments does the guiding with no live teacher, and the reader carries out each investigation in their own experience. |
| What happens | Goode's book presents a sequence of experiments that investigate sensation, perception, bodily feeling, waking and sleeping, emotion, attention, thought, and the sense of being a witness, proceeding from concrete to subtle objects. The reader is asked, for example, to stop and try to find any object that is apart from awareness. Goode calls the sequence a tattvopadeśa, a logically connected presentation of the teaching from beginning to end, and says the experiments are not ways to become awareness but ways to explore the consequences of being awareness. |
| Steps | (1) The reader begins with the most concrete experience of separation, such as an object in the world that seems other than the self. (2) The reader examines what is directly given in the experience of the object, such as color, texture, and taste, rather than the label. (3) The reader tries to find an object, any object, that is apart from awareness. (4) The reader moves in order to subtler objects: the body, thoughts, attention, and the witness itself. (5) The reader lets any new identification, even with awareness, drop away, which Goode calls right identification or no identification. |
| What is facilitated | The reader's own investigation of whether any object is found apart from awareness is facilitated, step by step, by a written sequence. The book supplies the order and the questions; Goode says the experiments explore what the reader already is rather than produce it. |
| Across scales | A graded series of self-administered observations that moves from easy to subtle cases is the structure of any workbook, and it holds wherever a reader can check each step against their own experience. It does not include a way for the reader to tell whether a step was understood, which the traditions assign to a teacher. |
| The evidence | The support is the author's own account of the method; I found no study of the experiments. Goode himself says that a person blocked by severe physical or mental pain should seek physical or mental therapy first, because nondual inquiry does not aim to improve the person. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Goode's book is a direct path text designed to be carried out alone, which is the closest existing counterpart to the book's form within the pointing-out traditions. Remaining the author of one's own change: Every experiment is performed and judged by the reader in their own experience, so the reader decides what they find. Harm and its conditions: Goode's own caution that severe pain calls for therapy first names a condition under which the experiments are not the right route. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Goode's book is written to guide the inquiry in print, and he counts "a book" among the things a reader may love along the way, which departs from the view of his lineage's founder, Atmananda, that a book is dead and a living guru is required. The book aims to guide a reader through an arc of experience in the world, whereas Goode's sequence proceeds from concrete objects until, in his account, one arrives at pure consciousness. |
| Limits and critiques | Goode states that the inquiry is not a treatment for severe physical or mental pain, for which therapy is the direct route. |
| Quoted from its sources | "What I mean by the “Direct Path” is the set of self-inquiry teachings attributed by Nitya Tripta to Shri Atmananda Krishna Menon and later elaborated upon by Jean Klein, John Levy, Alexander Smit, Philip Renard, Francis Lucille and Rupert Spira." (http://awakeningclarity.blogspot.com/2012/03/direct-path-users-guide-guest-teaching.html) "This can be experienced at any time if you just stop and try to find an object, any object that is apart from awareness." (http://awakeningclarity.blogspot.com/2012/03/direct-path-users-guide-guest-teaching.html) "they are simply ways to explore the consequences of being awareness." (http://awakeningclarity.blogspot.com/2012/03/direct-path-users-guide-guest-teaching.html) "Candidates include awareness, the teaching, the teacher, a book, Being, the universe, God, Krishna, Jesus, Buddha, or anything that represents the goal of your inquiry." (http://awakeningclarity.blogspot.com/2012/03/direct-path-users-guide-guest-teaching.html) "A person may want to do nondual inquiry, but may be blocked by severe physical or mental pain." (http://awakeningclarity.blogspot.com/2012/03/direct-path-users-guide-guest-teaching.html) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, attention awareness, body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/advaita reaches the uncovering move through neti neti and an ever-attained freedom.md`; This note holds Advaita's uncovering move, liberation never produced but only uncovered; Goode's experiments are a present-day written method for carrying out that uncovering, in the direct path line. |
| Sources | http://awakeningclarity.blogspot.com/2012/03/direct-path-users-guide-guest-teaching.html |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *tattvopadeśa* | Tattvopadeśa is a sequential, logically connected presentation of the teaching from the concrete to the subtle, which Goode uses as the structure of his experiments. |
| *wrong identification* | Wrong identification is the direct path's name, taken from Atmananda's Notes, for taking oneself to be a body or mind rather than awareness. |
| *standing as awareness* | Standing as awareness is Goode's phrase for knowingly occupying one's identity as awareness. |

#### The direct path as requiring no affiliation to a teacher or tradition

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Advaita Vedānta and Kashmir Śaivism (the direct path) |
| Tradition | The direct path (Atmananda Krishna Menon and successors) |
| Originators | Rupert Spira (born 1960), student of Francis Lucille, who received the teachings of Atmananda Krishna Menon and of Kashmir Śaivism from Jean Klein |
| Date | Spira met Lucille in 1997; statements from an interview with Mark Matousek published 10 November 2020 |
| Scale | a small group, a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The teacher shapes an inquiry in meetings, books, and recordings, and the listener tests it in their own experience. |
| What it holds | Spira describes the direct path as a contemporary formulation of the nondual understanding stripped of local and cultural traditions. He says it requires no affiliation to any teacher or tradition and nothing to believe in, and that teachings once given only in special, secret circumstances are now widely available. He also says teachings must give clear guidance that anyone can follow so that people have direct experiences for themselves, and he criticizes answering every question with the claim that there is nobody here and nothing to do. |
| What is facilitated | The listener's or reader's direct experience of their own nature is facilitated by clear guidance that anyone can follow without joining anything. Spira supplies the guidance; he says the experience must be the person's own. |
| Across scales | The move of detaching a practice from its founding institution so that anyone can take it up recurs whenever a discipline is popularized, and it holds wherever the practice does not depend on the institution's rites. The Dzogchen and Zen entries record the opposite position, that the teacher-student relationship conveys what the words cannot. |
| The evidence | The support is Spira's own interview statements and Wikipedia's biography; there is no study of his method. His lineage runs through Francis Lucille and Jean Klein, so his position is a development within the direct path rather than an independent tradition. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Spira removes affiliation and belief from the conditions of the practice, leaving the person to test the teaching in their own experience. Remaining the author of one's own change: His insistence that people have direct experiences for themselves keeps the verdict with the practitioner rather than with a lineage. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Spira holds that the teachings can now be widely available, and he teaches through books as well as meetings and retreats, which departs from Atmananda's view that books are dead and a living guru is required. His direct path still aims at recognizing pure consciousness as one's essential being, whereas the book leaves the recognized unnamed. |
| Limits and critiques | Without affiliation to a teacher there is no confirmation of the kind the Zen and Tibetan entries treat as essential. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The direct path requires no affiliation to any particular teacher or tradition. There is nothing to subscribe to, there is nothing to believe in or commit to." (https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-seekers-forum/202011/rupert-spira-the-direct-path-freedom) "For a very long time, these teachings were only given in special, secret circumstances." (https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-seekers-forum/202011/rupert-spira-the-direct-path-freedom) "Teachings need to be simple, direct, and easy. They need to provide clear guidance that anyone can follow so they have direct experiences for themselves." (https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-seekers-forum/202011/rupert-spira-the-direct-path-freedom) "I find it unsatisfactory to respond to every question with, “There’s nobody here and there’s nothing to do.”" (https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-seekers-forum/202011/rupert-spira-the-direct-path-freedom) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | identity self, attention awareness, spirit ultimate |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the book can only be auxiliary to a sovereign reader.md`; This note holds that the book can only serve a reader whose life remains their own; Spira's claim that the direct path needs no affiliation is a teacher's version of the same refusal of authority. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-seekers-forum/202011/rupert-spira-the-direct-path-freedom; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Spira |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *the direct path* | The direct path is the name used by Spira and others for the nondual teaching descended from Atmananda Krishna Menon, direct in the sense of not progressive. |
| *the Tantric path* | The Tantric path, in Spira's account, is the approach he met in his mid-thirties, which he pairs with Vedantic self-inquiry because it is stronger on turning back to integrate lived and felt experience. |

### The Headless Way (Douglas Harding)

#### The pointing experiment

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | The Headless Way (first-person experiments) |
| Tradition | The Headless Way (Douglas Harding) |
| Originators | Douglas Harding (1909–2007); presented today by Richard Lang at headless.org |
| Date | Harding's discovery 1942–1943; On Having No Head (1961); experiments developed in the late 1960s and early 1970s |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The experiment is written to be done by a reader alone, and Harding refused the role of guru; it is also led in workshops. |
| What happens | The reader points a finger at objects in the room and notices that pointing directs attention outward at things. The reader then points at the place where others see their face and looks at what the finger now points to, asking whether any face, color, shape, or movement is there. Harding and Richard Lang report finding no face but an open, aware capacity that is full of the scene, and Harding insisted that each person test this claim for themselves, saying "you are the sole and final authority on you". |
| Steps | (1) The reader points at objects in the room and notices that attention goes outward to things. (2) The reader points to their feet, legs, belly, and chest, and then to what is above that. (3) The reader turns the finger around: "Now point where others see your face." (4) The reader looks: "Do you see your face? Do you see anything at all there - any colour or shape, any movement?" (5) The reader notes what they find, and the instructions insist the reader actually do it: "it's imperative to actually DO the experiment." |
| What is facilitated | The reader's own look at the place they are looking from is facilitated, by the physical act of turning a pointing finger around. The experiment supplies a gesture and a question; Harding insists that the reader, not he, decides what is found. |
| Across scales | Reversing the direction of attention from objects to the one attending is the same turn made in Ramana's question and the Mahāmudrā lookings, and it holds wherever a single person can attend to their own point of view. It does not transfer to groups as such, although the tube experiment adapts it for two people and a questioner. |
| The evidence | The support is Harding's and Lang's first-person reports and participants' testimonials on headless.org; I found no controlled study of the experiments. Sam Harris, quoted in Wikipedia, reads Harding as describing a first-person view rather than a literal claim, and calls the emphasis on headlessness an unusually clear description of glimpsing the nonduality of consciousness. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Harding refused the role of guru and told people they were the sole and final authority on themselves, so the reader decides what the experiment shows. Participation rather than watching: The instructions insist that the experiment must be done and not just read, which is a text's own distinction between a participant and a spectator. Recognition: Harding's pages quote Wittgenstein's line that the most important aspects of things are hidden by their simplicity and familiarity, which is the vault's own reason why the always-present stays unseen. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Harding designed the experiments to be done from a book or a web page with no teacher, so his practice holds that a text can occasion the seeing, provided the reader performs it. Harding presented his claims as a hypothesis for each person to test rather than as a tradition's gift, whereas the book holds its company as observation rather than proof. |
| Limits and critiques | No teacher checks what the reader reports, so the experiment has no guard against a reader mistaking an idea about headlessness for the seeing it points to. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Now point where others see your face." (https://www.headless.org/experiments/pointing.htm) "Do you see your face? Do you see anything at all there - any colour or shape, any movement?" (https://www.headless.org/experiments/pointing.htm) "it's imperative to actually DO the experiment." (https://www.headless.org/experiments/pointing.htm) "The aspects of things that are most important for us are hidden because of their simplicity and familiarity" (https://www.headless.org/experiments/pointing.htm) "you are the sole and final authority on you" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Harding) "He rejected the role of 'guru', always pointing others back to themselves." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Harding) "Point to your feet, legs, belly, chest, then to what's above that. Go on looking at what your finger's now pointing to. Looking at what?" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Harding) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, attention awareness, body |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the always-there hides by being always there and wittgenstein said so without heidegger.md`; This note holds Wittgenstein's remark that the most important things are hidden by their familiarity; Harding quotes the same remark on the page of the pointing experiment, which is built to overcome exactly that hiding. |
| Sources | https://www.headless.org/experiments/pointing.htm; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Harding |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *headlessness* | Headlessness is Harding's word for the first-person observation that one does not see one's own head or face from where one looks. |
| *capacity* | Capacity is the Headless Way's word for the open awareness that Harding says one finds where others see a face. |
| *Seeing* | Seeing is Harding's name for noticing one's own headlessness directly rather than thinking about it. |

#### The tube experiment: face to no-face

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | The Headless Way (first-person experiments) |
| Tradition | The Headless Way (Douglas Harding) |
| Originators | Douglas Harding (1909–2007) |
| Date | Instructions written by Harding in the early 1970s; published at headless.org |
| Scale | three people, two people as equals |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. A third person asks the questions, and the two people in the tube supply all of the looking and the answers. |
| What happens | Two people, A and B, fit their faces into opposite ends of a paper tube, and a third person, C, slowly asks questions. The first questions ask how many faces there are in the tube, whether one's own end is closed or open, and whether one is face-to-face or face-to-no-face; later rounds ask whether one could register the other's face if one were not empty, and whether one can say to the partner and to all beings, "Here, I am you." Harding argued that the ordinary idea of a face-to-face meeting is not only mistaken but damaging, because it is confrontation. |
| Steps | (1) Two people fit their faces into the opposite ends of a paper tube; a third person holds the questions: "Get two friends (B and C) to help you (A)." (2) The questioner asks, going by what is given now: how many faces are in the tube, is your end closed or open, "Are you face-to-face or face-to-no-face?" (3) After a pause, the questioner asks whether one's own end is colored or colorless, opaque or transparent, moving or still, limited or boundless. (4) In a last round the questioner asks whether one can say to one's partner that one is their reality, and "Can you now say to all beings everywhere: 'Here, I am you'?" (5) The pair come out of the tube and ask whether what they found holds outside it; a participant's comment on the page suggests that a person alone can use a mirror at the far end instead of a partner. |
| What is facilitated | Each partner's noticing of the asymmetry between the other's visible face and their own open end is facilitated by the tube and by a third person's slow questions. The questioner supplies only the questions; the answers come from each person's looking. |
| Across scales | A structure in which a third person asks slow questions of two people looking at each other is a triad pattern found in mediation and couples work, and it holds where the third person stays neutral. Harding extends the claim from the pair to all beings, which goes beyond anything the experiment itself shows. |
| The evidence | The support is Harding's instructions and participants' testimonials on headless.org; I found no study of the experiment. Harding's claim that the face-to-face view underlies relationship problems and conflict is his interpretation, stated in a talk quoted on the page, not a tested finding. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The experiment reverses the usual picture of mutual gaze: each person sees the other's face and finds no face of their own, which reframes what being seen means. Conflict: Harding argued that the face-to-face picture is confrontation and that dropping it could ease conflict, a claim the book can quote as his and not as a finding. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tube experiment needs a partner and a questioner, and one participant's note on the page says a reader alone can use a mirror instead, so the practice allows a solitary version while designing for three people. Harding's aim is to show that one is capacity for the other rather than a face, whereas the book's account of being seen keeps two people who each remain themselves. |
| Limits and critiques | Harding's extension from the tube to the claim that face-to-face thinking causes personal and political conflict is his interpretation and is untested. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Get two friends (B and C) to help you (A)." (https://www.headless.org/experiments/the-tube.htm) "Are you face-to-face or face-to-no-face?" (https://www.headless.org/experiments/the-tube.htm) "Can you now say to all beings everywhere: 'Here, I am you'?" (https://www.headless.org/experiments/the-tube.htm) "if alone, you can use a mirror at the far end of the tube." (https://www.headless.org/experiments/the-tube.htm) "Well, my job is to question it, and say it's not only stupid, it's damaging—because it's confrontation." (https://www.headless.org/experiments/the-tube.htm) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, relationship present, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/jessica benjamin.md`; This note holds Jessica Benjamin's clinical account of mutual recognition between two subjects; Harding's tube experiment offers a contrasting first-person account in which each partner finds no face of their own. |
| Sources | https://www.headless.org/experiments/the-tube.htm; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Harding |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *face to no-face* | Face to no-face is Harding's description of the first-person view of a meeting, in which one sees the other's face and no face of one's own. |
| *space to face* | Space to face is a participant's phrase, on the experiment's page, for the same asymmetry between the open near end and the face at the far end. |

### Effortless mindfulness (Loch Kelly)

#### Glimpses and unhooking awareness from thought

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Effortless mindfulness (nondual mindfulness) |
| Tradition | Effortless mindfulness (Loch Kelly) |
| Originators | Loch Kelly, psychotherapist and meditation teacher, trained with Tulku Urgyen Rinpoche among others; founder of the Effortless Mindfulness Institute |
| Date | Kelly studied nondual meditation in Sri Lanka, India, and Nepal on a 1981–1982 fellowship and names Tulku Urgyen Rinpoche as a primary teacher; the method is taught in his book Shift into Freedom, at the Effortless Mindfulness Institute, and in the Mindful Glimpses app (current in 2026) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Kelly guides the glimpses live, in books, and in recorded app sessions, and the practitioner does the unhooking and notices what is found. |
| What happens | Kelly teaches short guided practices he calls glimpses, done with eyes open and seldom longer than a few minutes, in which the practitioner makes a small initial effort of letting go and unhooking awareness from thought, and then discovers that an awareness is already present that needs no effort. His site recounts his first meeting with the Dzogchen teacher Tulku Urgyen Rinpoche, who told him there are two types of mindfulness, deliberate and effortless, and whose direct pointer let him feel in minutes what he had felt at the end of a ten-day retreat. He recommends many small glimpses each day and combines the method with Internal Family Systems therapy. |
| Steps | (1) The practitioner makes a small initial effort to let go of thinking, which Kelly calls unhooking awareness from thought. (2) The practitioner discovers that the space around them is already aware, and rests in that awareness rather than in the thinking mind. (3) If doubt arises about doing it right, the practitioner treats the doubt as the small thinking mind offering a second opinion and steps back from it. (4) In parts work, the practitioner asks a distressed part, "Is this part aware of me?", to locate the self beyond the part. (5) The practitioner repeats small glimpses many times a day in ordinary activities. |
| What is facilitated | The practitioner's shift from thought-based attention to an awareness already present is facilitated, in minutes, by short guided instructions. Kelly supplies the instruction; he holds that the awareness found is not produced by the practice. |
| Across scales | Many brief resets of attention through the day, rather than long sessions, is a pattern shared with habit formation and with nervous system regulation practices, and it holds wherever a skill is built by frequent short repetitions. It stops holding where the glimpse is used to avoid feelings, which Kelly himself calls spiritual bypass. |
| The evidence | The support is Kelly's own teaching and reports of students; his site says he has collaborated with neuroscientists at Yale, the University of Pennsylvania, and New York University, but I did not find or read a study of glimpses. Kelly warns in his own writing against spiritual bypassing, in which meditation is used like a drug to numb or escape painful emotions. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Kelly's glimpses are a Dzogchen-derived pointing-out adapted for a reader or app user alone, which is the book's own situation. Knowing that something happened: Kelly tells practitioners to treat doubt about whether they did it right as the thinking mind's second opinion, which is a self-certifying instruction that the Zen and Tibetan entries would reject. Protection and defense: His warning about spiritual bypassing, and his pairing of glimpses with parts work, addresses the protective use of meditation to escape pain. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Kelly holds that glimpses can be learned from books, audio, and an app, so his method holds that a text or recording can occasion the shift, although it began for him with a teacher's pointer. He uses anupāya to mean a small initial effort open to everyone, whereas Abhinavagupta reserved the no-means for the most graced, and he frames the result as a healthier, more loving way of living as well as an awakening. |
| Limits and critiques | Kelly names spiritual bypassing as a pitfall: meditation used to numb, escape, or suppress painful emotions. |
| Quoted from its sources | "There are two types of mindfulness: deliberate and effortless." (https://lochkelly.org/about/) "In the first meeting Loch was introduced to this direct pointer that allowed him to feel in minutes, the same way he felt at the end of a 10 day retreat." (https://lochkelly.org/about/) "which means that we need to make a small initial effort of letting go, turning over, discovering" (https://lochkelly.org/effortless-mindfulness-and-living-in-the-flow/) "If this question occurs to you, it is your “small” thinking mind interjecting its doubt into the process." (https://lochkelly.org/faq) "Effortless Mindfulness can be done with your eyes open, and glimpse meditations seldom take more than a few minutes." (https://lochkelly.org/faq) "too often, meditation can become like a drug, helping us to numb, escape or suppress painful, troubling, or overwhelming emotions." (https://lochkelly.org/spiritual-bypass-meaning-definition-examples/) "Is this part aware of me?" (https://lochkelly.org/spiritual-bypass-meaning-definition-examples/) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self, trauma healing |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/the kashmir shaiva anupaya turns the negative way on method itself.md`; This note holds Abhinavagupta's anupāya as the no-means reserved for the most graced; Kelly uses the same word for a small effort anyone can make, which is a divergence the book should keep visible if it borrows either sense. |
| Sources | https://lochkelly.org/about/; https://lochkelly.org/effortless-mindfulness-and-living-in-the-flow/; https://lochkelly.org/faq; https://lochkelly.org/spiritual-bypass-meaning-definition-examples/; https://lochkelly.org/mindful-glimpses |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *glimpse* | A glimpse is Kelly's name for a short guided practice that shifts awareness out of thought into an awareness already present. |
| *effortless mindfulness* | Effortless mindfulness is Kelly's term for being aware of awareness itself, as distinct from deliberate mindfulness of the contents of experience. |
| *unhooking* | Unhooking is the small initial effort of detaching awareness from thought that begins a glimpse. |
| *anupāya, in Kelly's usage* | Kelly uses the Sanskrit word anupāya for the small initial effort of letting go, which differs from Abhinavagupta's anupāya, the no-means reserved for the most graced. |

### Kashmir Śaivism (Trika and Pratyabhijñā)

#### Gurur upāyaḥ, 'the guru is the means' (Śiva Sūtra II.6), in Kṣemarāja's two readings

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Kashmir Śaivism (Trika) |
| Tradition | Kashmir Śaivism (Trika and Pratyabhijñā) |
| Originators | The Śiva Sūtras attributed to Vasugupta (late 8th or early 9th century); Kṣemarāja's commentary, the Śivasūtravimarśinī (11th century) |
| Date | Sūtra c. 800; Kṣemarāja's commentary 11th century; English translation by P. T. Shrinivas Iyengar, 1912 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. In the first reading the human teacher reveals and shows; in the second the 'teacher' is the Lord's grace-bestowing power, which no person controls. |
| What it holds | The sūtra says in two words that the teacher is the means. Kṣemarāja's commentary gives two readings: first, the guru is the one who teaches the ultimate truth and is the means because he shows how the potencies of mantra and mudrā, discussed in the preceding sūtra, act; second, the guru is the Śakti of the supreme Lord, the root of grace, and the texts he cites place that power in the mouth of the guru, where it grants the disciple admittance. The sūtra stands in the Śiva Sūtras' second section, which the commentary and its translator assign to the śākta means (śāktopāya), and Kṣemarāja also cites the Spanda Kārikā's closing salute to the words of the guru as the boat across the ocean of doubt. |
| What is facilitated | The disciple's entry into recognition is facilitated either by a human teacher who shows the working of mantra and teaching, or by the Lord's grace, which Kṣemarāja calls the real guru that grants admittance. On the second reading no person supplies the grace, and the human teacher's speech is where the tradition says that power acts. |
| Across scales | The claim that learning a capacity needs someone who shows how it works holds for any embodied skill, and the first reading states it. The second reading, which makes the teacher an impersonal power, does not describe a scale at all but attributes the cause to something other than a person. |
| The evidence | The support is Kṣemarāja's commentary read in Iyengar's 1912 English translation from an archive.org scan; this is a translation more than a century old, and a modern edition should be checked before any line is set in print. The finding answers a question the vault has queued: the sūtra appears in a section devoted to one of a graded set of means, and its commentator extends the word 'teacher' to the Lord's grace itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: On the first reading the teacher reveals the truth and its working; on the second, grace decides, which removes the decision from both teacher and student. Recognition: Kṣemarāja's second reading makes the means of recognition the grace-bestowing power itself, so a human teacher conveys that power rather than being its source. The container that holds people while they change: The first reading makes the teacher the one who shows the working of the practice, which is a container supplied by a person who has done the practice. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition holds that the means is the teacher or the grace that acts through the teacher's words, and it places the sūtra in a graded scheme of means, so it does not offer a text read alone as the means; Kṣemarāja's second reading, however, makes the word 'guru' refer to grace itself rather than only to a human person. The book's participation law makes the reader the one who did it, which this sūtra would re-attribute to grace. |
| Quoted from its sources | "He is the means, because he shows how they act." (https://archive.org/download/zpgm-shiva-sutra-vimarshini-p.-t.-shrinivas-iyengar/Shiva%20Sutra%20Vimarshini%20-%20P.T.%20Shrinivas%20Iyengar_djvu.txt) "Or, the Guru is the Shakti of Paramēshvara, the root of grace." (https://archive.org/download/zpgm-shiva-sutra-vimarshini-p.-t.-shrinivas-iyengar/Shiva%20Sutra%20Vimarshini%20-%20P.T.%20Shrinivas%20Iyengar_djvu.txt) "It grants admittance to the disciple, hence it is the means." (https://archive.org/download/zpgm-shiva-sutra-vimarshini-p.-t.-shrinivas-iyengar/Shiva%20Sutra%20Vimarshini%20-%20P.T.%20Shrinivas%20Iyengar_djvu.txt) "The mouth of the Guru is said to be the Shakti-chakra." (https://archive.org/download/zpgm-shiva-sutra-vimarshini-p.-t.-shrinivas-iyengar/Shiva%20Sutra%20Vimarshini%20-%20P.T.%20Shrinivas%20Iyengar_djvu.txt) "I salute the words of the Guru, who is the boat with which we cross the deep ocean of doubt, full of wonderful meanings." (https://archive.org/download/zpgm-shiva-sutra-vimarshini-p.-t.-shrinivas-iyengar/Shiva%20Sutra%20Vimarshini%20-%20P.T.%20Shrinivas%20Iyengar_djvu.txt) "deals with the Shaktopaya and this is therefore a Mantrashastra." (https://archive.org/download/zpgm-shiva-sutra-vimarshini-p.-t.-shrinivas-iyengar/Shiva%20Sutra%20Vimarshini%20-%20P.T.%20Shrinivas%20Iyengar_djvu.txt) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, relationship present, learning skill |
| In the survey | Guru–shishya relationship (paramparā); this entry adds kṣemarāja's own commentary on the sūtra, with its second reading of the teacher as the Lord's grace-bestowing power, and the sūtra's place in the section on the śākta means. |
| In the vault | `notes/the kashmir shaiva tradition would decline four of the book's deepest moves.md`; This note cites 'the teacher is the means' as the tradition's refusal of the book alone as vehicle, from vault paraphrase; the second person survey (ops/second person ecology.md, question 3) queued a reading of Kṣemarāja's commentary, which this entry supplies in Iyengar's translation. |
| Sources | https://archive.org/download/zpgm-shiva-sutra-vimarshini-p.-t.-shrinivas-iyengar/Shiva%20Sutra%20Vimarshini%20-%20P.T.%20Shrinivas%20Iyengar_djvu.txt; https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.40271/40271_djvu.txt; https://iep.utm.edu/kashmiri/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *gurur upāyaḥ* | Gurur upāyaḥ is the Sanskrit sūtra 'the guru is the means,' Śiva Sūtra II.6. |
| *the Śakti of Parameśvara* | The Śakti of Parameśvara is the supreme Lord's power, which Kṣemarāja's second reading calls the root of grace and identifies as the guru. |
| *śāktopāya* | Śāktopāya is one of the tradition's means to recognition, the one that works through mantra, and the subject of the Śiva Sūtras' second section, in which this sūtra stands. |
| *mantra-vīrya* | Mantra-vīrya is the potency of mantra, which the first reading says the teacher shows the disciple. |

#### The grades of śaktipāta and which of them need a teacher

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Kashmir Śaivism (Trika) |
| Tradition | Kashmir Śaivism (Trika and Pratyabhijñā) |
| Originators | Abhinavagupta (c. 975–1025), Tantrāloka; as presented by Swami Lakshman Joo (Kashmir Shaivism: The Secret Supreme) and John Dupuche (2003); summarized by Jaideva Singh (1963) |
| Date | c. 1000 (Tantrāloka); modern presentations 1963–2003 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | not applicable. The grades describe kinds of recipients of grace rather than a way of working. |
| What it holds | The tradition classifies the descent of grace (śaktipāta) by intensity, and in Lakshman Joo's presentation the grades differ in what the recipient needs. At the most intense grades the person needs nothing external, and one grade is liberated on their own through intuition (pratibhā) without initiation or instruction from an outside guru; at the next, a touch, a look, or simply being in the master's presence is enough; at the middle grades the person needs a guru's initiation and instruction. Abhinavagupta, as Dupuche reports, distinguishes those whose intuition is firm from those whose intuition is hesitant, who take a guru, study the scriptures, or practice yoga. |
| What is facilitated | The grades specify what a person's recognition needs from outside: nothing, a teacher's presence, or a teacher's initiation and instruction, and for the hesitant, scripture study or yoga. The tradition holds that grace, not the teacher or the text, is what descends; the teacher and the text serve those graced less intensely. |
| Across scales | A classification of recipients by readiness, each grade needing a different amount of outside help, resembles any graded model of learners, and it holds wherever readiness varies. It stops being usable in practice where readiness cannot be observed beforehand. |
| The evidence | The grades come from Abhinavagupta's Tantrāloka through modern presentations quoted in Wikipedia; I did not read the Tantrāloka itself. The sources disagree on grace and merit: the vault records the Tantrāloka as holding grace independent of effort or merit, while Jaideva Singh's introduction to the Pratyabhijñāhṛdayam says that grace is not caprice and has to be earned by moral and spiritual discipline, the means serving to make one fit to receive it. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The tradition itself allows a grade of person who recognizes without any outside teacher, through intuition, which gives the book's solitary reader a place in the source tradition's own classification. Who decides the content: Grace decides the grade, so neither teacher nor recipient chooses how much outside help is needed. Remaining the author of one's own change: Singh's statement that grace must be earned by discipline and the vault's reading that grace is independent of merit give opposite answers to how much the person authors their own change. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The tradition allows scripture study as one support for those whose intuition is hesitant and allows the most graced to need no teacher at all, but it does not say a text alone brings recognition to anyone who reads it; the text serves a person already graced. The book offers its occasion to every reader, whereas the tradition sorts readers by a grace no book can confer. |
| Quoted from its sources | "It cannot be imposed by force, nor can a receiver make it happen." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktipata) "such a being becomes spiritually illuminated and liberated on his own, relying directly on" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktipata) "but he does not need instruction, but a simple touch, a look or simply being in the presence of his master is enough" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktipata) "those whose intuition is hesitant, who need more practice in order to stabilize; they would take a" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktipata) "study the scriptures or practice yoga" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktipata) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | nested levels; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, learning skill, relationship present |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/both poles of the convergence refuse the engineered epiphany.md`; This note holds the Tantrāloka's nine intensities of grace and grace's independence from merit, from secondary sources; this entry adds which grades need a guru, the clause about scripture for those of hesitant intuition, and Singh's contrary statement that grace must be earned. The second person survey (ops/second person ecology.md, question 4) had asked whether any grade requires a guru. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktipata; https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.40271/40271_djvu.txt; https://iep.utm.edu/kashmiri/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *śaktipāta* | Śaktipāta is the descent of grace, which the tradition says cannot be imposed by force and cannot be made to happen by the receiver. |
| *tīvra, madhya, manda* | Tīvra, madhya, and manda mean intense, middle, and moderate; combined with one another, they name the grades of grace. |
| *pratibhā* | Pratibhā is intuition, the self-arising insight by which the most graced recipients are liberated without an outside teacher. |

#### The recognition treatise as a means that leads students to recognition

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Kashmir Śaivism (Pratyabhijñā) |
| Tradition | Kashmir Śaivism (Trika and Pratyabhijñā) |
| Originators | Utpaladeva (c. 925–975), Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā; Abhinavagupta (c. 975–1025), its commentator; Kṣemarāja (c. 1000–1050), Pratyabhijñāhṛdayam; interpreted by David Peter Lawrence and Isabelle Ratié (2011) |
| Date | 10th–11th century; Jaideva Singh's translation of the Pratyabhijñāhṛdayam first published 1963; Lawrence's encyclopedia article and Ratié's study (2011) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. A treatise does the work, but it is addressed to readers already moved by grace and read within a tradition of teachers. |
| What it holds | Utpaladeva's treatise presents itself as a rational inquiry using only direct experience and inference, and Abhinavagupta reads the whole work as a single inference for others, an argument set out for another person to follow. David Peter Lawrence writes that Utpaladeva and Abhinavagupta conceived the system both as philosophical argument and as an internalized form of tantric ritual that leads students directly to identification with Śiva. Kṣemarāja opens his short summary by saying it is written for devoted people untrained in logic who aspire to immersion in the Lord, an immersion that, in Singh's translation, blossoms forth with the descent of Śakti; Singh's introduction describes these readers as inclined to the teaching by divine grace. |
| What is facilitated | A reader's recognition is facilitated by argument that leads them to see, step by step, that their own experience expresses what the tradition calls 'I am Śiva'. The treatise supplies the argument; Kṣemarāja's preface addresses readers whose aspiration comes with grace that has already descended, which no text supplies. |
| Across scales | An argument written so that a reader reaches its conclusion in their own understanding is the form of any demonstration, and it holds wherever the conclusion can be checked against the reader's own experience. The tradition's own framing shows where it stops: the demonstration serves a reader in whom grace has already produced the longing. |
| The evidence | The support is the texts' own framing, read through Singh's translation and through scholarship by Lawrence (in the Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy) and Ratié (as cited in Wikipedia); I did not read Ratié's book itself. Singh's introduction adds that the doctrine was for centuries imparted as a secret teaching to aspirants who lived and tested it, and that liberation does not come from logic-chopping but from the descent of grace. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The school that named recognition built treatises meant to lead readers to it, which is the closest precedent for the book's own claim that reading can be remembering. Participation rather than watching: An inference for others works only if the reader follows each step, so the treatise asks for the reader's participation rather than assent. Who decides the content: The treatise argues to a conclusion fixed in advance, 'I am Śiva', whereas the book leaves what is recognized unnamed. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | This tradition comes closest to holding that a text can occasion recognition, since its treatises are designed to lead students to it, but Kṣemarāja addresses readers on whom grace has already descended, and the treatises were read inside a lineage of teachers, so a text alone for an unprepared reader is not what they describe. The treatises also argue to a named conclusion and stake themselves on its truth, which the book's stance declines. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In this world, there are some devoted people, who are undeveloped in reflection and have not taken pains in studying difficult works like Logic and Dialectics" (https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.40271/40271_djvu.txt) "blossoms forth with the descent of Sakti" (https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.40271/40271_djvu.txt) "For centuries, it was imparted as a secret doctrine to the aspirant who had to live it and test it in the laboratory of the Self." (https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.40271/40271_djvu.txt) "for those who are inclined as a result of Divine grace to know the main principles of" (https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.40271/40271_djvu.txt) "Utpaladeva and Abhinavagupta ambitiously conceive the Pratyabhijnā system as both a philosophical apologetics (which follows Sanskritic standards of scholastic argument) and an internalized form of tantric ritual that leads students directly to identification with Shiva." (https://iep.utm.edu/kashmiri/) "Abhinavagupta reads the treatise as a single "inference for others"" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utpaladeva) "presents itself as a rational inquiry relying only on the two means of knowledge accepted by all Indian philosophers: direct experience and inference." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utpaladeva) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, identity self, learning skill |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/isabelle ratie.md`; This note records that Ratié's piece on the treatise as itself a means was not located; this entry records Abhinavagupta's reading of the treatise as one inference for others (Ratié 2011, via Wikipedia) and Lawrence's statement that the system leads students directly to identification with Śiva. The note on the entry texts each tradition wrote for itself (notes/both poles of the convergence supply their own front doors.md) already holds Kṣemarāja's address to readers untrained in logic. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.40271/40271_djvu.txt; https://iep.utm.edu/kashmiri/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utpaladeva |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *parārthānumāna (inference for others)* | An inference for others is an argument set out step by step so that another person can reach the conclusion, which is how Abhinavagupta reads Utpaladeva's treatise. |
| *pratyabhijñā* | Pratyabhijñā is recognition, the school's name for liberation as recognizing oneself as the Lord. |
| *samāveśa* | Samāveśa is immersion in the Lord, which Kṣemarāja's intended readers aspire to and which, in his opening, blossoms forth with the descent of grace. |

#### Shaktipat Intensives: śaktipāta offered to newcomers

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| Field | Modern śaktipāta lineage (Siddha Yoga), drawing on Kashmir Śaivism |
| Tradition | Kashmir Śaivism (Trika and Pratyabhijñā) |
| Originators | Swami Muktananda (1908–1982), founder of Siddha Yoga, who received śaktipāta from Bhagawan Nityananda in 1947 |
| Date | 1956–1982 (Muktananda's teaching period); allegations published 1981–1994 (Stan Trout's letter, 1981; William Rodarmor, 1983; Lis Harris, 1994) |
| Scale | a large group, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. The guru is held to transmit the awakening, and participants receive it. |
| What happens | Muktananda offered śaktipāta initiation, the awakening of what the tradition calls kuṇḍalinī, to large numbers of people at events called Shaktipat Intensives. Wikipedia's article records that such initiation had historically been reserved for the few who had done many years of service and practice, and that Muktananda offered it to newcomers and yogis alike. Scholars and former devotees later reported that he engaged in secret sexual relations with young female disciples, some of them teenagers, and that people who spoke of it were threatened and harassed into silence. |
| Steps | (1) Participants attend a program called a Shaktipat Intensive. (2) The guru gives shaktipat, which the tradition holds can be transmitted by a sacred word or mantra, a look, a thought, or a touch. (3) Participants are said to receive initiation, the awakening of kuṇḍalinī, and to deepen their practice of Siddha Yoga meditation. |
| What is facilitated | The method claimed to facilitate a participant's spiritual awakening through the guru's transmission, offered at scale to anyone who came. The reports show that the same guru authority made the sexual exploitation of young disciples easier. |
| Across scales | Scaling a rite once reserved for prepared individuals to large groups changes who receives it and what preparation surrounds it, a change that recurs whenever an intensive practice is popularized. The case shows the risks concentrating in the leader who confers the experience. |
| The evidence | The practice is documented in Siddha Yoga's own history and in scholarship (Williamson 2010; Jain 2014); the allegations rest on Rodarmor's 1983 article based on interviews, Lis Harris's 1994 New Yorker article, and scholars including Sarah Caldwell, who concluded that he engaged in actions that were not ethical, legal or liberatory with many disciples. No study of what the intensives produced exists that I found. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The case joins an experience-conferring guru, a practice opened to unprepared newcomers, and reported sexual abuse with pressure to stay silent. Who decides the content: In the intensives the guru is held to give the awakening itself, the opposite of the book's refusal to claim that anything it does causes the reader's change. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Siddha Yoga held that awakening passes from a living guru's presence, so it does not treat a text read alone as able to confer it; its widening of access resembles the book's offer to everyone while keeping the guru as the source. The book claims no power to confer anything, which removes this kind of authority and its misuse. |
| Limits and critiques | Former devotees and scholars reported sexual relations with young disciples, including teenagers, and threats and harassment to silence those who spoke of them. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Historically, Shaktipat initiation had been reserved for the few who had done many years of spiritual service and practices; Muktananda offered this initiation to newcomers and yogis alike." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muktananda) "engaged in actions that were not ethical, legal or liberatory with many disciples." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muktananda) "Muktananda stressed the value of celibacy for making progress on the spiritual path, but he almost certainly violated his own rules." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muktananda) "from the teachings of his guru, Nityananda, and philosophical and practical traditions that preceded him, especially premodern hatha yoga, Vedanta, and Kashmir Shaivism." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siddha_Yoga) |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, body, justice power |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `archive/reading — abhinavagupta on the anupaya (the no-means).md`; This reading record holds that the tradition's no-means is an endpoint for the most graced and not a democratic starting posture; Muktananda's intensives are a modern case of a formerly reserved rite offered to newcomers at scale, with documented harm. |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muktananda; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktipata; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siddha_Yoga |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *shaktipat* | Shaktipat is the transmission of spiritual energy from guru to disciple, held to occur by a word, a look, a thought, or a touch. |
| *Shaktipat Intensive* | A Shaktipat Intensive is Siddha Yoga's program at which participants were said to receive shaktipat initiation. |
| *kuṇḍalinī* | Kuṇḍalinī is the energy said to lie dormant in a person and to be awakened by shaktipat. |

### Research across traditions

#### Pointers mistaken for nondual awareness, and post-hoc labeling in self-reports

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| Field | Neuroscience and phenomenology of meditation |
| Tradition | Research across traditions |
| Originators | Zoran Josipovic (Frontiers in Psychology, 2025) |
| Date | 2025 |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | not applicable. The paper states problems for research and self-report rather than a way of working. |
| What it holds | Josipovic's opinion paper on researching nondual awareness warns that the methods used to reach it can be mistaken for it in participants' reports. He writes that conflations with pointing-out instructions are especially hard to see through when nondual awareness is confused with natural wakefulness, the expanse of sky, the first moment of perception, nowness, or ineffability, and he likens this to mistaking the finger pointing at the moon for the moon. He also warns that states induced by contemplative practice or by substances are labeled nondual awareness after the fact, although, in his account, it is no small feat for this awareness to recognize itself without concepts. |
| What is facilitated | The paper concerns what pointing facilitates and what it does not: a pointer directs attention, and the danger is that the practitioner or researcher takes the pointer's content for the awareness pointed to. Neither a pointer nor a researcher supplies the recognition, which he describes as awareness recognizing itself. |
| Across scales | The confusion of an indicator with the thing it indicates recurs in any measurement that relies on a proxy, and it holds wherever a report is shaped by the words used to elicit it. It becomes severe at the scale of research samples, where many participants' reports share the vocabulary of the same instructions. |
| The evidence | This is an opinion paper by a researcher with over forty years of contemplative practice, not an experiment; it draws on the research literature and on the traditions' own warnings. A 2026 preprint by Czajko and colleagues reports brain network differences in expert practitioners during a nondual practice, which shows the construct is being measured while its self-report remains contested. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The paper names, from inside research, the counterfeit the traditions warn of: a report of recognition that is really a report of the pointer's imagery. Recognition: Josipovic describes realization as awareness recognizing itself and cites Kṣemarāja for the term self-recognition, which connects the research vocabulary to the book's source tradition. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The paper does not take a position on whether a text alone can occasion recognition, but its warning applies to any text of pointers, since a reader can mistake the pointers' images for what they point to. It aims at better experiments, whereas the book aims at a reader's own recognition. |
| Limits and critiques | The paper's own caution is that self-reports of nondual awareness are vulnerable to conflation and after-the-fact labeling. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Mistaking such pointers for nondual awareness is akin to mistaking the proverbial finger pointing at the moon for the moon itself." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12685637/fullTextXML) "Conflations with various pointing-out instructions are especially difficult to see through when nondual awareness is conflated with" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12685637/fullTextXML) "labeling of various states induced via contemplative practice or by mind-altering substances as nondual awareness." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12685637/fullTextXML) "While nondual awareness is ubiquitous in experience, it is ordinarily only implicit, and it is no small feat for it to directly recognize itself without being mediated by concepts and other mental representations." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12685637/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | the life sciences. Josipovic writes as a neuroscientist and long-term contemplative practitioner, in what he calls a neurophenomenological and nondual contemplative approach |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | attention awareness, identity self |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `notes/awareness has a reflexive dimension distinct from its illuminating one.md`; This note holds the tradition's distinction between illuminating awareness and awareness aware of itself; Josipovic makes that reflexivity the defining property of nondual awareness and warns that pointers to it are easily mistaken for it. |
| Name | This name is a plain description; the practitioner gives the move no name. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12685637/fullTextXML; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=PPR1271362&format=json&resultType=core |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *nondual awareness* | Nondual awareness is Josipovic's term for consciousness knowing itself without mental representations or a subject-object structure. |
| *non-representational reflexivity* | Non-representational reflexivity is his name for awareness knowing itself without forming a representation of itself, which he says others have called self-recognition. |
| *post-hoc labeling* | Post-hoc labeling is his term for calling a state nondual awareness after the fact because of the practice or substance that induced it. |

#### Progress or pathology: how Buddhist teachers appraise students' difficult experiences

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a finding |
| Field | Religious studies and clinical psychology (qualitative interview study) |
| Tradition | Research across traditions |
| Originators | J. R. Lindahl, D. J. Cooper, N. E. Fisher, L. J. Kirmayer, and W. B. Britton (the Varieties of Contemplative Experience project, Brown University) |
| Date | 2020 (Frontiers in Psychology), drawing on the 2017 Varieties of Contemplative Experience study |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a community or public |
| Who decides | not applicable. The study reports how teachers and practitioners judge experiences rather than a way of working. |
| What it holds | The study interviewed Western practitioners and teachers in the Theravāda, Zen, and Tibetan Buddhist traditions about how they tell experiences that count as progress from ones that signal psychopathology or need intervention. It found that the task is complicated by Buddhist worldviews that expect some distressing experiences as part of progress, that 55 of the project's 59 categories of experience were appraised as expected but extraneous to development, as signs of progress, or as indications of potentially concerning psychopathology, and that decisions depended on context. One practitioner reported asking her teacher whether a distressing change in her sense of self was enlightenment and being told that it was not, because she was suffering too much. |
| What is facilitated | The teacher's appraisal facilitates a practitioner's decision about what an experience means and whether to seek help, and the study shows teachers treating distress both as expected and as a possible sign that intervention is needed. The teacher supplies the interpretation that the practitioner cannot supply alone. |
| Across scales | Deciding whether a hard experience is growth or harm depends on context at every scale, from a person's retreat to a community's norms, and the study shows the community's worldview shaping the individual verdict. It stops where no interpreter is present, which is the situation of a person practicing from a book. |
| The evidence | The study is qualitative, based on interviews with a purposive sample of mostly white practitioners from the United States, 72% of whose challenges began on intensive retreat, so it describes how judgments are made rather than how often they are right. It extends the 2017 Varieties of Contemplative Experience study, which the vault holds at second hand. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The study documents teachers telling a student that an experience was not enlightenment because she was suffering too much, which is a present-day instance of the confirming and disconfirming role. Harm and its conditions: A worldview that expects distress as progress can delay recognizing an experience as harmful, which is a harm condition for any practice that promises a descent. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The study does not address whether a text alone can occasion recognition; it shows that the interpretation of difficult experiences in these traditions routinely passes through a teacher, which a book cannot provide. It is concerned with safety and diagnosis, not with occasioning recognition. |
| Limits and critiques | The study documents that some difficult meditation experiences need intervention and that the traditions' expectation of distress can complicate recognizing them. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The use of certain existing criteria is complicated by Buddhist worldviews that some difficult or distressing experiences may be expected as a part of progress on the contemplative path." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7403193/fullTextXML) "No, it wasn’t enlightenment because you were suffering too much." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7403193/fullTextXML) "while some distress was normalized as part of the path, teachers and practitioners alike also looked to distress as potentially indicating a need for intervention" (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7403193/fullTextXML) |
| Mental model | other. The study combines religious studies with clinical psychology, using qualitative interviews with Western Buddhist practitioners and teachers |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | trauma healing, spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| In the survey | no |
| In the vault | `ops/reading queue.md`; The reading queue holds the 2017 Varieties of Contemplative Experience study (entry 17) at second hand, with its question whether any diagnostic is usable by a person alone; this 2020 paper from the same project reports how teachers and practitioners actually make the judgment. |
| Sources | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC7403193/fullTextXML |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *differential diagnosis* | Differential diagnosis, here, is telling religiously significant experiences apart from psychopathology. |
| *appraisal* | An appraisal is the judgment a practitioner or teacher makes about what an experience means and whether it needs intervention. |
